Jump to:

These notes assume no prior knowledge and are built backwards from what the written exam actually asks. Each chapter follows the same rhythm: first the intuition (why the idea exists, explained in plain language with an everyday analogy before any symbols appear), then the definitions (every symbol explained before use), then the central results, then the derivations that examiners ask you to reproduce (written as explicit numbered steps, each with the reasoning behind it, not just the algebra), then a box listing the real exam question patterns, and finally the common mistakes that cost marks. Past exams themselves are for practice — these notes deliberately explain the method rather than walk through full past questions, so that the practice papers stay genuinely useful once you sit down to solve them yourself.

The single most important fact about this exam: it rewards reproducible derivations, not memorised trivia. Roughly 80\

1Foundations: Derivatives, Payoffs and No-Arbitrage (background for every exam)

The Core Idea

Start with the simplest possible picture. A derivative is a contract whose value comes entirely from the price of something else: a stock, a currency, an interest rate, a barrel of oil. It is a side bet on an asset you may never actually own. A call option on a stock, for example, is like a refundable deposit on a house: you pay a small amount now for the right to buy later at a price fixed today. If the market turns against you, you simply walk away and lose only the deposit.

The question this whole course answers is: what should that deposit cost? The answer never involves guessing where the stock is going. It rests on one principle, so important that almost every formula you will learn is just this idea wearing a different costume: no arbitrage, i.e. there is no free lunch. If two portfolios are guaranteed to produce the same payoff in the future, they must cost exactly the same today. If they did not, you could buy the cheap one, sell the expensive one, and pocket a riskless profit — something markets do not allow to persist.

This gives you a universal recipe, usable on almost any exam question: build a portfolio out of things you can already trade that copies the derivative's payoff exactly; the derivative must then cost the same as that portfolio. This is called replication. Forward prices, binomial trees, Black–Scholes, bond-pricing PDEs, CDS spreads: all of them are found by replication, dressed up in progressively fancier mathematics. Whenever you get lost in the algebra later in the course, come back to this one sentence.

The second idea you need before anything else is discounting: a krone tomorrow is worth less than a krone today, simply because today's krone can be put in the bank and earn interest at the risk-free rate \(r\). So a sure payment of 1 received at time \(T\) is worth only \(e^{-rT}\) today — less the further away \(T\) is, and less the higher \(r\) is. Every price in this course is, deep down, a discounted amount: a bond price is a discounted certain payment, and a derivative price is a discounted expected payoff. The subtlety that occupies the rest of the course is: expected under which probabilities?

Key Definitions and Notation

SymbolMeaning
\(S_t\) (or \(S\))price of the underlying asset at time \(t\)
\(K\)strike (exercise) price, or delivery price of a forward
\(T\)maturity/expiration date; \( = T-t\) is time to maturity
\(r\)continuously compounded risk-free interest rate
\(c_t,\ p_t\)prices of a European call and put at time \(t\)
\(C_t,\ P_t\)prices of American call and put (when the distinction matters)
\(F_0,\ F(t,T)\)forward price agreed at time \(0\) (resp. \(t\)) for delivery at \(T\)
\(f_t\)value of an already existing forward contract at time \(t\)
\(P(t,T)\)price at \(t\) of a zero-coupon bond paying 1 at \(T\)
\((x)^+\)\((x,0)\)
  • Long = you own the asset/claim (you gain if the price rises). Short = you owe it (you gain if the price falls).
  • A forward contract is an agreement, made today, to buy (long) or sell (short) the underlying at a fixed future date \(T\) for a fixed delivery price \(K\). It costs nothing to enter: \(K\) is chosen as the forward price \(F_0\) so that the initial value is zero. Payoff to the long at \(T\): \(S_T - K\).
  • A futures contract is an exchange-traded forward: standardised, marked to market daily (gains/losses settled every day through margin accounts), with a clearing house eliminating counterparty risk.
  • A European call (put) gives the right, not the obligation, to buy (sell) the underlying at strike \(K\) at time \(T\). Payoffs: \(c_T = (S_T-K)^+\), \(p_T = (K-S_T)^+\). An American option can be exercised at any time up to \(T\).
  • A swap exchanges two streams of cash flows, e.g. fixed-rate interest against floating-rate interest (interest rate swap) or premium payments against default protection (credit default swap).
  • Moneyness: a call is in the money if \(S>K\), at the money if \(S\approx K\), out of the money if \(S<K\) (reversed for puts).
  • Arbitrage: a strategy with zero initial cost, no possibility of loss, and a positive probability of gain. The no-arbitrage principle says such strategies cannot persist in equilibrium.
  • Law of one price: two portfolios with identical payoffs in every future state must have identical prices today.

Central Results

Present value / discounting

With a constant continuously compounded rate \(r\), the value today of receiving 1 for sure at time \(T\) is \[ P(0,T) = e^{-rT}. \] Why this shape: \(e^{rT}\) is what 1 unit grows to under continuous compounding (\(\lim_{m\to\infty}(1+r/m)^{mT}\)); discounting is just growth run backwards.

Put--call parity (Europeans, no dividends)

\[ c + Ke^{-rT} = p + S_0 . \] Reading: "call plus cash" \(=\) "put plus stock". Both sides are worth \(\max(S_T,K)\) at maturity, so they must cost the same today. With a continuous dividend yield \(q\), replace \(S_0\) by \(S_0e^{-qT}\).

Option price bounds and early exercise
  • \(c \le S_0\) and \(p \le Ke^{-rT}\); \(c \ge (S_0 - Ke^{-rT})^+\) and \(p \ge (Ke^{-rT}-S_0)^+\).
  • An American call on a non-dividend stock is never exercised early: \(C = c\). Intuition: exercising early pays \(S-K\); the live option is worth at least \(S - Ke^{-r\tau} > S-K\), i.e. selling beats exercising — exercising throws away the time value of the strike and the insurance against the stock falling.
  • An American put may be exercised early (deep in the money, getting \(K\) now beats \(K\) later): \(P \ge p\).

Key Derivations

Proof of put–call parity. This is the simplest possible example of the replication recipe above, and it is the template every later proof in the course follows: build two portfolios, check they pay the same in every future state, conclude they cost the same today.
  1. Build two portfolios today. Portfolio A: buy one European call, and put cash \(Ke^{-rT}\) in the bank (it grows to exactly \(K\) by time \(T\)). Portfolio B: buy one European put, and buy one share of the stock.
  2. Work out what each portfolio pays at maturity \(T\), case by case. If the stock ends up above the strike (\(S_T>K\)): the call is worth \(S_T-K\) and the cash has grown to \(K\), so A pays \((S_T-K)+K=S_T\); the put is worthless and you hold the share, so B pays \(0+S_T=S_T\). If the stock ends up at or below the strike (\(S_T\le K\)): the call is worthless and the cash is still \(K\), so A pays \(0+K=K\); the put pays \(K-S_T\) and you hold the share worth \(S_T\), so B pays \((K-S_T)+S_T=K\).
  3. Compare. In both cases A and B pay exactly the same amount, \(\max(S_T,K)\). Two portfolios with identical payoffs in every possible future state are, by the law of one price, worth the same today.
  4. Conclude. Their costs today must match: \(c + Ke^{-rT} = p + S_0\).
Why must the law of one price hold? If, say, A were more expensive than B today, you would sell A (receive its price), buy B (pay its price), and pocket the difference immediately. At \(T\) the two portfolios pay exactly the same, so whatever A owes you, B covers exactly — you locked in a riskless profit with no capital of your own. That is precisely what "no arbitrage" rules out, and it is what forces the formula to hold.

Exam Questions — What They Actually Ask

Exam Pattern
  • "Define a forward contract / a European call option / a swap." (definitions worth easy marks — give the payoff formula too)
  • "Draw the payoff diagram of a long call / short put / long forward."
  • "State and prove put–call parity." (the two-portfolio table above is the answer)
  • "Explain why an American call on a non-dividend-paying stock should not be exercised early."
  • "What is an arbitrage opportunity?" — always give the three-part definition (zero cost, no downside, possible upside).

Common Mistakes

Common Mistakes
  • Forgetting to discount the strike: parity uses \(Ke^{-rT}\), never plain \(K\).
  • Confusing the payoff of an option (\((S_T-K)^+\), at maturity) with its profit (payoff minus the future value of the premium) — exam payoff diagrams usually ask for the payoff unless "profit" is explicit.
  • Claiming American puts are never exercised early — that result holds only for calls on non-dividend stocks.
2Forward and Futures Pricing (15/21 exams)

The Core Idea

Suppose gold costs \(S_0\) today and you promise, in a contract signed today, to deliver one ounce in exactly one year. What price should you charge for that ounce, to be paid on delivery? The tempting instinct is to forecast where gold will be in a year and charge that. This instinct is wrong, and seeing why is the whole point of this chapter.

You do not need a forecast at all, because there is a strategy that removes all uncertainty: buy the ounce today for \(S_0\), financed by borrowing that amount at the risk-free rate \(r\), hold it for the year, and simply hand it over at delivery. You know today, with certainty, exactly what this costs you by the delivery date: the loan has grown to \(S_0e^{rT}\). Since this strategy is available to anyone, competition forces the forward price to be exactly this all-in cost: \[ F_0 = S_0e^{rT}. \] This is the cleanest possible example of pricing by replication: the forward price is not a prediction of the future, it is simply today's spot price plus the cost of financing it until delivery (the "cost of carry"). Anything that makes holding the asset cheaper while you wait — dividends paid by a stock, interest earned on a foreign currency, the convenience of having physical oil in your tank — lowers the forward price, because you effectively get paid to wait. Anything that makes holding costlier, such as storage fees for a commodity, raises it.

Tiny numeric check. \(S_0=100\), \(r=5\%\), \(T=1\): \(F_0 = 100\,e^{0.05\times1} = 100\times1.0513 = 105.13\). If someone quoted you a forward price of 110, you could borrow 100, buy the asset, sell the forward at 110, and at \(T\) deliver the asset while repaying \(100e^{0.05}=105.13\) — a riskless profit of \(110-105.13=4.87\) per contract, with no capital of your own. That is exactly the arbitrage the derivation below rules out.

The second question exams love: once a forward contract already exists, what is it worth part-way through its life? At the moment you sign it, its value is zero by construction (nobody pays anything to enter). Later on, the market's current forward price \(F_t\) will have drifted away from the delivery price \(K\) you locked in. Your existing contract is now worth the discounted difference \(f_t = (F_t - K)e^{-r(T-t)}\): you are guaranteed to end up \(F_t-K\) ahead of a brand-new contract at delivery, and a sure amount to be received at \(T\) is worth its discounted value today.

Key Definitions and Notation

  • \(F_0 = F(0,T)\): the delivery price that makes a new forward contract worth zero today ("the forward price").
  • \(K\): the delivery price written into an existing contract (fixed once and for all at inception).
  • \(f_t\): the market value at time \(t\) of an existing (long) forward with delivery price \(K\).
  • \(I\): present value of known discrete income (e.g. dividends in cash) paid by the asset during the contract's life.
  • \(q\): continuous dividend/income yield of the asset; for a currency, \(q = r_f\), the foreign risk-free rate.
  • \(u\): storage cost expressed as a continuous yield; \(y\): convenience yield (benefit of physical possession).
  • \(r_d, r_f\): domestic and foreign risk-free rates; \(S_0\) for FX is the spot exchange rate in units of domestic currency per unit of foreign currency.

Central Results

Forward prices --- the cost-of-carry family

\[ \begin{array}{ll} \text{No income:} & F_0 = S_0e^{rT} \\[2pt] \text{Known cash income (PV \(=I\)):} & F_0 = (S_0 - I)\,e^{rT} \\[2pt] \text{Continuous yield } q: & F_0 = S_0\,e^{(r-q)T} \\[2pt] \text{Currency (interest-rate parity):} & F_0 = S_0\,e^{(r_d-r_f)T} \\[2pt] \text{Commodity (storage \(u\), convenience \(y\)):} & F_0 = S_0\,e^{(r+u-y)T} \end{array} \] All are one formula: forward \(=\) spot grown at the net cost of carry. Income received while holding the asset (\(q\), \(r_f\), \(y\)) enters with a minus sign; costs of holding (\(r\), \(u\)) with a plus sign.

Value of an existing forward

For a long forward with delivery price \(K\) and maturity \(T\), at any time \(t\le T\): \[ f_t = (F_t - K)\,e^{-r(T-t)}, \] where \(F_t = F(t,T)\) is the current forward price. Equivalently \(f_t = S_t e^{-q(T-t)} - Ke^{-r(T-t)}\) for an asset with yield \(q\). A short forward is worth \(-f_t\). At inception \(K=F_0\) so \(f_0 = 0\).

Forwards vs. futures

With deterministic interest rates, forward and futures prices coincide. With stochastic rates they differ slightly: daily marking-to-market means a long futures receives cash exactly when rates are high (if \(S\) and \(r\) are positively correlated), which is valuable, so then futures price \(>\) forward price. In this course you may normally treat them as equal unless the question asks for this discussion.

Key Derivations

Derivation of \(F_0 = S_0e^{rT}\) (cash-and-carry). To earn full marks you must show both directions of the arbitrage — one direction alone only proves an inequality, not the equality.
  1. Suppose the forward is quoted too high, \(F_0 > S_0e^{rT}\). Today, do three things simultaneously, at zero net cost: borrow \(S_0\) from the bank, use it to buy the asset, and short (sell) the forward contract. At \(T\): hand over the asset under the forward contract and receive \(F_0\) for it; separately, repay the loan, which has grown to \(S_0e^{rT}\). Your net cash at \(T\) is \(F_0 - S_0e^{rT} > 0\) — a riskless profit from zero initial investment. This is an arbitrage, so this situation cannot persist.
  2. Suppose the forward is quoted too low, \(F_0 < S_0e^{rT}\). Run the mirror strategy: today, short-sell the asset (borrow it and sell it for \(S_0\)), invest the proceeds at rate \(r\), and go long the forward. At \(T\): your deposit has grown to \(S_0e^{rT}\); you pay \(F_0\) under the forward and receive the asset, which you use to close out the short sale. Net cash at \(T\) is \(S_0e^{rT} - F_0 > 0\), again riskless.
  3. Combine. Since neither \(F_0>S_0e^{rT}\) nor \(F_0<S_0e^{rT}\) can survive in an arbitrage-free market, the only possibility left is equality: \(F_0 = S_0e^{rT}\).
  4. With an income yield \(q\) (e.g. dividends). Instead of buying 1 unit of the asset today, buy only \(e^{-qT}\) units and continuously reinvest the income back into more units; by time \(T\) this has grown to exactly 1 unit through reinvestment alone. The upfront cost is only \(S_0e^{-qT}\), financed at \(r\), so the all-in cost at \(T\) becomes \(S_0e^{-qT}e^{rT} = S_0e^{(r-q)T}\).

Derivation of \(f_t = (F_t-K)e^{-r(T-t)}\). At some time \(t\) before maturity, you hold an old forward contract with delivery price \(K\) (fixed when you signed it) and want its current value \(f_t\). Compare it to a brand-new forward signed today (at time \(t\)) with delivery price \(F_t\) (today's forward price); that new contract is worth exactly 0, by the definition of a forward price.

  1. Build a portfolio: go long your old contract (delivery price \(K\)) and short a freshly written contract (delivery price \(F_t\)).
  2. At maturity \(T\), the old contract pays \(S_T - K\) and the new short contract pays \(-(S_T-F_t)\). Add them: \((S_T-K) - (S_T-F_t) = F_t - K\). The random part \(S_T\) cancels exactly, leaving a certain amount known already at time \(t\).
  3. A certain payment of \(F_t-K\) received at \(T\) is worth its discounted value today: \((F_t-K)e^{-r(T-t)}\).
  4. This portfolio's value today is (value of old contract) \(-\) (value of new contract) \(= f_t - 0 = f_t\). Equating with step 3 gives \(f_t = (F_t-K)e^{-r(T-t)}\).

Exam Questions — What They Actually Ask

Exam Pattern
  • "Define a forward contract and explain how the forward price is determined." (definition + cash-and-carry both directions)
  • "Find the no-arbitrage forward price of a stock paying dividends / a currency / a commodity with storage costs."
  • "Six months into the contract the spot has moved to … Find the value of the forward position." (\(f_t\) formula)
  • "Explain the difference between forward and futures contracts." (OTC vs. exchange, daily settlement, margins, counterparty risk)
  • "What is the convenience yield and why can the futures curve be in backwardation?" (\(y>r+u\) makes \(F_0<S_0\))

Common Mistakes

Common Mistakes
  • Using the original forward price where the current one belongs: the value formula is \(f_t = (F_t - K)e^{-r(T-t)}\) with \(F_t\) recomputed from today's spot.
  • Sign errors on carry: dividends, foreign interest and convenience yield reduce \(F_0\); storage costs increase it.
  • Writing \(f_t = F_t - K\) without discounting — the difference is only realised at \(T\), so it must be discounted back.
3The Binomial Model and State Prices (8/21 exams)

The Core Idea

The binomial model squeezes the entire theory of derivative pricing into the simplest possible setting, which is exactly why it is taught first. Pretend that over the next short period the stock can do only one of two things: move up by a factor \(u\), or move down by a factor \(d\). That sounds far too crude to be useful, but crude is the point: with only two possible future outcomes, and two things you can trade (the stock, and a risk-free bond), you have exactly enough freedom to build a portfolio that reproduces any option's payoff perfectly in both outcomes. Two unknowns (how much stock, how much cash), two equations (match the payoff in the up-state, match it in the down-state) — always solvable. No-arbitrage then forces the option's price to equal the cost of building that replica. Chain together many short periods, each with a smaller up/down move, and this crude model converges to Black–Scholes.

Here is the surprising punchline that the entire rest of the course is built on. When you actually solve those two equations, the real-world probability that the stock goes up never enters the formula at all. Two traders who violently disagree about whether the stock will rise must nonetheless agree on the option's price, because the replication argument works regardless of anyone's opinion. Instead, the algebra produces an artificial probability \(q\) (with \(q\) for up, \(1-q\) for down) under which the stock's expected return would be exactly the risk-free rate \(r\) — even though nobody actually believes this is the real probability. Every derivative price then equals the discounted expected payoff, but computed using these artificial risk-neutral probabilities instead of real ones. This is your first encounter with the risk-neutral measure \(Q\), a concept that will reappear in every remaining chapter.

A useful alternative viewpoint: let \(\phi_u\) be today's price of a security that pays exactly 1 if the up-state occurs and 0 otherwise (called an Arrow–Debreu security, or a state price), and define \(\phi_d\) the same way for the down-state. Think of \(\phi_u\) and \(\phi_d\) as a menu of prices for pure "state lottery tickets." Once you know this menu, pricing any payoff \((V_u,V_d)\) is just shopping: buy \(V_u\) tickets for the up-state and \(V_d\) tickets for the down-state, total cost \(V_u\phi_u+V_d\phi_d\).

Key Definitions and Notation

  • \(S_0\): stock price now; after one step of length \(\Delta t\) it is \(S_0u\) (up) or \(S_0d\) (down), with \(d < a < u\) where \(a = e^{r\Delta t}\).
  • \(u, d\): up and down multipliers; \(r\): risk-free rate, continuous compounding; \(a = e^{r\Delta t}\): the growth factor of cash over one step (use \(a = e^{(r-q)\Delta t}\) for a stock with dividend yield \(q\), and \(a=1\) for a futures price).
  • \(V_u, V_d\) (or \(c_u, c_d\)): derivative payoffs in the up/down state.
  • \(\Delta\): number of shares in the replicating portfolio; \(B\): cash position.
  • \(q_u\) (often just \(q\)): risk-neutral probability of the up-move; \(q_d = 1-q_u\).
  • \(\phi_u, \phi_d\): state (Arrow–Debreu) prices of the up- and down-state.
  • CRR calibration: to match a lognormal volatility \(\sigma\), take \(u = e^{\sigma\sqrt{\Delta t}}\), \(d = 1/u = e^{-\sigma\sqrt{\Delta t}}\).

Central Results

One-period binomial pricing

The replicating portfolio \(\Delta\) shares \(+\) cash \(B\) satisfies \(\Delta S_0u + Be^{r\Delta t} = V_u\) and \(\Delta S_0d + Be^{r\Delta t} = V_d\), giving \[ \Delta = \frac{V_u - V_d}{S_0(u-d)}, \qquad q = \frac{a - d}{u - d},\quad a = e^{r\Delta t}, \] \[ V_0 = \Delta S_0 + B = e^{-r\Delta t}\bigl[q\,V_u + (1-q)\,V_d\bigr]. \] Reading: price \(=\) discounted expectation under \(q\). The shape of \(q\): it is exactly the weight that makes the stock's expected growth equal the risk-free growth, \(qS_0u + (1-q)S_0d = S_0a\). Absence of arbitrage \(\iff d < a < u \iff 0 < q < 1\).

State prices and the fundamental theorem

\[ \phi_u = e^{-r\Delta t}\,q, \qquad \phi_d = e^{-r\Delta t}(1-q), \qquad V_0 = V_u\phi_u + V_d\phi_d . \] Consistency checks worth quoting in an exam: \(\phi_u + \phi_d = e^{-r\Delta t}\) (a sure 1 is a zero-coupon bond) and \(\phi_u S_0u + \phi_d S_0d = S_0\) (the stock prices itself). Fundamental theorem of asset pricing: no arbitrage \(\iff\) strictly positive state prices exist \(\iff\) an equivalent risk-neutral measure \(Q\) exists; the market is complete \(\iff\) they are unique.

Multi-period trees and American options

Work backwards from maturity. European: at each node \(V = e^{-r\Delta t}[qV_u + (1-q)V_d]\). American: at each node \[ V = \max\Bigl(\underbrace{\text{exercise value}}_{(K-S)^+ \text{ for a put}},\; \underbrace{e^{-r\Delta t}[qV_u+(1-q)V_d]}_{\text{continuation value}}\Bigr). \] As the number of steps \(n\to\infty\) with CRR calibration, the European call price converges to the Black–Scholes formula.

Key Derivations

Deriving \(q=(a-d)/(u-d)\) from replication, step by step. We want a portfolio of \(\Delta\) shares plus cash \(B\) that pays exactly \(V_u\) if the stock goes up and \(V_d\) if it goes down.
  1. Write the two matching equations. If the stock moves up, the portfolio is worth \(\Delta S_0u + Be^{r\Delta t}\), and this must equal \(V_u\). If it moves down, \(\Delta S_0d + Be^{r\Delta t} = V_d\). Two linear equations, two unknowns (\(\Delta\) and \(B\)).
  2. Solve for \(\Delta\). Subtract the down-equation from the up-equation: the \(B\) terms cancel, leaving \(\Delta S_0(u-d) = V_u - V_d\), so \[ \Delta = \frac{V_u-V_d}{S_0(u-d)}. \]
  3. Solve for \(B\). Substitute \(\Delta\) back into either equation and isolate \(B\); after simplifying, \[ B = e^{-r\Delta t}\,\frac{uV_d - dV_u}{u-d}. \]
  4. The option price is the cost of building this portfolio today: \(V_0 = \Delta S_0 + B\). Substitute the expressions for \(\Delta\) and \(B\) from steps 2–3 and collect all terms multiplying \(V_u\) together, and all terms multiplying \(V_d\) together: \[ V_0 = e^{-r\Delta t}\left[\frac{a-d}{u-d}\,V_u + \frac{u-a}{u-d}\,V_d\right], \qquad a = e^{r\Delta t}. \]
  5. Name the coefficients. Define \(q := (a-d)/(u-d)\). Check that the other coefficient is automatically \(1-q\): \(\frac{u-a}{u-d} = 1 - \frac{a-d}{u-d} = 1-q\). So the price is \(V_0 = e^{-r\Delta t}\bigl[qV_u + (1-q)V_d\bigr]\) — a discounted expectation, with \(q\) playing the role of a probability (and indeed \(0<q<1\) exactly when \(d<a<u\)).

Tiny numeric example. Let \(S_0=100\), \(u=1.2\), \(d=0.9\), \(r=5\%\), \(\Delta t=1\) year, and price a call with strike \(K=100\).

  1. Growth factor: \(a = e^{0.05} = 1.0513\).
  2. Risk-neutral probability: \(q = \dfrac{a-d}{u-d} = \dfrac{1.0513-0.9}{1.2-0.9} = \dfrac{0.1513}{0.3} = 0.5043\).
  3. Terminal stock prices: up state \(S_u=120\), down state \(S_d=90\). Call payoffs: \(V_u=(120-100)^+=20\), \(V_d=(90-100)^+=0\).
  4. Discounted expected payoff: \(V_0 = e^{-0.05}\bigl[0.5043\times20 + 0.4957\times0\bigr] = 0.9512\times10.086 = 9.59\).

Exam Questions — What They Actually Ask

Exam Pattern
  • "In a one/two-step tree with given \(S_0,u,d,r\), price a European call/put." (risk-neutral probabilities + backward induction)
  • "Find the replicating portfolio \((\Delta, B)\) at time 0."
  • "Find the state prices \(\phi_u, \phi_d\) and use them to price the claim."
  • "Price the American put in the same tree; at which nodes is early exercise optimal?"
  • "Show that no arbitrage requires \(d < e^{r\Delta t} < u\)."

Common Mistakes

Common Mistakes
  • Using \(\Delta t = T\) instead of \(T/n\) in \(a = e^{r\Delta t}\) — the single most common numerical slip in two-step questions.
  • Treating \(q\) as a real-world probability, or "estimating" it from the stock's expected return \(\mu\). \(q\) comes only from \(a,u,d\).
  • For American options, forgetting to compare exercise vs. continuation at intermediate nodes (not just at maturity).
4GBM, Stochastic Calculus and Itô's Lemma (19/21 exams)

The Core Idea

To price options when time flows continuously (not in discrete steps like the binomial tree), we first need a mathematical description of how a stock price wiggles from one instant to the next. The building block is Brownian motion, written \(z_t\): imagine a drunkard taking an enormous number of infinitesimally small, independent steps, some left, some right. Over any interval of length \(\Delta t\), his total displacement turns out to be normally distributed with mean 0 and variance \(\Delta t\) — the longer you wait, the further he might have wandered, but the expected direction is nowhere in particular.

There is one strange, crucial fact about this randomness that you must simply accept and get comfortable with: the wiggles are so violent, and there are so infinitely many of them, that the square of the noise over a small interval \(\mathrm{d} t\) is not negligible — it behaves exactly like \(\mathrm{d} t\) itself: \((\mathrm{d} z)^2 = \mathrm{d} t\). In ordinary calculus, squared small quantities vanish (if \(\mathrm{d} t\) is small, \((\mathrm{d} t)^2\) is negligibly smaller). Here that intuition fails for \(\mathrm{d} z\). This single fact is the reason ordinary calculus does not work for random processes, and it is exactly what forces Itô's lemma to have an extra term beyond the usual chain rule.

Stock prices cannot go negative, and what actually matters to an investor is the percentage return, not the price change in kroner. So instead of modelling the price directly, we model the return: \(\mathrm{d} S/S = \mu\,\mathrm{d} t + \sigma\,\mathrm{d} z\). This is geometric Brownian motion (GBM): in each tiny instant, the stock earns a predictable drift \(\mu\,\mathrm{d} t\) plus a random shock whose standard deviation is \(\sigma\sqrt{\mathrm{d} t}\). When you solve this equation (see the derivations below), it turns out that \(\ln S_T\) — the logarithm of the future price — is normally distributed. Equivalently, the price itself is lognormal: it can never go negative, and it is skewed, with a long right tail (occasional very large gains) but a floor at zero.

Itô's lemma is the chain rule adapted to this random world. Suppose \(S\) follows GBM, and you care about some smooth function of it, \(G(S,t)\) — this could be an option price, or \(\ln S\), or a bond yield. Ordinary calculus would say \(G\)'s change is just \(G_t\,\mathrm{d} t + G_S\,\mathrm{d} S\). Itô's lemma says you must add one more term: \(\tfrac12 G_{SS}(\mathrm{d} S)^2\). Using \((\mathrm{d} S)^2 = \sigma^2S^2\,\mathrm{d} t\) (from the strange fact above), this extra term becomes \(\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2G_{SS}\,\mathrm{d} t\) — a purely deterministic addition to the drift, caused entirely by the curvature of \(G\) (its second derivative \(G_{SS}\)). Intuitively: if \(G\) is convex, a random up-move helps you more than an equally-sized down-move hurts you, so on average, noise gets converted into extra upward drift. This one lemma is the single most frequently used tool on the exam — some version of it appears in nearly every past paper.

Key Definitions and Notation

  • \(z_t\) (also written \(W_t\)): a Wiener process / Brownian motion: \(z_0=0\); increments \(z_{t+\Delta t}-z_t \sim N(0,\Delta t)\), independent across non-overlapping intervals; continuous paths. Informally \(\mathrm{d} z = \epsilon\sqrt{\mathrm{d} t}\) with \(\epsilon\sim N(0,1)\).
  • Multiplication table (memorise): \((\mathrm{d} z)^2 = \mathrm{d} t\), \(\mathrm{d} z\,\mathrm{d} t = 0\), \((\mathrm{d} t)^2 = 0\).
  • Generalised Wiener process: \(\mathrm{d} x = a\,\mathrm{d} t + b\,\mathrm{d} z\) with constants \(a\) (drift) and \(b\) (volatility coefficient).
  • Itô process: \(\mathrm{d} x = a(x,t)\,\mathrm{d} t + b(x,t)\,\mathrm{d} z\) — drift and volatility may depend on the state and time.
  • GBM: \(\mathrm{d} S = \mu S\,\mathrm{d} t + \sigma S\,\mathrm{d} z\); \(\mu\) = expected return (drift, per year), \(\sigma\) = volatility (per \(\sqrt{\text{year}}\)).
  • \(G(S,t)\) (or \(G(x,t)\)): any twice-differentiable function of the process and time; subscripts denote partials: \(G_S = \partial G/\partial S\), etc.

Central Results

Itô's lemma --- THE exam tool

If \(\mathrm{d} x = a\,\mathrm{d} t + b\,\mathrm{d} z\) and \(G = G(x,t)\), then \[ \mathrm{d} G \;=\; \Bigl(\frac{\partial G}{\partial t} + a\,\frac{\partial G}{\partial x} + \tfrac12 b^2\,\frac{\partial^2 G}{\partial x^2}\Bigr)\mathrm{d} t \;+\; b\,\frac{\partial G}{\partial x}\,\mathrm{d} z . \] For GBM (\(a = \mu S\), \(b = \sigma S\)): \[ \mathrm{d} G = \Bigl(G_t + \mu S\,G_S + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\,G_{SS}\Bigr)\mathrm{d} t + \sigma S\,G_S\,\mathrm{d} z . \] Why the shape: Taylor-expand \(G\) to second order, \(\mathrm{d} G = G_t\mathrm{d} t + G_x\mathrm{d} x + \tfrac12 G_{xx}(\mathrm{d} x)^2 + \dots\), and use \((\mathrm{d} x)^2 = b^2(\mathrm{d} z)^2 = b^2\mathrm{d} t\) (everything of higher order dies). The Itô correction \(\tfrac12 b^2G_{xx}\,\mathrm{d} t\) is the fingerprint of randomness.

Solution of GBM and lognormality

Applying Itô to \(G=\ln S\) (worked below) gives \(\mathrm{d}\ln S = (\mu - \tfrac12\sigma^2)\mathrm{d} t + \sigma\,\mathrm{d} z\), hence \[ S_T = S_0\exp\Bigl[(\mu - \tfrac12\sigma^2)T + \sigma z_T\Bigr], \qquad \ln S_T \sim N\Bigl(\ln S_0 + (\mu-\tfrac12\sigma^2)T,\ \sigma^2T\Bigr), \] and \(\mathrm{E}[S_T] = S_0e^{\mu T}\). Note the famous gap: the median growth rate is \(\mu-\tfrac12\sigma^2\), less than the mean rate \(\mu\), because a few huge lognormal outcomes drag the mean up. The \(-\tfrac12\sigma^2\) is pure Itô correction (with \(G=\ln S\) concave, noise subtracts drift).

Standard Itô applications --- learn these transformations

Given \(\mathrm{d} S = \mu S\,\mathrm{d} t + \sigma S\,\mathrm{d} z\):

\[ \begin{array}{lll} G = \ln S: & \mathrm{d} G = (\mu - \tfrac12\sigma^2)\,\mathrm{d} t + \sigma\,\mathrm{d} z & \text{(constant coefficients!)}\\[3pt] G = S^n: & \mathrm{d} G = \bigl(n\mu + \tfrac12 n(n-1)\sigma^2\bigr)G\,\mathrm{d} t + n\sigma\,G\,\mathrm{d} z & \text{(again GBM, new coefficients)}\\[3pt] G = 1/S: & \mathrm{d} G = (-\mu + \sigma^2)\,G\,\mathrm{d} t - \sigma\,G\,\mathrm{d} z & \text{(the } n=-1 \text{ case)}\\[3pt] G = e^{aS}: & \mathrm{d} G = \bigl(a\mu S + \tfrac12 a^2\sigma^2S^2\bigr)G\,\mathrm{d} t + a\sigma S\,G\,\mathrm{d} z & \\[3pt] G = Se^{r(T-t)}: & \mathrm{d} G = (\mu - r)G\,\mathrm{d} t + \sigma G\,\mathrm{d} z & \text{(forward price of }S) \end{array} \] Bond-flavoured favourites: if \(\mathrm{d} P = \mu_P P\,\mathrm{d} t + \sigma_P P\,\mathrm{d} z\) for a bond price \(P(t,T)\), then the yield \(R = -\ln P/(T-t)\) follows (Itô applied to \(G(P,t) = -\ln P/(T-t)\)): \[ \mathrm{d} R = \frac{1}{T-t}\Bigl(R - \mu_P + \tfrac12\sigma_P^2\Bigr)\mathrm{d} t \;-\; \frac{\sigma_P}{T-t}\,\mathrm{d} z . \]

Shortcomings of GBM (a recurring essay part)
  • Constant volatility contradicted by data: volatility clusters in time and varies with strike/maturity (the implied-volatility smile/skew).
  • Normal log-returns underestimate extremes: empirical returns have fat tails and skewness; crashes (jumps) occur far more often than GBM allows. Remedies: jump-diffusions, stochastic volatility (e.g. Heston), local volatility.
  • Continuous paths: no jumps, so sudden news moves are impossible in the model.
  • Constant \(r\) and \(\mu\); no dividends unless added; independence of increments rules out momentum/mean reversion; and for interest rates, GBM is inappropriate because rates mean-revert and do not grow exponentially.

Key Derivations

Itô's lemma applied to \(G=\ln S\) — the single most important computation in the course, worked in full. This exact four-step routine (identify \(\to\) differentiate \(\to\) substitute \(\to\) simplify) is what you repeat for every Itô question, whatever \(G\) is.
  1. Identify the process for \(S\). We are given \(\mathrm{d} S = \mu S\,\mathrm{d} t + \sigma S\,\mathrm{d} z\), i.e. in the general notation \(\mathrm{d} S = a\,\mathrm{d} t + b\,\mathrm{d} z\) with \(a=\mu S\) and \(b=\sigma S\).
  2. Compute the three partial derivatives of \(G=\ln S\). Treat \(S\) as the variable and differentiate: \(G_S = \dfrac{\partial}{\partial S}\ln S = \dfrac1S\); \(G_{SS} = \dfrac{\partial}{\partial S}\Bigl(\dfrac1S\Bigr) = -\dfrac1{S^2}\); and since \(\ln S\) has no explicit \(t\)-dependence, \(G_t = 0\).
  3. Substitute into Itô's lemma, \(\mathrm{d} G = \bigl(G_t + aG_S + \tfrac12 b^2G_{SS}\bigr)\mathrm{d} t + bG_S\,\mathrm{d} z\): \[ \mathrm{d} G = \Bigl(0 + \mu S\cdot\frac1S + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\cdot\Bigl(-\frac1{S^2}\Bigr)\Bigr)\mathrm{d} t + \sigma S\cdot\frac1S\,\mathrm{d} z . \]
  4. Simplify. The \(S\)'s cancel term by term: \(\mu S\cdot\frac1S=\mu\); \(\sigma^2S^2\cdot\frac1{S^2}=\sigma^2\); \(\sigma S\cdot\frac1S=\sigma\). This leaves \[ \mathrm{d}(\ln S) = \Bigl(\mu - \tfrac12\sigma^2\Bigr)\mathrm{d} t + \sigma\,\mathrm{d} z . \] Notice the coefficients are now constants (no more \(S\)), which is exactly what makes the next step possible.
  5. Integrate. A process with constant drift \(m=\mu-\tfrac12\sigma^2\) and constant volatility \(\sigma\) over a period of length \(T\) simply accumulates: \(\ln S_T = \ln S_0 + mT + \sigma z_T\), where \(z_T\sim N(0,T)\). Exponentiating gives \(S_T = S_0\exp\bigl[(\mu-\tfrac12\sigma^2)T + \sigma z_T\bigr]\), confirming \(S_T\) is lognormal.
Every other transformation in the table above (\(S^n\), \(1/S\), \(e^{aS}\), a forward price, a bond yield) is produced by exactly this same five-step routine, only with different partial derivatives in step 2.

Exam Questions — What They Actually Ask

Exam Pattern
  • "Assume \(\mathrm{d} S = \mu S\,\mathrm{d} t + \sigma S\,\mathrm{d} z\). Use Itô's lemma to find the process followed by \(G\)" with \(G = \ln S,\ S^n,\ 1/S,\ e^{aS},\ S^2\), a forward price \(Se^{r(T-t)}\), a bond price \(P(r,t)\), \(\ln P\), or the yield \(R(t,T)\).
  • "Show that \(S_T\) is lognormally distributed and give \(\mathrm{E}[S_T]\)."
  • "Define a Wiener process / an Itô process."
  • "What are the shortcomings of GBM as a model of stock prices?"
  • "Is \(G\) itself a GBM? What are its drift and volatility?" (for \(G = S^n\): yes, with drift \(n\mu + \tfrac12n(n-1)\sigma^2\) and volatility \(n\sigma\))

Common Mistakes

Common Mistakes
  • Dropping the Itô correction \(\tfrac12b^2G_{SS}\,\mathrm{d} t\) (i.e. using the ordinary chain rule). This is the error the question is designed to catch.
  • Writing the correction as \(\tfrac12\sigma^2G_{SS}\) instead of \(\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2G_{SS}\): the diffusion coefficient of GBM is \(b=\sigma S\), so it enters squared as \(\sigma^2S^2\).
  • Sign slip in \(G=\ln S\): \(G_{SS} = -1/S^2\), which produces the famous minus \(\tfrac12\sigma^2\).
  • Confusing \(\mathrm{E}[S_T] = S_0e^{\mu T}\) with \(S_0e^{(\mu-\sigma^2/2)T}\) (the latter is the median, i.e. \(e^{\mathrm{E}[\ln S_T]}\)).
5The Market Price of Risk (14/21 exams)

The Core Idea

Imagine two shops selling the same brand of coffee, but in different package sizes: one sells 250g bags, the other 1kg bags. Whatever the price on the label, the price per kilogram has to be the same in both shops — otherwise customers would simply buy from the cheap shop and effectively arbitrage the price difference away. The market price of risk is exactly this idea, applied to risk instead of coffee.

Suppose two securities \(f\) and \(g\) are both exposed to the same single source of randomness, the same Brownian motion \(z\) (think: both derivatives written on the same underlying source of uncertainty). They can have completely different expected returns \(\mu_f,\mu_g\) and completely different volatilities \(\sigma_f,\sigma_g\) — but their excess return per unit of volatility (the "price per kilogram of risk") must be identical: \[ \lambda = \frac{\mu_f - r}{\sigma_f} = \frac{\mu_g - r}{\sigma_g}. \] Here \(\lambda\) is the extra return the market pays you, above the risk-free rate, for bearing one unit of exposure to the risk factor \(z\). If \(f\) paid more per unit of risk than \(g\) did, you could go long \(f\) and short \(g\) in exactly the right proportions to make the random \(\mathrm{d} z\) terms cancel, leaving a riskless portfolio — and if that riskless portfolio earned more than the bank account, that would be an arbitrage. The derivation below makes this precise.

Why does this one idea matter enough to get its own chapter? Because it is the bridge connecting the real world (where different assets really do have different expected returns \(\mu\)) to the risk-neutral world (where, for pricing purposes, we pretend every asset drifts at \(r\)). If you take an asset's real-world dynamics and substitute \(\mu = r + \lambda\sigma\), then shift the Brownian motion by \(\lambda\) (this shift is Girsanov's theorem, covered properly in the Martingales chapter), every asset's drift becomes exactly \(r\). This is what justifies "discount the expected payoff at the risk-free rate" as a valid pricing recipe — it works because \(\lambda\) is the same number for every asset driven by that particular source of risk, so it can be absorbed once and for all into a change of probability measure.

There is one more subtlety examiners love to probe. In interest-rate or commodity models, the state variable driving everything (the short rate \(r\), a convenience yield) is often not itself something you can buy and sell. You cannot walk into a shop and buy "one unit of the short rate." When the underlying risk factor is not traded, no-arbitrage arguments alone cannot pin down \(\lambda\) — it survives in the pricing equations as a free parameter that must be estimated from data or calibrated to match observed prices.

Key Definitions and Notation

  • \(f, g\): prices of two traded securities (derivatives) driven by the same single Brownian motion \(z\): \[ \mathrm{d} f = \mu_f f\,\mathrm{d} t + \sigma_f f\,\mathrm{d} z, \qquad \mathrm{d} g = \mu_g g\,\mathrm{d} t + \sigma_g g\,\mathrm{d} z . \] \(\mu_f,\mu_g\): expected returns; \(\sigma_f,\sigma_g\): volatilities (possibly functions of state and time).
  • \(\lambda\): the market price of risk of the factor \(z\) — excess expected return per unit volatility, common to all assets driven by \(z\).
  • \(\theta\): a state variable (e.g. the short rate, a commodity convenience yield) with dynamics \(\mathrm{d}\theta = m\,\mathrm{d} t + s\,\mathrm{d} z\); it may or may not be a traded asset.
  • Real-world (physical) measure \(P\); risk-neutral measure \(Q\); under \(Q\) the Brownian motion is \(z^Q\) with \(\mathrm{d} z^Q = \mathrm{d} z + \lambda\,\mathrm{d} t\).

Central Results

Market price of risk

All traded securities exposed only to the Brownian motion \(z\) satisfy \[ \frac{\mu_f - r}{\sigma_f} = \lambda \quad\text{for a common }\lambda(t), \qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad \mu_f = r + \lambda\,\sigma_f . \] Shape of the formula: it is a security-market-line in continuous time — expected return \(=\) risk-free rate \(+\) (price of risk)\(\times\)(quantity of risk).

Risk-neutral dynamics

Setting \(\mathrm{d} z^Q = \mathrm{d} z + \lambda\,\mathrm{d} t\) (Girsanov) converts real-world dynamics into risk-neutral dynamics: \[ \mathrm{d} f = \mu_f f\,\mathrm{d} t + \sigma_f f\,\mathrm{d} z = (\mu_f - \lambda\sigma_f)f\,\mathrm{d} t + \sigma_f f\,\mathrm{d} z^Q = r f\,\mathrm{d} t + \sigma_f f\,\mathrm{d} z^Q . \] Under \(Q\) every traded asset drifts at \(r\); volatility is unchanged. For a non-traded state variable \(\theta\) with \(\mathrm{d}\theta = m\,\mathrm{d} t + s\,\mathrm{d} z\), the risk-neutral drift is \(m - \lambda s\): \[ \mathrm{d}\theta = (m - \lambda s)\,\mathrm{d} t + s\,\mathrm{d} z^Q . \] Because \(\theta\) itself is not an asset one can buy, no-arbitrage does not pin down \(m-\lambda s\) separately — \(\lambda\) must be specified exogenously or backed out from prices of traded derivatives. (This is exactly the situation in short-rate models, Section 10.)

Key Derivations

The two-security riskless-portfolio argument. This is the exam classic for this chapter, and the pattern (build a portfolio, kill the noise, invoke "riskless \(\Rightarrow\) earns \(r\)") reappears verbatim in the BSM and short-rate chapters. Given \(\mathrm{d} f = \mu_f f\,\mathrm{d} t + \sigma_f f\,\mathrm{d} z\) and \(\mathrm{d} g = \mu_g g\,\mathrm{d} t + \sigma_g g\,\mathrm{d} z\), both driven by the same \(z\):
  1. Choose portfolio weights designed to cancel the randomness. Hold \(\sigma_g g\) units of security \(f\) and \(-\sigma_f f\) units of security \(g\) (hold these fixed over the next instant). Call the resulting portfolio value \[ \Pi = (\sigma_g g)\,f - (\sigma_f f)\,g . \] It may look mysterious why these particular weights are chosen — the next step reveals why.
  2. Compute the instantaneous change in \(\Pi\), treating the weights as constants over \(\mathrm{d} t\): \[ \mathrm{d}\Pi = \sigma_g g\,\mathrm{d} f - \sigma_f f\,\mathrm{d} g = \sigma_g g\,(\mu_f f\,\mathrm{d} t + \sigma_f f\,\mathrm{d} z) - \sigma_f f\,(\mu_g g\,\mathrm{d} t + \sigma_g g\,\mathrm{d} z). \] Expand and collect the \(\mathrm{d} z\) terms: they are \(\sigma_g g\sigma_f f\,\mathrm{d} z - \sigma_f f\sigma_g g\,\mathrm{d} z = 0\) — they cancel exactly, which is precisely why these weights were chosen in step 1. What remains is \[ \mathrm{d}\Pi = (\mu_f\sigma_g - \mu_g\sigma_f)\,fg\,\mathrm{d} t . \] \(\Pi\) is now instantaneously riskless: its change over the next instant is completely deterministic.
  3. Invoke no-arbitrage. A riskless portfolio must earn exactly the risk-free rate, or else you could borrow at \(r\) to buy it (if it earns more) or short it and invest at \(r\) (if it earns less) for a riskless profit. So \(\mathrm{d}\Pi = r\,\Pi\,\mathrm{d} t\). Substitute \(\Pi=\sigma_g gf - \sigma_f fg\) and equate the two expressions for \(\mathrm{d}\Pi\): \[ (\mu_f\sigma_g - \mu_g\sigma_f)\,fg = r(\sigma_g - \sigma_f)fg . \]
  4. Simplify. Both sides have a common factor \(fg\); cancel it and rearrange the remaining terms so that all \(f\)-related quantities are on one side: \[ \mu_f\sigma_g - \mu_g\sigma_f = r\sigma_g - r\sigma_f \;\Longrightarrow\; \sigma_g(\mu_f - r) = \sigma_f(\mu_g - r) \;\Longrightarrow\; \boxed{\ \frac{\mu_f - r}{\sigma_f} = \frac{\mu_g - r}{\sigma_g} \equiv \lambda\ }. \]
Since \(f\) and \(g\) were arbitrary securities driven by the same \(z\), this ratio must be the same for every such pair — it depends only on the underlying risk factor \(z\), never on which particular security you picked. That is why it deserves its own name, the market price of risk.

Exam Questions — What They Actually Ask

Exam Pattern
  • "Two derivatives \(f\) and \(g\) depend on the same Brownian motion. Show that \((\mu_f-r)/\sigma_f = (\mu_g-r)/\sigma_g\)." (the derivation above, verbatim)
  • "Define the market price of risk and interpret it."
  • "Derive the risk-neutral process for the underlying / for \(f\)." (substitute \(\mu = r+\lambda\sigma\) and shift the BM)
  • "The state variable \(\theta\) is not traded. Explain the role of the market price of risk in pricing derivatives on \(\theta\)." (risk-neutral drift \(m-\lambda s\); \(\lambda\) not determined by no-arbitrage alone)
  • "How does this relate to the Black–Scholes PDE / to the drift term \(r - \lambda\sigma\) in short-rate models?"

Common Mistakes

Common Mistakes
  • Choosing portfolio weights that do not cancel the noise (e.g. \(1\) of \(f\) minus \(1\) of \(g\)): the weights must be \(\sigma_g g\) and \(\sigma_f f\) (value-weighted by the other asset's volatility).
  • Forgetting that "riskless" portfolios must earn exactly \(r\) — stating the cancellation but never setting \(\mathrm{d}\Pi = r\Pi\,\mathrm{d} t\) leaves the derivation unfinished.
  • Claiming no-arbitrage determines \(\lambda\) for non-traded state variables. It does not; it only forces \(\lambda\) to be common across derivatives.
  • Sign conventions: some books use \(\mathrm{d} z^Q = \mathrm{d} z + \lambda\mathrm{d} t\) (drift decreases by \(\lambda\sigma\)). Pick one convention and stay consistent through the answer.
6The Black–Scholes–Merton Model (16/21 exams)

The Core Idea

Black, Scholes and Merton asked a very specific question: if the stock follows GBM and you are allowed to trade continuously (rebalance your portfolio at every instant, at no cost), what is a European option really worth? Their answer rests on one beautiful trick, delta hedging, which is really the continuous-time cousin of the binomial replication argument from Chapter 3.

The key observation is that an option's price and its underlying stock are driven by the same random shock \(\mathrm{d} z\) — whatever randomness hits the stock also, indirectly, hits the option price (through Itô's lemma). This means that if you hold the option and simultaneously go short exactly \(\Delta = \partial V/\partial S\) shares of stock, the random shocks in the two positions cancel out instant by instant, leaving a portfolio whose value changes deterministically over the next tiny interval of time. And a portfolio with no risk at all must earn exactly the risk-free rate \(r\) — otherwise there would be an arbitrage.

Writing that single statement, "this hedged portfolio earns exactly \(r\)," as an equation produces a partial differential equation (the BSM PDE) that every derivative written on the stock must obey, regardless of what payoff it has. The specific option price is then found by solving this one equation with the option's specific payoff plugged in as the terminal condition (e.g. \((S_T-K)^+\) for a call). Solve the same equation with a different terminal condition and you get the price of a completely different derivative — the PDE is universal, only the boundary condition changes.

Notice what vanished: the stock's expected return \(\mu\). Just as in the binomial model, disagreement about where the stock is headed is irrelevant, because hedging removes direction. Only the volatility \(\sigma\) — the size of the wiggles — matters. Equivalently (Feynman–Kac), the solution of the PDE is a discounted expectation under the risk-neutral measure: \(c = e^{-rT}\mathrm{E}^Q[(S_T-K)^+]\), where under \(Q\) the stock drifts at \(r\). Evaluating that lognormal integral gives the famous closed formula.

Read the call formula \(c = S_0\Phi(d_1) - Ke^{-rT}\Phi(d_2)\) like a sentence: "what I expect to receive minus what I expect to pay, both risk-neutrally discounted". \(\Phi(d_2)\) is the risk-neutral probability the option finishes in the money, so \(Ke^{-rT}\Phi(d_2)\) is the PV of the strike you will pay; \(S_0\Phi(d_1)\) is the PV of the stock you will receive, with \(d_1 > d_2\) because given exercise, the stock tends to be high (a conditional expectation, not just a probability, hides in \(\Phi(d_1)\)).

Key Definitions and Notation

  • \(V(S,t)\): price of a generic derivative as a function of stock price and time; \(c\), \(p\): European call and put prices.
  • \(\Phi(\cdot)\): standard normal cdf; \(\varphi(\cdot) = \Phi'\): standard normal density.
  • \(d_1 = \dfrac{\ln(S_0/K) + (r + \sigma^2/2)T}{\sigma\sqrt T}\), \(d_2 = d_1 - \sigma\sqrt T = \dfrac{\ln(S_0/K) + (r - \sigma^2/2)T}{\sigma\sqrt T}\).
  • \(q\): continuous dividend yield; \(r_d, r_f\): domestic/foreign rates (FX options); \(F_0\): forward/futures price (Black's model).
  • Assumptions of BSM: GBM with constant \(\mu,\sigma\); constant \(r\); no dividends (relaxed below); frictionless continuous trading, short-selling allowed, no arbitrage.

Central Results

The BSM partial differential equation

Any derivative \(V(S,t)\) on a non-dividend stock satisfies \[ \frac{\partial V}{\partial t} + rS\frac{\partial V}{\partial S} + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\frac{\partial^2V}{\partial S^2} = rV . \] Shape: it says "time decay \(+\) risk-neutral drift pickup \(+\) convexity pickup \(=\) risk-free growth of the value". Note \(\mu\) is absent and the drift coefficient is \(rS\) — the PDE lives in the risk-neutral world. With dividend yield \(q\), replace \(rS\,\partial V/\partial S\) by \((r-q)S\,\partial V/\partial S\).

BSM formulas

European call and put on a non-dividend stock: \[ c = S_0\Phi(d_1) - Ke^{-rT}\Phi(d_2), \qquad p = Ke^{-rT}\Phi(-d_2) - S_0\Phi(-d_1), \] with \(d_{1,2}\) as above. Consistency: \(c - p = S_0 - Ke^{-rT}\) (put–call parity), using \(\Phi(x)+\Phi(-x)=1\).

Dividend yield \(q\) (Merton): replace \(S_0 \to S_0e^{-qT}\) everywhere (also inside \(d_1\)): \(c = S_0e^{-qT}\Phi(d_1) - Ke^{-rT}\Phi(d_2)\), \(d_1 = \frac{\ln(S_0/K)+(r-q+\sigma^2/2)T}{\sigma\sqrt T}\).

FX options (Garman–Kohlhagen): an exchange rate is an asset paying the foreign rate: set \(q = r_f\), \(r = r_d\): \(c = S_0e^{-r_fT}\Phi(d_1) - Ke^{-r_dT}\Phi(d_2)\).

Options on futures (Black's formula): a futures price has zero drift under \(Q\) (it costs nothing to enter), i.e. \(q=r\): \[ c = e^{-rT}\bigl[F_0\Phi(d_1) - K\Phi(d_2)\bigr], \qquad d_1 = \frac{\ln(F_0/K)+\sigma^2T/2}{\sigma\sqrt T},\ d_2 = d_1 - \sigma\sqrt T . \] Black's formula is the workhorse for caps and swaptions in Sections 9 and 12.

Boundary sanity checks (quick marks)

As \(S_0\to\infty\): \(c \to S_0 - Ke^{-rT}\) (sure exercise = forward). As \(S_0\to0\): \(c\to0\). As \(\sigma\to0\): \(c \to (S_0 - Ke^{-rT})^+\). As \(T\to0\): \(c\to(S_0-K)^+\). Implied volatility is the \(\sigma\) that makes the BSM price match the market price; its variation across strikes (the smile) is evidence against constant-\(\sigma\) GBM.

Tiny numeric example (no dividends): \(S_0=100\), \(K=100\), \(r=5\%\), \(\sigma=20\%\), \(T=1\).

  1. \(d_1 = \dfrac{\ln(100/100)+(0.05+0.02)\times1}{0.2\times\sqrt1} = \dfrac{0+0.07}{0.2} = 0.35\).
  2. \(d_2 = d_1 - \sigma\sqrt T = 0.35-0.20 = 0.15\).
  3. Look up (or compute) the normal cdf: \(\Phi(0.35)\approx0.6368\), \(\Phi(0.15)\approx0.5596\).
  4. \(c = 100\times0.6368 - 100e^{-0.05}\times0.5596 = 63.68 - 95.12\times0.5596 = 63.68-53.23 = 10.45\).

Key Derivations

Deriving the BSM PDE. This is the single most-asked derivation in the whole course. It follows the exact same three-move pattern as the market-price-of-risk derivation: build a portfolio, make the noise cancel, invoke "riskless \(\Rightarrow\) earns \(r\)."
  1. Write down the stock's dynamics and apply Itô to the derivative price. We are given \(\mathrm{d} S = \mu S\,\mathrm{d} t + \sigma S\,\mathrm{d} z\). The derivative's value \(V(S,t)\) is a smooth function of \(S\) and \(t\), so Itô's lemma applies directly (exactly as in Chapter 4): \[ \mathrm{d} V = \Bigl(V_t + \mu S V_S + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS}\Bigr)\mathrm{d} t + \sigma S V_S\,\mathrm{d} z . \]
  2. Build a hedge portfolio. Go long one unit of the derivative and short \(\Delta\) shares of stock: \(\Pi = V - \Delta S\). Hold \(\Delta\) fixed over the next tiny interval \([t,t+\mathrm{d} t]\) (this is the "instantaneous" part of the argument). Its change is \[ \mathrm{d}\Pi = \mathrm{d} V - \Delta\,\mathrm{d} S = \Bigl(V_t + \mu S V_S + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS} - \Delta\mu S\Bigr)\mathrm{d} t + \sigma S\,(V_S - \Delta)\,\mathrm{d} z . \]
  3. Choose \(\Delta\) to kill the randomness. The coefficient of \(\mathrm{d} z\) is \(\sigma S(V_S-\Delta)\); setting \(\Delta = V_S = \partial V/\partial S\) makes it exactly zero. Notice that this choice also removes every appearance of \(\mu\) from the drift term (since \(\mu SV_S - \Delta\mu S = \mu S(V_S-\Delta)=0\) with the same \(\Delta\)). What's left is completely deterministic: \[ \mathrm{d}\Pi = \Bigl(V_t + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS}\Bigr)\mathrm{d} t . \]
  4. Invoke no-arbitrage. A riskless portfolio must earn exactly \(r\): \(\mathrm{d}\Pi = r\Pi\,\mathrm{d} t\). Since \(\Pi = V - SV_S\) (using \(\Delta=V_S\)), this reads \[ V_t + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS} = r(V - SV_S). \]
  5. Rearrange by moving the \(rSV_S\) term to the left-hand side: \[ V_t + rSV_S + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS} = rV . \qquad\blacksquare \]
  6. Select the specific instrument via the boundary (terminal) condition: \(V(S,T) = (S-K)^+\) for a call, \((K-S)^+\) for a put, \(S-K\) for a forward. Solving the PDE with the call's terminal condition (using the risk-neutral expectation of a lognormal \(S_T\)) produces the closed-form BSM formula below.
One subtlety worth a sentence on the exam: the hedge is riskless only for the next instant \(\mathrm{d} t\) — as soon as \(S\) or \(t\) moves, \(\Delta=V_S\) changes too, so the hedge must be continuously rebalanced. The portfolio is riskless instant by instant, not once and for all.

Verifying the formula solves the PDE / the Greeks identity — see the Greeks chapter (Section 7), where \(\Theta + rS\Delta + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\Gamma = rc\) is shown to be nothing but the PDE evaluated at the call.

Exam Questions — What They Actually Ask

Exam Pattern
  • "Derive the Black–Scholes–Merton PDE" — steps 1–4 above; always state where \(\mu\) drops out and why the portfolio is only instantaneously riskless.
  • "State the BSM call formula and interpret \(\Phi(d_1)\) and \(\Phi(d_2)\)." (\(\Phi(d_2) = Q(S_T>K)\); \(\Phi(d_1)\) = delta / expected stock conditional on exercise)
  • "Compute a call/put price for given numbers."
  • "Adapt the formula to a dividend-paying stock / FX (Garman–Kohlhagen) / futures (Black)."
  • "List the assumptions of the model / discuss which are violated in practice." (link to GBM shortcomings, smile)
  • "Show the price satisfies the PDE / find \(\Delta,\Gamma,\Theta\) and verify \(\Theta + rS\Delta + \frac12\sigma^2S^2\Gamma = rc\)."

Common Mistakes

Common Mistakes
  • Writing the PDE with \(\mu\) in it. The drift term is \(rS\,V_S\); if your final PDE contains \(\mu\), the hedge step went wrong.
  • Forgetting \(\sigma^2/2\) (not \(\sigma\) or \(\sigma^2\)) inside \(d_1\), or using \(\sigma T\) instead of \(\sigma\sqrt T\) in the denominator.
  • In dividend/FX variants, adjusting the drift inside \(d_1\) but forgetting to also discount the spot: it is \(S_0e^{-qT}\Phi(d_1)\).
  • Claiming the hedged portfolio is riskless forever: it is riskless only over \(\mathrm{d} t\) and must be rebalanced continuously.
7The Greeks and Hedging (15/21 exams)

The Core Idea

An option's price depends on several things at once: the stock price, how much time is left, volatility, and interest rates. The Greeks are simply the partial derivatives of the option price with respect to each of these inputs, one at a time — think of them as the option's dashboard of instrument readings. Delta tells you how fast the price moves when the stock moves; Gamma tells you how fast Delta itself changes (i.e. the curvature of the price function); Theta tells you how the price bleeds away purely from time passing; Vega tells you the sensitivity to volatility; Rho to interest rates.

Why do traders care about these numbers specifically? A trader running a book of options does not want to be exposed to guessing which way the stock will move. So she delta-hedges: for every option she holds, she also holds \(-\Delta\) shares of the underlying stock, so that a small move in \(S\) leaves the combined position's value unchanged to first order. This is exactly the same hedging trick that produced the BSM PDE in the previous chapter — Delta is not a new idea, it is the \(\Delta\) from that derivation, just given a name.

But a delta hedge is like balancing a ball on the palm of your hand: it only works for the stock price right now. As soon as the stock moves, the correct \(\Delta\) changes too, and the hedge needs adjusting — how fast \(\Delta\) changes is exactly what Gamma measures. A high-Gamma position needs constant rebalancing.

Here is the trade-off that examiners love to probe. Because an option's price is a convex function of the stock price (this is what positive Gamma means), a delta-hedged option position actually gains money whenever the stock moves, in either direction — Gamma is your friend when the market is volatile. But this benefit is not free: you pay for it continuously, every single day, through Theta — the value quietly bleeds away purely from time passing, even if the stock does not move at all. Theta is the rent you pay for holding Gamma. The BSM PDE makes this trade-off precise and exact, not just a vague intuition: it says \(\Theta + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\Gamma = r\times(\text{hedged portfolio value})\), meaning time-decay losses and convexity gains exactly balance out at the risk-free rate. This identity, derived below, is a direct restatement of the BSM PDE from the previous chapter.

Key Definitions and Notation

For a derivative value \(V(S,t)\) (call \(c\), put \(p\)), with \(\varphi\) the standard normal pdf and \(\tau = T-t\):
GreekDefinitionMeasuresBSM call value (no dividends)
Delta\( = V S\)exposure to \(S\)\((d_1) (0,1)\)
Gamma\( = ^2 V S^2\)curvature / hedge decay\((d_1)S_0 > 0\)
Theta\( = V t\)time decay\(-S_0(d_1)2 - rKe^-r(d_2)\)
Vega\( V = V\)volatility exposure\(S_0\,(d_1) > 0\)
Rho\( = V r\)rate exposure\(K e^-r(d_2) > 0\)
Put Greeks via put–call parity \(p = c - S_0 + Ke^{-r\tau}\) (differentiate!): \[ \Delta_p = \Phi(d_1) - 1 \in (-1,0), \qquad \Gamma_p = \Gamma_c, \qquad \mathcal V_p = \mathcal V_c, \] \[ \Theta_p = \Theta_c + rKe^{-r\tau}, \qquad \rho_p = \rho_c - K\tau e^{-r\tau}. \] A portfolio is delta-neutral (resp. gamma-, vega-neutral) if the corresponding portfolio Greek is 0. Greeks add across positions.

Central Results

The Greeks identity (from the BSM PDE)

Since every derivative satisfies \(V_t + rSV_S + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS} = rV\), and \(\Theta = V_t\), \(\Delta = V_S\), \(\Gamma = V_{SS}\): \[ \Theta + rS\Delta + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\Gamma = rV . \] Consequences examiners like: (i) for a delta-neutral portfolio, \(\Theta + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\Gamma = r\Pi\) — large positive Gamma must be paid for with large negative Theta, and vice versa; (ii) Theta is not a hedgeable risk (time is not random), it is the cost of the Gamma position.

Delta hedging mechanics

To delta-hedge a position with portfolio delta \(\Delta_\Pi\), trade \(-\Delta_\Pi\) shares (the stock has delta 1; a futures has delta \(e^{r\tau}\) per contract... in this course usually treated as 1 with continuous settlement caveats). The hedge must be rebalanced as \(S\) and \(t\) change; the rebalancing frequency needed grows with \(|\Gamma|\). To neutralise Gamma or Vega you need another option (stock has \(\Gamma = \mathcal V = 0\)): first set the Gamma/Vega with traded options, then re-adjust delta with the stock (stock trades do not disturb Gamma or Vega).

Shapes worth sketching

\(\Delta_c\) rises from 0 (deep OTM) through \(\approx0.5\) (near ATM, more precisely \(\Phi(d_1)\)) to 1 (deep ITM) — an S-curve. \(\Gamma\) is a bell peaking near ATM and exploding as \(\tau\to0\) at \(S\approx K\): near-expiry ATM options are hedging nightmares. Theta for a call is most negative ATM near expiry. Vega peaks ATM and grows with \(\sqrt\tau\).

Key Derivations

Deriving call Delta. The formula \(\Delta=\Phi(d_1)\) looks suspiciously simple given that \(d_1\) and \(d_2\) both depend on \(S\) — shouldn't differentiating \(c=S\Phi(d_1)-Ke^{-r\tau}\Phi(d_2)\) produce extra terms from the product and chain rules? It does, but they cancel, and seeing exactly why is good practice for exam-style algebra.
  1. Differentiate term by term, using the product rule on \(S\Phi(d_1)\). Since both \(d_1\) and \(d_2\) depend on \(S\): \[ \Delta = \frac{\partial c}{\partial S} = \Phi(d_1) + S\varphi(d_1)\frac{\partial d_1}{\partial S} - Ke^{-r\tau}\varphi(d_2)\frac{\partial d_2}{\partial S}. \] The first term \(\Phi(d_1)\) comes from differentiating the leading \(S\); the second comes from differentiating \(\Phi(d_1)\) itself (chain rule, giving the density \(\varphi(d_1)\)); the third from differentiating \(\Phi(d_2)\) similarly.
  2. Note that \(d_1\) and \(d_2\) have the same derivative with respect to \(S\), since \(d_2=d_1-\sigma\sqrt\tau\) and the constant \(\sigma\sqrt\tau\) vanishes on differentiating: \(\dfrac{\partial d_1}{\partial S}=\dfrac{\partial d_2}{\partial S}=\dfrac{1}{S\sigma\sqrt\tau}\).
  3. Show the last two terms cancel each other exactly. This relies on the key identity \(S\varphi(d_1) = Ke^{-r\tau}\varphi(d_2)\), which you can verify directly: write \(\varphi(d_2)=\varphi(d_1-\sigma\sqrt\tau)\), expand using the normal density formula, and use the definition of \(d_1\) to show the exponential factors combine to give exactly \(S/K \times e^{r\tau}\). Given this identity, the second and third terms in step 1 are literally the same number, appearing with opposite signs (both share the same factor \(\varphi(d_1)/(S\sigma\sqrt\tau)\) once you substitute step 2), so they cancel.
  4. Conclude. Only the first term survives: \(\Delta = \Phi(d_1)\).
The same cancellation trick (the identity \(S\varphi(d_1)=Ke^{-r\tau}\varphi(d_2)\)) is what makes the Theta and Vega formulas simplify to closed forms rather than staying as messy sums.

Verifying the Greeks identity \(\Theta + rS\Delta + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\Gamma = rc\). This is just a check that the closed-form Greeks are consistent with the BSM PDE from the previous chapter — substitute the boxed formulas and confirm the equation balances.

  1. Write out each term using the table above: \(\Theta = -\dfrac{S\varphi(d_1)\sigma}{2\sqrt\tau} - rKe^{-r\tau}\Phi(d_2)\); \(rS\Delta = rS\Phi(d_1)\); \(\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\Gamma = \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\cdot\dfrac{\varphi(d_1)}{S\sigma\sqrt\tau} = \dfrac{S\varphi(d_1)\sigma}{2\sqrt\tau}\).
  2. Add all three. The first term of \(\Theta\) is \(-\dfrac{S\varphi(d_1)\sigma}{2\sqrt\tau}\), and the Gamma term computed above is exactly \(+\dfrac{S\varphi(d_1)\sigma}{2\sqrt\tau}\): these two cancel completely.
  3. What remains is \(-rKe^{-r\tau}\Phi(d_2) + rS\Phi(d_1) = r\bigl(S\Phi(d_1) - Ke^{-r\tau}\Phi(d_2)\bigr) = rc\), using the BSM call formula itself.
This confirms the call price does indeed satisfy the PDE \(V_t + rSV_S+\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS}=rV\) from the previous chapter — reassuring, since it was derived to solve exactly that equation.

Exam Questions — What They Actually Ask

Exam Pattern
  • "Define Delta, Gamma and Theta for a call and give their signs."
  • "Use the BSM PDE to express Theta in terms of Delta and Gamma" — i.e. \(\Theta = rV - rS\Delta - \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\Gamma\).
  • "Show \(\Theta + rS\Delta + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\Gamma = rc\) for the BSM call."
  • "Differentiate put–call parity to relate put and call Greeks."
  • "A bank has sold options with total delta …; how does it delta-hedge? What happens as \(S\) moves (Gamma)?"
  • "How would you make the book gamma- and vega-neutral?" (use options, then fix delta with stock)

Common Mistakes

Common Mistakes
  • Using \(\Phi(d_1)\) (cdf) where \(\varphi(d_1)\) (density) is needed: \(\Gamma\) and Vega use the density.
  • Sign of Theta: for a long call \(\Theta<0\); a common slip is dropping the leading minus sign.
  • Hedging direction: a written call has delta \(-\Phi(d_1)\), so the hedge buys shares. Getting the sign of the position wrong reverses everything.
  • Trying to gamma-hedge with the stock: the stock's Gamma and Vega are zero; only options can adjust them.
8Martingales, Numeraires and Change of Measure (9/21 exams)

The Core Idea

A martingale is a process whose expected future value, given everything you know today, is simply equal to today's value — in plain words, a "fair game" with no built-in drift in either direction. Casino intuition: if you play a fair coin-toss game (win 1 kroner on heads, lose 1 on tails, 50/50), your wealth over time is a martingale, because your best forecast of tomorrow's wealth, given what you know now, is exactly what you have today.

This concept turns out to give an elegant restatement of everything we have built so far. Under the risk-neutral measure \(Q\), we know every traded asset's price grows on average at rate \(r\) (that is what "risk-neutral" means). Now here is the trick: if you measure the asset's value not in kroner, but in units of the bank account \(B_t=e^{rt}\) (i.e. divide the asset price by \(B_t\)), the growth at rate \(r\) exactly cancels the growth of the bank account, and what is left, \(S_t/B_t\), has zero drift — it is a martingale. So "discounted asset prices are \(Q\)-martingales" is really just a fancy restatement of "every asset earns \(r\) on average under \(Q\)," which in turn is just risk-neutral pricing from the earlier chapters, dressed in new vocabulary.

Now for the genuinely new idea. The bank account is only one possible choice of measuring stick, called a numeraire. You could equally well measure every asset's value in units of some other traded asset \(N_t\) (as long as it always has a positive price) instead of the bank account. It turns out that for any such choice, there exists a corresponding probability measure \(Q^N\) under which every asset price, divided by \(N_t\), becomes a martingale. Changing the numeraire is exactly like switching currencies when doing accounting: nothing about the underlying economics changes, but some calculations become dramatically simpler in the right units.

The star example, used constantly in the rest of the course: choose the numeraire to be a zero-coupon bond \(P(t,T)\) instead of the bank account. The resulting measure \(Q^T\) is called the \(T\)-forward measure. Under it, the forward price \(F(t,T)=S_t/P(t,T)\) turns out to be a martingale (proved below) — meaning the forward price today is simply the \(Q^T\)-expected future spot price. This single trick is what justifies Black's formula for pricing caps and swaptions, and it is the theoretical foundation underneath the entire LIBOR market model later in the course.

Finally, the tool that lets us move from one measure to another is Girsanov's theorem: switching to an equivalent probability measure only ever shifts the drift of a Brownian motion; the volatility is completely unaffected. This is why, throughout the course, volatilities are treated as fixed, "objective" model inputs, while drifts are constantly being adjusted depending on which measure is most convenient for the calculation at hand.

Key Definitions and Notation

  • \(\mathcal{F}_t\): the information available at time \(t\) (a filtration). \(\mathrm{E}[\,\cdot\,|\mathcal{F}_t]\): expectation given that information.
  • Martingale: \(M_t\) with \(\mathrm{E}[M_T|\mathcal{F}_t] = M_t\) for all \(t\le T\) (and \(\mathrm{E}|M_t|<\infty\)). An Itô process is a martingale iff its drift is zero.
  • \(B_t = e^{rt}\): the money-market account (bank account, value of 1 continuously reinvested at \(r\)).
  • Numeraire \(N_t\): any traded asset with \(N_t>0\) used as unit of account; \(Q^N\): the measure under which \(X_t/N_t\) is a martingale for every traded \(X\). \(Q = Q^B\): risk-neutral measure. \(Q^T := Q^{P(\cdot,T)}\): the \(T\)-forward measure.
  • Radon–Nikodym derivative \(\mathrm{d} Q/\mathrm{d} P\): the state-by-state ratio of probabilities that converts \(P\)-expectations into \(Q\)-expectations: \(\mathrm{E}^Q[X] = \mathrm{E}^P[X\,\mathrm{d} Q/\mathrm{d} P]\).
  • \(F(t,T) = S_t/P(t,T)\): the forward price of \(S\) for delivery at \(T\) (cost-of-carry in bond units).

Central Results

Girsanov's theorem

Let \(z_t\) be a \(P\)-Brownian motion and \(\lambda_t\) an (adapted, suitably integrable) process. Define the new measure \(Q\) by \[ \frac{\mathrm{d} Q}{\mathrm{d} P}\Big|_{\mathcal{F}_T} = \exp\Bigl(-\int_0^T\lambda_s\,\mathrm{d} z_s - \tfrac12\int_0^T\lambda_s^2\,\mathrm{d} s\Bigr). \] Then \(z^Q_t = z_t + \int_0^t\lambda_s\,\mathrm{d} s\) (i.e. \(\mathrm{d} z^Q = \mathrm{d} z + \lambda\,\mathrm{d} t\)) is a Brownian motion under \(Q\). Only the drift changes; the volatility is measure-invariant. Taking \(\lambda\) = the market price of risk converts real-world dynamics into risk-neutral dynamics — this is the rigorous version of Section 5.

Risk-neutral valuation as a martingale statement

No arbitrage \(\iff\) there is a measure \(Q\sim P\) such that discounted traded-asset prices \(S_t/B_t\) are \(Q\)-martingales. Then any attainable claim with payoff \(V_T\) has price \[ V_t = B_t\,\mathrm{E}^Q\Bigl[\frac{V_T}{B_T}\Big|\mathcal{F}_t\Bigr] = e^{-r(T-t)}\mathrm{E}^Q[V_T|\mathcal{F}_t] \quad(\text{constant }r). \] General numeraire version: \(\dfrac{V_t}{N_t} = \mathrm{E}^{Q^N}\Bigl[\dfrac{V_T}{N_T}\Big|\mathcal{F}_t\Bigr]\) for any numeraire \(N\).

The forward measure and Black's formula

With numeraire \(P(t,T)\) (note \(P(T,T)=1\)): \[ V_t = P(t,T)\,\mathrm{E}^{Q^T}[V_T|\mathcal{F}_t], \qquad F(t,T) = \frac{S_t}{P(t,T)} = \mathrm{E}^{Q^T}[S_T|\mathcal{F}_t] . \] The first line says: discounting comes out of the expectation even when rates are stochastic — the great advantage of \(Q^T\). The second: forward prices are \(Q^T\)-martingales and unbiased \(Q^T\)-forecasts of the future spot. If \(F\) is lognormal under \(Q^T\) with volatility \(\sigma\), a call struck at \(K\) costs \(c = P(0,T)[F_0\Phi(d_1) - K\Phi(d_2)]\): Black's formula, now justified with stochastic rates.

Key Derivations

Showing the discounted stock is a \(Q\)-martingale. We know that under the risk-neutral measure \(Q\), the stock drifts at rate \(r\): \(\mathrm{d} S = rS\,\mathrm{d} t + \sigma S\,\mathrm{d} z^Q\). We want to confirm that \(G:=e^{-rt}S_t\), the stock price measured in bank-account units, has zero drift.
  1. Compute the partial derivatives of \(G(S,t)=e^{-rt}S\), needed for Itô's lemma: \(G_t = -re^{-rt}S\) (differentiating the \(e^{-rt}\) factor), \(G_S = e^{-rt}\) (differentiating \(S\)), and \(G_{SS}=0\) (since \(G\) is linear in \(S\), there's no curvature).
  2. Apply Itô's lemma, using the stock's risk-neutral drift \(a=rS\) and diffusion \(b=\sigma S\): \[ \mathrm{d} G = \bigl(G_t + a\,G_S + \tfrac12 b^2 G_{SS}\bigr)\mathrm{d} t + bG_S\,\mathrm{d} z^Q = \bigl(-re^{-rt}S + rS\,e^{-rt} + 0\bigr)\mathrm{d} t + \sigma S e^{-rt}\,\mathrm{d} z^Q . \]
  3. Simplify the drift. The two terms \(-re^{-rt}S\) and \(+rS\,e^{-rt}\) are identical in size and opposite in sign — they cancel exactly. What remains is \[ \mathrm{d} G = \sigma S e^{-rt}\,\mathrm{d} z^Q = \sigma\,G\,\mathrm{d} z^Q . \]
  4. Conclude. A process with zero drift is, by definition, a martingale. So \(e^{-rt}S_t\) is a \(Q\)-martingale. (The same computation works for any traded asset, not just the stock — this is exactly what "risk-neutral" means.)

Showing \(F(t,T) = \mathrm{E}^{Q^T}[S_T|\mathcal{F}_t]\). We want to show the forward price is the expected future spot under the forward measure.

  1. Recognise \(F(t,T)=S_t/P(t,T)\) as "a traded asset divided by the numeraire of \(Q^T\)." By definition, the \(T\)-forward measure \(Q^T\) is precisely the measure under which any traded asset's price, divided by the bond \(P(\cdot,T)\), is a martingale. Since \(S_t\) is a traded asset and \(P(t,T)\) is the \(Q^T\)-numeraire, \(F(t,T)=S_t/P(t,T)\) is automatically a \(Q^T\)-martingale — no extra computation needed.
  2. Use the martingale property directly: for a martingale, today's value equals the conditional expectation of any future value: \(F(t,T) = \mathrm{E}^{Q^T}[F(T,T)|\mathcal{F}_t]\).
  3. Evaluate \(F(T,T)\). At the maturity date itself, \(F(T,T) = S_T/P(T,T)\), and a zero-coupon bond maturing today is worth exactly its face value, \(P(T,T)=1\). So \(F(T,T)=S_T\).
  4. Combine steps 2–3: \(F(t,T) = \mathrm{E}^{Q^T}[S_T|\mathcal{F}_t]\). \(\blacksquare\)

Exam Questions — What They Actually Ask

Exam Pattern
  • "Define a martingale. Show that the discounted stock price is a martingale under \(Q\)." (Itô on \(e^{-rt}S_t\))
  • "State Girsanov's theorem. What changes under an equivalent measure change, and what does not?" (drift changes, volatility doesn't)
  • "Define the \(T\)-forward measure and show \(F(t,T) = \mathrm{E}^{Q^T}[S_T|\mathcal{F}_t]\)."
  • "Explain the role of the numeraire; state the general pricing formula \(V_t/N_t = \mathrm{E}^{Q^N}[V_T/N_T|\mathcal{F}_t]\)."
  • "Use the forward measure to justify Black's formula when interest rates are stochastic."

Common Mistakes

Common Mistakes
  • "The stock price is a \(Q\)-martingale." False — the discounted stock price is. Under \(Q\) the stock itself drifts at \(r\).
  • Believing a measure change alters volatility. Girsanov moves drifts only; \(\sigma\) is common to \(P\), \(Q\) and \(Q^T\).
  • Interpreting \(F(t,T) = \mathrm{E}^{Q^T}[S_T]\) as a real-world forecast. The expectation is under the forward measure.
  • Using \(e^{-rT}\) discounting together with stochastic rates: with random \(r\) use \(V_t = P(t,T)\mathrm{E}^{Q^T}[V_T|\mathcal{F}_t]\) (discounting outside), not \(\mathrm{E}^Q\) with a constant discount factor.
9Interest Rate Derivatives: Caps, Floors, Swaptions (10/21 exams)

The Core Idea

Picture a company that has taken out a floating-rate loan and is worried that interest rates will rise, making its payments more expensive. A cap is exactly the insurance product it needs: at the end of every accrual period, the cap reimburses the company for whatever amount the floating rate exceeded some agreed ceiling rate \(K\). Structurally this is nothing more than a strip of call options on the interest rate, one for each period (each individual one is called a caplet). A floor is the mirror-image product: it protects a depositor against rates falling too low, using put options on the rate instead. Just as calls and puts on a stock are linked by put–call parity, caps and floors are linked by an analogous relationship: long cap minus long floor equals a payer swap struck at rate \(K\).

Here is a beautiful and useful duality to internalise before going further: an option on an interest rate is secretly an option on a bond, viewed inside out. Why? Because rates and bond prices always move in opposite directions — over one period, \(P=1/(1+\delta L)\), so when the rate \(L\) rises, the bond price \(P\) falls, and vice versa. This means a caplet (a call on the rate, which pays off when rates rise) is equivalent to a put on a zero-coupon bond (which pays off when bond prices fall). By the same logic, a floorlet becomes a call on a bond.

The same duality extends to swaptions. A swaption is an option to enter into a swap at a pre-agreed fixed rate \(s_K\). A receiver swaption (the right to receive the fixed rate) becomes valuable exactly when market rates have fallen — and when rates fall, bond prices rise, so the underlying coupon bond becomes worth more than its par value. This means a receiver swaption is precisely a call on the coupon bond, struck at par; a payer swaption is the corresponding put. This translation matters a great deal in practice, because bond options are exactly what short-rate models (the next chapter) know how to price in closed form.

For everyday market quoting, practitioners do not bother running a full short-rate model at all. Instead, they simply assume the relevant forward rate (or forward swap rate) is lognormal under its own natural forward measure, and plug straight into Black's formula — the same Black-76 formula for options on futures from the BSM chapter. The LIBOR market model, covered later in the course, is what makes this shortcut theoretically rigorous rather than just a convenient approximation.

Key Definitions and Notation

  • Tenor structure \(T_0 < T_1 < \dots < T_n\) with accrual factors \(\delta_k = T_{k+1}-T_k\) (e.g. 0.5 for semiannual). Notional \(N\) (set \(N=1\) below).
  • \(L_k = L(T_k, T_{k+1})\): the floating (LIBOR-type) simple rate fixed at \(T_k\) for the period \([T_k, T_{k+1}]\): \(1 + \delta_kL_k = 1/P(T_k,T_{k+1})\).
  • \(F_k(t)\): the forward rate seen at \(t\) for that period: \(F_k(t) = \dfrac{P(t,T_k) - P(t,T_{k+1})}{\delta_k\,P(t,T_{k+1})}\); note \(F_k(T_k) = L_k\).
  • Caplet \(k\): pays \(\delta_k(L_k - K)^+\) at \(T_{k+1}\) (fixed in advance at \(T_k\), paid in arrears). Cap = sum of caplets; floorlet/floor: \(\delta_k(K - L_k)^+\).
  • Swap rate \(s(t)\): the fixed rate making a swap over \([T_0,T_n]\) worth zero: \(s(t) = \bigl(P(t,T_0) - P(t,T_n)\bigr)/A(t)\), where \(A(t) = \sum_{k=1}^{n}\delta_{k-1}P(t,T_k)\) is the annuity (PV of 1 paid on each fixed date).
  • Payer swaption: right, at \(T_0\), to enter a swap paying fixed \(s_K\); receiver swaption: right to receive fixed \(s_K\).

Central Results

Black's formulas for caplets and swaptions (market standard)

Caplet \(k\) with cap rate \(K\), forward-rate volatility \(\sigma_k\): \[ \text{caplet}_k(0) = \delta_k\,P(0,T_{k+1})\,\bigl[F_k(0)\Phi(d_1) - K\Phi(d_2)\bigr], \qquad d_{1,2} = \frac{\ln(F_k(0)/K) \pm \tfrac12\sigma_k^2T_k}{\sigma_k\sqrt{T_k}} . \] (Floorlet: \(\delta_kP(0,T_{k+1})[K\Phi(-d_2) - F_k(0)\Phi(-d_1)]\).) Note the volatility accrues only to the fixing date \(T_k\), while discounting runs to the payment date \(T_{k+1}\).

Payer swaption with strike \(s_K\), forward swap-rate volatility \(\sigma\), exercise \(T_0\): \[ \text{PS}(0) = A(0)\,\bigl[s(0)\Phi(d_1) - s_K\Phi(d_2)\bigr], \qquad d_{1,2} = \frac{\ln(s(0)/s_K) \pm \tfrac12\sigma^2T_0}{\sigma\sqrt{T_0}} , \] and the receiver swaption is \(\text{RS}(0) = A(0)[s_K\Phi(-d_2) - s(0)\Phi(-d_1)]\). The annuity \(A(0)\) is the natural numeraire: under \(Q^A\) the swap rate \(s(t)\) is a martingale, which is what legitimises Black here.

The rate-option / bond-option dictionary

\[ \text{caplet } = (1+\delta_kK)\ \text{\textbf{puts} on } P(\cdot,T_{k+1})\ \text{with strike } \tfrac{1}{1+\delta_kK},\ \text{expiry } T_k ; \] \[ \text{floorlet } = (1+\delta_kK)\ \text{\textbf{calls} on the same bond, same strike} ; \] \[ \text{receiver swaption } = \text{\textbf{call} on } B_c \text{ struck at par (1)}; \] \[ \text{payer swaption } = \text{\textbf{put} on } B_c \text{ struck at par}, \] where \(B_c = \sum_{k=1}^n s_K\delta_{k-1}P(T_0,T_k) + P(T_0,T_n)\) is the coupon bond (fixed leg plus notional) valued at exercise \(T_0\).
