Also: cap-lognormal and swaprate-lognormal are mutually inconsistent assumptions — market convention, not one coherent model (the LMM makes the cap side rigorous).
Short Rate Models 14/21 exams
▼

Short-rate models compress the whole yield curve into a single state variable: the instantaneous short rate \(r(t)\), modelled directly under the risk-neutral measure \(Q\) as \(dr = m(r,t)\,dt + s(r,t)\,dz\). Once you know the \(Q\)-dynamics of \(r\), every zero-coupon bond price follows from the martingale pricing formula \(P(t,T) = E^Q_t[e^{-\int_t^T r\,ds}]\) — and from bond prices, the whole term structure \(R(t,T)\), plus bond options, caps, and swaptions.

📋 Nøgleformler
General setup (under \(Q\)) \[dr = \bigl(m(r,t) - \lambda(r,t)s(r,t)\bigr)dt + s(r,t)\,dz,\qquad P(t,T) = E^Q_t\bigl[e^{-\int_t^Tr(s)ds}\bigr]\] \(m\) = real-world drift, \(s\) = diffusion, \(\lambda\) = market price of interest-rate risk (calibrated, not derived).
Bond-pricing PDE (Feynman-Kac) \[\frac{\partial V}{\partial t} + \tfrac12 s^2\frac{\partial^2V}{\partial r^2} + (m-\lambda s)\frac{\partial V}{\partial r} - rV = 0,\qquad V(r,T,T)=1\] Same PDE (different boundary condition) for European bond options. With continuous coupon \(C(r,t)\): add \(+C\) on the left.
Affine models \[m - \lambda s = m_1(t) - m_2(t)r,\qquad s = \sqrt{s_1(t) + s_2(t)r}\ \Longrightarrow\ P(t,T) = A(t,T)e^{-B(t,T)r(t)}\] ODE system (from substituting into the PDE, with \(A_1 = \ln A\)): \[\frac{\partial A_1}{\partial t} = -\tfrac12 s_1B^2 + m_1B,\qquad \frac{\partial B}{\partial t} = \tfrac12 s_2B^2 + m_2B - 1,\qquad A_1(T,T)=B(T,T)=0\] Yields are affine in \(r\): \(R(t,T) = -\frac{\ln A}{T-t} + \frac{B}{T-t}r(t)\).
🎯 Fremgangsmåde til eksamen

When you see: "Solve the Vasicek/OU process" or "find the distribution of \(r(t)\)"

Step 1 — Multiply by the integrating factor: consider \(Y = e^{at}r\); show \(dY = abe^{at}dt + \sigma e^{at}dz\) (the \(r\)-terms cancel — state this).
Step 2 — Integrate and divide by \(e^{at}\): \[r_t = r_0e^{-at} + b(1-e^{-at}) + \sigma\int_0^te^{-a(t-u)}dz_u\]
Step 3 — Conclude normality and give moments: \[\boxed{E[r_t] = r_0e^{-at}+b(1-e^{-at}),\qquad \operatorname{Var}[r_t] = \frac{\sigma^2}{2a}(1-e^{-2at})}\] Comment on limits \(t\to\infty\) (stationary \(N(b,\sigma^2/2a)\)) and \(a\to0\) (Ho-Lee-like, variance \(\sigma^2t\)).

Variant A: "Derive the bond-pricing PDE"

Reproduce the two-bond hedge: portfolio \(\Pi = V_1 - \Delta V_2\), \(\Delta = \frac{\partial V_1/\partial r}{\partial V_2/\partial r}\), riskless ⇒ earns \(r\), separate maturities ⇒ common function \(\lambda\), PDE with drift \(m - \lambda s\). Close with: "since \(r\) is not traded, \(\lambda\) is exogenous/calibrated — unlike BSM where the underlying is traded."

Variant B: "Show \(P = Ae^{-Br}\) works / derive \(B(t,T)\)"

Steps: compute \(P_t, P_r, P_{rr}\) from the ansatz; substitute; collect powers of \(r\); obtain the two ODEs; solve \(\dot B = aB - 1\), \(B(T,T)=0\): \[\boxed{B(t,T) = \frac{1-e^{-a(T-t)}}{a}}\] (Ho-Lee/Merton special case \(a=0\): \(B = T-t\), \(P = e^{\frac{\sigma^2}{6}\tau^3 - \frac{\theta}{2}\tau^2 - \tau r}\).)

Variant C: "Compute a bond price / yield; find bond volatility or duration"

Bond price: plug \(r\), \(A\), \(B\) into \(P = Ae^{-Br}\). Yield: \(R(t,T) = -\frac{\ln P}{T-t} = -\frac{\ln A}{T-t} + \frac{B}{T-t}r\). Duration to \(r\): \(\hat D = B(t,T)\). Bond dynamics by Itô: \(dP = rP\,dt - sBP\,dz\) under \(Q\) (drift must be \(r\) — good check).

Variant D: "Compare models / discuss"

Structure your answer: (1) equilibrium (Vasicek, CIR — curve is output) vs. no-arbitrage (Ho-Lee, HW, BDT — curve is input); (2) Gaussian (analytic, negative rates) vs. square-root (positive rates, \(\chi^2\)) vs. lognormal (trees only); (3) one factor ⇒ perfectly correlated curve moves — motivates HJM/LMM and multi-factor HW; (4) calibration: HW to today's curve + cap or swaption vols.
HJM Model 9/21 exams
▼

Short-rate models drive the curve from one number \(r(t)\) — parsimonious but rigid (all yields perfectly correlated, calibration indirect). Heath-Jarrow-Morton (1992) flipped the perspective: model the entire instantaneous forward curve \(f(t,T)\), one process per maturity \(T\), simultaneously. Today's curve \(f(0,T)\) is the initial condition by construction — perfect fit for free. The question becomes: what dynamics can the forward curve have without creating arbitrage?

📋 Nøgleformler
Setup: bond prices under \(Q\) \[dP(t,T) = r(t)P(t,T)\,dt + v(t,T,\Omega_t)P(t,T)\,dz\] \(v(t,T,\Omega)\) = (possibly state-dependent) proportional volatility of the \(T\)-maturity ZCB; drift is \(r\) because discounted bond prices are \(Q\)-martingales. Boundary: \(v(t,t) = 0\) (a maturing bond has no risk — "pull to par").
Forward rate definitions \[f(t,T_1,T_2) = \frac{\ln P(t,T_1) - \ln P(t,T_2)}{T_2 - T_1},\qquad f(t,T) = -\frac{\partial\ln P(t,T)}{\partial T}\]
HJM forward-rate dynamics + THE drift condition (under \(Q\)) \[df(t,T) = m(t,T)\,dt + s(t,T)\,dz,\qquad \boxed{m(t,T) = s(t,T)\int_t^T s(t,\tau)\,d\tau}\] Equivalently \(df(t,T) = v(t,T)\,v_T(t,T)\,dt + v_T(t,T)\,dz\) with \(s = v_T = \partial v/\partial T\), \(v(t,T) = \int_t^Ts(t,\tau)d\tau\). The most cited formula of this chapter.
🎯 Fremgangsmåde til eksamen

When you see: "State/derive the HJM drift condition"

Step 1 — Write \(dP = rP\,dt + vP\,dz\) under \(Q\) and justify drift \(= r\) (discounted bonds are \(Q\)-martingales).
Step 2 — Itô: \(d\ln P = (r - \tfrac12v^2)dt + v\,dz\).
Step 3 — Forward rate = scaled difference of log bond prices; take the \(\Delta T\to0\) limit.
Step 4 — With \(s = v_T\) and \(v(t,t)=0\): \[\boxed{df(t,T) = \Bigl(s(t,T)\int_t^Ts(t,\tau)d\tau\Bigr)dt + s(t,T)\,dz}\] Add the interpretive sentence: "under \(Q\), specifying the volatility structure fully determines the drift — no arbitrage leaves no freedom."

Variant A: "Given \(s(t,T) = \ldots\), find the drift / the model"

Integrate: \(m = s(t,T)\int_t^Ts(t,\tau)d\tau\). Constants: \(s=\sigma \Rightarrow m = \sigma^2(T-t)\) (Ho-Lee). Exponential: \(s = \sigma e^{-a(T-t)} \Rightarrow m = \frac{\sigma^2}{a}e^{-a(T-t)}(1-e^{-a(T-t)})\) (Hull-White). Box the result and name the model.

Variant B: "Verify the drift condition in the Merton/Ho-Lee model"

From \(P = \exp[\frac{\sigma^2}6\tau^3 - \frac\theta2\tau^2 - \tau r]\): compute \(f = -\partial_T\ln P = -\frac{\sigma^2}2\tau^2 + \theta\tau + r\); differentiate in \(t\), insert \(dr\); show \[\boxed{df = \sigma^2(T-t)dt + \sigma dz}\] and remark that \(\theta\) drops out — "the drift depends only on volatility".

Variant C: "Discuss HJM: pros, cons, implementation"

Pros: fits today's curve by construction; unifying framework (all models = vol choices); multi-factor natural. Cons: models unobservable instantaneous forwards; generally non-Markov (\(t\) inside and outside the stochastic integral) ⇒ non-recombining trees ⇒ Monte Carlo; lognormal vols explode. Markov exceptions: Ho-Lee/Hull-White vol structures. Practice: short-rate models or LMM cover the useful cases; HJM is the reference theory.
LMM / LIBOR Market Model 8/21 exams
▼

HJM's state variable — the instantaneous forward rate — is a theoretical idealisation; nobody quotes it. The LIBOR Market Model (BGM) instead models the discrete, simply-compounded forward rates \(F_k(t) = F(t; t_k, t_{k+1})\) actually observable in FRAs and swaps, on a tenor grid \(t_0, t_1, \ldots\) with accruals \(\tau_k = t_{k+1} - t_k\). Its killer feature: it is the rigorous model behind the market's Black formula habit. Market practice prices caplets with Black's formula, implicitly assuming lognormal forward rates. No short-rate model reproduces that exactly. The LMM does — by design.

📋 Nøgleformler
Objects and notation Tenor dates \(t_0\lt t_1\lt\cdots\lt t_{n+1}\), accruals \(\tau_k = t_{k+1}-t_k\); \(m(t)\) = index of next reset date after \(t\). \[F_k(t) = F(t;t_k,t_{k+1}) = \frac{1}{\tau_k}\Bigl(\frac{P(t,t_k)}{P(t,t_{k+1})}-1\Bigr),\qquad F_k(t_k) = L(t_k,t_{k+1})\ \text{(spot rate at fixing)}\] \[1 + \tau_kF_k(t) = \frac{P(t,t_k)}{P(t,t_{k+1})}\] \(\zeta_k(t)\) = instantaneous vol of \(F_k\); \(\nu_k(t)\) = vol of \(P(t,t_k)\).
Core dynamics: each rate driftless under its own measure \[dF_k(t) = \zeta_k(t)F_k(t)\,dz\qquad\text{under }Q^{k+1}\ (\text{numeraire }P(t,t_{k+1})),\ t\le t_k\] Deterministic \(\zeta_k\) ⇒ \(F_k(t_k)\) lognormal ⇒ Black caplet formula exact.
Black caplet formula in LMM notation \[\text{Caplet}_k = N\,P(0,t_{k+1})\,\tau_k\bigl(F_k(0)\Phi(d_1) - R_K\Phi(d_2)\bigr),\quad d_1 = \frac{\ln(F_k/R_K)+\tfrac12\sigma_k^2t_k}{\sigma_k\sqrt{t_k}}\] \[\sigma_k^2\,t_k = \int_0^{t_k}\zeta_k(t)^2\,dt\] \(\sigma_k\) = implied (Black) caplet vol; flat vols (one per cap) vs. spot vols (one per caplet, bootstrapped).
🎯 Fremgangsmåde til eksamen

When you see: "Explain the LMM / why is Black's caplet formula consistent?"

Step 1 — Define the objects: tenor grid, \(F_k(t) = \frac1{\tau_k}(\frac{P(t,t_k)}{P(t,t_{k+1})}-1)\), \(F_k(t_k) = L(t_k,t_{k+1})\).
Step 2 — Martingale argument: \(F_k\) = traded portfolio / numeraire \(P(t,t_{k+1})\) ⇒ driftless under \(Q^{k+1}\): \(dF_k = \zeta_kF_k\,dz\).
Step 3 — Assume deterministic \(\zeta_k\) ⇒ lognormal \(F_k(t_k)\) with total variance \(\sigma_k^2t_k = \int_0^{t_k}\zeta_k^2dt\); apply \(f_0 = P(0,t_{k+1})E^{k+1}[\text{payoff}]\) + lognormal lemma: \[\boxed{\text{Caplet} = N\tau_kP(0,t_{k+1})(F_k\Phi(d_1) - R_K\Phi(d_2))}\]

Variant A: "Derive/state the drift under the rolling forward risk-neutral measure"

Steps: (1) change-of-numeraire drift rule \((\sigma^h-\sigma^g)\sigma^f\); (2) vol relation \(\nu_i - \nu_{i+1} = \frac{\tau_i\zeta_iF_i}{1+\tau_iF_i}\) from \(\ln(1+\tau_iF_i)\); (3) telescope: \[\boxed{\frac{dF_k}{F_k} = \zeta_k\sum_{i=m(t)}^k\frac{\tau_i\zeta_iF_i}{1+\tau_iF_i}\,dt + \zeta_k\,dz}\] Comment: stochastic drift ⇒ Monte Carlo; driftless again once \(m(t) = k+1\).

Variant B: "Compare LMM with HJM / short-rate models"

Key contrasts: observable simple rates vs. unobservable instantaneous ones; Black caplet formula exact vs. impossible; vol parameters directly interpretable (caplet vols) vs. needing recalibration mapping; both HJM and LMM fit today's curve by construction and are simulated by MC; short-rate models remain useful for trees/PDE and American-style products. Drift conditions mirror each other: integral (HJM) ↔ sum (LMM).

Variant C: "Calibration / implementation question"

Calibrate \(\zeta_k\) to caplet (spot) vols via \(\sigma_k^2t_k = \int_0^{t_k}\zeta_k^2dt\); correlations / factor structure to swaption vols; reduce dimensionality with parametric vol forms or PCA factors. Implementation: log-Euler Monte Carlo of all rates jointly under one measure (rolling CD numeraire); price cash flows pathwise. Mention: lognormal ⇒ no negative rates ⇒ shifted-lognormal variants in low-rate environments.
Credit Risk 13/21 exams
▼

Everything so far assumed cash flows arrive for sure. Credit risk drops that assumption: a borrower may default, and we must price both the probability of that event and the loss given default. Two modelling philosophies exist, and exams test both:

📋 Nøgleformler
Hazard rate and survival probability \[\frac{\partial V(t,T)}{\partial T} = -\lambda(t,T)V(t,T)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad V(t,T) = V(0,t)\,e^{-\int_t^T\lambda(t,\tau)d\tau}\] \(V(t,T)\) = survival probability to \(T\) seen at \(t\); \(\lambda\) = hazard rate (default intensity, forward default rate). Equivalently \(\lambda(t,T) = -\frac{\partial}{\partial T}\ln V(t,T)\). Analogy: \(\lambda \leftrightarrow f(t,T)\), \(V \leftrightarrow P(t,T)\).
Cumulative default probability \[Q(t,T) = 1 - V(t,T) = 1 - e^{-\bar\lambda(t,T)(T-t)}\ \ (\text{from }t,\ V(0,t)=1)\] \(\bar\lambda\) = average hazard rate. Constant hazard: \(Q(t) = 1 - e^{-\lambda t}\). Unconditional PD over \([T, T+\Delta T]\): \(V(T) - V(T+\Delta T)\); conditional: \(\frac{V(T)-V(T+\Delta T)}{V(T)}\).
Credit spread ↔ hazard rate (the workhorse) \[\lambda \approx \frac{s}{1-R}\] \(s\) = credit spread (per year), \(R\) = recovery rate. Zero-recovery risky ZCB: \(B_0 = P(0,T)V(0,T)\), so spread \(= \bar\lambda\). Most-used numeric formula in credit exam questions.
🎯 Fremgangsmåde til eksamen

When you see: "Compute default probabilities from spreads / hazard rates"

Step 1 — Back out the hazard rate: \(\lambda = \dfrac{s}{1-R}\) (state the approximation).
Step 2 — Survival to \(t\): \(V(t) = e^{-\lambda t}\); cumulative PD: \(Q(t) = 1 - e^{-\lambda t}\).
Step 3 — For a specific year: unconditional PD\((t_1,t_2) = V(t_1) - V(t_2)\); conditional PD \(= 1 - e^{-\lambda(t_2-t_1)}\). Read the question carefully — conditional vs. unconditional is the trap. \[\boxed{Q(t) = 1 - e^{-st/(1-R)}}\]

Variant A: "Price a CDS / find the fair spread"

Steps: (1) survival curve \(V(t_i)\) from given \(\lambda\); (2) \(A = \sum\tau_iP(0,t_i)V(t_i)\); (3) \(C = (1-R)\sum P(0,t^{mid}_i)(V(t_{i-1})-V(t_i))\); (4) \(B\) = accrual (half-period) if required; \[\boxed{s = \frac{C}{A+B}}\] MTM of existing CDS: \((s_{mkt}-s_{contract})\times(A+B)\), sign per position.

Variant B: "Merton model — value equity/debt, find PD and spread"

Step 1 — Identify \(V_0, D, \sigma_V, r, T\). Say the magic sentence: "equity is a European call on the firm's assets with strike \(D\)".
Step 2 — \(d_1 = \frac{\ln(V_0/D)+(r+\frac12\sigma_V^2)T}{\sigma_V\sqrt T}\), \(d_2 = d_1 - \sigma_V\sqrt T\).
Step 3 — \(E_0 = V_0\Phi(d_1) - De^{-rT}\Phi(d_2)\); \(B_0 = V_0 - E_0\).
Step 4 — \(\boxed{PD^Q = \Phi(-d_2)}\); spread \(z = -\frac1T\ln(B_0/D_0)\), \(D_0 = De^{-rT}\). If asked for real-world PD: replace \(r\) by \(\mu_V\) in \(d_2\) and note \(PD^P \lt PD^Q\) typically.

Variant C: "Copula / CDO question"

Steps: (1) \(Q(T) = 1-e^{-\lambda T}\) from CDS; (2) conditional PD given factor \(F\): \[Q(T|F) = \Phi\Bigl(\frac{\Phi^{-1}(Q(T)) - \sqrt\rho F}{\sqrt{1-\rho}}\Bigr)\] (3) given \(F\), defaults independent ⇒ number of defaults binomial\((n, Q(T|F))\); (4) tranche loss between \(n_L = \alpha_Ln/(1-R)\) and \(n_H = \alpha_Hn/(1-R)\); integrate over \(F\sim N(0,1)\). Explain in words: senior tranches suffer only when many default together — correlation risk.

Formula Reference Sheet

All key results for Pricing Financial Assets — University of Copenhagen

● GBM & Itô's Lemma
GBM
\[dS = \mu S\,dt + \sigma S\,dz\]
Log price
\[d(\ln S) = \bigl(\mu - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2\bigr)dt + \sigma\,dz\]
Solution
\[S_T = S_0\exp\!\Bigl[(\mu-\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2)T + \sigma z_T\Bigr]\]
Itô's Lemma — for \(G(S,t)\)
\[dG = \Bigl(G_t + \mu S G_S + \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2 S^2 G_{SS}\Bigr)dt + \sigma S G_S\,dz\]
\(G=S^n\)
\[dG = \bigl(n\mu + \tfrac{1}{2}n(n-1)\sigma^2\bigr)G\,dt + n\sigma G\,dz\]
● Black-Scholes-Merton
Call
\[c = Se^{-\delta T}\Phi(d_1) - Ke^{-rT}\Phi(d_2)\]
Put
\[p = Ke^{-rT}\Phi(-d_2) - Se^{-\delta T}\Phi(-d_1)\]
\[d_1 = \frac{\ln(S/K)+(r-\delta+\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2)T}{\sigma\sqrt{T}},\quad d_2 = d_1 - \sigma\sqrt{T}\]
PCP
\[c - p = Se^{-\delta T} - Ke^{-rT}\]
FX (G-K)
\[c_{\text{FX}} = Se^{-r_f T}\Phi(d_1) - Ke^{-rT}\Phi(d_2)\]
Greeks
\(\Delta\)
\[\Delta_c = e^{-\delta T}\Phi(d_1),\quad \Delta_p = -e^{-\delta T}\Phi(-d_1)\]
\(\Gamma,\mathcal{V}\)
\[\Gamma = \frac{e^{-\delta T}\phi(d_1)}{S\sigma\sqrt{T}},\quad \mathcal{V} = Se^{-\delta T}\phi(d_1)\sqrt{T}\]
BSM PDE
\[\Theta + \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2 S^2\Gamma + rS\Delta = rV\]
● Forward & Futures Pricing
No dividend
\[F = S_0 e^{rT}\]
Div. yield \(\delta\)
\[F = S_0 e^{(r-\delta)T}\]
FX (\(r_f\))
\[F = S_0 e^{(r-r_f)T}\]
Commodity
\[F = S_0 e^{(r+u-y)T}\]
Value at \(t\)
\[V_t = S_t e^{-\delta(T-t)} - Ke^{-r(T-t)}\]
● Binomial Model
RN prob.
\[q = \frac{e^{(r-\delta)h}-d}{u-d},\quad 1-q = \frac{u-e^{(r-\delta)h}}{u-d}\]
Price
\[f_0 = e^{-rh}\bigl[qf_u + (1-q)f_d\bigr]\]
Delta
\[\Delta = \frac{f_u-f_d}{(u-d)S},\quad B = e^{-rh}f_d - \Delta dS\]
CRR
\[u = e^{\sigma\sqrt{h}},\quad d = e^{-\sigma\sqrt{h}}\]
● Market Price of Risk
Asset driven by \(dx = a\,dt + b\,dz\):
\[\frac{\partial f}{\partial t} + (a-\lambda b)\frac{\partial f}{\partial x} + \tfrac{1}{2}b^2\frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x^2} = rf\]
MPR
\[\lambda = \frac{\alpha-r}{\sigma}\quad\text{(same for all derivatives on }x\text{)}\]
Risk-neutral measure \(\mathbb{Q}\):
\[d\widetilde{z} = dz + \lambda\,dt,\quad dx = (a-\lambda b)\,dt + b\,d\widetilde{z}\]
● Short-Rate Models
Vasicek
\[dr = a(b-r)\,dt + \sigma\,d\widetilde{z}\]
CIR
\[dr = a(b-r)\,dt + \sigma\sqrt{r}\,d\widetilde{z}\]
Ho-Lee
\[dr = \theta(t)\,dt + \sigma\,d\widetilde{z}\]
Hull-White
\[dr = (\theta(t)-ar)\,dt + \sigma\,d\widetilde{z},\quad B=\tfrac{1-e^{-a(T-t)}}{a}\]
ZCB price \(P(t,T) = A(t,T)e^{-B(t,T)r}\):
Fwd rate
\[f(t,T) = -\frac{\partial\ln P(t,T)}{\partial T}\]
Spot
\[r(t) = \lim_{T\to t}f(t,T) = f(t,t)\]
● HJM Framework
Dynamics
\[df(t,T) = \alpha(t,T)\,dt + \sigma(t,T)\,d\widetilde{z}\]
No-arb drift
\[\alpha(t,T) = \sigma(t,T)\int_t^T\!\sigma(t,u)\,du\]
ZCB
\[P(t,T) = \exp\!\left(-\int_t^T\!f(t,u)\,du\right)\]
● LMM & IR Derivatives
LIBOR fwd
\[dL_i(t) = \sigma_i L_i(t)\,d\widetilde{z}_i\quad(\text{under }\mathbb{Q}^{T_{i+1}})\]
Caplet
\[\tau P(0,T_{i+1})\bigl[L_i\Phi(d_1)-K\Phi(d_2)\bigr]\]
Swap rate
\[S_0 = \frac{P(0,T_0)-P(0,T_n)}{\sum_{i=1}^n\tau P(0,T_i)}\]
Swaption
\[A\bigl[S_0\Phi(d_1)-K\Phi(d_2)\bigr],\quad A=\sum\tau P(0,T_i)\]
● Credit Risk & Numerics
Merton
\[E = V\Phi(d_1) - De^{-rT}\Phi(d_2)\]
Credit spread
\[s = -\tfrac{1}{T}\ln\!\left(\tfrac{P_{\text{risky}}}{P_{\text{rf}}}\right) \approx h(1-\phi)\]
CDS spread
\[s_{\text{CDS}} = h(1-\phi),\quad h = \frac{s_{\text{CDS}}}{1-\phi}\]
Floor (Black)
\[\tau P(0,T_{i+1})\bigl[K\Phi(-d_2)-F_i\Phi(-d_1)\bigr]\]
Log-Euler
\[\ln S_{t+h} = \ln S_t + (\mu-\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2)h + \sigma\varepsilon\sqrt{h}\]
MC error
\[\text{SE} = \hat{\sigma}/\sqrt{n}\]
● Martingales & Change of Measure
RN pricing
\[V_0 = e^{-rT}\,\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}[V_T]\]
Girsanov
\[\frac{d\mathbb{Q}}{d\mathbb{P}} = \exp\!\left(-\lambda z_T - \tfrac{1}{2}\lambda^2 T\right)\]
Fwd measure
\[V_0 = P(0,T)\,\mathbb{E}^{T}[V_T]\]
Martingale
\[dM = \sigma_M M\,d\widetilde{z} \implies M\text{ is }\mathbb{Q}\text{-martingale}\]

June 2026 — Ordinary Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

Question 1

Consider a non-dividend-paying stock with current price (at time \(t = 0\)) \(S_0\). Over each of the next two time steps of length \(\Delta t\), the stock price is expected to move up by a factor \(u\) or down by a factor \(d\), \(u > d\). The risk-free interest rate is \(r\) (constant and continuously compounded).
(a) Derive the formula for the risk-neutral probability \(q\) of an up-move in a recombining binomial tree, and explain the principle of risk-neutral valuation.
(b) Consider a two-period tree. Derive the price, \(c_0\), of a European call option on the stock at \(t=0\) with strike \(K\) and expiry at \(t=2\Delta t\), expressed using \(q,u,d\) and \(r\).
(c) Assume that \(r \geq 0\) and provide a non-arbitrage argument for why an American call option on this non-dividend-paying stock should never be exercised early. How does the presence of a large dividend change this conclusion?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) See Hull, sections 13.1 and 13.2. To derive the risk-neutral probability \(q\), we consider a portfolio consisting of a long position in \(\Delta\) shares and a short position in one option (of any type). The portfolio is riskless if the value of \(\Delta\) is chosen such that the portfolio's final value is the same for an up-move (\(S_0u\Delta - f_u\)) and a down-move (\(S_0d\Delta - f_d\)), where \(f_u, f_d\) is the payoff of the option in the up-move and down-move, respectively. This implies \(\Delta = \frac{f_u-f_d}{S_0u-S_0d}\). In the absence of arbitrage, this riskless portfolio must earn the risk-free rate \(r\), such that its cost (\(S_0\Delta - f\)) equals its discounted future value.
Solving for the option price \(f\) yields \(f = e^{-r\Delta t}[qf_u + (1-q)f_d]\), where the risk-neutral probability of an up-move is defined as: \[q = \frac{e^{r\Delta t}-d}{u-d}\]
The principle of risk-neutral valuation states that when valuing a derivative by an arbitrage argument, we can assume investors are risk-neutral. In this world, the expected return on all investment assets is the risk-free rate, and the correct discount rate for any expected payoff is also the risk-free rate. This principle is a powerful tool because the resulting formulas are valid in all worlds, not just a risk-neutral one.
(b) See Hull, section 13.3. In a two-period tree, we apply the risk-neutral valuation principle by working backward from the end of the option's life. At expiration (\(t=2\Delta t\)), the possible call payoffs are: \(f_{uu} = \max(u^2S_0-K,0)\), \(f_{ud} = \max(udS_0-K,0)\), \(f_{dd} = \max(d^2S_0-K,0)\). Using backward induction, the value at \(t=0\) is the expected payoff in the risk-neutral world discounted at the risk-free rate: \[c_0 = e^{-2r\Delta t}\bigl[q^2f_{uu} + 2q(1-q)f_{ud} + (1-q)^2f_{dd}\bigr]\]
(c) See Hull, section 13.5. There are two elements in the argument for why an American call option on a non-dividend-paying stock should never be exercised early: (1) Pure Optionality or Insurance Value — a call option provides insurance against the stock price falling below the strike price \(K\); exercising early kills this insurance. (2) Time Value of Money — exercising early requires the holder to pay the strike price \(K\) immediately rather than at expiration; waiting allows the holder to earn interest on \(K\) for the remaining life of the option.
Formally, for a non-dividend-paying stock, the lower bound of a European call is the value of a corresponding forward (with no optionality) \(c \geq S_0 - Ke^{-rT}\). Since an American call \(C\) is at least as valuable as a European call \(c\), and \(S_0 - Ke^{-rT} > S_0 - K\) when \(r > 0\), the option is always worth more than its intrinsic value before maturity, making early exercise suboptimal.
However, the presence of a large dividend changes this conclusion because the stock price is expected to drop on the ex-dividend date. Early exercise may become optimal immediately before an ex-dividend date if the dividend is large enough to offset the loss of interest on the strike price and the lost insurance value.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Binomial Model | Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Risk-neutral probability and the valuation principle: Hold a portfolio long \(\Delta\) shares and short one option. Its value after an up-move is \(S_0u\Delta - f_u\) and after a down-move \(S_0d\Delta - f_d\). Choosing \(\Delta\) to make these equal makes the portfolio riskless: \[S_0u\Delta - f_u = S_0d\Delta - f_d \implies \Delta = \frac{f_u-f_d}{S_0(u-d)}.\] By no-arbitrage, this riskless portfolio must grow at the risk-free rate: \[(S_0\Delta - f)e^{r\Delta t} = S_0u\Delta - f_u.\] Solving for \(f\) and substituting \(\Delta\) gives \[\boxed{f = e^{-r\Delta t}\bigl[qf_u + (1-q)f_d\bigr],\qquad q = \frac{e^{r\Delta t}-d}{u-d}.}\] This hedge argument never used the real-world probability \(p\) of an up-move, so the formula holds in any world — in particular a fictitious risk-neutral world where every asset earns \(r\) in expectation. This is the principle of risk-neutral valuation: we may price as if investors were risk-neutral and discount expected payoffs at \(r\), even though real investors are risk-averse.

(b) Two-period European call price: The terminal payoffs after two moves are \[f_{uu}=\max(u^2S_0-K,0),\quad f_{ud}=\max(udS_0-K,0),\quad f_{dd}=\max(d^2S_0-K,0).\] Working backward one step at a time, at the middle nodes \[f_u = e^{-r\Delta t}[qf_{uu}+(1-q)f_{ud}],\qquad f_d = e^{-r\Delta t}[qf_{ud}+(1-q)f_{dd}],\] and at \(t=0\), \[c_0 = e^{-r\Delta t}[qf_u+(1-q)f_d].\] Substituting the expressions for \(f_u\) and \(f_d\) into \(c_0\) gives \[c_0 = e^{-r\Delta t}\Bigl[q\cdot e^{-r\Delta t}\bigl(qf_{uu}+(1-q)f_{ud}\bigr) + (1-q)\cdot e^{-r\Delta t}\bigl(qf_{ud}+(1-q)f_{dd}\bigr)\Bigr].\] Collecting the two factors of \(e^{-r\Delta t}\) into \(e^{-2r\Delta t}\) gives \[c_0 = e^{-2r\Delta t}\Bigl[q\bigl(qf_{uu}+(1-q)f_{ud}\bigr) + (1-q)\bigl(qf_{ud}+(1-q)f_{dd}\bigr)\Bigr].\] Expanding the brackets gives \[c_0 = e^{-2r\Delta t}\bigl[q^2f_{uu} + q(1-q)f_{ud} + (1-q)qf_{ud} + (1-q)^2f_{dd}\bigr].\] Collecting the two \(f_{ud}\) terms, \(q(1-q)f_{ud}+(1-q)qf_{ud}=2q(1-q)f_{ud}\), gives \[\boxed{c_0 = e^{-2r\Delta t}\bigl[q^2f_{uu} + 2q(1-q)f_{ud} + (1-q)^2f_{dd}\bigr].}\] The coefficients \(q^2\), \(2q(1-q)\), \((1-q)^2\) are exactly the binomial probabilities of reaching the \(uu\), \(ud\)/\(du\), and \(dd\) nodes.

(c) Why an American call is never exercised early on a non-dividend stock: Consider two portfolios at time \(0\): Portfolio A is a European call \(c\) plus cash of \(Ke^{-rT}\); Portfolio B is one share worth \(S_0\). At maturity \(T\), the cash in Portfolio A has grown to \(K\), so Portfolio A is worth \[\max(S_T-K,0) + K.\] Since \(\max(S_T-K,0)+K = \max(S_T,K)\), Portfolio A's value can be written as \[\max(S_T,K).\] Because \(\max(S_T,K) \geq S_T\), Portfolio A is worth at least as much as Portfolio B at \(T\) in every state: \[\max(S_T,K) \geq S_T = \text{value of Portfolio B}.\] By no-arbitrage, a portfolio that is never worth less at \(T\) must be worth at least as much today: \[c + Ke^{-rT} \geq S_0.\] Rearranging for \(c\) gives \[\boxed{c \geq S_0 - Ke^{-rT}.}\] Since \(r \geq 0\), we have \(e^{-rT}\leq 1\), so \[Ke^{-rT} \leq K.\] Multiplying by \(-1\) reverses the inequality, \[-Ke^{-rT} \geq -K,\] and adding \(S_0\) to both sides gives \[S_0 - Ke^{-rT} \geq S_0 - K.\] Combining this with the bound on \(c\) gives \[c \geq S_0-Ke^{-rT} \geq S_0-K = \text{intrinsic value from immediate exercise}.\] Therefore \[\boxed{C_{\text{Am}} = c\ \text{(European)} \quad \text{for a non-dividend-paying stock when } r\geq 0.}\] Exercising early only throws away the time value of money on \(K\) and the insurance against \(S_T<K\). A large dividend changes this conclusion: the stock price drops on the ex-dividend date, reducing the value of holding the call going forward. If the dividend loss exceeds the interest saved by waiting plus the remaining insurance value, exercising immediately before the ex-dividend date becomes optimal.

Question 2

In a model of Merton, a company's debt and equity are valued as derivatives on the company's total assets \(V\) (Enterprise Value).
(a) Explain the logic behind viewing equity as a call option on the company's assets. Define the strike price \(K\) and the maturity \(T\) of this implicit option in the context of a company debt only consisting of one zero-coupon bond.
(b) How can this model be used to estimate the risk-neutral probability of default? Specify which parameters (e.g., asset value \(V_0\) and asset volatility \(\sigma_V\)) are usually not directly observable and how they might be inferred.
(c) Discuss the concept of credit contagion and how it influences the default correlation between different issuers in a portfolio.

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) See Hull, section 24.6. The logic behind viewing equity as a call option on a company's assets stems from the principle of limited liability. Consider a company with total assets \(V\) and a sole debt in the form of a single zero-coupon bond with face value \(D\) maturing at time \(T\). At maturity: if \(V_T > D\), the equity holders will pay off the debt \(D\) and keep the remaining value \(V_T-D\); if \(V_T \leq D\), the equity holders will choose to declare bankruptcy, leaving the assets to the bondholders, and the value of equity becomes zero.
The payoff to equity holders at \(T\) is therefore \(E_T = \max(V_T-D,0)\), which is identical to the payoff of a European call option on the company's assets. In this interpretation, the strike price \(K\) is equal to the debt repayment \(D\) (the face value of the bond), and the maturity of the option is the maturity of the bond \(T\).
(b) In Merton's model, the risk-neutral probability of default is given by the term \(1-\Phi(d_2) = \Phi(-d_2)\) from the Black-Scholes-Merton framework, which represents the probability that the call option will expire out-of-the-money (i.e., \(V_T < D\)).
The parameters current asset value (\(V_0\)) and asset volatility (\(\sigma_V\)) are usually not directly observable because assets are not traded in a single liquid market. They can be inferred by solving two simultaneous equations: (1) The Black-Scholes-Merton formula relating the observable equity price \(E_0\) to \(V_0\) and \(\sigma_V\). (2) The relationship derived from Itô's lemma: \(\sigma_EE_0 = \frac{\partial E}{\partial V}\sigma_VV_0 = \Phi(d_1)\sigma_VV_0\), where \(\sigma_E\) is the observable equity volatility.
For a listed company with market data for \(E_0\) and \(\sigma_E\) (the latter estimated from historical returns or implied from traded options), these equations can be solved numerically for \(V_0\) and \(\sigma_V\).
(c) See Hull, section 24.5 and 24.8. Credit contagion refers to the "ripple effect" where a default by one financial institution or company increases the likelihood of defaults by other companies, thereby threatening the stability of the entire financial system.
This concept is a key driver of default correlation within a portfolio. In normal market conditions, default correlations may be moderate, but they tend to increase significantly during stressed periods as contagion spreads.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Credit Risk | Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Equity as a call option on company assets: With assets \(V_T\) and a single zero-coupon debt of face value \(D\), equity holders repay \(D\) and keep \(V_T-D\) if \(V_T>D\), and walk away with equity worth zero otherwise: \[E_T = \max(V_T-D,0).\] This payoff is exactly that of a European call on the underlying \(V\), so \[\boxed{K = D\ (\text{face value of the bond}),\qquad T = \text{bond maturity}.}\] By the balance-sheet identity \(V_T = E_T + B_T\), debt at maturity is \[B_T = V_T - E_T = V_T - \max(V_T-D,0).\] If \(V_T>D\), \(B_T = V_T-(V_T-D)=D\); if \(V_T\leq D\), \(B_T=V_T-0=V_T\); together this is \[B_T = \min(V_T,D).\] Adding and subtracting \(D\) inside, \(\min(V_T,D) = D-\max(D-V_T,0)\), so \[\boxed{B_T = D-\max(D-V_T,0)} \quad\text{— a risk-free bond minus a put on the assets.}\]

(b) Risk-neutral default probability and the unobservable inputs: Applying the BSM call formula to equity, \[E_0 = V_0\Phi(d_1) - De^{-rT}\Phi(d_2),\qquad d_1 = \frac{\ln(V_0/D)+(r+\tfrac12\sigma_V^2)T}{\sigma_V\sqrt T},\quad d_2 = d_1-\sigma_V\sqrt T.\] Default occurs when \(V_T < D\), which under \(Q\) has probability \[\boxed{\text{PD}_{\text{risk-neutral}} = 1-\Phi(d_2) = \Phi(-d_2).}\] Only \(E_0\) and \(\sigma_E\) are observed in the market, so \(V_0\) and \(\sigma_V\) must be backed out from two equations: \[E_0 = V_0\Phi(d_1)-De^{-rT}\Phi(d_2)\qquad\text{and}\qquad \sigma_EE_0 = \Phi(d_1)\sigma_VV_0,\] solved numerically for the two unknowns \(V_0,\sigma_V\).

(c) Credit contagion and default correlation: Credit contagion is the ripple effect whereby one default (or near-default) triggers further defaults — through counterparty exposures, funding stress, fire-sales, or loss of confidence — propagating through the financial system. It is a structural source of positive default correlation, creating clustering of defaults beyond what shared exposure to common factors (e.g. asset-value correlation in a multi-firm Merton setting) alone would imply: \[\boxed{\text{Normal times: moderate correlation}\quad\longrightarrow\quad \text{Stress: correlation spikes as contagion spreads.}}\] This is why tail/systemic credit risk is understated by models that assume constant default correlation.

Question 3

In the Cox-Ingersoll-Ross (CIR) Model the (instantaneous) short term interest rate \(r\) is described by the process: \[dr = a(b-r)dt + \sigma\sqrt{r}\,dz\] where \(a,b\) and \(\sigma\) are constants (\(\sigma^2 < 2ab\)), and \(dz\) a Brownian increment.
(a) What does this model mean for the behaviour of the short term interest rate?
(b) In this model the solution for the price of a zero-coupon bond can be written \[P(t,T) = A(t,T)e^{-B(t,T)r(t)}\] Derive the duration of the bond.
(c) In some models of the short term interest rate \(r\) (e.g. the Hull-White model) the drift rate is made a function of calendar time \(t\). What is the purpose of the extra flexibility compared to the CIR (or Vasicek) type of models?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) See Hull, section 31.2. It should be noted that the model implies a mean reversion of the spot rate to a level of \(b\) at a rate of \(a\). It should also be explained that the volatility term means that the short term rate cannot go negative.
(b) The answer depends on the definition of duration applied. The version in Hull using the short term interest rate (rather than the yield of the bond) is then \[D = -\frac{\partial P/\partial r}{P} = B(t,T)\]
(c) The time-dependent drift term in the Hull-White model is introduced to be able to incorporate a given, initial term structure, making the values derived from it "arbitrage-free" in relation to the existing securities priced on the current term structure (assuming these to be arbitrage free). This is in contrast to the CIR and Vasicek models of the "equilibrium" or "endogenous"-type that put restrictions on the possible initial term structure (as it is a function of the current spot rate with only few parameters).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Short Rate Models | Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Behaviour of \(r\) under CIR: The drift term \(a(b-r)\) pulls \(r\) down when \(r>b\) and up when \(r<b\), so \[\boxed{r\ \text{mean-reverts to long-run level } b\ \text{at speed } a.}\] The diffusion term \(\sigma\sqrt r\) vanishes as \(r\to 0\), so the random shock cannot push \(r\) below zero, while the drift \(ab>0\) at \(r=0\) pushes it back up: \[\boxed{\sigma^2<2ab \implies r(t) \geq 0\ \text{always (Feller condition)}.}\] This is the key advantage of CIR over Vasicek, where constant volatility \(\sigma\,dz\) allows \(r<0\).

(b) Duration of the bond: Differentiating \(P(t,T)=A(t,T)e^{-B(t,T)r(t)}\) with respect to \(r\), only the exponential term depends on \(r\): \[\frac{\partial P}{\partial r} = A(t,T)\cdot\bigl(-B(t,T)\bigr)e^{-B(t,T)r(t)} = -B(t,T)\,P(t,T).\] The duration ratio therefore reduces to \[D = -\frac{\partial P/\partial r}{P} = -\frac{-B(t,T)P(t,T)}{P(t,T)},\] \[\boxed{D = B(t,T).}\] \(B(t,T)\) plays the same role as Macaulay/modified duration does for a standard bond yield: it measures the sensitivity of the bond price to a small parallel shock in the short rate \(r\), fully determined once the affine model's \(A(t,T)\), \(B(t,T)\) functions are known.

(c) Why make the drift time-dependent (Hull-White vs. CIR/Vasicek): CIR and Vasicek have few constant parameters \((a,b,\sigma)\), so the entire initial term structure \(P(0,T)\) is an output of the model — it need not match the observed market curve exactly. Hull-White instead replaces the constant drift with a time-dependent function \(\theta(t)\), chosen so the model's bond prices match the observed initial term structure exactly: \[\boxed{\theta(t)\ \text{gives enough free parameters to fit today's entire yield curve} \implies \text{model is arbitrage-free relative to currently traded bonds.}}\] Without this flexibility, a pure CIR/Vasicek model could imply theoretical bond prices inconsistent with (and hence arbitrageable against) the bonds actually observed in the market today.

June 2025 — Ordinary Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

Question 1

Let the spot exchange rate for a foreign currency be \(S\), which denotes the value of one unit of the foreign currency measured in the domestic currency. Assume that the exchange rate can be modeled (under the original probability measure \(P\)) by the geometric Brownian motion \[dS = \mu_S S\,dt + \sigma_S S\,dz\] where \(\mu_S\) and \(\sigma_S > 0\) are constants, and where \(dt\) and \(dz\) are the standard shorthand notations for a small time-step and a Brownian increment. Assume that the continuously compounded domestic and foreign risk-free interest rates are constants \(r\) and \(r_f\), respectively.
(a) What is the forward exchange rate, \(F(0,T)\), i.e. the contracted price for the delivery of one unit of the foreign currency at \(T\)? Find the answer by an arbitrage argument.
(b) What will the drift rate of the exchange rate \(S\) be under the domestic risk neutral probability measure (\(Q\)) and a no-arbitrage assumption? Comment on the influence of the difference of domestic and foreign interest rates on the result.
(c) Let \(Z = 1/S\) be the exchange rate as measured in the foreign currency. Use Itô's lemma to find the process followed by \(Z\) where you eventually substitute \(S\) by \(1/Z\).

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Refer Hull, Section 17.5. The forward exchange rate \(F(0,T)\) is the price for one unit of the foreign currency to be delivered and paid for at time \(T\) as contracted at time 0. There are the following two ways at time 0 to attain one unit of the foreign currency at \(T\):
A) Borrow \(S_0 e^{-r_f T}\) in the domestic currency at the domestic interest rate \(r\), and use the proceeds to buy \(e^{-r_f T}\) units of the foreign currency. Place the foreign currency at deposit earning the foreign interest rate \(r_f\), and at time \(T\) you will have 1 unit of the foreign currency in your deposit (with no net outlay at time 0). At time \(T\) also repay the domestic currency loan which now stands at \(S_0 e^{-r_f T}e^{rT} = S_0 e^{(r-r_f)T}\).
B) Or you can enter into the forward contract to buy 1 unit of the foreign currency paying \(F(0,T)\) at time \(T\).
If there is to be no arbitrage opportunities we must have \[F(0,T) = S_0 e^{(r-r_f)T}\]
(b) Refer Hull, Chapter 17. The drift rate under \(Q\) will be \((r - r_f)\) (proportional to \(S\)) because the total return on holding the foreign currency must be \(r\) (we can treat the return on the foreign currency as we treat a continuously paid dividend on a stock).
(c) Refer Hull, Questions 17.31 (11th ed). The transformation \(Z\) is independent of \(t\), and you (using \(\frac{\partial Z}{\partial S} = -S^{-2}\) and \(\frac{\partial^2 Z}{\partial S^2} = 2S^{-3}\)) get \[dZ = \Bigl(-S^{-2}\mu_S S + \tfrac{1}{2}\cdot 2S^{-3}\sigma_S^2 S^2\Bigr)dt + S^{-2}\sigma_S S\,dz\] where we for the volatility use the sign convention that this is positive. By substitution we get \[dZ = \bigl(\sigma_S^2 - \mu_S\bigr)Z\,dt + \sigma_S Z\,dz\] (under \(P\)) or equivalently \(dZ = (\sigma_S^2 + r_f - r)Z\,dt + \sigma_S Z\,dz\) (under \(Q\)). A solution under either of the two measures is satisfactory. The exchange rate when measured in the foreign currency has the same volatility, but the drift changes, such that \(\mu_S\) quite intuitively enters with a negative sign. However, since the transformation is not linear, the two PDEs are not symmetric, and a volatility adjustment also enters into the drift rate. In what is not part of the syllabus and thus not required of the answer, this asymmetry (Siegel's Paradox, Hull Business Snapshot 30.1) is resolved if we change measure to the foreign risk neutral probability measure \(Q_f\) (where we should add \(-\sigma_S^2\) to the drift).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: GBM  ·  Forward Pricing   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Forward exchange rate — arbitrage argument: There are two ways to own one unit of the foreign currency at \(T\) with no cash outlay at \(t=0\). Strategy A: borrow \(S_0 e^{-r_f T}\) domestically at rate \(r\), buy \(e^{-r_f T}\) units of foreign currency, and invest it at \(r_f\) so the foreign account grows to exactly 1 unit at \(T\); the domestic loan then stands at \(S_0 e^{-r_f T}\cdot e^{rT} = S_0 e^{(r-r_f)T}\). Strategy B: enter a forward contract, paying \(F(0,T)\) at \(T\). Since both deliver the same payoff with no upfront cost, no-arbitrage requires their costs to match: \[\boxed{F(0,T) = S_0\,e^{(r-r_f)T}.}\]

(b) Drift under the domestic risk-neutral measure \(Q\): Holding foreign currency earns the foreign rate \(r_f\), much like a continuous dividend yield. Under \(Q\), the total expected return on holding it must equal the domestic risk-free rate \(r\), so the capital-gain drift plus \(r_f\) equals \(r\), giving a capital-gain drift of \(r-r_f\): \[\boxed{dS = (r - r_f)S\,dt + \sigma_S S\,dz^Q.}\] If \(r > r_f\), the currency is expected to appreciate; if \(r < r_f\), it is expected to depreciate (uncovered interest rate parity).

(c) Process for \(Z = 1/S\) via Itô's lemma: The partial derivatives of \(Z=S^{-1}\) are \[\frac{\partial Z}{\partial t} = 0,\quad \frac{\partial Z}{\partial S} = -S^{-2},\quad \frac{\partial^2 Z}{\partial S^2} = 2S^{-3}.\] Applying Itô's lemma with \((dS)^2 = \sigma_S^2 S^2\,dt\), \[dZ = \frac{\partial Z}{\partial S}\,dS + \tfrac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^2 Z}{\partial S^2}(dS)^2 = (-S^{-2})(\mu_S S\,dt + \sigma_S S\,dz) + \tfrac{1}{2}(2S^{-3})\sigma_S^2 S^2\,dt.\] Distributing \(-S^{-2}\) across the first bracket gives \[dZ = \bigl(-\mu_S S^{-1}\,dt - \sigma_S S^{-1}\,dz\bigr) + \tfrac12(2S^{-3})\sigma_S^2S^2\,dt.\] Simplifying the coefficient of the last term, \(\tfrac12(2S^{-3})\sigma_S^2S^2=\sigma_S^2S^{-1}\), gives \[dZ = -\mu_S S^{-1}\,dt - \sigma_S S^{-1}\,dz + \sigma_S^2 S^{-1}\,dt.\] Collecting the two \(dt\)-terms gives \[dZ = (-\mu_S S^{-1} + \sigma_S^2 S^{-1})\,dt - \sigma_S S^{-1}\,dz.\] Substituting \(S^{-1}=Z\), \[\boxed{dZ = (\sigma_S^2 - \mu_S)Z\,dt + \sigma_S Z\,dz}\quad\text{(under }P\text{).}\] Under \(Q\), replacing \(\mu_S\) with \(r-r_f\) gives \(dZ = (\sigma_S^2 + r_f - r)Z\,dt + \sigma_S Z\,dz^Q\).

Question 2

Suppose that certain securities have prices that depend on a single state variable \(\theta\) given by the process \[\frac{d\theta}{\theta} = m\,dt + s\,dz\] where \(dz\) is a Brownian increment. Consider two such securities (i.e. with risk derived from \(\theta\)) with non-zero prices \(f_1\) and \(f_2\) and no dividends. Assume that the prices of the securities follow geometric Brownian motions. Also, assume a continuously compounded risk-free interest rate of \(r\).
(a) Find an arbitrage relationship between the drift rate of the two securities, their risk (expressed as volatility) and the risk free interest rate.
(b) Derive an expression for a general risk premium, \(\lambda\), per unit of \(\theta\)-risk.
(c) What will the relationship be for a security that earns a continuously compounded dividend rate of \(\delta\)?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Refer Hull, section 28.1. Note that \(\theta\) is a state variable and not itself a traded asset. Suppose that the processes followed by \(f_1\) and \(f_2\) are \(\frac{df_1}{f_1} = \mu_1\,dt + \sigma_1\,dz\) and \(\frac{df_2}{f_2} = \mu_2\,dt + \sigma_2\,dz\) where \(\mu_1, \mu_2, \sigma_1\), and \(\sigma_2\) are functions of \(\theta\) and \(t\). Now form a portfolio of \(\sigma_2 f_2\) units of the first security and \(-\sigma_1 f_1\) of the second security, and note that this portfolio is locally risk free, i.e. does not depend on \(z\). Thus it must in arbitrage equilibrium have a drift rate equal to the risk free interest rate \(r\). The value of the portfolio \(\Pi\) will be \(\Pi = (\sigma_2 f_2)f_1 - (\sigma_1 f_1)f_2\) and the (risk-less) drift of this will be \(\mu_1\sigma_2 f_1 f_2 - \mu_2\sigma_1 f_1 f_2 = r(\sigma_2 f_1 f_2 - \sigma_1 f_1 f_2)\) where the equal sign follows from the above arbitrage argument. Divide by \(f_1 f_2\) and rearrange to get: \[(\mu_1 - r)\sigma_2 = (\mu_2 - r)\sigma_1 \quad \text{and} \quad \frac{\mu_1 - r}{\sigma_1} = \frac{\mu_2 - r}{\sigma_2}\] Thus the two securities have the same return in excess of the risk free interest rate per unit of risk.
(b) Since the two securities were chosen arbitrarily the above relationship must hold for all such traded securities in this economy. This motivates a definition of a risk premium per unit of risk by \[\lambda = \frac{\mu - r}{\sigma}\]
(c) Refer Hull, question 28.7. If there is a security with price \(f\) and a continuous dividend rate of \(\delta\) and drift rate of \(\mu\), consider a new security identical but for dividends continuously being reinvested in the security. This new security must have a drift rate of \(\mu + \delta\) and same proportional risk. Thus this security can be treated as the previously analysed securities, and it must satisfy: \[\mu + \delta - r = \lambda\sigma\]

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Market Price of Risk   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) No-arbitrage: market price of risk from a riskless portfolio: Both \(f_1\) and \(f_2\) depend on \(\theta\), so \[\frac{df_i}{f_i} = \mu_i\,dt + \sigma_i\,dz,\quad i=1,2.\] Hold \(+\sigma_2 f_2\) units of \(f_1\) and \(-\sigma_1 f_1\) units of \(f_2\), so the portfolio value is \(\Pi = \sigma_2 f_2 f_1 - \sigma_1 f_1 f_2\). Substituting \(df_1=f_1(\mu_1dt+\sigma_1dz)\) and \(df_2=f_2(\mu_2dt+\sigma_2dz)\), its change is \[d\Pi = \sigma_2 f_2\,df_1 - \sigma_1 f_1\,df_2 = \sigma_2f_2f_1(\mu_1dt+\sigma_1dz) - \sigma_1f_1f_2(\mu_2dt+\sigma_2dz).\] Expanding the brackets gives \[d\Pi = f_1f_2\mu_1\sigma_2\,dt + f_1f_2\sigma_1\sigma_2\,dz - f_1f_2\mu_2\sigma_1\,dt - f_1f_2\sigma_1\sigma_2\,dz.\] Collecting the \(dt\)-terms and the \(dz\)-terms separately gives \[d\Pi = f_1 f_2(\mu_1\sigma_2 - \mu_2\sigma_1)\,dt + f_1 f_2(\sigma_1\sigma_2 - \sigma_1\sigma_2)\,dz.\] The \(dz\)-terms cancel exactly, so \(\Pi\) is riskless and must earn the risk-free rate, \(d\Pi = r\Pi\,dt\); substituting \(\Pi=f_1f_2(\sigma_2-\sigma_1)\) on the right-hand side gives \[f_1 f_2(\mu_1\sigma_2 - \mu_2\sigma_1)\,dt = r(\sigma_2-\sigma_1)f_1 f_2\,dt.\] Dividing both sides by \(f_1 f_2\,dt\) gives \[\mu_1\sigma_2-\mu_2\sigma_1 = r\sigma_2 - r\sigma_1.\] Moving the \(r\)-terms across the equals sign gives \(\mu_1\sigma_2-r\sigma_2 = \mu_2\sigma_1-r\sigma_1\), i.e. \((\mu_1-r)\sigma_2 = (\mu_2-r)\sigma_1\), so \[\boxed{\frac{\mu_1-r}{\sigma_1} = \frac{\mu_2-r}{\sigma_2} =: \lambda.}\]

(b) Market price of risk \(\lambda\): The ratio \(\lambda = (\mu-r)/\sigma\) is the same for every security driven by the same Brownian motion \(z\) — it is the excess return per unit of risk. \(\lambda\) depends on \(\theta\) and \(t\) but not on which derivative is chosen.

(c) Security with continuous dividend rate \(\delta\): Consider reinvesting the dividends continuously back into \(f\). This reinvested process has drift \(\mu+\delta\) and the same volatility \(\sigma\), and is itself non-dividend-paying, so the no-arbitrage condition above applies to it directly: \[\frac{(\mu + \delta) - r}{\sigma} = \lambda,\] \[\boxed{\mu + \delta - r = \lambda\sigma.}\] Dividend income is part of total return, so it is the excess total return per unit of risk that equals \(\lambda\).

Question 3

Suppose that the price at time \(t\) of a zero-coupon bond maturing at time \(T\), \(P(t,T)\) follows the Itô-process \[dP(t,T) = \mu_P P(t,T)\,dt + \sigma_P P(t,T)\,dz\] and the price \(f\) of a derivative dependent on the bond follows the process \[df = \mu_f f\,dt + \sigma_f f\,dz\] Assume only one source of uncertainty represented by \(z\), and that \(f\) provides no income.
(a) What is the forward price \(F(t,T)\) of \(f\) at time \(t\) for a contract maturing at time \(T\)?
(b) What is the process followed by \(F\) in a world defined by \(P(t,T)\) as numeraire? (Hint: Analyze the ratio \(f/P(t,T)\) by using Itô's lemma on the logarithm.)
(c) What is the process followed by \(F\) in the traditional (\(Q\)) risk-neutral world?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) With no income from \(f\) no-arbitrage means that \[F(t,T) = \frac{f(t)}{P(t,T)}\] since you can either buy the derivative with price \(f\) at time \(t\) financed by a short sale of \(\frac{f(t)}{P(t,T)}\) bonds (you issue bonds to receive the needed \(f\)), you repay the loan at time \(T\); or enter into the forward contract. In both cases, there is no cash flow at time \(t\), and you have one unit of \(f\) at time \(T\).
(b) Refer Hull section 28.3. From Itô's lemma we get: \[d\ln P = \bigl(\mu_P - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_P^2\bigr)dt + \sigma_P\,dz\] \[d\ln f = \bigl(\mu_f - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_f^2\bigr)dt + \sigma_f\,dz\] Therefore, \[d\ln\frac{f}{P} = d(\ln f - \ln P) = \bigl(\mu_f - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_f^2 - \mu_P + \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_P^2\bigr)dt + (\sigma_f - \sigma_P)\,dz\] so that (in a last step again using the Itô-lemma, but now on the exponential function) \[d\frac{f}{P} = \bigl(\mu_f - \mu_P + \sigma_P^2 - \sigma_P\sigma_f\bigr)\frac{f}{P}\,dt + (\sigma_f - \sigma_P)\frac{f}{P}\,dz\] or \(dF = (\mu_f - \mu_P + \sigma_P^2 - \sigma_P\sigma_f)F\,dt + (\sigma_f - \sigma_P)F\,dz\).
(c) In the (\(Q\)-) risk neutral world prices of both the traded instruments \(f(t)\) and \(P(t,T)\) will have a drift rate equal to the instantaneous short rate \(r\), thus \(\mu_f = \mu_P = r\). We see that the drift derived above is altered by the change of probability measure but is not equal to zero. However, the volatility part remains unchanged. Note also that while the drift rate of \(F\) under \(Q\) will not generally be \(r\) (because \(F\) is not the price of a traded asset, however for a given fixed contract price \(K\) the value of that contract will have a drift rate of \(r\) under \(Q\)). You may also note that in a (\(Q^T\)-) risk neutral world defined by the numeraire \(P(t,T)\), the drift of \(F\) is zero. The process for \(F\) in this world is thus \(dF = (\sigma_f - \sigma_P)F\,dz\). Also here, the volatility is unchanged by the change of measure. Either of the above observations (under \(Q\) or \(Q^T\)) will be considered as a satisfactory answer.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Forward Pricing  ·  Numeraire & Change of Measure   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Forward price \(F(t,T) = f(t)/P(t,T)\): There are two ways to hold one unit of \(f\) at time \(T\) with no cash outlay at \(t\). A) Buy \(f\) now, financed by selling \(f(t)/P(t,T)\) bonds, repaid at \(T\). B) Enter a forward contract at price \(F(t,T)\). Both give one unit of \(f\) at \(T\) at zero upfront cost, so no-arbitrage requires \[\boxed{F(t,T) = \frac{f(t)}{P(t,T)}.}\]

(b) Process for \(F\) under the \(T\)-forward measure: Since \(df/f = \mu_f\,dt + \sigma_f\,dz\) and \(dP/P = \mu_P\,dt + \sigma_P\,dz\), Itô's lemma on the logarithms gives \[d\ln f = \bigl(\mu_f - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_f^2\bigr)dt + \sigma_f\,dz,\qquad d\ln P = \bigl(\mu_P - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_P^2\bigr)dt + \sigma_P\,dz.\] Since \(\ln F = \ln f - \ln P\), \[d\ln F = \bigl(\mu_f - \mu_P + \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_P^2 - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_f^2\bigr)dt + (\sigma_f - \sigma_P)\,dz.\] Write this as \(d\ln F = a\,dt+b\,dz\) with \[a = \mu_f - \mu_P + \tfrac{1}{2}\bigl(\sigma_P^2 - \sigma_f^2\bigr),\qquad b = \sigma_f - \sigma_P.\] Applying Itô's lemma to \(F=e^{\ln F}\) (general formula for the exponential of an Itô process) gives \[dF = F\Bigl[\bigl(a + \tfrac{1}{2}b^2\bigr)dt + b\,dz\Bigr].\] Substituting the (still unsimplified) expressions for \(a\) and \(b\) gives \[dF = F\left[\left(\mu_f - \mu_P + \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_P^2 - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_f^2 + \tfrac{1}{2}(\sigma_f-\sigma_P)^2\right)dt + (\sigma_f-\sigma_P)\,dz\right].\] Expanding \(\tfrac12(\sigma_f-\sigma_P)^2 = \tfrac12\sigma_f^2 - \sigma_f\sigma_P + \tfrac12\sigma_P^2\) gives \[dF = F\left[\left(\mu_f - \mu_P + \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_P^2 - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_f^2 + \tfrac12\sigma_f^2 - \sigma_f\sigma_P + \tfrac12\sigma_P^2\right)dt + (\sigma_f-\sigma_P)\,dz\right].\] The \(\pm\tfrac12\sigma_f^2\) terms cancel and the two \(\tfrac12\sigma_P^2\) terms combine to \(\sigma_P^2\), giving \[dF = F\left[\left(\mu_f - \mu_P + \sigma_P^2 - \sigma_f\sigma_P\right)dt + (\sigma_f-\sigma_P)\,dz\right].\] Factoring \(\sigma_P^2-\sigma_f\sigma_P = \sigma_P(\sigma_P-\sigma_f)\) gives \[\boxed{dF = \bigl(\mu_f - \mu_P + \sigma_P(\sigma_P-\sigma_f)\bigr)F\,dt + (\sigma_f-\sigma_P)F\,dz.}\] Under \(Q^T\), the measure defined by taking the bond \(P(t,T)\) as numeraire, \(F\) is by construction a martingale: \[\boxed{dF = (\sigma_f - \sigma_P)F\,dz^T.}\]

(c) Process for \(F\) under the standard risk-neutral measure \(Q\): Under \(Q\), all traded assets earn \(r\), so \(\mu_f = \mu_P = r\), and the drift term above collapses: \[\boxed{dF = \sigma_P(\sigma_P-\sigma_f)F\,dt + (\sigma_f-\sigma_P)F\,dz^Q.}\] Under \(Q\), \(F\) has non-zero drift because \(F=f/P\) is not itself a traded asset price. Only under \(Q^T\), with \(P(t,T)\) as numeraire, does \(F\) become driftless.

August 2025 — Re-Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

Question 1

Consider an economy with securities available for investment at time \(t = 0\) and with ultimate payment at time \(t = T\). A stock and a zero coupon bond can be purchased at time 0 at prices \(S_0\) and \(e^{-rT}\), respectively, where \(r\) is a constant. Neither of these securities has payments between time 0 and \(T\). Assume that at time \(T\) the realization of two possible states of the world is revealed. The stock has a payoff of \(uS_0\) and \(dS_0\), depending on the state, where \(u > a > d \geq 0\) are constants, and \(a = e^{rT}\). The bond has a payment of 1 at \(t = T\), irrespective of the state.
(a) Explain what is meant by risk neutral probabilities and (Arrow-Debreu) state prices and determine these for this economy.
(b) What is the forward price \(F(0,T)\) at time 0 for delivery of the stock at time \(T\)? What is the arbitrage argument?
(c) Consider a forward contract on a stock maturing at time \(T\). Compare the price of a European put option on the stock with the price of a European put on the forward contract on the stock, both maturing at \(T\) and having the same strike \(K\).

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) State prices \(\psi\) are implicit prices that satisfy that current prices of each security is the sum of the time and state dependent payoffs of the security at corresponding (security independent) state prices. Risk neutral probabilities \(q\) are probabilities that satisfy that current prices of each security is the discounted expected payoff using of these (security independent) probabilities and discounting with a risk free rate. The existence of both follows if there are no arbitrage opportunities in the economy. In the concrete case, the state prices \((\psi_u, \psi_d)\) satisfy the two equations: \[1 = a\psi_u + a\psi_d \quad \text{and} \quad 1 = u\psi_u + d\psi_d\] with the solution \(\psi = (\psi_u, \psi_d) = \left(a^{-1}\frac{a-d}{u-d},\, a^{-1}\frac{u-a}{u-d}\right)\) which are well defined and positive with our assumptions. The risk neutral probabilities are \(q = (q_u, q_d) = a\psi\), and note that \(q_u + q_d = 1\).
(b) Consider two strategies to end with the stock at time \(T\): A) Enter into a forward contract. There is no cash flow at time 0. You pay the agreed forward price at time \(T\). B) Borrow \(S_0\) at the risk free rate (i.e. shorting the bond), buy the stock and hold it to time \(T\), where you repay the loan by paying \(aS_0\). Here also there is no net cash flow at time 0. In the absence of arbitrage opportunities, the forward price contracted at time 0 is thus \(F(0,T) = aS_0\).
(c) Since the forward contract will expire with \(F(T,T) = S_T\), the two put options will have the same price.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Binomial Model  ·  Forward Pricing   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Risk-neutral probabilities and Arrow-Debreu state prices: Risk-neutral probabilities \(q,1-q\) are (not real-world) probabilities such that every asset's expected payoff discounted at \(r\) equals its current price. For the stock, \[S_0 = e^{-rT}[q\cdot uS_0 + (1-q)\cdot dS_0].\] Dividing both sides by \(S_0\) gives \[1 = e^{-rT}[qu + (1-q)d].\] Multiplying both sides by \(e^{rT}=a\) gives \[a = qu+(1-q)d.\] Expanding the right-hand side, \(a = qu+d-qd\), so \[a-d = qu-qd = q(u-d).\] Dividing by \((u-d)\) gives \[\boxed{q = \frac{a-d}{u-d},\qquad 1-q = \frac{u-a}{u-d}.}\] Arrow-Debreu state prices \(\psi_u,\psi_d\) are the price today of £1 paid in one state and £0 in the other: \[\boxed{\psi_u = e^{-rT}\cdot q = a^{-1}\frac{a-d}{u-d},\qquad \psi_d = e^{-rT}(1-q) = a^{-1}\frac{u-a}{u-d}.}\] As a check, \(\psi_u+\psi_d = e^{-rT}\), the bond price.

(b) Forward price \(F(0,T)\) — arbitrage argument: There are two strategies to own the stock at \(T\). A) Buy the stock at \(S_0\), financed by a loan at \(r\); the cost at \(T\) is \(S_0e^{rT}=aS_0\). B) Enter a forward at price \(F(0,T)\), paid at \(T\). No-arbitrage requires these to coincide: \[\boxed{F(0,T) = S_0 e^{rT} = aS_0.}\]

(c) Put on the stock vs. put on the forward: The put on the stock pays \(\max(K-S_T,0)\) at \(T\). The put on the forward pays \(\max(K-F_T,0)\), and since the forward price converges to the spot price at expiry, \(F_T=S_T\), so it pays \(\max(K-S_T,0)\) as well. The payoffs are identical at \(T\), so by no-arbitrage \[\boxed{p_{\text{stock}} = p_{\text{forward}}.}\]

Question 2

(a) In the one-factor Gaussian model of defaults the default of an obligor \(i\) is indicated by \[x_i = a_i F + \sqrt{1-a_i^2}\,Z_i\] where constants \(a_i \in [-1,1]\), and the random variables \(F\) and \(Z_i\), \(i = 1,\ldots,n\), have independent standard normal distributions. Give an interpretation of the model and its parameters. What is the (copula) default correlation between \(x_i\) and \(x_j\) for some \(j \neq i\)?
(b) In this model the indicator \(x_i\) is compared to the probability \(Q_i(T)\) that the obligor has defaulted before or at time \(T\). Suppose that the obligor defaults at time \(t\). Which value of the indicator \(x_i\) does this correspond to?
(c) Explain for a tranched structured product such as a Credit Debt Obligation what the terms attachment point and detachment point refer to.
(d) With many obligors, is the value of the most senior tranche in general positively or negatively influenced by an increase in the default correlation of obligors, keeping the average credit spread constant? (Hint: Consider the extreme case where defaults are totally dependent, so all obligors either default or not, and the extreme case where there is no systematic risk and very many obligors.)

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Refer to Hull sections 24.8 and 25.10. Note that \(F\) is a common factor determining the covariance structure, whereas \(Z_i\) are idiosyncratic factors, and \(a_i\) are sensitivities to the common factor. Note that \(\text{Var}[x_i] = a_i^2 + (1-a_i^2) = 1\) and \(\text{Cov}[x_i,x_j] = a_i a_j\) using that \(F\) and \(Z_i\) are standard normal and independent. In implementation the \(a_i\) are often approximated as the correlation between \(i\)'s equity return and some broad market index (i.e. P-type correlations). They could also be inferred from e.g. prices of tranched CDOs (i.e. Q-type correlations)).
(b) The indicator can be interpreted as corresponding to a value of \(\Phi^{-1}(Q_i(t))\) (where \(\Phi\) is the standard normal distribution function), i.e. we investigate defaults on the inverse normal evaluated on the relevant percentile of the given (weakly increasing) cumulated probability of default such that an observation of a larger \(x_i\) than this critical value means a default before or at \(t\), i.e. a large \(x_i\) indicates a late default (or no default if after the maturity of the debt).
(c) Refer to Hull section 25.8. The payoff of the debt instruments underlying a cash CDO (or credit derivatives underlying a synthetic CDO) is distributed to the different tranches of the CDO so that the most senior tranche has priority to the payoffs (in a waterfall structure). Losses thus first hit the most junior tranche, called the equity tranche. The specific tranche is characterized by its attachment point and detachment point. The attachment point is the level of total losses relative to the total underlying portfolio that are protected by more junior tranches (thus for the equity tranche the attachment point is zero). For larger losses than the attachment point of the tranche the tranche will suffer losses. The detachment point is the level of total losses relative to the total portfolio that will deplete the tranche completely. There are further arrangements to protect the senior tranches, but the above will be a satisfactory answer.
(d) Refer to Hull section 25.9. The most senior tranche will — expected loss and recovery being equal as reflected in a constant credit spread — in general benefit from diversification (and thus from low correlations), since then the stochastic losses will be closer to the expected losses and less likely to hit the most senior tranche. On the contrary, with high correlation the dispersion of total losses will be high with some risk of hitting the senior tranche.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Credit Risk   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Gaussian copula model — interpretation and copula correlation: In \(x_i = a_i F + \sqrt{1-a_i^2}\,Z_i\), with \(F,Z_i\sim N(0,1)\) independent, \(F\) is a common systematic factor (macro/market conditions), \(Z_i\) is an idiosyncratic firm-specific factor, and \(a_i\) is the sensitivity to the systematic factor, so \(a_i^2\) is the share of systematic risk and \(1-a_i^2\) the idiosyncratic share. The variance checks out: \[\text{Var}(x_i) = a_i^2\text{Var}(F) + (1-a_i^2)\text{Var}(Z_i) = a_i^2 + (1-a_i^2) = 1.\] For \(j\neq i\), since the cross terms vanish by independence, \[\text{Cov}(x_i,x_j) = \text{Cov}\bigl(a_i F + \sqrt{1-a_i^2}Z_i,\; a_j F + \sqrt{1-a_j^2}Z_j\bigr) = a_i a_j.\] Therefore \[\boxed{\rho_{ij} = a_i a_j.}\]

(b) Default threshold and conditional default probability: Since \(x_i\) is standard normal, default before \(T\) corresponds to \(\Phi(x_i)\leq Q_i(T)\), i.e. \[x_i \leq \Phi^{-1}(Q_i(T)) =: C_i(T).\] Substituting \(x_i = a_iF+\sqrt{1-a_i^2}Z_i\) into this inequality and conditioning on the systematic factor \(F\) gives \[a_iF + \sqrt{1-a_i^2}Z_i \leq C_i(T).\] Moving \(a_iF\) across the inequality gives \[\sqrt{1-a_i^2}Z_i \leq C_i(T) - a_i F.\] Dividing both sides by \(\sqrt{1-a_i^2}>0\) gives \[Z_i \leq \frac{C_i(T) - a_i F}{\sqrt{1-a_i^2}}.\] Since \(Z_i\sim N(0,1)\), the conditional default probability is \[\boxed{P(\text{default}_i \mid F) = \Phi\!\left(\frac{C_i(T) - a_i F}{\sqrt{1-a_i^2}}\right).}\]

(c) Tranched CDO — attachment and detachment points: Losses flow to tranches bottom-up in a waterfall structure. The equity tranche absorbs losses first (attachment 0%, detachment e.g. 3%), the mezzanine tranche absorbs intermediate losses (e.g. 3%–7%), and the senior tranche only absorbs losses once the tranches below it are wiped out (e.g. 7%–100%). The attachment point is the portfolio loss percentage at which a tranche starts losing value; the detachment point is the loss percentage at which it is fully wiped out.

(d) Effect of default correlation on tranches: With low correlation, losses are diversified and concentrated in the junior tranches, so the senior tranche stays very safe and its spread is low. With high correlation, either most obligors default or almost none do, so the senior tranche also becomes exposed and its spread rises. The senior tranche benefits from low correlation, while the equity tranche benefits from high correlation, since it already absorbs losses in the "typical" scenario and gains from the all-or-nothing outcomes that high correlation produces.

Question 3

Consider a one factor interest rate model with the following process for the instantaneous short rate \(r\): \[dr = m(r,t)\,dt + s(r,t)\,dz\] where \(z\) is a Brownian motion.
(a) View the price of a zero-coupon bond with maturity date \(T\) as a function \(P_T(r,t)\). Use Itô's lemma to find an expression for the process that \(dP_T(r,t)\) follows. What will the drift rate be under the traditional risk neutral probability measure (\(Q\))?
(b) In the Vasicek model the instantaneous short term interest rate \(r\) is described by the process: \[dr = a(b-r)\,dt + \sigma\,dz\] where \(a \geq 0\), \(b\) and \(\sigma\) are constants. Give an interpretation of the model and its parameters.
(c) Explain that the Vasicek model is not generally consistent with a given initial interest rate curve. How can the model be modified to be consistent with such an initial curve?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) A direct implementation of the Itô-lemma yields (with function arguments suppressed): \[dP_T = \Bigl(\frac{\partial P_T}{\partial t} + \tfrac{1}{2}s^2\frac{\partial^2 P_T}{\partial r^2} + m\frac{\partial P_T}{\partial r}\Bigr)dt + s\frac{\partial P_T}{\partial r}\,dz\] The expected return under \(Q\) must be the risk free rate, so the drift rate of the zero coupon price must be \(rP_T\).
(b) Refer Hull section 31.2. The interest rate will exhibit a mean reversion to a level of \(b\) at a rate determined by \(a\). It will have normally distributed innovations of size determined by \(\sigma\). As rates conditional on the initial level are normally distributed they can become negative.
(c) Refer Hull section 32.1. The Vasicek model is of the so-called equilibrium type of interest rate models that has an initial yield curve as an output. The model has only a few parameters to calibrate to an initial yield curve. The so-called no-arbitrage models can take an initial yield curve as an input. As shown by Hull and White the Vasicek model can be modified to a no-arbitrage model by letting the parameter \(b\) above be a function of time. It can be explicitly derived (which is not required here).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Short Rate Models   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Itô's lemma on \(P_T(r,t)\) — process and \(Q\)-measure drift: Applying the general Itô formula with partial-derivative placeholders, \[dP_T = \frac{\partial P_T}{\partial t}dt + \frac{\partial P_T}{\partial r}dr + \tfrac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^2 P_T}{\partial r^2}(dr)^2.\] Substituting \(dr = m\,dt+s\,dz\) into the linear term gives \[dP_T = \frac{\partial P_T}{\partial t}dt + \frac{\partial P_T}{\partial r}(m\,dt+s\,dz) + \tfrac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^2 P_T}{\partial r^2}(dr)^2.\] Substituting \((dr)^2 = s^2\,dt\) into the quadratic term gives \[dP_T = \frac{\partial P_T}{\partial t}dt + \frac{\partial P_T}{\partial r}(m\,dt+s\,dz) + \tfrac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^2 P_T}{\partial r^2}s^2\,dt.\] Expanding the middle term and collecting all \(dt\)-terms together gives \[\boxed{dP_T = \left(\frac{\partial P_T}{\partial t} + m\frac{\partial P_T}{\partial r} + \tfrac{1}{2}s^2\frac{\partial^2 P_T}{\partial r^2}\right)dt + s\frac{\partial P_T}{\partial r}\,dz.}\] Under \(Q\), the bond price must earn the short rate \(r\), so the drift equals \(rP_T\): \[\boxed{\frac{\partial P_T}{\partial t} + m^Q\frac{\partial P_T}{\partial r} + \tfrac{1}{2}s^2\frac{\partial^2 P_T}{\partial r^2} = r P_T.}\]

(b) Vasicek model — mean reversion: Under \(Q\), \(dr = a(b-r)\,dt + \sigma\,dz^Q\). The rate mean-reverts to \(b\) at speed \(a\): when \(r>b\) the drift is negative (pulled down), and when \(r<b\) it is positive (pulled up). Increments are normally distributed with constant volatility \(\sigma\), so the rate can go negative.

(c) Hull-White extension vs. Vasicek: Vasicek is an equilibrium model — the initial term structure is determined by \(a,b,r_0\), and may not match observed market prices. Hull-White replaces \(ab\) with a time-dependent \(\theta(t)\) to fit the current term structure exactly, making it arbitrage-free with respect to currently traded bonds.

June 2024 — Ordinary Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

Question 1

Consider an economy with securities available for investment at time \(t = 0\) and with ultimate payment at time \(t = T\). A stock and a zero coupon bond can be purchased at \(t = 0\) at prices \(S_0\) and \(e^{-rT}\), respectively, where \(r\) is a constant. Neither of these securities has payments between time 0 and \(T\). At time \(T\) the realization of two possible states of the world is revealed. The stock has a payoff of \(uS_0\) and \(dS_0\), depending on the state, where \(u > e^{rT} > d \geq 0\) are constants. The bond has a payment of 1 at \(t = T\), irrespective of the state.
(a) Explain what is meant by (Arrow-Debreu) state prices and risk neutral probabilities and determine these for this economy.
(b) What is the arbitrage free price at \(t = 0\) for a call and a put on the stock, both options with maturity at \(t = T\) and strike \(K\)?
(c) Consider a portfolio at \(t = 0\) that is long the call and short the put. Also consider a forward contract on the stock priced so the current value is zero. What is the difference in value and payoff of the option portfolio and the forward?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Refer Hansen chapter 1 and Hull chapter 13. State prices \(\psi\) are implicit prices that satisfy that current prices of each security is the sum of the time and state dependent payoffs of the security and corresponding (security independent) state prices. Risk neutral probabilities \(q\) are probabilities that satisfy that current prices of each security is the discounted expected payoff using of these (security independent) probabilities and discounting with a risk-free rate. Existence of both follows if there are no arbitrage opportunities in the economy. Let for ease of notation \(a = e^{rT}\). In the concrete case the state prices \((\psi_u, \psi_d)\) satisfies the two equations: \[1 = a\psi_u + a\psi_d \quad \text{and} \quad 1 = u\psi_u + d\psi_d\] with the solution \(\psi = (\psi_u, \psi_d) = \left(a^{-1}\frac{a-d}{u-d},\, a^{-1}\frac{u-a}{u-d}\right)\) which are well defined and positive with our assumptions. The risk neutral probabilities are \(q = (q_u, q_d) = a\psi\), and note that \(q_u + q_d = 1\) and \(\psi_u + \psi_d = 1/a\).
(b) This can be equivalently formulated using the state-prices and the risk neutral probabilities. The payoff of the call \(c_T\) and put \(p_T\) at time \(T\) respectively will be \(c_T = \max[S_T - K;\, 0]\) and \(p_T = \max[K - S_T;\, 0] = K - S_T + \max[S_T - K;\, 0]\). We find the price at \(t = 0\) to be \[c_0 = \psi_u\max[uS_0 - K;\, 0] + \psi_d\max[dS_0 - K;\, 0]\] \[p_0 = K/a - S_0 + c_0\]
(c) The value of a portfolio of the call and short the put at time 0 will by the above be \[c_0 - p_0 = c_0 - K/a + S_0 - c_0 = S_0 - K/a\] This is obviously also the value difference to the forward contract that by the definition has value 0 at time 0. Define the forward price that gives the forward contract value 0 at time \(t\) as \(F_0^T\). If arbitrage free it must be so that \(F_0^T = aS_0\) (the arbitrage argument is that you can get one stock at time \(T\) by either buying spot at price \(S_0\) or by buying the forward contract and place \(F_0^T/a\) at interest). The payoff at time \(T\) from the option-portfolio was shown to be \(c_T - p_T = S_T - K\), whereas the payoff from the forward is \(S_T - F_0^T = S_T - aS_0\). The difference in payoffs at time \(T\) is thus \(-K + aS_0 = F_0^T - K\). Since this is risk-free it has a time-0 value of \(S_0 - K/a\), i.e. the same as the difference in time-0 prices found above (consistent with the arbitrage free pricing assumed). Also it is the present value of the difference between the current forward price and the strike of the two options.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Binomial Model   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Arrow-Debreu state prices and risk-neutral probabilities: Risk-neutral probabilities \(q,1-q\) are those for which discounted expected payoffs equal current prices. Letting \(a=e^{rT}\), the bond and stock no-arbitrage conditions are \[1 = a\psi_u + a\psi_d,\qquad 1 = u\psi_u + d\psi_d.\] Dividing the bond equation by \(a\) gives \[\psi_u+\psi_d = 1/a.\] Multiplying this by \(d\) gives \(d\psi_u+d\psi_d = d/a\); subtracting this from the stock equation \(u\psi_u+d\psi_d=1\) gives \[(u-d)\psi_u = 1 - d/a.\] Dividing both sides by \((u-d)\) gives \[\psi_u = \frac{1-d/a}{u-d} = \frac{a-d}{a(u-d)}.\] Substituting back into \(\psi_u+\psi_d=1/a\) and solving for \(\psi_d\) gives \[\psi_d = \frac1a - \frac{a-d}{a(u-d)} = \frac{(u-d)-(a-d)}{a(u-d)},\] \[\psi_u = \frac{a-d}{a(u-d)},\qquad \psi_d = \frac{u-a}{a(u-d)}.\] The risk-neutral probabilities follow from \(q=a\psi_u\): \[\boxed{q = \frac{a-d}{u-d},\qquad 1-q = \frac{u-a}{u-d}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\boxed{\psi_u = e^{-rT}q,\qquad \psi_d = e^{-rT}(1-q).}\]

(b) Arbitrage-free call and put prices: With call payoffs \(C_u=\max(uS_0-K,0)\), \(C_d=\max(dS_0-K,0)\) and put payoffs \(P_u=\max(K-uS_0,0)\), \(P_d=\max(K-dS_0,0)\), pricing as the sum of Arrow-Debreu prices times payoffs gives the unique no-arbitrage prices in this complete market: \[\boxed{c = e^{-rT}\bigl[q\cdot\max(uS_0-K,0) + (1-q)\cdot\max(dS_0-K,0)\bigr],}\] \[\boxed{p = e^{-rT}\bigl[q\cdot\max(K-uS_0,0) + (1-q)\cdot\max(K-dS_0,0)\bigr].}\]

(c) Long call plus short put vs. a forward contract: At \(T\), the combined payoff of a long call and short put is \[\max(S_T-K,0) - \max(K-S_T,0) = S_T - K,\] exactly the payoff of a forward on the stock with delivery price \(K\). Discounted risk-neutral expected payoff gives the value of this portfolio at \(t=0\): \[c - p = e^{-rT}E^Q[S_T - K].\] By linearity of expectation, \[c-p = e^{-rT}\bigl(E^Q[S_T] - K\bigr).\] Since the stock earns the risk-free rate under \(Q\), \(E^Q[S_T]=S_0e^{rT}\), substituting gives \[c-p = e^{-rT}\bigl(S_0 e^{rT} - K\bigr).\] Distributing \(e^{-rT}\) across the bracket gives \[\boxed{c - p = S_0 - Ke^{-rT}}\quad\text{(put-call parity).}\] Setting \(c-p=0\), the forward delivery price that gives the forward zero current value is \(K = S_0 e^{rT} = F(0,T)\). If \(K=F(0,T)\), then \(c=p\).

Question 2

In the BSM-model for options on a stock paying no dividends before the expiry of the option we have the formula \(c = S_0\Phi(d_1) - Ke^{-rT}\Phi(d_2)\) for the price \(c\) of a call at current time 0, where \(S_0\) is the current stock price, \(K\) is the strike of the call, \(\Phi\) is the cumulative standard normal probability function, \(r\) is a constant, continuously compounded risk free interest rate, \(d_1 = \frac{\ln(S_0/K) + (r + \frac{1}{2}\sigma^2)T}{\sigma\sqrt{T}}\), \(d_2 = d_1 - \sigma\sqrt{T}\), where the stock price follows the Geometric Brownian Motion \(dS = \mu S\,dt + \sigma S\,dz\), and \(z\) is a Brownian Motion.
(a) In a model by Merton the value of equity in a limited liability company with a zero coupon debt of a notional \(N\) maturing at \(T\) is seen as a call option on the value \(V\) of company assets. Assuming that the value of these assets can be described by a geometric Brownian motion derive a formula for pricing the zero coupon debt using the above model.
(b) Give an interpretation of \(\Phi(d_2)\) in this model.
(c) Define the gearing \(G = V_0/(Ne^{-rT})\). Show that the credit spread \(s\) on the zero coupon bond (implicitly defined by \(B_0 = e^{-(r+s)T}N\)) is \[-\frac{1}{T}\Bigl[\ln\Phi(d_2) + \Phi(-d_1)G\Bigr]\]

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Refer Hull section 24.6. Let the market value of equity be \(E_0\) at time \(t\). The market value of the debt will be \(B_0 = V_0 - E_0\) assuming no costs or frictions in the form of third party payments in the event of default. Using the Black-Scholes-Merton formula to price the equity as a call on the assets with strike \(N\) and maturity \(T\) you get \[B_0 = V_0 - V_0\Phi(d_1) + Ne^{-rT}\Phi(d_2) = V_0\Phi(-d_1) + Ne^{-rT}\Phi(d_2)\] where we have altered the definition of \(d_1, d_2\) to fit our interpretation, i.e. \[d_1 = \frac{\ln(V_0/N) + (r + \frac{1}{2}\sigma_V^2)T}{\sigma_V\sqrt{T}}, \quad d_2 = d_1 - \sigma_V\sqrt{T}\] where \(\sigma_V\) is the volatility of the asset value.
(b) In this model \(\Phi(d_2)\) is the risk neutral probability that the implicit call option of the shareholders is in the money at maturity — i.e. that the company is not in default (and equivalently \(1 - \Phi(d_2) = \Phi(-d_2)\) is the risk neutral probability that the implicit short put option of the creditors is in the money, i.e. that the company will be in default at the maturity of the debt).
(c) Refer Hull Practice Question 24.21. Let \(s\) be the credit spread implicitly defined by \(B_0 = e^{-(r+s)T}N\). By equating this with the formula above you get \[e^{-(r+s)T}N = V_0\Phi(-d_1) + Ne^{-rT}\Phi(d_2)\] Substitute \(V_0/G\) for \(Ne^{-rT}\) to get \(V_0 e^{-sT}/G = V_0\Phi(-d_1) + V_0/G \cdot \Phi(d_2)\). Divide by \(V_0/G > 0\) to get \(e^{-sT} = \Phi(-d_1)G + \Phi(d_2)\). Taking the natural logarithm and dividing by \(-T\) gives the desired result. It may be added that this is not the standard definition of gearing (which is with reference to equity rather than debt).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: BSM Model · Credit Risk | Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Merton model — equity as a call, deriving the debt value \(B_0\): By limited liability, shareholders receive \(\max(V_T-N,0)\) at maturity \(T\). Pricing this with BSM, using asset value \(V_0\), volatility \(\sigma_V\), and strike equal to the debt face value \(N\), \[E_0 = V_0\Phi(d_1) - Ne^{-rT}\Phi(d_2),\qquad d_1 = \frac{\ln(V_0/N) + (r+\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_V^2)T}{\sigma_V\sqrt{T}},\quad d_2 = d_1 - \sigma_V\sqrt{T}.\] With no bankruptcy costs, debt equals assets minus equity: \[B_0 = V_0 - E_0 = V_0 - V_0\Phi(d_1) + Ne^{-rT}\Phi(d_2),\] \[\boxed{B_0 = V_0\Phi(-d_1) + Ne^{-rT}\Phi(d_2).}\]

(b) Interpretation of \(\Phi(d_2)\): In BSM, \(\Phi(d_2)\) is the risk-neutral probability that \(V_T > N\) at maturity, i.e. that the firm's assets exceed its debt: \[\boxed{\Phi(d_2) = Q(\text{no default}), \quad \Phi(-d_2) = Q(\text{default}).}\]

(c) Credit spread — deriving \(s\): The credit spread is defined by \(B_0 = e^{-(r+s)T}N\). Equating this with the Merton formula for debt, \[e^{-(r+s)T}N = V_0\Phi(-d_1) + Ne^{-rT}\Phi(d_2).\] Substituting the gearing \(G = V_0/(Ne^{-rT})\), so \(V_0 = G\cdot Ne^{-rT}\), \[e^{-(r+s)T}N = G\cdot Ne^{-rT}\Phi(-d_1) + Ne^{-rT}\Phi(d_2).\] Dividing both sides by \(Ne^{-rT}>0\), \[e^{-sT} = G\Phi(-d_1) + \Phi(d_2) = \Phi(d_2) + \Phi(-d_1)G.\] Taking the natural log and solving for \(s\), \[-sT = \ln\!\bigl[\Phi(d_2) + \Phi(-d_1)G\bigr],\] \[\boxed{s = -\frac{1}{T}\ln\!\bigl[\Phi(d_2) + \Phi(-d_1)G\bigr].}\]

Question 3

In the Vasicek Model the (instantaneous) short term interest rate \(r\) is described by the process: \[dr = a(b-r)\,dt + \sigma\,dz\] where \(a\), \(b\) and \(\sigma\) are positive constants, and \(z\) a Brownian Motion.
(a) What does this mean for the behaviour of the short term interest rate?
(b) In this model the solution for the price of a zero-coupon bond can be written \[P(t,T) = A(t,T)e^{-B(t,T)r(t)}\] Derive the duration of the bond, where the interest sensitivity is with respect to the short rate.
