(c) In some models of the short term interest rate \(r\) (e.g. the Hull-White model) the drift rate is made a function of calendar time. What is the purpose of the extra flexibility compared to the Vasicek (or CIR) type of models?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Refer Hull chapter 31. It should be noted that the model implies a mean reversion of the spot rate to a level of \(b\) at a rate of \(a\). It should also be noted that the volatility term means that the short-term rate can go below 0.
(b) When the duration is thus defined we get \[D = -\frac{\partial P/\partial r}{P} = B(t,T)\] One may note that the standard definition of duration will give the maturity of a zero coupon bond as the duration.
(c) Refer Hull section 31.3. The time-dependent drift term in the Hull-White model is introduced to be able to incorporate a given, initial term structure, making the values derived from it “arbitrage-free” in relation to the existing securities priced on the current term structure (assuming these to be arbitrage free). This is in contrast to the CIR and Vasicek models of the “Equilibrium”-type that put restrictions on the possible initial term structure (as it is a function of the current spot rate only).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Short Rate Models   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Vasicek model — behaviour of the short rate: With \(dr = a(b-r)\,dt + \sigma\,dz\), the rate mean-reverts to level \(b\) at speed \(a\): when \(r>b\), the drift \(a(b-r)<0\) pulls the rate down, and when \(r<b\), the drift is positive and pulls it up. The stochastic term \(\sigma\,dz\) has constant volatility and normally distributed increments, so the rate can go negative.

(b) Duration of the Vasicek zero-coupon bond: With \(P(t,T)=A(t,T)e^{-B(t,T)r(t)}\), differentiating with respect to \(r\) gives \[\frac{\partial P}{\partial r} = A(t,T)\cdot(-B(t,T))e^{-B(t,T)r} = -B(t,T)P.\] The duration is therefore \[\boxed{D = -\frac{1}{P}\frac{\partial P}{\partial r} = B(t,T) = \frac{1-e^{-a(T-t)}}{a}.}\] For a standard zero-coupon bond the duration equals \(T-t\), but here \(D=B(t,T)\leq T-t\) because of mean reversion.

(c) Hull-White model — purpose of the time-dependent drift: Vasicek is an equilibrium model, so the initial term structure is determined by a few constant parameters and may not match market prices. Hull-White replaces the constant \(ab\) with a time-dependent \(\theta(t)\), calibrated to exactly fit the current observed term structure, making the model arbitrage-free with respect to current bond prices.

August 2024 — Re-Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

Question 1

Assume that the log of price \(Z\) of a non-dividend paying stock can be modelled (under the original probability measure \(P\)) by the geometric Brownian motion \[dZ = \mu\,dt + \sigma\,dz\] where \(\mu\) and \(\sigma > 0\) are constants, and where \(dt\) and \(dz\) are the standard shorthand notations for a small time-step and a Brownian increment.
(a) Consider the transformation \(G\) of \(Z\) given by the exponential function, i.e. \(G(x) = e^x\). Use Itô's lemma to find the process followed by \(S := G(Z)\).
(b) For a given initial value \(Z_t = Z_0\) what is the distribution of \(Z_T\)?
(c) What are main empirical shortcomings of the model of \(dZ\) as a model of stock returns?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Refer Hull, chapter 14 and 15. To use Itô's lemma on the transformation \(G\), note that \(\frac{\partial G}{\partial x} = e^x = \frac{\partial^2 G}{\partial x^2}\) (and that \(G\) does not depend on time \(t\)). Thus we have \[dS = dG(Z) = \Bigl(\frac{\partial G}{\partial Z}\mu + \tfrac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^2 G}{\partial Z^2}\sigma^2\Bigr)dt + \frac{\partial G}{\partial Z}\sigma\,dz = \bigl(\mu + \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2\bigr)S\,dt + \sigma S\,dz\]
(b) By integration the change \(Z_T - Z_0\) will have a Normal distribution with mean \(\mu T\) and variance \(\sigma^2 T\). One may note that the prices will be log-normally distributed, but this comment is not required.
(c) One may first note that the model has continuous sample paths, which is at best an approximation to real world discrete price changes. Already in the assumptions we have also excluded dividends. The normal distribution of returns is at odds with empirically observed fat tailed return distributions. The model has a constant return variance (and price volatility) which is not consistent with observations — and a stochastic volatility may be one reason for fat-tailed return distributions. Also, even if empirical data here is weak, one may question whether mean returns are constant over long time periods.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: GBM   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Itô's lemma on \(G(Z)=e^Z\) to find the process for \(S=e^Z\): The partial derivatives of \(G(x)=e^x\) are \[\frac{\partial G}{\partial x} = e^x = \frac{\partial^2 G}{\partial x^2},\qquad \frac{\partial G}{\partial t} = 0.\] Applying Itô's lemma with \(dZ=\mu\,dt+\sigma\,dz\) and \((dZ)^2=\sigma^2\,dt\), \[dS = dG = \frac{\partial G}{\partial Z}dZ + \tfrac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^2 G}{\partial Z^2}(dZ)^2 = e^Z(\mu\,dt+\sigma\,dz) + \tfrac{1}{2}e^Z\sigma^2\,dt.\] Replacing \(e^Z=S\), \[\boxed{dS = \bigl(\mu + \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2\bigr)S\,dt + \sigma S\,dz.}\] So \(S\) follows GBM with drift \(\mu+\tfrac12\sigma^2\) and volatility \(\sigma\).

(b) Distribution of \(Z_T\) and \(S_T\): Integrating \(dZ=\mu\,dt+\sigma\,dz\) from \(t\) to \(T\), and since \(\mu,\sigma\) are constants, \[Z_T - Z_t = \mu(T-t) + \sigma(z_T-z_t).\] With \(Z_t=Z_0\), this gives \(Z_T = Z_0 + \mu(T-t) + \sigma(z_T-z_t)\). Since \(z_T-z_t\sim N(0,T-t)\), scaling by \(\sigma\) and shifting by the constant \(Z_0+\mu(T-t)\) gives \[\boxed{Z_T \sim N\bigl(Z_0+\mu(T-t),\;\sigma^2(T-t)\bigr).}\] In the special case \(t=0\), \[\boxed{Z_T \sim N(Z_0+\mu T,\;\sigma^2 T).}\] Therefore \(S_T=e^{Z_T}\) is lognormally distributed.

(c) Shortcomings of GBM as a stock model: Continuous sample paths are an approximation, since real prices can jump. Volatility is assumed constant, but empirically it varies over time (volatility clustering, leverage effect). Returns are assumed normal, but empirical returns show heavier tails and negative skewness. The model also assumes the Markov property, that only the current price matters for future dynamics.

Question 2

Assume that there is a constant continuously compounded interest rate of \(r\). Let \(S_t\) be the price of a stock that pays a constant, continuously compounded dividend rate of \(\delta\). Consider a derivative with price \(V(S,t)\) as some function of the current stock price \(S_t\) and time \(t\) (and further implicit parameters).
(a) Define and interpret the Delta, Gamma and Theta of the derivative.
(b) Let \(c(S,K,T,r,\delta)\) and \(p(S,K,T,r,\delta)\) be the price at time \(t = 0\) of a European call and a European put, respectively, on the stock with the same strike \(K\) and expiry \(T\). Derive the call-put-parity.
(c) Use the call-put-parity to find a relationship between the deltas of the call and put. Repeat this for Gamma.
(d) Suppose a portfolio of the stock and/or derivatives on the stock is Delta-neutral, and that there are no arbitrage possibilities. Let the value of the portfolio be \(\Pi(S,t)\). What can we say about the relation between the Theta and Gamma of the portfolio? (You may wish to refer to the BSM fundamental PDE \(\frac{\partial V}{\partial t} + \frac{1}{2}\sigma^2 S^2\frac{\partial^2 V}{\partial S^2} + (r-\delta)S\frac{\partial V}{\partial S} - rV = 0\).)

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Refer Hull, chapter 19. Delta is the first partial derivative of the price with respect to the price of the underlying instrument, Gamma the second partial derivative also with respect to the underlying instrument, whereas Theta is the partial derivative of the price with respect to calendar time.
(b) A portfolio of the long call and a short put can be seen as a forward contract on the stock with \(K\) as the forward price. This contract has current value of \(S_0 e^{-\delta T} - Ke^{-rT}\) (you may also use an arbitrage argument to find this: The same payoff as the forward with fixed forward price \(K\) can be achieved by having \(S_0 e^{-\delta T}\) of the stock at time 0 and then accumulate the dividend until \(T\) and borrowing \(Ke^{-rT}\) repaying this at \(T\)). Thus barring arbitrage \[c + Ke^{-rT} = p + S_0 e^{-\delta T}\]
(c) Refer also Hull, Table 19.6. This follows directly, e.g. for the case of the Delta \(\Delta_c = \Delta_p + e^{-\delta T}\). Then you also see that the Gammas are identical.
(d) It follows from the fundamental Black-Scholes-Merton PDE that \(\frac{\partial V}{\partial t} + \frac{1}{2}\sigma^2 S^2\frac{\partial^2 V}{\partial S^2} + (r-\delta)S\frac{\partial V}{\partial S} - rV = 0\). Substituting the definitions in and using that the delta is zero, you get \[\Theta + \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2 S^2\Gamma = r\Pi\] Thus beyond the risk free rate the Theta and the Gamma works in opposite directions. For a portfolio that is long Gamma (e.g. a long option) you will in this sense be short Theta — in this case you earn from your Gamma when there is price movement, but the loss from the Theta dominates when there is not.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: BSM Model  ·  Put-Call Parity   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Delta, Gamma, Theta: Delta \(\Delta=\partial V/\partial S\) measures price sensitivity to the stock. Gamma \(\Gamma=\partial^2 V/\partial S^2\) measures the rate of change of delta, i.e. convexity. Theta \(\Theta=\partial V/\partial t\) measures time decay.

(b) Put-call parity with continuous dividend \(\delta\): A long call and short put replicate a forward, since at \(T\), \(\max(S_T-K,0)-\max(K-S_T,0)=S_T-K\). To replicate \(S_T\) at time 0, buy \(e^{-\delta T}\) shares and reinvest the dividends, at a cost of \(S_0e^{-\delta T}\) today. Equating the cost of the option portfolio to this replicating cost, discounted for the strike, gives \[\boxed{c - p = S_0 e^{-\delta T} - Ke^{-rT}.}\]

(c) Delta and Gamma relationships from put-call parity: Differentiating with respect to \(S_0\), \[\frac{\partial c}{\partial S} - \frac{\partial p}{\partial S} = e^{-\delta T},\qquad \boxed{\Delta_c - \Delta_p = e^{-\delta T}.}\] The call delta exceeds the put delta by the discount factor \(e^{-\delta T}\). Differentiating once more, \[\frac{\partial^2 c}{\partial S^2} - \frac{\partial^2 p}{\partial S^2} = 0,\qquad \boxed{\Gamma_c = \Gamma_p.}\] A call and put with the same strike and maturity have identical Gamma.

(d) Theta–Gamma relationship for a delta-neutral portfolio: The BSM PDE with dividends reads \(\Theta + (r-\delta)S\Delta + \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2S^2\Gamma = rV\). Setting \(\Delta=0\) for a delta-neutral portfolio \(\Pi\), \[\boxed{\Theta + \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2S^2\Gamma = r\Pi.}\] For such a portfolio, long gamma (\(\Gamma>0\)) implies negative theta: you pay time decay to hold convexity. High gamma brings faster re-hedging gains but also a higher time-decay cost.

Question 3

Let \(R(t,T)\) denote the continuously compounded yield at time \(t\) of a zero coupon bond that matures at time \(T > t\). Assume that \(R\) follows an Itô-process of the form \[dR = \mu\,dt + \sigma\,dz\] where \(z\) is a Brownian motion, and \(\mu\) and \(\sigma > 0\) are bounded functions of \(R\) and \(t\).
(a) For a bond with given maturity \(T\) derive the variance rate of the bond price \(P(t,T)\).
(b) Now assume \(\mu = m(k-R)\) and \(\sigma = sR\), where \(m\), \(k\) and \(s\) are positive constants. What is the process followed by the bond price?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Refer Hull Practice Question 14.11. Let \(P(t,T) = e^{-(T-t)R}\). Using Itô's lemma we have that the volatility of the price is \[\frac{\partial P}{\partial R}\sigma = -(T-t)P(t,T)\sigma\] Since \(\sigma\) stays bounded, and \(P(t,T)\) converges to 1 when \(t\) converges to \(T\), we have the required result.
(b) We find the partials \[\frac{\partial P}{\partial t} = RP, \quad \frac{\partial P}{\partial R} = -(T-t)P, \quad \frac{\partial^2 P}{\partial R^2} = (T-t)^2 P\] and get by Itô's lemma the process \[dP = \Bigl(R - (T-t)m(k-R) + \tfrac{1}{2}s^2R^2(T-t)^2\Bigr)P\,dt + (T-t)sRP\,dz\] By the symmetry of the Brownian increment the sign on the last part is immaterial.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Short Rate Models   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Variance rate of the bond price \(P(t,T)=e^{-(T-t)R}\): The partial derivative with respect to \(R\) is \[\frac{\partial P}{\partial R} = -(T-t)e^{-(T-t)R} = -(T-t)P.\] By Itô's lemma, \(dP\) has diffusion term \(\dfrac{\partial P}{\partial R}\sigma\,dz = -(T-t)P\sigma\,dz\), so the variance rate is \[\boxed{\text{Var rate of }P = \bigl((T-t)\sigma P\bigr)^2 = (T-t)^2\sigma^2 P^2.}\] As \(t\to T\), the variance rate goes to zero, since the bond converges to 1 at maturity.

(b) Specific model \(\mu=m(k-R)\), \(\sigma=sR\): The partials of \(P(t,T)=e^{-(T-t)R}\) are \(P_t=RP\), \(P_R=-(T-t)P\), \(P_{RR}=(T-t)^2P\). Applying Itô's lemma, \[dP = P_t\,dt + P_R\,dR + \tfrac{1}{2}P_{RR}(dR)^2,\] Substituting \(dR = m(k-R)\,dt + sR\,dz\) into the middle term gives \[dP = RP\,dt - (T-t)P\bigl(m(k-R)\,dt + sR\,dz\bigr) + \tfrac{1}{2}(T-t)^2P\,(dR)^2.\] Substituting \((dR)^2=s^2R^2\,dt\) into the last term gives \[dP = RP\,dt - (T-t)P\bigl(m(k-R)\,dt + sR\,dz\bigr) + \tfrac{1}{2}(T-t)^2P\,s^2R^2\,dt.\] Collecting the \(dt\)-terms, \[\boxed{dP = \bigl(R - (T-t)m(k-R) + \tfrac{1}{2}s^2 R^2(T-t)^2\bigr)P\,dt + (T-t)sRP\,(-dz).}\] Under \(Q\), the drift of a traded bond must equal \(rP=RP\), which gives the constraint linking the \(R\) dynamics to arbitrage-free bond pricing.

June 2023 — Ordinary Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

Question 1

Consider an economy over two time periods, \(T = \{0,1,2\}\), described by a recombining binomial tree. Two securities are traded: A non-dividend-paying stock with a price \(S_t\) that in each period can move up by a factor \(u\) (an up-move) or down with a factor \(d\) (a down-move), and risk free asset with a price \(B_t\) that will accumulate by a factor of \(e^r\) in each period. Assume \(u > e^r > d > 0\), \(S_0 > 0\), and \(B_0 = 1\).
(a) Consider a European call option written on the stock with expiry at \(t = 2\) and strike \(K\). Use an arbitrage argument to find the value of the call at \(t = 0\) expressed using a risk-neutral probability \(q\) of an up-move. Explain why the real-world probability \(p\) of an up-move does not enter directly in the expression.
(b) Find the state prices (or Arrow-prices) at \(t = 0\) for payments at \(t = 1\).
(c) Now consider an American call in this model. Provide a non-arbitrage argument with a condition on \(r\) for why the American call is not more valuable than the similar European.

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Cf. Hull Chapter 13 and Hansen. First, consider a one-period branch of the tree. Consider a derivative with current value \(f\) and values \((f_u, f_d)\) one step forward in the tree depending on whether the stock price moves up to \(S_u = uS\) or down to \(S_d = dS\), when the current stock price is \(S\). Construct a portfolio of \(\Delta\) stocks. If you choose \[\Delta = \frac{f_u - f_d}{S_u - S_d}\] it will cost \(\Delta S\) at the current time. The position of a short derivative position and long the delta stock position will be risk free, i.e. have the same payoff one period later, namely \(f_u - \frac{f_u - f_d}{u-d}u = f_d - \frac{f_u - f_d}{u-d}d\). The current cost of this risk free position of the derivative and delta stock is \(f - \frac{f_u - f_d}{u-d}\), which can be borrowed and repaid by a factor of \(a := e^r\) one period later. Thus to avoid arbitrage opportunities we must have \[\Bigl[f - \frac{f_u - f_d}{u-d}u\Bigr]e^{-r} = f - \frac{f_u - f_d}{u-d}\] Solve this for \(f\) to get: \(f = a^{-1}\Bigl(\frac{a-d}{u-d}f_u + \frac{u-a}{u-d}f_d\Bigr)\). Define \(q = \frac{a-d}{u-d}\) and then \(f = e^{-r}[qf_u + (1-q)f_d]\). Using backward induction you can find an explicit formula for the call premium at time 0 as \[f_0 = a^{-2}\bigl(q^2 f_{uu} + 2q(1-q)f_{ud} + (1-q)^2 f_{dd}\bigr)\] where \(f_{uu} = \max[0;\, u^2S_0 - K]\), \(f_{ud} = \max[0;\, udS_0 - K]\), and \(f_{dd} = \max[0;\, d^2S_0 - K]\). The real world probability does not enter directly into the formula because that information is already embedded in the prices of the traded instruments that do enter into the formula.
(b) The state prices are the discounted risk neutral probabilities i.e. \(\psi_u = e^{-r}q\) and \(\psi_d = e^{-r}(1-q)\) (thus \(f = \psi_u f_u + \psi_d f_d\)).
(c) We will analyze whether an early exercise is optimal at time \(t = 1\). Let us take outset in the case that this is after an initial up-move (as the argument below will show this is not essential). The analysis is only relevant if the option is in the money, e.g. \(uS_0 > K\). We have just found that the value of the call in this situation is \(f_u = e^{-r}[qf_{uu} + (1-q)f_{ud}]\) and this value can, if wanted, be hedged by a short stock position to a risk free position with this resulting value (or it can be sold). Thus an exercise is only optimal if \(uS_0 - K > e^{-r}[qf_{uu} + (1-q)f_{ud}]\). Now \(S_u = uS_0 = e^{-r}(qS_{uu} + (1-q)S_{ud})\) so this requirement is equivalent to \(qS_{uu} + (1-q)S_{ud} - e^r K > qf_{uu} + (1-q)f_{ud}\). If the option is in the money in both the uu and the ud state then the requirement is \(qS_{uu} + (1-q)S_{ud} - e^r K > q(S_{uu}-K) + (1-q)(S_{ud}-K)\) or \(e^r K < K\), i.e. not with positive interest rates. If the option is in the money only in the uu-state i.e. \(S_{ud} < K\) then the requirement becomes \((1-q)S_{ud} > (e^r - q)K > (e^r - q)S_{ud}\) implying \(1 > e^r\), which also does not hold with a positive rate. Thus with a positive interest rate an early exercise of an American option on a stock with no dividends is not optimal.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Binomial Model   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) European call in a two-period recombining binomial tree: The risk-neutral up-probability is \(q=\dfrac{e^r-d}{u-d}\). The terminal stock prices at \(T=2\) are \[S_{uu}=u^2S_0,\quad S_{ud}=udS_0,\quad S_{dd}=d^2S_0,\] giving call payoffs \(C_{uu}=\max(u^2S_0-K,0)\), \(C_{ud}=\max(udS_0-K,0)\), \(C_{dd}=\max(d^2S_0-K,0)\). One step back, at each middle node the one-period pricing formula \(f=e^{-r}[qf_u+(1-q)f_d]\) gives \[C_u = e^{-r}\bigl[qC_{uu}+(1-q)C_{ud}\bigr],\qquad C_d = e^{-r}\bigl[qC_{ud}+(1-q)C_{dd}\bigr].\] Applying the same formula once more from \(t=1\) to \(t=0\), \[c = e^{-r}\bigl[qC_u+(1-q)C_d\bigr].\] Substituting the expressions for \(C_u\) and \(C_d\) gives \[c = e^{-r}\Bigl[q\cdot e^{-r}\bigl(qC_{uu}+(1-q)C_{ud}\bigr) + (1-q)\cdot e^{-r}\bigl(qC_{ud}+(1-q)C_{dd}\bigr)\Bigr].\] Collecting the two factors of \(e^{-r}\) into \(e^{-2r}\) and expanding gives \[c = e^{-2r}\bigl[q^2C_{uu}+q(1-q)C_{ud}+(1-q)qC_{ud}+(1-q)^2C_{dd}\bigr].\] Collecting the two \(C_{ud}\) terms gives \[c = e^{-2r}\bigl[q^2C_{uu}+2q(1-q)C_{ud}+(1-q)^2C_{dd}\bigr],\] \[\boxed{c = e^{-2r}\bigl[q^2\max(u^2S_0-K,0)+2q(1-q)\max(udS_0-K,0)+(1-q)^2\max(d^2S_0-K,0)\bigr].}\] The real-world probability \(p\) does not appear, since risk-neutral pricing uses \(q\) derived from no-arbitrage rather than subjective beliefs about up and down moves.

(b) State prices at \(t=0\) for payments at \(t=1\): The Arrow-Debreu price \(\psi_u\) is the price today of £1 paid in the up-state at \(t=1\) (and £0 otherwise), and similarly for \(\psi_d\). The bond and stock no-arbitrage conditions are \[e^{-r} = \psi_u + \psi_d,\qquad S_0 = u S_0\psi_u + d S_0\psi_d.\] Dividing the stock equation by \(S_0\) gives \[u\psi_u+d\psi_d=1.\] Multiplying the bond equation by \(d\) gives \(d\psi_u+d\psi_d=de^{-r}\); subtracting this from the stock equation gives \[(u-d)\psi_u = 1-de^{-r}.\] Dividing both sides by \((u-d)\) gives \[\psi_u = \frac{1-de^{-r}}{u-d}.\] Substituting back into \(\psi_u+\psi_d=e^{-r}\) and solving for \(\psi_d\) gives \[\psi_d = e^{-r} - \frac{1-de^{-r}}{u-d} = \frac{e^{-r}(u-d)-1+de^{-r}}{u-d} = \frac{ue^{-r}-1}{u-d},\] \[\boxed{\psi_u = \frac{1-de^{-r}}{u-d} = e^{-r}q,\qquad \psi_d = e^{-r}(1-q) = \frac{ue^{-r}-1}{u-d}.}\]

(c) American call vs. European call — no-arbitrage argument: At any node, the continuation value \(e^{-r}[qC_u+(1-q)C_d]\) exceeds the intrinsic value \(S-K\) whenever \(r\geq 0\), since the unexercised call is worth at least \(S_t-Ke^{-r(T-t)}\geq S_t-K\). Exercising early sacrifices the interest earned on \(K\), so it is never optimal: \[\boxed{\text{American call} = \text{European call for a non-dividend-paying stock when }r\geq 0.}\]

Question 2

Consider European call and put options on the same futures contract. Both options have strike price \(K\) and expiry at time \(T\). The futures contract expires after that time. Assume that there is a constant, continuously compounded, risk free interest rate of \(r\). Let the futures price at time \(t\) be \(F_t\) (You may ignore margining and treat the futures as forward contracts).
(a) Derive the put-call parity for the futures options for time \(t < T\).
(b) Use the put-call parity to find the relationship between
1. The delta of the European futures call and the delta of the European futures put (use the delta with respect to the futures price rather than the spot price)
2. The gamma of the European futures call and the gamma of the European futures put
3. The theta of the European futures call and the theta of the European futures put and comment on the results.

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) See Hull section 17.4 and 18.4. Let \(t \leq T\) and let \(c_t\) and \(p_t\) be the prices of the call and the put, respectively. Consider the portfolio (A) of the call and \(e^{-r(T-t)}K\) placed at interest and the portfolio (B) of a put, a long position in the future with expiry at \(T\) and \(e^{-r(T-t)}F_t\) placed at interest. At maturity the portfolios will have the payouts: \[A: \max[F_T - K;\, 0] + K\] \[B: \max[K - F_T;\, 0] + (F_T - F_t) + F_t\] which are identical. Barring arbitrage we thus must have \[c_t + e^{-r(T-t)}K = p_t + e^{-r(T-t)}F_t\]
(b) See Hull chapter 19. The different Greeks shall be defined as partial derivatives, and the put-call-parity gives the relationships. The Delta of an option is the partial derivative with respect to (in this case) the futures price. The relationship between the Deltas for otherwise identical calls and puts is \[\Delta_c - \Delta_p = e^{-r(T-t)}\] i.e. dependent on the interest rate (and thus slightly different from the case with the spot price delta, where it is dependent on the dividend rate). The Gamma of an option is the second partial derivative with respect to the underlying. As can be seen from the result on Deltas, the Gammas on the two options will be identical. The Theta of an option is the partial derivative with respect to calendar time (not the expiry date). The relationship between the Thetas for otherwise identical calls and puts is \[\Theta_c - \Theta_p = re^{-r(T-t)}(F_t - K)\] i.e. the Thetas are identical for an at-the-money call and similar put and generally close for low interest rates.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Put-Call Parity  ·  Forward Pricing   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Put-call parity for futures options: Consider Portfolio A, a long call plus \(e^{-r(T-t)}K\) in cash, and Portfolio B, a long put plus a long futures position plus \(e^{-r(T-t)}F_t\) in cash. Their payoffs at expiry \(T\) are \[A: \max(F_T-K,0) + K,\qquad B: \max(K-F_T,0) + (F_T - F_t) + F_t = \max(K-F_T,0) + F_T,\] and both simplify to \(\max(F_T,K)\), so the payoffs are identical. By no-arbitrage, \[\boxed{c_t + Ke^{-r(T-t)} = p_t + F_t e^{-r(T-t)},}\] equivalently \(c_t - p_t = (F_t - K)e^{-r(T-t)}\).

(b) Greeks from put-call parity for futures: Differentiating \(c-p=(F_t-K)e^{-r(T-t)}\) with respect to \(F_t\), \[\boxed{\Delta_c - \Delta_p = e^{-r(T-t)}.}\] Differentiating again, \[\boxed{\Gamma_c = \Gamma_p.}\] Differentiating with respect to \(t\), \[\boxed{\Theta_c - \Theta_p = r(F_t-K)e^{-r(T-t)}.}\]

Question 3

(a) In the Ho-Lee Model in continuous time the (instantaneous) short term interest rate \(r\) is described by the process: \[dr = \theta(t)\,dt + \sigma\,dz\] where \(\theta(t)\) is a function of \(t\), \(\sigma\) is constant, and \(dz\) the increment of a Brownian motion. What does this mean for the behaviour of the short term interest rate?
(b) Here as in other models of the short term interest rate \(r\) the drift rate is made a function of calendar time. What is the purpose of the extra flexibility compared to the CIR (or Vasicek) type of models?
(c) In this model the solution for the price of a zero-coupon bond can be written \[P(t,T) = A(t,T)e^{-r(t)(T-t)}\] Use Itô's lemma to derive the volatility of \(P(t,T)\) and comment on the result.
(d) Derive the duration of the bond (using the sensitivity to the short rate rather than the yield of the bond).

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Cfr. Hull section 32.1. It should be noted that the short term rates at any time are normally distributed with a constant volatility, but time-varying mean. In particular it does not rule out negative interest rates.
(b) The time-dependent drift term in these models is introduced to be able to incorporate a given, initial term structure, making the values derived from it “arbitrage-free” in relation to the existing securities priced on the current term structure (assuming these to be arbitrage free). This is in contrast to the CIR and Vasicek models of the “Equilibrium”-type that put restrictions on the possible initial term structure (as it is a function with few parameters and of the current spot rate only).
(c) Using the form of the solution for the zero coupon bond price we find that \[\frac{\partial P}{\partial r} = -(T-t)P\] Thus (from Itô's lemma) the volatility term process for \(dP\) is \((T-t)\sigma P\) (as the sign does not matter). The volatility decreases linearly to zero with the remaining time to maturity on the Zero Coupon Bond.
(d) Cf. Hull Section 31.2. With one factor rate models we can define a duration measure with respect to the short rate rather (as most often) the yield of the bond. You then get \[D = -\frac{\partial P/\partial r}{P} = (T-t)\] (In this case this is equal to the traditional duration measure of a Zero Coupon Bond).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Short Rate Models   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Ho-Lee model — behaviour of the short rate: With \(dr = \theta(t)\,dt + \sigma\,dz\), the drift is a time-varying function \(\theta(t)\) and the volatility \(\sigma\) is constant. There is no mean reversion, and the normal increments mean the rate can go negative. The function \(\theta(t)\) is calibrated to fit the current observed term structure exactly: \[\boxed{\theta(t) = f^M_t(0,t) + \sigma^2 t.}\]

(b) Purpose of the time-dependent drift: Vasicek and CIR are equilibrium models with constant parameters, so they cannot in general match the current market term structure. Ho-Lee and Hull-White instead choose \(\theta(t)\) to exactly reproduce current bond prices, making the model arbitrage-free with respect to the observed yield curve: \[\boxed{\text{No-arbitrage model: fits initial term structure exactly. Equilibrium model: may not.}}\]

(c) Volatility of \(P(t,T)\) via Itô's lemma: For Ho-Lee, \(P(t,T)=A(t,T)e^{-r(t)(T-t)}\), and since there is no mean reversion, the \(r\)-sensitivity factor is \(B(t,T)=T-t\). Applying Itô's lemma to \(P(r,t)\) with \(dr=\theta(t)dt+\sigma dz\), \[dP = \frac{\partial P}{\partial t}dt + \frac{\partial P}{\partial r}dr + \tfrac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^2 P}{\partial r^2}(dr)^2,\] the diffusion term alone is \(\dfrac{\partial P}{\partial r}\cdot\sigma\,dz = -(T-t)P\cdot\sigma\,dz\), so \[\boxed{\sigma_P = \sigma(T-t)P.}\] The percentage volatility of the bond price, \(\sigma(T-t)\), grows with time-to-maturity — longer bonds are more volatile, as expected.

(d) Duration of the Ho-Lee bond: From \(\partial P/\partial r = -(T-t)P\), \[D = -\frac{1}{P}\frac{\partial P}{\partial r} = T-t,\] \[\boxed{D = T-t,}\] the same as the standard Macaulay duration for a zero-coupon bond. Unlike Vasicek, there is no mean reversion to dampen interest rate sensitivity, so duration equals time-to-maturity exactly.

August 2023 — Re-Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

Question 1

Assume that the price of a non-dividend paying stock \(S\) can be modelled (under the original probability measure \(P\)) by the geometric Brownian motion \[dS = \mu S\,dt + \sigma S\,dz\] where \(\mu\) and \(\sigma > 0\) are constants, and where \(dt\) and \(dz\) are the standard shorthand notations for a small time-step and a Brownian increment.
(a) Describe the qualitative characteristics of this model, and discuss its possible shortcomings as a model of a stock.
(b) Consider the transformation \(G\) of \(S\) given by the natural logarithm (ln), i.e. \(G(x) = \ln(x)\). Use Itô's lemma to find the process followed by \(G(S)\).
(c) At time \(t\) consider a forward contract on the stock maturing at time \(T > t\). Compare the pricing of a call option on the stock with a pricing of a call on the forward contract on the stock both maturing at \(T\) and having the same strike \(K\).

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) The model describes the stochastic movement of the stock price with a drift proportional to the stock price with a constant drift parameter \(\mu\) and with the stochastic behaviour driven by the Brownian increment that is giving a variance rate that is also proportional to the stock price with a constant volatility parameter \(\sigma\). The nature of the Brownian increment implies, inter alia, that the sample paths of prices are (with probability one) continuous. They are also very “wiggly”. The resulting price distribution will be lognormal and the distribution of return normal; the latter with expected return and variance proportional to the elapsed time. The student may also note that the model does not adhere to empirical sample paths — especially for a single stock — may show large jumps (e.g. at the publication of new information), and that realized returns empirically tend to show heteroscedasticity at odds with the given normal distribution. The student may also note that empirical return data tend to show negative skewness and excess kurtosis, but this is not directly covered by the syllabus (but at the lectures and in other parts of Hull).
(b) The use of Itô's lemma is straightforward. The partial derivative of the \(G\) w.r.t. time is 0, and w.r.t. \(x\) you find \(\frac{\partial G}{\partial x} = 1/x\) and \(\frac{\partial^2 G}{\partial x^2} = -1/x^2\). Thus with the given process for the underlying \(S\) you get from Itô's lemma: \[dG(S) = \bigl(\mu - \sigma^2/2\bigr)dt + \sigma\,dz\] thus taking the geometric Brownian motion for the stock price to a Brownian motion (with a constant drift rate and a constant variance rate) for the stock return (on this transformation, see Hull section 14.7).
(c) First the answer should note the definition of a forward contract, in particular that it has no payments before maturity. Then the answer should cover the forward price that will be agreed barring arbitrage: With an assumption of a constant risk free interest rate this is for the forward price \(F_t\) contracted at time \(t \leq T\), where \(T\) is the maturity of the contract, \[F_t = E^Q[S_T] = S_t e^{r(T-t)}\] where we only for the last equation use the assumption of no dividends. Last the answer should note that \(F\) follows a Geometric Brownian Motion similar to that derived earlier for \(S\), but that the drift rate of returns is \(\mu - r\) rather than the \(\mu\) for the stock. Under the Q-measure the drift rates will be 0 and \(r\), respectively. Thus comparing two call options with the same strike, but where the first is on the forward contract and the second is on the stock, \[c^F = e^{-r(T-t)}E^Q\bigl[\max(F_T - K;\, 0)\bigr] = e^{-r(T-t)}E^Q\bigl[\max(S_T - K;\, 0)\bigr] = c^S\] since they will have the same payout at maturity, even if the forward price initially (with a positive interest rate) is higher than the spot price. This may be verified by looking at the formulae for call prices on stocks and forwards (if you e.g. substitute the relation between forward and spot prices into the Black formula you get the Black-Scholes formula for a call), (see Hull 17.3).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: GBM  ·  Forward Pricing   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) GBM — qualitative characteristics and shortcomings: Under GBM, sample paths are continuous, and both the drift (\(\mu\)) and volatility (\(\sigma\)) are proportional to the price and constant. Prices are lognormal, returns are normal, and returns are independent and identically distributed. The mean and variance of returns are proportional to elapsed time. The main shortcomings are that real prices can jump, so continuous paths are only an approximation; volatility empirically clusters and varies, producing implied volatility smiles; empirical returns have fat tails and negative skewness rather than being normal; and dividends are not incorporated by default.

(b) Itô's lemma on \(G(S_t)=\ln S_t\): The partial derivatives are \[\frac{\partial G}{\partial S} = \frac{1}{S},\quad \frac{\partial^2 G}{\partial S^2} = -\frac{1}{S^2},\quad \frac{\partial G}{\partial t} = 0.\] Applying Itô's lemma with \((dS)^2=\sigma^2S^2\,dt\), \[d\ln S = \frac{1}{S}(\mu S\,dt + \sigma S\,dz) + \tfrac{1}{2}\left(-\frac{1}{S^2}\right)\sigma^2 S^2\,dt,\] \[\boxed{d\ln S = \bigl(\mu - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2\bigr)dt + \sigma\,dz.}\] Integrating gives the distribution of \(\ln S_T\): \[\boxed{\ln S_T \sim N\!\left(\ln S_0 + (\mu-\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2)T,\; \sigma^2 T\right).}\]

(c) Forward price, and comparing a call on the stock with a call on the forward: The forward price \(F_t\) is the price agreed at \(t\) for delivery at \(T\) such that the contract has zero value at inception. Under \(Q\), the expected stock price is \(E^Q[S_T]=S_te^{r(T-t)}\), and by no-arbitrage this equals the forward price: \[\boxed{F_t = S_t\,e^{r(T-t)}.}\] The value of an existing forward with delivery price \(K\) is \(V = e^{-r(T-t)}(F_t - K)\). Now compare a call with strike \(K\) and maturity \(T\) on the stock to a call with the same strike and maturity on the forward contract. Since the forward converges to the spot price at maturity, \(F_T=S_T\), so under \(Q\) both options share the same terminal payoff: \[c^F = e^{-r(T-t)}E^Q\bigl[\max(F_T-K,0)\bigr] = e^{-r(T-t)}E^Q\bigl[\max(S_T-K,0)\bigr] = c^S.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{c^F = c^S,}\] even though the forward price \(F_t\) itself generally differs from (and, with \(r>0\), exceeds) the spot price \(S_t\).

Question 2

Consider a Credit Default Swap (CDS).
(a) Give a precise description of the CDS contract its payment structure.
(b) What is the difference between a cash CDO (Collateralized Debt Obligation) and a synthetic CDO?
(c) Explain the payment structure of a tranched CDS or CDO, and define the terms attachment point and detachment point.
(d) Consider a tranched CDS or CDO on a large portfolio on underlying reference bonds (names). For a given average level of credit risk, e.g. expressed by the average credit spread on the underlying portfolio, explain how different levels of the assessed correlation of defaults of the issuers will influence the relative pricing of the tranches.

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Ref. Hull Section 25.1. A formal definition including key concepts is: Definition (Credit Default Swap). A Credit Default Swap (CDS) is a contract between a protection seller and a protection buyer based on a specified Nominal Principal. The protection buyer pays a running premium (the CDS spread) until maturity of the contract or a Credit Event on the Reference Entity of the CDS. At a Credit Event the protection seller pays a Specified Amount (Fixed or (1-Recovery) times the Nominal Principal) to the protection buyer. The Reference Entity is typically a company or sovereign state (with reference to specific loans or bonds issued). The Credit Event of the definition can be bankruptcy, a failure to pay, or a debt restructuring. The payment in a Credit is meant to reflect a situation where the protection buyer delivers the defaulted bonds or loans to the protection seller and receives the Notional Principal. Usually the CDS specifies a number of different bonds or loans that can be delivered, which gives the protection buyer a “Cheapest-to-Deliver” option. In practise there is often specified an auction procedure to determine the value of cheapest-to-deliver bonds or loans, and the (1-Recovery) is paid as a net cash transfer rather than through delivery. Also the (1-Recovery) may be specified as a Fixed amount in the contract.
(b) A cash CDO is an example of an Asset Backed Security where the underlying assets are bonds. A synthetic CDO (or a tranched CDS) is based on a portfolio of CDS-contracts where protection is sold. In both cases the credit risk from the underlying or referenced portfolio (of bonds or derivatives) is transferred into different instruments (tranches) of different credit risk (or “seniority”), as explained below.
(c) Ref. Hull Section 25.8–10. The cash flows to the debt tranches in the CDO (or tranched CDS) is defined through a waterfall structure, so that the most senior debt tranches of the CDO are most likely to receive promised interest payments and repayment of principal. The most junior debt tranche is called the “Equity”-tranche and receives the residual if and when all other tranches have been repaid in full. The Equity-tranche is typically not rated.
(d) This discussion is covered in Hull section 25.9. Note that the credit risk on the underlying portfolio is assumed constant. Thus the correlation will only influence the distribution of losses between tranches. A low level of correlation will make it likely that the losses can be absorbed by the tranches low in the capital structure in most states of the world whereas a high correlation will make it more likely that even high tranches occasionally will be hit. Thus for a given average level of credit risk the equity tranche will benefit from an increase in the perceived correlation and the highest (super senior) tranche will suffer. The effect on mezzanine tranches can be ambiguous.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Credit Risk   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Credit Default Swap (CDS): A CDS is a bilateral contract on a reference entity with nominal \(N\), between a protection buyer and a protection seller. Before a credit event, the buyer pays the CDS spread \(s\times N\) periodically; at a credit event (default, failure-to-pay, or restructuring), the seller pays \((1-R)\times N\) to the buyer. This spread is approximately \[\boxed{\text{CDS spread} \approx \lambda(1-R)\quad\text{where }\lambda=\text{hazard rate}.}\]

(b) Cash CDO vs. synthetic CDO: In a cash CDO, a special-purpose vehicle buys the reference assets (bonds or loans) directly and funds the tranches by issuing notes backed by these assets — a physical transfer of assets. In a synthetic CDO, the SPV does not hold the assets at all; credit risk is transferred through CDS contracts, so only the risk (not the assets) is tranched and sold, making it cheaper to create.

(c) Tranched CDS/CDO — structure: Losses flow bottom-up through a waterfall: equity, then mezzanine, then senior. The attachment point is the portfolio loss percentage at which a tranche starts absorbing losses; the detachment point is the loss percentage at which it is fully wiped out — for example, equity 0–3%, mezzanine 3–7%, senior 7–100%.

(d) Correlation effect on tranches: With low correlation, defaults are diversified, so the junior tranche absorbs most losses and the senior tranche stays safe with a low spread. With high correlation, losses become lumpy, so the senior tranche is also hit and its spread rises.

Question 3

Let \(P(t,t_k)\) denote the price at \(t\) of a zero coupon bond that matures at time \(t_k\). Let \(F_k(t)\) be the simple forward rate between times \(t_k\) and \(t_{k+1}\) that can be contracted for at time \(t \leq t_k < t_{k+1}\), and let \(\tau_k\) be the compounding period between \(t_k\) and \(t_{k+1}\).
(a) Consider a forward rate \(F_k\). How can this be expressed as a function of prices of zero coupon bonds?
(b) Assume that the forward rates are driven by one factor. What choice of numeraire will lead to the forward rate \(F_k(t)\) being a martingale, e.g. of the form \[dF_k(t) = \zeta_k(t)F_k(t)\,dz\] where \(dz\) is the standard shorthand notation for the change of a Brownian motion.
(c) The market models of interest rates describes the evolution of the term structure of simple interest rates. In the LMM-model a rolling numeraire is used. Explaining in general terms how this is constructed (explicit formulas are not required, but you should discuss change of numeraires).

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) We define or see as an arbitrage relationship \[1 + \tau_k F_k = \frac{P(t,t_k)}{P(t,t_{k+1})}\] One interpretation is that the (simple) forward rate at time \(t\) for future dates \(t_k < t_{k+1}\) is the interest rate that you implicitly can contract for bringing a value at time \(t_k\) forward with that interest rate (linearly, or simply multiplied by the length of the time interval) to a value at time \(t_{k+1}\).
(b) Ref. Hull, Section 31.7 (8th ed). The numeraire must be the zero coupon bond that is used for discounting when there is the payment on the forward rate contract, i.e. the numeraire \(P(t,t_{k+1})\). This is the choice that is said to be forward risk neutral with respect to this numeraire.
(c) In the market models there is a rolling change of numeraire where we use the next reset date of discretely compounded, i.e. simple, short term rates. If current date is \(t\) we use the notation \(m(t)\) for the next reset date. Using \(P(t,t_{m(t)})\) as a numeraire the forward rate will not be a martingale if \(m(t) < k\). But we know that the change in drift is determined by the difference in volatilities of the two numeraires (Hull, chapters 27 and 31, 8th ed). This constitutes a full answer. One may however also note that if we assume that zero coupon prices are driven by geometric Brownian motions, and let \(v_t\) denote the volatility of the zero coupon bond maturing at \(t\) we have \[dF_k(t) = \zeta_k(t)\bigl[v_{m(t)}(t) - v_{k+1}(t)\bigr]F_k(t)\,dt + \zeta_k(t)F_k(t)\,dz\]

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: LIBOR Market Model   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Forward rate \(F_k(t)\) from zero-coupon bond prices: No-arbitrage requires that investing from \(t_k\) to \(t_{k+1}\), whether at the forward rate \(F_k\) under simple compounding or by rolling bonds, gives the same result: \[1 + \tau_k F_k(t) = \frac{P(t,t_k)}{P(t,t_{k+1})}.\] Solving for \(F_k\), \[\boxed{F_k(t) = \frac{1}{\tau_k}\left(\frac{P(t,t_k)}{P(t,t_{k+1})} - 1\right).}\]

(b) Numeraire for \(F_k\) in the LIBOR Market Model: Choosing \(P(t,t_{k+1})\), the bond maturing when the rate is paid, as numeraire makes \(F_k(t)\) a martingale (driftless) under the \(t_{k+1}\)-forward measure \(Q^{k+1}\): \[\boxed{dF_k = \sigma_k F_k\,d\tilde{z}^{k+1}.}\] This is the key LMM result: forward rates are lognormal under their natural measure.

(c) Rolling numeraire in the LMM: Each forward rate \(F_k\) is a martingale only under its own \(t_{k+1}\)-forward measure, so with many rates \(F_1,\dots,F_n\) in the model, a single fixed bond \(P(t,t_{k+1})\) cannot serve as numeraire for all of them simultaneously while keeping every \(F_k\) driftless. Instead, define \(m(t)\) as the next reset date after \(t\), and use the discretely-compounded rolling numeraire \[\boxed{\text{Numeraire}(t) = P\bigl(t,t_{m(t)}\bigr)\times\prod_{\text{past resets}}\bigl(1+\tau_j L_j\bigr),}\] a strategy that holds the shortest-maturity bond available and, on each reset date, rolls the proceeds into the next bond, compounding at the realised simple LIBOR rate. Under this rolling-numeraire measure, only the forward rate whose payment date coincides with the current reset is exactly driftless; every other \(F_k\) picks up a drift term determined by the difference between the volatility of its own \(t_{k+1}\)-bond and the volatility of the rolling numeraire's current bond — a standard change-of-numeraire (Girsanov) adjustment: \[dF_k(t) = \zeta_k(t)\bigl[v_{m(t)}(t) - v_{k+1}(t)\bigr]F_k(t)\,dt + \zeta_k(t)F_k(t)\,dz.\] This construction lets the LMM keep every forward rate lognormal, so Black-type caplet formulas apply rate-by-rate, while still allowing payoffs that depend jointly on multiple rates to be priced under one consistent, arbitrage-free measure.

June 2022 — Ordinary Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

Question 1

Let the time \(t\) spot exchange rate for a foreign currency be \(S_t\), denoting the value of one unit of the foreign currency as measured in the domestic currency. Assume that the exchange rate can be modelled (under the original probability measure \(P\)) by the geometric Brownian motion \[dS = \mu_S S\,dt + \sigma_S S\,dz\] where \(\mu_S\) and \(\sigma_S > 0\) are constants, and where \(dt\) and \(dz\) are the standard shorthand notations for a small time-step and a Brownian increment. Assume that the (continuously compounded) domestic and foreign risk free interest rates are constants \(r\) and \(r_f\), respectively.
(a) What will the drift rate of the exchange rate be under the domestic risk neutral probability measure (\(Q\)) and a no-arbitrage assumption? Comment on the influence of the difference of domestic and foreign interest rates on the result.
(b) Consider at time \(t < T\) a forward contract on the foreign currency for payment and delivery at time \(T\). What is the arbitrage free forward price \(F_t^T\)?
(c) Use Itô's lemma to show how the volatility of forward prices \(F_t^T\) evolves as time \(t\) approaches maturity \(T\).

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) (Refer to Hull section 17.3, 17.5 and also Practise Question 17.2) The drift rate will be \((r - r_f)S\), i.e. determined by the domestic risk free interest rate less the foreign interest rate (analogous to a stock paying a continuous dividend rate). Under \(Q\) the currency with a high interest rate is expected to depreciate at a rate corresponding to the interest rate differential (Not named as such in the syllabus, but this is the uncovered interest rate parity).
(b) Consider two strategies to end with the foreign currency at time \(T\): A) Enter into a forward contract at time \(t\). You pay the agreed forward price \(F_t^T\) at time \(T\). B) Borrow \(S_t e^{-r_f(T-t)}\) at the domestic risk free rate and buy the currency spot placing on deposit at the foreign rate. At \(T\) you have accumulated one unit of the foreign currency at the deposit. You repay the loan that have accumulated to \(S_t e^{(r-r_f)(T-t)}\). In the absence of arbitrage opportunities the forward price contracted at \(t\) thus must be \(F_t^T = S_t e^{(r-r_f)(T-t)}\) (the covered interest rate parity).
(c) There are two different approaches to answering the question, either of which will be considered as valid: For a family of forward contracts of continually shortened maturity the volatility can be found from the previous formula by Itô's lemma to be \(\frac{\partial F_t^T}{\partial S_t}\sigma_S S_t = e^{(r-r_f)(T-t)}S_t\sigma_S\). Thus it goes to the volatility of the currency as the maturity of the forward goes to zero. For a forward contract contracted at time \(t\) at the forward price \(K = F_t^T\) the value of that contract will develop as \(V(S,t;K) = Se^{-r_f(T-t)} - Ke^{-r(T-t)}\) with volatility only arising from the first term. Also this goes to the volatility of the currency as the maturity of the forward goes to zero.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: GBM  ·  Forward Pricing   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Drift of the exchange rate under domestic \(Q\): The foreign currency earns interest continuously at rate \(r_f\), just like a stock paying a continuous dividend yield. Under the domestic risk-neutral measure the total expected rate of return from holding the currency must equal the domestic risk-free rate \(r\). This total return decomposes into the capital-gain drift \(\mu_Q\) plus the continuous foreign-interest earnings rate \(r_f\): \[\mu_Q + r_f = r\] Solving for \(\mu_Q\), \[\mu_Q = r - r_f\] Therefore, \[\boxed{dS = (r-r_f)S\,dt + \sigma_S S\,dz^Q.}\] If \(r > r_f\), the currency is expected to appreciate; if \(r < r_f\), it is expected to depreciate.

(b) Forward exchange rate by arbitrage: Compare two strategies for holding one unit of foreign currency at \(T\). Strategy A: enter a forward contract at \(t\), paying \(F_t^T\) at \(T\). Strategy B: borrow \(S_t e^{-r_f(T-t)}\) domestically at rate \(r\), convert it to \(e^{-r_f(T-t)}\) units of foreign currency, and invest at the foreign rate \(r_f\). By \(T\) this grows to exactly one unit of foreign currency, while the domestic loan has grown to \[S_t\,e^{(r-r_f)(T-t)}.\] Both strategies deliver one unit of foreign currency at \(T\), so by no-arbitrage, \[\boxed{F_t^T = S_t\,e^{(r-r_f)(T-t)}.}\]

(c) Volatility of the forward price as \(t\to T\): Write the forward price as \[F_t^T = S_t\,g(t), \qquad g(t) = e^{(r-r_f)(T-t)},\] where \(g(t)\) is deterministic. Since \(g\) is deterministic, differentiating it with respect to \(t\) (the chain rule picks up a minus sign from the \((T-t)\) exponent) gives \[dg = g'(t)\,dt = -(r-r_f)\,g(t)\,dt.\] By the product rule for Itô processes (applied to a stochastic factor \(S_t\) times a deterministic factor \(g(t)\)), \[dF_t^T = g(t)\,dS_t + S_t\,dg.\] Substituting \(dS_t\) and \(dg\), \[dF_t^T = g(t)\bigl(\mu_S S_t\,dt+\sigma_S S_t\,dz\bigr) + S_t\bigl(-(r-r_f)g(t)\,dt\bigr).\] Expanding both terms, \[dF_t^T = \mu_S S_t g(t)\,dt + \sigma_S S_t g(t)\,dz - (r-r_f)S_t g(t)\,dt.\] Collecting the \(dt\) terms, \[dF_t^T = \bigl[\mu_S-(r-r_f)\bigr]S_t g(t)\,dt + \sigma_S S_t g(t)\,dz.\] Since \(S_t g(t) = F_t^T\), \[dF_t^T = \bigl(\mu_S-(r-r_f)\bigr)F_t^T\,dt + \sigma_S F_t^T\,dz.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{\text{volatility of }F_t^T = \sigma_S F_t^T,}\] the same percentage volatility as the spot rate. As \(t\to T\), \(F_t^T\to S_T\) and the forward's volatility converges to the spot volatility \(\sigma_S\).

Question 2

Let the probability of a borrower not defaulting at or before time \(t\) be given by \(V(t)\), \(V(0) = 1\).
(a) What is the probability that the borrower will default between time \(t\) and \(t + \Delta t\) conditional on not being in default at time \(t\)? Use this to define the continuously compounded default hazard rate \(\lambda(t)\).
(b) Define a Credit Default Swap (CDS) and describe the payment structure.
(c) How, and under which assumptions, may we estimate the hazard rate from a CDS spread \(s\) on the relevant borrower? Under what probability measure would we say this estimate is derived? Compare this to a hazard rate that is derived from default frequencies and recovery rates published by a rating agency.
(d) Which type of derived hazard rate should be applied for pricing financial derivatives, and what kind of discounting is then consistent with this approach?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Refer to Hull section 24.2. The conditional default probability from \(t\) to \(t + \Delta t\) is \(\frac{V(t) - V(t+\Delta t)}{V(t)}\). Given \(V(t)\) the hazard rate is the rate of decay of survivors. Assuming differentiability you may define it as \[\lambda(t) = -\frac{\partial V(t)/\partial t}{V(t)}\] This can be motivated by considering the above discrete time step and letting the time step approach zero.
(b) Refer to Hull section 25.1. Definition (Credit Default Swap). A Credit Default Swap (CDS) is a contract between a protection seller and a protection buyer based on a specified Nominal Principal. The protection buyer pays a running premium (the CDS spread) until maturity of the contract or a Credit Event on the Reference Entity of the CDS. At a Credit Event the protection seller pays a Specified Amount (Fixed or (1-Recovery) times the Nominal Principal) to the protection buyer.
(c) Refer to Hull section 24.4–5. Under a risk neutral probability measure the hazard rate (also called default intensity) may be derived directly from CDS-spreads if you make an appropriate assumption on the recovery rate \(R\). Assume a given recovery rate of \(R\) (this may be fixed in the contract), and that the CDS-spread is \(s\) for a given maturity. Then the implied average hazard rate for this maturity is \(\bar{\lambda} = \frac{s}{1-R}\). You may similarly bootstrap a hazard rate structure from a term structure of CDS spreads. This analysis is conducted under a risk neutral (“Q”) measure, so that \((\lambda, R)\) will not be the same as frequencies and averages published by rating agencies, i.e. under real world probabilities (“P”).
(d) The Q-approach should be applied for pricing when using the short term risk free rate for discounting, being consistent with the numeraire that is the cumulative return from the risk free rate.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Credit Risk   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Default hazard rate: The conditional default probability between \(t\) and \(t+\Delta t\), given survival to \(t\), is \[\frac{V(t)-V(t+\Delta t)}{V(t)}.\] Letting \(\Delta t\to 0\) gives the instantaneous rate of decay of survivors: \[\lambda(t) = -\frac{V'(t)}{V(t)} = -\frac{d\ln V}{dt}.\] Separating variables, \[d\ln V = -\lambda(t)\,dt.\] Integrating both sides from \(0\) to \(t\), \[\int_0^t d\ln V = -\int_0^t \lambda(s)\,ds \implies \ln V(t) - \ln V(0) = -\int_0^t \lambda(s)\,ds.\] Since \(V(0)=1\), \(\ln V(0)=0\), so \[\ln V(t) = -\int_0^t \lambda(s)\,ds.\] Exponentiating, \[\boxed{V(t) = e^{-\int_0^t \lambda(s)\,ds}.}\] If \(\lambda\) is constant this integrates to \(V(t) = e^{-\lambda t}\).

(b) Credit Default Swap: A CDS is a contract between a protection buyer and a protection seller on a reference entity with nominal \(N\). The buyer pays the CDS spread \(s\times N\) periodically until maturity or a credit event. At a credit event, the seller pays \((1-R)\times N\) to the buyer, where \(R\) is the recovery rate. The CDS isolates and transfers the credit risk of the reference entity.

(c) Estimating the hazard rate from the CDS spread: By no-arbitrage, the present value of the protection leg equals the present value of the premium leg. Under the risk-neutral measure, with constant hazard rate \(\lambda\) and recovery \(R\), \[\text{PV(protection)} = (1-R)\int_0^T e^{-rt}\lambda e^{-\lambda t}\,dt, \qquad \text{PV(premium)} = s\int_0^T e^{-rt}e^{-\lambda t}\,dt.\] Both integrals contain the same factor \(e^{-(r+\lambda)t}\), so they can be rewritten as \[\text{PV(protection)} = (1-R)\lambda\int_0^T e^{-(r+\lambda)t}\,dt, \qquad \text{PV(premium)} = s\int_0^T e^{-(r+\lambda)t}\,dt.\] Setting PV(protection) equal to PV(premium), \[(1-R)\lambda\int_0^T e^{-(r+\lambda)t}\,dt = s\int_0^T e^{-(r+\lambda)t}\,dt.\] Dividing both sides by the common (nonzero) integral \(\int_0^T e^{-(r+\lambda)t}\,dt\), \[(1-R)\lambda = s.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{s = \lambda(1-R),}\] so \(\lambda = s/(1-R)\): the hazard rate equals the credit spread divided by the loss-given-default. This is a risk-neutral (Q) estimate, so it will generally differ from the real-world (P) frequencies and recovery rates published by rating agencies.

(d) Which hazard rate to use for pricing: When pricing under the risk-neutral measure \(Q\), we discount at the risk-free rate \(r\); the numeraire is the cumulative risk-free bank account \(e^{rt}\). Under \(Q\), all traded assets earn the risk-free rate in expectation, so pricing reduces to \[\text{Price} = e^{-rT}E^Q[\text{payoff}].\] Therefore, \[\boxed{\text{use the Q-measure hazard rate, discounted at the risk-free rate.}}\] This keeps the numeraire and the discount rate consistent, avoiding arbitrage.

Question 3

Consider an interest rate floor with a life of \(T\), a principal of \(N\), and a floor rate of \(L_K\). Consider reset dates \(0 = t_0 < t_1 < t_2 < \cdots < t_n\), and let \(L_k\) be the Libor rate for the period from \(t_k\) to \(t_{k+1}\) known at \(t_k\).
(a) Describe the payments of the floor, and define a floorlet.
(b) Show that the floor can be considered as a portfolio of European call options on zero-coupon bonds (for notational ease you may assume \(N = 1\)). (Hint: Start by analyzing a single floorlet.)
(c) A standard market practice is to price a floorlet (at \(t = 0\)) with the Black formula: \[\text{FloorletBlack}(0,k,N,L_K,\sigma_k) = NP(0,t_{k+1})\tau_k\bigl(L_K\Phi(-d_2) - F_k\Phi(-d_1)\bigr)\] with \(d_1 = \frac{\ln(F_k/L_K) + 0.5\sigma_k^2 t_k}{\sigma_k\sqrt{t_k}}\), \(d_2 = d_1 - \sigma_k\sqrt{t_k}\), where \(P(t,T)\) is the price at \(t\) of a zero coupon bond maturing at \(T\), \(F_k\) is the forward rate at 0 for the time interval \((t_k, t_{k+1})\) with length \(\tau_k\), and \(\sigma_k\) the volatility of this rate. The function \(\Phi\) is the standard Normal distribution function. What assumptions can justify this formula?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Refer to Hull section 29.2. Definition (Floors). Let the variable (Libor-) rate set at \(t_k\) for the period \((t_k, t_{k+1})\) be \(L_k\). Consider a maturity \(T\), a principal of \(N\) and a Floor Rate of \(L_K\). Let reset dates \(t_1, t_2, \ldots, t_n\) be given and \(t_{n+1} = T\). A Floor is a contract that gives its holder the payments \(N\tau_k \max(L_K - L_k;\, 0)\) at time \(t_{k+1}\) (i.e. in arrears), where \(\tau_k\) is the time length between \(t_k\) and \(t_{k+1}\). The potential payment at each payoff date defines the payment of a single floorlet starting on the previous reset date. Definition (Floorlet). A floorlet is a contract that gives its holder the payments \(\tau_k \max(L_K - L_k;\, 0)\) at time \(t_{k+1}\), where \(\tau_k\) is the time fraction between \(t_k\) and \(t_{k+1}\).
(b) A floor can be seen as a portfolio of calls on properly defined Zero Coupon Bonds (ZCBs). The present value of \(N\) floorlets covering the period from \(t_k\) to \(t_{k+1}\) and with payment at \(t_{k+1}\) is at time \(t_k\) (discounting with the variable rate \(L_k\), simple compounding): \[\frac{N\tau_k}{1 + L_k\tau_k}\max(L_K - L_k;\, 0) = \frac{1 + L_K\tau_k}{1 + L_k\tau_k}\max(N - N;\, 0)\] In the last expression the first part in the first argument under the max-operator is the present value at \(t_k\) of a ZCB with payoff \(N(1 + L_K\tau_k)\) at \(t_{k+1}\); thus the payoff is a call on this particular ZCB with strike \(N\). As the floor can be seen (and valued) as a portfolio of floorlets, it can be valued as a portfolio of such calls.
(c) The application of the Black model is consistent with a world that is forward risk neutral wrt a ZCB maturing at \(t_{k+1}\). Under this probability measure assume \(L_k\) has a lognormal distribution with a standard deviation of \(\ln(L_k)\) of \(\sigma_k\sqrt{t_k}\). We also have under this measure that the expectation of \(L_k\) is the forward rate \(F_k\).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Interest Rate Derivatives   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Interest rate floor — payments and floorlets: A floor pays \(N\tau_k\max(L_K-L_k,0)\) at each date \(t_{k+1}\), where \(L_k\) is the LIBOR rate set at \(t_k\) for the period \((t_k,t_{k+1})\) and \(\tau_k=t_{k+1}-t_k\). Each individual such payment is one floorlet: it pays off if LIBOR falls below the floor rate \(L_K\).

(b) Floor as a portfolio of calls on zero-coupon bonds: Express \(L_k\) in terms of the bond price: \[1+\tau_kL_k=\frac{1}{P(t_k,t_{k+1})} \implies L_k=\frac{1}{\tau_k}\!\left(\frac1P-1\right).\] Expanding the payoff bracket, \[\tau_k(L_K-L_k) = \tau_kL_K-\tau_kL_k.\] Substituting \(\tau_kL_k=\frac1P-1\), \[\tau_k(L_K-L_k) = \tau_kL_K-\frac1P+1.\] Combining the terms over the common denominator \(P\), \[\tau_k(L_K-L_k) = \frac1P\bigl(P(1+\tau_kL_K)-1\bigr),\] so \[N\tau_k\max(L_K-L_k,0) = \frac{N}{P}\max\bigl(P(1+\tau_kL_K)-1,\,0\bigr).\] Defining \(K_B=1/(1+\tau_kL_K)\), we have \(P(1+\tau_kL_K)-1=(1+\tau_kL_K)(P-K_B)\), so this becomes \[\frac{N(1+\tau_kL_K)}{P}\max(P-K_B,0).\] The floorlet pays at \(t_{k+1}\); multiplying through by \(P(t_k,t_{k+1})\) converts the \(1/P\) factor into a \(t_k\)-value: \[\boxed{\text{floorlet value at }t_k = N(1+\tau_kL_K)\max\bigl(P(t_k,t_{k+1})-K_B,\,0\bigr).}\] This is \(N(1+\tau_kL_K)\) European calls on the bond \(P(t_k,t_{k+1})\) with strike \(K_B=1/(1+\tau_kL_K)\), so the floor as a whole is a portfolio of such calls, \[\boxed{\text{floor} = \sum_k N(1+\tau_kL_K)\cdot\text{call on }P(t_k,t_{k+1})\text{ with strike }K_B.}\]

(c) Floorlet pricing with Black's model: Under the \(t_{k+1}\)-forward measure, \(L_k\) is lognormal with mean equal to the forward rate \(F_k=E^{Q^{k+1}}[L_k]\). Black's formula, applied to a floorlet as a put on the rate, gives \[\text{Floorlet}_k = N\tau_kP(0,t_{k+1})\bigl[L_K\Phi(-d_2)-F_k\Phi(-d_1)\bigr],\] with \[d_1=\frac{\ln(F_k/L_K)+\tfrac12\sigma_k^2t_k}{\sigma_k\sqrt{t_k}},\qquad d_2=d_1-\sigma_k\sqrt{t_k}.\] Summing over all floorlets, \[\boxed{\text{Floor} = \sum_{k=0}^{n-1} N\tau_kP(0,t_{k+1})\bigl[L_K\Phi(-d_2^k)-F_k\Phi(-d_1^k)\bigr].}\]

August 2022 — Re-Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

Question 1

Assume that the price of a non-dividend paying stock \(S_t\) can be modelled (under the original probability measure \(P\)) by the geometric Brownian motion \[dS_t = \mu S_t\,dt + \sigma S_t\,dz\] where \(\mu\) and \(\sigma > 0\) are constants, and where \(dt\) and \(dz\) are the standard short hand notations for a small time-step and a Brownian increment.
(a) Consider the transformation \(G\) of \(S_t\) by the natural logarithm (ln), i.e. \(G(x) = \ln(x)\). Use Itô's lemma to find the process followed by \(G(S_t)\).
(b) Consider a forward contract on the stock maturing at time \(T\). Assume a constant continuously compounded interest rate of \(r\). What is the arbitrage free relation between stock price \(S_t\) and forward price \(F_t^T\)?
(c) First, compare the pricing of a call option on the stock with a pricing of a call on the forward contract on the same stock with both forward contracts maturing at \(T\) and having the same strike \(K\).
(d) Now assume that the stock pays a continuous dividend rate of \(\delta\), i.e. at each time \(t\) proportional to the price of the stock. Does this change the result?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Refer to Hull section 14.7. The use of Itô's lemma is straightforward, using that \(\frac{\partial G}{\partial x} = 1/x\), \(\frac{\partial^2 G}{\partial x^2} = -1/x^2\), \(\frac{\partial G}{\partial t} = 0\). You then get: \[dG = \bigl(\mu - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2\bigr)dt + \sigma\,dz\]
(b) Refer to Hull section 14.6 and 18.3. First the answer should note the definition of a forward contract, in particular that it has no payments before maturity. Then the answer should cover the forward price that will be agreed barring arbitrage: With an assumption of a constant risk free interest rate this is for the forward price \(F_t^T\) contracted at time \(t \leq T\), where \(T\) is the maturity of the contract, \[F_t^T = E^Q[S_T] = S_t e^{r(T-t)}\] where we only for the last equation use the assumption of no dividends. A constructive argument with the same result compares two strategies to achieve ownership of one stock at time \(T\); namely (1) Buy the stock at the price \(S_t\) at time \(t\) and hold it to time \(T\). (2) Enter into the forward contract at time \(t\), and at the same time place the amount \(e^{-r(T-t)}F_t^T\) at interest. At time \(T\) the amount has grown with interest to \(F_t^T\) that can be paid in the forward contract to gain one stock. Since these strategies yield the same result at time \(T\), the outlays at time \(t\) must be the same if there is to be no arbitrage. Last the answer may note that \(F\) follows a Geometric Brownian Motion similar to that derived earlier for \(S\), but that the drift rate of returns is \(\mu - r\) rather than the \(\mu\) for the stock. Under the Q-measure the drift rates will be 0 and \(r\), respectively.
(c) Thus comparing two call options with the same strike, but where the first is on the forward contract and the second is on the stock, \[c^F = e^{-r(T-t)}E^Q\bigl[\max(F_T^T - K;\, 0)\bigr] = e^{-r(T-t)}E^Q\bigl[\max(S_T - K;\, 0)\bigr] = c^S\] since they will have the same payout at maturity, even if the forward price initially (with a positive interest rate) is higher than the spot price. This may be verified by looking at the formulae for call prices on stocks and forwards (if you e.g. substitute the relation between forward and spot prices into the Black formula you get the Black-Scholes formula for a call).
(d) This then also holds for the case of dividends (as earlier dividends are irrelevant for the payments at \(T\)).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: GBM  ·  Forward Pricing   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Itô's lemma applied to \(G(S_t)=\ln S_t\): The partial derivatives are \[\frac{\partial G}{\partial S}=\frac1S,\qquad \frac{\partial^2 G}{\partial S^2}=-\frac1{S^2},\qquad \frac{\partial G}{\partial t}=0.\] Applying Itô's lemma with \((dS_t)^2=\sigma^2S_t^2\,dt\), \[d\ln S = \frac{dS}{S} + \tfrac12\left(-\frac1{S^2}\right)\sigma^2S^2\,dt.\] Substituting \(dS=\mu S\,dt+\sigma S\,dz\) gives \[d\ln S = \frac1S(\mu S\,dt+\sigma S\,dz) - \tfrac12\sigma^2\,dt.\] Distributing the \(1/S\) factor, \[d\ln S = \mu\,dt+\sigma\,dz - \tfrac12\sigma^2\,dt.\] Collecting the \(dt\) terms, \[\boxed{d\ln S_t = \bigl(\mu-\tfrac12\sigma^2\bigr)dt + \sigma\,dz.}\]

(b) Forward price \(F_t^T=E^Q[S_T]\): Under the risk-neutral measure the drift of \(S\) becomes \(r\), so \[d\ln S = (r-\tfrac12\sigma^2)dt + \sigma\,dz^Q, \qquad \ln S_T \sim N\bigl(m,\ v^2\bigr),\quad m=\ln S_t+(r-\tfrac12\sigma^2)(T-t),\ v^2=\sigma^2(T-t).\] For a lognormal variable \(S_T=e^{\ln S_T}\), the expectation is \(E^Q[S_T]=e^{m+\frac12v^2}\). Substituting \(m\) and \(v^2\), \[E^Q[S_T] = e^{\ln S_t + (r-\frac12\sigma^2)(T-t) + \frac12\sigma^2(T-t)}.\] The \(-\tfrac12\sigma^2(T-t)\) and \(+\tfrac12\sigma^2(T-t)\) terms in the exponent cancel, \[E^Q[S_T] = e^{\ln S_t + r(T-t)}.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{F_t^T = S_t\,e^{r(T-t)}.}\]

(c) Call on the futures vs. call on the stock, same \(K,T\): At maturity, the futures price converges to the spot price, \(F_T^T=S_T\), so the payoff of a call on the futures, \(\max(S_T-K,0)\), is identical to the payoff of a call on the stock. Since both options are European with the same maturity, their prices must match: \[\boxed{c^{\text{futures}} = c^{\text{stock}} = e^{-rT}E^Q[\max(S_T-K,0)].}\] This holds even though the futures price initially exceeds the spot price when \(r>0\).

(d) With dividends: The same result holds: at maturity \(T\), \(F_T^T=S_T\) regardless of any dividends paid before \(T\). Therefore, \[\boxed{c^{\text{futures}} = c^{\text{stock}}\text{ for any dividend policy.}}\]

Question 2

(a) In the one-factor Gaussian model of defaults the default of an obligor \(i\) is indicated by \[x_i = a_i F + \sqrt{1 - a_i^2}\,Z_i\] where \(F\) and \(Z_i\), \(i = 1,\ldots,n\), have independent standard normal distributions. Give an interpretation of the model and its parameters. What is the (copula) default correlation between \(x_i\) and \(x_j\) for some \(j \neq i\)?
(b) In this model the indicator \(x_i\) is compared to the probability \(Q_i(T)\) that the obligor has defaulted before or at time \(T\). Suppose that the obligor defaults at time \(t\). Which value of the indicator \(x_i\) does this correspond to?
(c) Explain for a tranched structured product such as a Credit Debt Obligation what the terms attachment point and detachment point refer to.
(d) Is the value of the most senior tranche in general positively or negatively influenced by an increase in the default correlation of obligors?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Refer to Hull sections 24.8 and 25.10. Note that \(F\) is a common factor determining the covariance structure, whereas \(Z_i\) are idiosyncratic factors, and \(a_i\) are sensitivities to the common factor. Note that \(\text{Var}[x_i] = a_i^2 + (1-a_i^2) = 1\) and \(\text{Cov}[x_i,x_j] = a_i a_j\) using that \(F\) and \(Z_i\) are standard normal and independent. In implementation the \(a_i\) are often approximated as the correlation between \(i\)'s equity return and some broad market index (i.e. P-type correlations). They could also be inferred from e.g. prices of tranched CDOs (i.e. Q-type correlations)).
(b) The indicator can be interpreted as corresponding to a value of \(\Phi^{-1}(Q_i(t))\) (where \(\Phi\) is the standard normal distribution function), i.e. we investigate defaults on the inverse normal evaluated on the relevant percentile of the given cumulated probability of default such that an observation of a larger \(x_i\) than this critical value means a default before or at \(t\).
(c) The payoff of the debt instruments underlying a cash CDO (or credit derivatives underlying a synthetic CDO) is distributed to the different tranches of the CDO so that the most senior tranche has priority to the payoffs (in a waterfall structure). Losses thus first hit the most junior tranche, called the equity tranche. There are further arrangements to protect the senior tranches, but the above will be a satisfactory answer.
(d) The most senior tranche will — expected loss and recovery being equal — in general benefit from diversification (and thus from low correlations) since then the stochastic losses will be closer to the expected losses and less likely to hit the most senior tranche.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Credit Risk   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Gaussian copula model — interpretation and default correlation: In the model \(x_i=a_iF+\sqrt{1-a_i^2}Z_i\), with \(F,Z_i\sim N(0,1)\) independent, \(F\) is a common systematic factor (the macro/market state) and \(Z_i\) is idiosyncratic, firm-specific noise, while \(a_i\) is obligor \(i\)'s loading on the common factor. Since \(F\) and \(Z_i\) are independent standard normals, \[\text{Var}(x_i) = a_i^2 + (1-a_i^2) = 1,\] and, expanding the covariance bilinearly, \[\text{Cov}(x_i,x_j) = \text{Cov}\bigl(a_iF+\sqrt{1-a_i^2}Z_i,\ a_jF+\sqrt{1-a_j^2}Z_j\bigr)\] \[= a_ia_j\text{Cov}(F,F) + a_i\sqrt{1-a_j^2}\text{Cov}(F,Z_j) + a_j\sqrt{1-a_i^2}\text{Cov}(Z_i,F) + \sqrt{(1-a_i^2)(1-a_j^2)}\text{Cov}(Z_i,Z_j).\] Since \(F,Z_i,Z_j\) are mutually independent, every covariance term except \(\text{Cov}(F,F)=\text{Var}(F)=1\) vanishes, \[\text{Cov}(x_i,x_j) = a_ia_j\,\text{Var}(F) = a_ia_j.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{\rho_{ij} = \frac{\text{Cov}(x_i,x_j)}{\sqrt{\text{Var}(x_i)\text{Var}(x_j)}} = a_ia_j.}\]

(b) Default threshold and probability conditional on the common factor: Obligor \(i\) defaults before \(T\) exactly when \(x_i\leq\Phi^{-1}(Q_i(T))=:C_i\), i.e. \[a_iF+\sqrt{1-a_i^2}\,Z_i \leq C_i.\] Subtracting \(a_iF\) from both sides, \[\sqrt{1-a_i^2}\,Z_i \leq C_i-a_iF.\] Dividing both sides by \(\sqrt{1-a_i^2}\) to isolate \(Z_i\), \[Z_i \leq \frac{C_i-a_iF}{\sqrt{1-a_i^2}}.\] Since \(Z_i\) is standard normal, this gives \[\boxed{P(\text{default}_i\mid F) = \Phi\!\left(\frac{C_i-a_iF}{\sqrt{1-a_i^2}}\right).}\]

(c) Attachment and detachment points: Losses in a tranched structure flow bottom-up: the attachment and detachment points define the range of cumulative portfolio losses (as a percentage of total notional) that a given tranche absorbs. A tranche is untouched while losses are below its attachment point, absorbs losses one-for-one as they rise from the attachment to the detachment point, and is wiped out once losses exceed the detachment point; the equity (most junior) tranche has the lowest attachment point and the senior tranche the highest.

(d) Effect of correlation on the senior tranche: When correlation \(\rho\) is low, defaults are diversified and losses stay close to their expected value, so the equity tranche absorbs almost everything and the senior tranche stays safe, keeping its spread low. When \(\rho\) is high, defaults cluster: losses become lumpy, sometimes sparing equity entirely and other times spilling deep into the senior tranche. Therefore, \[\boxed{\uparrow\rho \implies \text{senior-tranche risk (and spread) rises.}}\]

Question 3

(a) Define interest rate receiver and payer swaptions (also called swap options) and explain their payoffs.
(b) Give an argument that a receiver swaption can be seen as a call option on a properly defined fixed rate bond. How will the coupon and principal of such a bond then be characterised?
(c) Let \(PS_K\) denote the value at time \(t\) of a swaption to pay a fixed rate \(s_K\) and receive a variable (Libor-) rate between times \(T_1\) and \(T_2\). Let \(RS_K\) denote the value at time \(t\) of a swaption to receive a fixed rate of \(s_K\) and pay variable (Libor-) rate between times \(T_1\) and \(T_2\). Finally, let \(RFS_K\) denote the value at time \(t\) of a forward starting swap that receives a fixed rate of \(s_K\) and pays the variable (Libor-) rate between times \(T_1\) and \(T_2\), \(t < T_1 < T_2\). Assume that there are no arbitrage opportunities and (i) show that \(PS_K + RFS_K = RS_K\) and (ii) deduce that \(PS_K = RS_K\) when \(s_K\) equals the forward swap rate at time \(t\) for a swap running from \(T_1\) to \(T_2\).

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Refer to Hull section 29.3. Definition (Swaption). A payer (receiver) swaption or swap option gives the holder the right to enter into a payer (receiver) Interest Rate Swap with a specified swap rate \(s_K\) at some future date \(T_1\) with the swap running from \(T_1\) to \(T_2\), \(T_2 > T_1\). Some swaptions pay the value of the underlying swap at exercise. The value of the underlying swap will be the value of the cashflow that is the difference of the fixed rate leg calculated with the strike rate \(s_K\) and calculated with the market swap rate at the maturity of the swaption \(s_{T_1}\), i.e. an annuity. Note that the decision of whether to exercise the swaption will depend on the level of the market swap rate compared with the strike rate.
(b) Consider a bond with a fixed rate coupon equal to the swap rate, the principal is the par value of the bond (barring credit risks, liquidity considerations etc.), where the bond begins paying its fixed coupons in arrears from the maturity of the swaption until the maturity of the underlying swap with coupon dates equal to the payment dates on the swap. The receiver swaption can be seen as an option to receive this bond by delivery of the principal (which we, somewhat idealized, assume will be equal to the continuing value of the variable leg of the swap). We can note that both swaption and bond option will increase in value with lower longer rates.
(c) Consider the payments from \(T_1\) to \(T_2\) depending on the realized level of the swap rate \(s_{T_1}\) at \(T_1\) compared with the strike rate \(s_K\), and let Libor be the running variable rate:
Case \(s_K < s_{T_1}\): \(PS_K\) pays \(s_K\) and receives Libor; \(RFS_K\) receives \(s_K\) and pays Libor; \(RS_K\) has payment 0.
Case \(s_K \geq s_{T_1}\): \(PS_K\) has payment 0; \(RFS_K\) receives \(s_K\) and pays Libor; \(RS_K\) receives \(s_K\) and pays Libor.
In the first case the receiver swaption is not exercised since you would then receive a rate lower than current market swap rate. In the second case the payer swaption is not exercised since you would then pay a rate higher than current market swap rate. From the table it is seen that the payments for the payer swaption and the forward starting swap equals the payments on the receiver swaption. Thus barring arbitrage the equation must hold. If \(s_K\) is the current forward swap rate the value \(RFS_K\) is by definition zero. Thus \(PS_K = RS_K\).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Interest Rate Derivatives   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Payer and receiver swaptions — definitions: A payer swaption gives the right to enter an interest rate swap paying the fixed rate \(s_K\) and receiving LIBOR, running from \(T_1\) to \(T_2\); it is exercised when the market swap rate at \(T_1\) is above \(s_K\). A receiver swaption gives the right to enter the opposite swap, receiving fixed \(s_K\) and paying LIBOR; it is exercised when the market rate at \(T_1\) is below \(s_K\).

(b) Receiver swaption as a call on a bond: Receiving a fixed coupon stream from \(T_1\) to \(T_2\) at rate \(s_K\) is equivalent to owning a fixed-rate coupon bond with coupon \(s_K\). The swaption lets you acquire this bond by paying par (the value of the floating leg). Therefore, \[\boxed{\text{receiver swaption} = \text{call option on the coupon bond, struck at par,}}\] with the bond's coupon equal to the swap rate \(s_K\) and its principal equal to the notional.

(c)(i) Swaption parity — showing \(PS_K+RFS_K=RS_K\): Consider a long receiver swaption together with a short payer swaption. At \(T_1\), regardless of the market rate \(s_{T_1}\), exactly one of the two is exercised, and the combined position always ends up as a swap receiving fixed \(s_K\) and paying LIBOR from \(T_1\) to \(T_2\) — precisely the payoff of the forward-starting swap \(RFS_K\), but now with certainty instead of optionality. By no-arbitrage the values must match today, \[RS_K - PS_K = RFS_K,\] so \[\boxed{PS_K + RFS_K = RS_K,}\] where \(RFS_K = \sum_k P(t,t_{k+1})\tau_k(s_K-f_k)\) and \(f_k\) are the current forward rates.

(c)(ii) Deducing \(PS_K=RS_K\) at the forward swap rate: The forward swap rate \(s^*\) is defined as the fixed rate that makes the forward-starting swap worth zero, \[RFS_{s^*} = \sum_k P(t,t_{k+1})\tau_k(s^*-f_k) = 0.\] Setting \(s_K=s^*\) in the parity relation above, \[PS_{s^*} + \underbrace{RFS_{s^*}}_{=\,0} = RS_{s^*}.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{PS_{s^*} = RS_{s^*}.}\] When the strike equals the forward swap rate, payer and receiver swaptions have equal value — the interest-rate analogue of put-call parity struck at the forward price.

June 2021 — Ordinary Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

Question 1
