Consider a stock with price \(S_t\) at time \(t\) and a zero coupon bond maturing at time \(T > t\) with price \(P(t,T)\) at time \(t\).
(a) Assume that the stock pays no dividend. What is the forward price \(F(t,T)\) at \(t\) for the stock with delivery at \(T\)?
(b) Now assume that the stock pays a continuous dividend rate of \(\delta\). What is the forward price in this case?
(c) Let \(c(t,K,T)\) and \(p(t,K,T)\) denote the prices respectively of a call and a put option on the stock, both with exercise price \(K\) and maturity \(T\). What is the put-call-parity for the case with the dividend paying stock?
(d) If the prices of the call, put and stock as defined above are known at \(t\), derive the implied dividend rate.

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Consider the following two strategies to get 1 stock at time \(T\): (a) Enter into a forward contract with forward price \(F(t,T)\) to get the stock at \(T\) and at the same time buy \(F(t,T)\) zero coupon bonds. The total payment at \(t\) is \(P(t,T)F(t,T)\). At time \(T\) the zero coupon bond portfolio matures and gives the \(F(t,T)\) to be paid to receive the stock. (b) Buy the stock at time \(t\) and keep it to time \(T\). If there is to be no arbitrage opportunities the two different outlays at time \(t\) must be identical, i.e. \[S(t) = P(t,T)F(t,T)\] \[F(t,T) = S(t)/P(t,T)\]
(b) If there is a continuous dividend rate of \(\delta\) on the stock, in strategy (b) buy only \(e^{-\delta(T-t)}\) of the stock, but reinvest dividends continuously to get 1 stock at time \(T\). Thus \[e^{-\delta(T-t)}S(t) = P(t,T)F(t,T)\] or \[F(t,T) = e^{-\delta(T-t)}S(t)/P(t,T)\]
(c) A long call and a short put with same strike \(K\) and maturity \(T\) is equivalent to a long forward with delivery price \(K\) and maturity \(T\). The value of this forward contract is \(P(t,T)(F(t,T)-K)\). So (abbreviating the notation a little) \[P(t,T)(F(t,T)-K) = c_t - p_t\] or \[e^{-\delta(T-t)}S(t) - P(t,T)K = c_t - p_t\]
(d) Solve \[e^{-\delta(T-t)}S(t) = c_t - p_t + P(t,T)K\] or \[\delta = -\frac{1}{T-t}\ln\frac{c_t - p_t + P(t,T)K}{S(t)}\] (Hull 9th ed, section 17.3–17.4).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Forward Pricing  ·  Put-Call Parity   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Forward price with no dividend: Compare two strategies for owning one stock at \(T\). Strategy A: enter the forward at \(F(t,T)\) and buy \(F(t,T)\) zero-coupon bonds maturing at \(T\), costing \(P(t,T)F(t,T)\) at \(t\). Strategy B: simply buy the stock now, costing \(S(t)\). Both strategies deliver one stock at \(T\), so by no-arbitrage, \[S(t) = P(t,T)F(t,T).\] Therefore, \[\boxed{F(t,T) = \frac{S(t)}{P(t,T)} = S(t)e^{r(T-t)}.}\]

(b) Forward price with a continuous dividend \(\delta\): Adjust Strategy B: buy \(e^{-\delta(T-t)}\) shares now and reinvest the dividends continuously, growing the holding to exactly one share by \(T\); this costs \(S(t)e^{-\delta(T-t)}\) at \(t\). By no-arbitrage, \[P(t,T)F(t,T) = S(t)e^{-\delta(T-t)}.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{F(t,T) = S(t)e^{(r-\delta)(T-t)}.}\]

(c) Put-call parity: At maturity, a long call and a short put with the same strike \(K\) have combined payoff \[\max(S_T-K,0) - \max(K-S_T,0) = S_T - K.\] Discounting this terminal payoff under \(Q\), \[c - p = e^{-r(T-t)}E^Q[S_T-K].\] Distributing the expectation, \[c - p = e^{-r(T-t)}E^Q[S_T] - e^{-r(T-t)}K.\] From part (b), \(E^Q[S_T]=F(t,T)=S(t)e^{(r-\delta)(T-t)}\); substituting, \[c - p = e^{-r(T-t)}S(t)e^{(r-\delta)(T-t)} - Ke^{-r(T-t)}.\] Simplifying the exponent on the first term, \[c - p = S(t)e^{-\delta(T-t)} - Ke^{-r(T-t)}.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{c(t,K,T) - p(t,K,T) = S(t)e^{-\delta(T-t)} - Ke^{-r(T-t)}.}\]

(d) Implied dividend rate from put-call parity: Solving the parity relation above for \(\delta\), first isolate the dividend-discounted stock term, \[S(t)e^{-\delta(T-t)} = c-p+Ke^{-r(T-t)}.\] Dividing both sides by \(S(t)\), \[e^{-\delta(T-t)} = \frac{c-p+Ke^{-r(T-t)}}{S(t)}.\] Taking the natural log of both sides, \[-\delta(T-t) = \ln\frac{c-p+Ke^{-r(T-t)}}{S(t)}.\] Dividing by \(-(T-t)\), \[\boxed{\delta = -\frac{1}{T-t}\ln\frac{c-p+Ke^{-r(T-t)}}{S(t)}.}\] This lets you back out the market-implied dividend yield from observed option prices.

Question 2

Consider a situation with credit risk, and let the probability of a borrower not defaulting at or before time \(t\) be given by \(V(t)\), \(V(0) = 1\).
(a) What is the probability that the borrower will default between time \(t\) and \(t+\Delta t\) conditional on not being in default at time \(t\)? Use this to define the continuously compounded default hazard rate \(\lambda\).
(b) How, and under which assumptions, may we estimate the hazard rate from the interest rate spread \(s\) on bonds issued by the borrower? Under what probability measure would we say this estimate is derived? Compare this to a hazard rate that is derived from default frequencies and recovery ratios published by a rating agency.
(c) In one model by Merton the value of a claim on a company with limited liability is modelled using a variation of the Black-Scholes-Merton option model. What is the option features embedded in such a claim? What parameters that are not directly observable must be determined to price the claim in this model? Comment on the model.

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) The conditional default probability from \(t\) to \(t+\Delta t\) is \[\frac{V(t) - V(t+\Delta t)}{V(t)}\] Given \(V(t)\) the hazard rate is the rate of decay of survivors. Assuming differentiability you may define it as \[\lambda(t) = -\frac{\partial V(t)/\partial t}{V(t)}\] This can be motivated by considering the above discrete time step and letting the time step approach zero (Hull 9th ed, section 24.2–24.4).
(b) Under a risk neutral probability measure the hazard rate may be derived from prices of traded assets, e.g. bonds from the company in question. Assuming that the recovery rate \(\text{Rec}\) is known you may estimate the average hazard rate as the hazard rate that makes the discounted expected payoff (i.e. taking defaults into account) to be the price of a risk free bond. Assuming the risky bond pays a spread of \(s\) over the risk free bond for a given maturity \(T\) you can estimate the average hazard rate over the full period by \[\bar{\lambda}(T) = \frac{s(T)}{1-\text{Rec}}\] You may then use this to bootstrap a hazard rate structure from a term structure of credit spreads. This analysis should, however, be seen as conducted under a risk neutral (“Q”) measure, so that \((\lambda, \text{Rec})\) will not be the same as frequencies and averages published by rating agencies, real world probabilities (“P”) (Hull 9th ed., section 24.5).
(c) In the Merton-model the value of risky debt issued by a limited liability entity is modelled as the value of the assets of the entity less the value of the equity held by the owners of the company. Due to the limited liability the owners can be seen to hold a call option on the assets. Assuming there is only one form of debt, a zero coupon debt of face value \(D\) maturing at time \(T\), the Black-Scholes-Merton Model can be applied. The underlying asset for that model would be the value of the entity assets \(V_t\), which is typically unknown, as is its volatility, that is assumed constant \(\sigma_V\). Note first that the value of equity at time \(T\) is \(E_T = \max(V_T - D, 0)\) where you can use the BSM-call price formula if you know asset values and their volatility. Secondly, when both assets value and equity value are described by geometric Brownian motions you can use Itô's lemma to derive the relationship between the equity volatility \(\sigma_E\) and the asset volatility: \[\sigma_E E_t = \frac{\partial E}{\partial V}\sigma_V V_t\] Assuming that the value of the equity and the equity volatility is known from markets, you have two equations in two unknowns \((V_t, \sigma_V)\) that can be solved numerically for the value of the debt, using \(D_t = V_t - E_t\). A problem with Merton's model is that values are assumed to follow Itô-processes, i.e. with continuous sample paths. This makes the likelihood of a default a short time step ahead very small (no “jump-to-default”) and credit spreads to go to zero as the maturity of risky bonds goes to zero, which is not in line with empirical evidence (Hull 9th ed, section 24.6).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Credit Risk   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Default hazard rate \(\lambda(t)\): The conditional default probability over \([t,t+\Delta t]\), given survival to \(t\), is \[\frac{V(t)-V(t+\Delta t)}{V(t)}.\] Letting \(\Delta t\to 0\) gives \[\lambda(t) = -\frac{V'(t)}{V(t)} = -\frac{d\ln V}{dt}.\] Integrating both sides from \(0\) to \(t\), \[\int_0^t -\lambda(s)\,ds = \ln V(t) - \ln V(0).\] Since \(V(0)=1\), \(\ln V(0)=0\), so \[\int_0^t -\lambda(s)\,ds = \ln V(t).\] Exponentiating gives \[\boxed{V(t) = e^{-\int_0^t\lambda(s)\,ds}.}\] For constant \(\lambda\) this reduces to \(V(t)=e^{-\lambda t}\).

(b) Hazard rate from bond spreads under the Q-measure: A risky bond's yield equals the risk-free yield plus a spread \(s\). Under the risk-neutral measure, with recovery rate \(R\), the spread compensates for expected loss, \[s \approx \lambda(1-R).\] Therefore, \[\boxed{\lambda \approx \frac{s}{1-R}.}\] This \(\lambda\) is a risk-neutral (Q) hazard rate. Rating-agency hazard rates instead use real-world (P) default frequencies; because Q-measure spreads carry a risk premium, \(\lambda\) from bond spreads is typically higher than the rating-agency estimate. For pricing derivatives, the Q-measure hazard rate should be used, discounted at the risk-free rate.

(c) Merton's structural model — embedded option and unobservable parameters: Firm assets \(V\) follow a GBM. At debt maturity \(T\), limited liability makes equity a call on the assets struck at the face value of debt \(N\), \[E_T = \max(V_T-N,0).\] Debt is assets minus equity, \[B_T = V_T-E_T = V_T - \max(V_T-N,0).\] Case-splitting on whether \(V_T\gtrless N\): if \(V_T\geq N\), \(\max(V_T-N,0)=V_T-N\) so \(B_T=N\); if \(V_T<N\), \(\max(V_T-N,0)=0\) so \(B_T=V_T\). Both cases are captured by \[B_T = \min(V_T,N).\] Rewriting the min in terms of a max (again by the same case-split: if \(V_T\geq N\), \(N-\max(N-V_T,0)=N-0=N\); if \(V_T<N\), \(N-\max(N-V_T,0)=N-(N-V_T)=V_T\)), \[\boxed{B_T = N-\max(N-V_T,0),}\] i.e. debt is a risk-free bond minus a put on the assets struck at \(N\). Pricing this with Black-Scholes-Merton requires the asset value \(V_0\) and asset volatility \(\sigma_V\), neither of which is directly observable — only equity value \(E_0\) and equity volatility \(\sigma_E\) are. These must instead be backed out numerically from two equations, \[E_0 = V_0\Phi(d_1) - Ne^{-rT}\Phi(d_2), \qquad \sigma_E E_0 = \sigma_V V_0\Phi(d_1).\] The model gives a useful structural link between default probability, leverage, and asset volatility, but its main limitation is that continuous (Itô) asset paths make short-term default risk unrealistically small — there is no "jump to default", so predicted short-term credit spreads go to zero, unlike in practice. Once \((V_0,\sigma_V)\) are known, the debt value follows from \[\boxed{B_0 = V_0\Phi(-d_1) + Ne^{-rT}\Phi(d_2), \qquad d_1 = \frac{\ln(V_0/N)+(r+\tfrac12\sigma_V^2)T}{\sigma_V\sqrt{T}}.}\]

Question 3

(a) Give a definition of a payer and a receiver interest rate swaption (also called a swap option).
(b) Give an argument that a receiver swaption can be seen as a call option on a properly defined fixed rate bond with no credit risk. What can you say about the coupon and principal of such a bond?
(c) Let \(PS\) denote the value of a swaption to pay a fixed rate \(s_K\) and receive LIBOR between times \(T_1\) and \(T_2\), let \(RS\) denote the value of a swaption to receive a fixed rate of \(s_K\) and pay LIBOR between times \(T_1\) and \(T_2\), and let \(RFS\) denote the value of a forward starting swap that receives a fixed rate of \(s_K\) and pays LIBOR between times \(T_1\) and \(T_2\). Assume that there are no arbitrage opportunities and i) show that \(PS + RFS = RS\) and ii) deduce that \(PS = RS\) when \(s_K\) equals the current forward swap rate.

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Cf. Hull p.659f (8th ed) and p.684f (9th ed). Definition (Swaption). A payer (receiver) swaption or swap option gives the holder the right to enter into a payer (receiver) Interest Rate Swap in the future.
(b) Cf. Hull p. 660 (8th ed), p. 685 (9th ed). If the fixed rate coupon on the bond is equal to the swap rate the principal is the par value of the bond (barring credit risks, liquidity considerations etc.). The receiver swaption can be seen as an option to receive this bond by delivery of the principal (which we, somewhat idealized, assume will be equal to the continuing value of the variable leg of the swap). We can note that both swaption and bond option will increase in value with lower longer rates.
(c) Consider the payments from \(T_1\) to \(T_2\) in the following two cases that depend on the realized level of the swap rate \(s_{T_1}\) at \(T_1\) with maturity at \(T_2\):
Case \(s_K < s_{T_1}\): PS pays \(s_K\) and receives LIBOR; RFS receives \(s_K\) and pays LIBOR; RS has payment 0.
Case \(s_K \geq s_{T_1}\): PS has payment 0; RFS receives \(s_K\) and pays LIBOR; RS receives \(s_K\) and pays LIBOR.
From the table it is seen that the payments for the payer swaption and the forward starting swap equals the payments on the receiver swaption. Thus barring arbitrage the equation must hold. If \(s_K\) is the current forward swap rate the value \(RFS\) is by definition zero. (Hull 9th ed., practice question 29.18).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Interest Rate Derivatives   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Payer and receiver swaption definitions: A payer swaption gives the right to pay fixed \(s_K\) and receive LIBOR from \(T_1\) to \(T_2\); it is valuable when the market swap rate \(s_{T_1}\) exceeds \(s_K\). A receiver swaption gives the right to receive fixed \(s_K\) and pay LIBOR; it is valuable when \(s_{T_1}\) is below \(s_K\).

(b) Receiver swaption as a call on a bond: Consider a fixed-rate coupon bond with coupon equal to the swap rate \(s_K\), principal equal to the notional, and maturity \(T_2\). Receiving fixed \(s_K\) and paying LIBOR from \(T_1\) to \(T_2\) is the same as owning this bond financed at par by floating-rate debt. The receiver swaption is exercised exactly when the bond's value at \(T_1\) exceeds par, i.e. when \(s_{T_1}<s_K\). Therefore, \[\boxed{\text{receiver swaption} = \text{call option on the bond, struck at par,}}\] with coupon \(s_K\) and principal equal to the notional.

(c)(i) Swaption parity — showing \(PS+RFS=RS\): Take a long receiver swaption together with a short payer swaption. At \(T_1\), exactly one of the two is exercised, and the combined position always ends up receiving fixed \(s_K\) and paying LIBOR from \(T_1\) to \(T_2\) with certainty — the same payoff as the forward-starting swap \(RFS\). By no-arbitrage, \[RS - PS = RFS,\] so \[\boxed{PS + RFS = RS,}\] where \(RFS = \sum_k P(t,t_{k+1})\tau_k(s_K-f_k)\) and \(f_k\) are the current forward rates.

(c)(ii) Deducing \(PS=RS\) at the forward swap rate: The forward swap rate \(s^*\) is the value of \(s_K\) that makes the forward-starting swap worth zero, \[RFS_{s^*} = \sum_k P(t,t_{k+1})\tau_k(s^*-f_k) = 0.\] Substituting \(s_K=s^*\) into the parity relation, \[PS + \underbrace{RFS_{s^*}}_{=\,0} = RS.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{PS = RS.}\] When the strike equals the forward swap rate, payer and receiver swaptions are equally valued — the interest-rate analogue of put-call parity struck at the forward.

August 2021 — Re-Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

Question 1

(a) Define a forward contract on a stock.
(b) Consider a stock that does not pay dividends with price \(S_t\) at time \(t\). Assume a constant continuously compounded interest rate of \(r\). Using an arbitrage argument find the forward price \(F_t\) at time \(t\) on a forward contract on the stock that matures at time \(T > t\).
(c) Consider the value of a forward contract with forward price \(K\) at time \(t < T\) and assume that the stock price follows the geometric Brownian motion \[dS_t = \mu S_t\,dt + \sigma S_t\,dz\] Using Itô's lemma find the process followed by the value of the forward contract. What will the drift be under the risk neutral measure?
(d) Assume now that the stock pays a constant continuous dividend rate of \(\delta\). What will the forward price be?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Definition (Forward Contract). A Forward Contract is an agreement to buy or sell a quantity of an asset of a specified quality and type (called the underlying asset) delivered at a specified future time \(T\) (the expiration date) and at a specified place for an agreed delivery price \(K\) as measured in some defined numeraire/currency. The price is initially (at \(t\)) set at a level \(K = F(t,T)\), such that the value \(V_K(t,T)\) of the contract is 0, i.e. by definition \(V_{F(t,T)}(t,T) = 0\). The price \(F(t,T)\) is called the forward price at \(t\) for delivery at \(T\).
(b) The following arbitrage argument can be made: Buy the stock spot financed by a loan of \(S_t\) repaid at \(T\). The only cashflow on this will be the repayment \(S_t e^{r(T-t)}\) at \(T\), leaving you with the stock. An alternative is to enter into a forward contract with delivery at \(T\). To eliminate arbitrage it must be that the forward price at \(t\) for delivery at \(T\) is \(F_t = S_t e^{r(T-t)}\).
(c) For a forward contract with forward price \(K\) the value is \(V_t = S_t - Ke^{-r(T-t)}\). Using Itô's lemma you get that the differential form for the value process for the forward: \[dV_t = \bigl[\mu S_t - rKe^{-r(T-t)}\bigr]dt + \sigma S_t\,dz\] For a currently priced forward (i.e. where \(K\) gives the forward contract a zero net present value \(K = F_t = e^{r(T-t)}S_t\)) we see that the drift is 0 under the risk neutral measure (by setting \(\mu = r\)), while a non-negative market value will grow at a rate \(r\). It will be considered ok just to derive this for the currently priced forward.
(d) In the arbitrage argument above you get too much if you buy the stock paying dividend, but you can repay and reduce the interest payments continuously leaving only \(S_t e^{(r-\delta)(T-t)}\) to repay at \(T\). Alternatively consider the price of a synthetic stock paying no dividends and compare that to \(S_t\) and go on from there. In either case \(F_t = S_t e^{(r-\delta)(T-t)}\).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Forward Pricing   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Definition of a forward contract: A forward contract is an agreement to buy (long) or sell (short) a specified asset at a specified future date \(T\) for a delivery price \(K\) agreed at inception. The forward price \(F(t,T)\) is set so that the contract has zero value at inception, \[\boxed{V_{F(t,T)}(t,T) = 0.}\]

(b) Forward price by arbitrage, no dividends: Compare two strategies to own one stock at \(T\). A) Enter the forward at \(F_t\), paying \(F_t\) at \(T\) to receive the stock. B) Buy the stock now for \(S_t\), financed by borrowing at rate \(r\); at \(T\) you own the stock and repay \(S_t e^{r(T-t)}\). Both deliver one stock at \(T\), so by no-arbitrage, \[\boxed{F_t = S_t\,e^{r(T-t)}.}\]

(c) Process for the forward value \(V_K(t,T)\) under Itô: The value of a forward with delivery price \(K\) is \[V_K(t,T) = e^{-r(T-t)}E^Q[S_T-K].\] Distributing the expectation, \[V_K(t,T) = e^{-r(T-t)}E^Q[S_T] - e^{-r(T-t)}K.\] Since \(E^Q[S_T]=F_t=S_te^{r(T-t)}\) (no dividends), substituting, \[V_K(t,T) = e^{-r(T-t)}S_te^{r(T-t)} - Ke^{-r(T-t)}.\] Simplifying, \[V_K(t,T) = S_t - Ke^{-r(T-t)}.\] Its partial derivatives are \(\partial V/\partial S=1\), \(\partial^2V/\partial S^2=0\), \(\partial V/\partial t=-rKe^{-r(T-t)}\). By Itô's lemma, \[dV = \frac{\partial V}{\partial t}\,dt + \frac{\partial V}{\partial S}\,dS_t + \tfrac12\frac{\partial^2V}{\partial S^2}\sigma^2S_t^2\,dt.\] Substituting the partial derivatives, \[dV = -rKe^{-r(T-t)}\,dt + dS_t.\] Substituting \(dS_t=\mu S_t\,dt+\sigma S_t\,dz\), \[dV = -rKe^{-r(T-t)}\,dt + \mu S_t\,dt + \sigma S_t\,dz.\] Under the risk-neutral measure \(\mathbb{Q}\), replace \(\mu\to r\): \[dV = -rKe^{-r(T-t)}\,dt + rS_t\,dt + \sigma S_t\,dz.\] Collecting the \(dt\) terms and factoring out \(r\), \[dV = r\bigl(S_t-Ke^{-r(T-t)}\bigr)dt + \sigma S_t\,dz.\] Since \(S_t-Ke^{-r(T-t)}=V_K\) (from above), \[dV = rV_K\,dt + \sigma S_t\,dz.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{dV_K = rV_K\,dt + \sigma S_t\,dz.}\] The drift under \(\mathbb{Q}\) is \(rV_K\), so the forward value earns the risk-free rate, while its diffusion \(\sigma S_t\,dz\) carries the same absolute risk as the stock.

(d) Forward price with a continuous dividend \(\delta\): Buy \(e^{-\delta(T-t)}\) shares now and continuously reinvest the dividends, growing the holding to exactly one share by \(T\); this costs \(S_te^{-\delta(T-t)}\) now, financed by borrowing at \(r\), so the debt at \(T\) is \(S_te^{-\delta(T-t)}e^{r(T-t)}\). By no-arbitrage, this equals the forward price: \[\boxed{F_t = S_t\,e^{(r-\delta)(T-t)}.}\]

Question 2

Assume that a stock with price \(S_t\) at time \(t\) pays no dividends before time \(T > t\), and that there is a constant continuously compounded risk free interest rate of \(r\). Let \(c(S_0,K,T,r)\) and \(p(S_0,K,T,r)\) be the price at time \(t=0\) of a European call and a European put, respectively, on the stock with the same strike \(K\) and expiry \(T\).
(a) Use the put-call-parity to find a relationship between the Thetas (\(\Theta\)) of the call and put. Comment on the result.
(b) Consider two call options with the same strike \(K\) but different expiry dates \(T_1\) and \(T_2\). Assume \(r \geq 0\) and use an arbitrage argument to show that the price of the longer call, \(c_2\), is (weakly) greater than the price of the shorter call, \(c_1\).

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) The put-call parity for options with same maturity and same strike price on the same stock paying no dividend states that the price of long call less the short put must be equal to the forward price on the stock with the same delivery prices as the strike and the same maturity, i.e. \[c(S_0,K,T,r) - p(S_0,K,T,r) = S_0 - e^{-rT}K\] The Theta is the partial derivative with respect to calendar time, i.e. the negative partial derivative with respect to maturity \(T\). By taking the partial derivative we find: \[\Theta_c - \Theta_p = -re^{-rT}K\] This depends on the sign of \(r\). E.g. with a positive rate there is from discounting an implicit advantage for the call option of the later payment of the strike compared to the current date (and a similar disadvantage for the put of the strike received later). The negative sign on the right-hand side reflects that this relative advantage of the call between the two otherwise similar options becomes smaller as the maturity date gets closer. Hull (9ed, p.409) reports this for the special case of the BSM-model.
(b) One may first use the same argument as when showing that an early exercise of an American call under these assumptions is non-optimal. Consider a portfolio long the long call, long \(K\) zero coupon bonds maturing at \(T_2\) and short one stock. At time \(T_1\) this portfolio has a value of \[c_2(T_1) + e^{-r(T_2-T_1)}K - S_{T_1}\] while at \(T_2\) it will have a value of \[c_2(T_2) + K - S_{T_2} = \max[S_{T_2}-K;0] + K - S_{T_2} \geq 0\] So if this has a non-negative value at \(T_2\) the value found at \(T_1\) must also be non-negative, i.e. \[c_2(T_1) \geq S_{T_1} - e^{-r(T_2-T_1)}K\] Now we consider a portfolio of a long position in the long call and a short position in the short call. At time \(T_1\) first consider the case of \(S_{T_1} > K\). Then \[c_2(T_1) - c_1(T_1) \geq (S_{T_1} - e^{-r(T_2-T_1)}K) - (S_{T_1}-K) > 0\] Finally consider the case of \(S_{T_1} \leq K\). Then \(c_2(T_1) - c_1(T_1) \geq c_2(T_1) - 0 \geq 0\). We have just shown that for a European call the theta is non-positive (when rates are non-negative).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: BSM Model  ·  Put-Call Parity   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Theta relationship from put-call parity: With no dividends, put-call parity states \[c - p = S_0 - Ke^{-r(T-t)}.\] Differentiating both sides with respect to calendar time \(t\) (\(\Theta = \partial V/\partial t\)) gives \[\Theta_c - \Theta_p = \frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bigl(S_0-Ke^{-r(T-t)}\bigr) = -rKe^{-r(T-t)}.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{\Theta_c - \Theta_p = -rKe^{-r(T-t)}.}\] With \(r>0\) this makes \(\Theta_c<\Theta_p\): the call loses value more slowly than the put over time, because holding the call delays paying the strike \(K\), and the interest earned on that deferral partially offsets its time decay.

(b) Showing \(c_2\geq c_1\) when \(T_2>T_1\): At the earlier expiry \(T_1\), the holder of the longer option \(c_2\) still holds an option with remaining life \(T_2-T_1>0\), whereas the holder of \(c_1\) receives at most \(\max(S_{T_1}-K,0)\). Suppose instead \(c_2<c_1\); then at \(t=0\) buy \(c_2\) and sell \(c_1\), pocketing \(c_1-c_2>0\). If \(c_1\) is exercised against you at \(T_1\), you must pay \(S_{T_1}-K\), but \(c_2\) is worth at least \(\max(S_{T_1}-K,0)\), so it can always cover this obligation. This leaves no cost at \(T_1\) and a strictly positive profit at \(t=0\) — an arbitrage. Therefore, \[\boxed{c_2 \geq c_1 \text{ whenever } T_2 > T_1,\ r\geq 0.}\] A longer maturity carries more time value, so the price is weakly higher.

Question 3

In the Cox-Ingersoll-Ross (CIR) Model the (instantaneous) short term interest rate \(r\) is described by the process: \[dr = a(b-r)\,dt + \sigma\sqrt{r}\,dz\] where \(a\), \(b\) and \(\sigma\) are constants (\(\sigma^2 < 2ab\)), and \(dz\) a Brownian increment.
(a) What does this model mean for the behaviour of the short term interest rate?
(b) In this model the solution for the price of a zero-coupon bond can be written \[P(t,T) = A(t,T)e^{-B(t,T)r(t)}\] Derive the duration of the bond.
(c) In some models of the short term interest rate \(r\) (e.g. the Hull-White model) the drift rate is made a function of calendar time \(t\). What is the purpose of the extra flexibility compared to the CIR (or Vasicek) type of models?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) It should be noted that the model implies a mean reversion of the spot rate to a level of \(b\) at a rate of \(a\). It should also be explained that the volatility term means that the short term rate cannot go negative. (Hull 9th ed, p.710).
(b) The answer depends on the definition of duration applied. The version in Hull (9th ed., p.712) is \[D = -\frac{\partial P/\partial r}{P} = B(t,T)\]
(c) The time-dependent drift term in the Hull-White model is introduced to be able to incorporate a given, initial term structure, making the values derived from it “arbitrage-free” in relation to the existing securities priced on the current term structure (assuming these to be arbitrage free). This is in contrast to the CIR and Vasicek models of the “equilibrium” or “endogenous”-type that put restrictions on the possible initial term structure (as it is a function of the current spot rate with only few parameters), (Hull 9th ed., section 31.2–31.3).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Short Rate Models   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) CIR model — behaviour of the short rate: The model \(dr = a(b-r)\,dt+\sigma\sqrt{r}\,dz\) mean-reverts to level \(b\) at speed \(a\), just like Vasicek. The key difference is that volatility \(\sigma\sqrt{r}\) scales with \(\sqrt{r}\): as \(r\to 0\) the volatility vanishes while the drift \(ab>0\) keeps pushing the rate back up, so the process cannot cross zero. Under the condition \[\boxed{\sigma^2 < 2ab}\] (the Feller condition), \(r\) stays strictly positive at all times.

(b) Duration of a CIR bond: With bond price \(P(t,T)=A(t,T)e^{-B(t,T)r(t)}\), differentiating with respect to \(r\) gives \[\frac{\partial P}{\partial r} = -B(t,T)\,A(t,T)e^{-B(t,T)r} = -B(t,T)P.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{D = -\frac{1}{P}\frac{\partial P}{\partial r} = B(t,T).}\]

(c) Hull-White extension of CIR: Replacing the constant \(ab\) with a time-dependent \(\theta(t)\) gives the model enough flexibility to fit the initial term structure exactly. Therefore, \[\boxed{\theta(t)\text{ makes the model arbitrage-free against today's bond prices, unlike the equilibrium CIR/Vasicek models.}}\]

June 2020 — Ordinary Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

Question 1

Consider a stock that does not pay dividends during the period under analysis. Let the price be \(S_t\) at time \(t\). At time 0 the stock price is 200, and during the next year the price can go up by 10% or down by 15%. The one year risk free interest rate is 0.
(a) Find the arbitrage free price \(p_0\) at time 0 of a put option with strike 200 and expiry after one year by constructing a risk free portfolio of the stock and the option.
(b) What is the risk neutral probability \(q\) of the up-move in the stock price?
(c) Suppose that the real world probability \(p\) of an up-move is 4/5. Why would you expect \(p > q\)? What is the expected return on the put option? Explain the result.

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

The answer can be found using the binomial model:
Stock goes up 10%: \(S_u = 220\), put payoff = 0
Stock goes down 15%: \(S_d = 170\), put payoff = 30
(a) Consider a portfolio of 1 put option and long \(\Delta\) stocks. The payoffs at time 1 will be \(220\Delta\) and \(170\Delta + 30\). This is risk free if \(\Delta\) is chosen to 3/5 and will give a payoff of 132 at time 1. That amount can be borrowed at time 0 giving a cash-flow of 132. Deduct the price of the delta position in the stock of 120 and you have 12 left. This must be the price of the put if there is to be no arbitrage opportunities.
(b) The risk neutral probability \(q\) of the up-move can be found by \(200 = 220q + 170(1-q)\). Thus \(q = 3/5\). That provides another way to calculate the arbitrage free put price, since the discounted expected payoff is \(30 \times (1 - 3/5) = 12\).
(c) The scenario may be rationalised by the following: The real world probability of a state that is “good”, most likely with high stock prices, is higher than the risk neutral probability, since the risk neutral probability will correct for a risk premium penalizing payoffs in “good” states and benefiting payoffs in “bad” states. The expected return of the put option under the real world probability is negative \(((30/5 - 12)/12 = -50\%)\) and lower than the risk free rate (of 0%) since the put can serve as an insurance against the “bad” down-state. Note that the real world expected return of the stock is \((220 \times 4/5 + 170 \times 1/5)/200 - 1 = 5\%\), much higher than the risk free rate, thus indicating a high risk premium.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Binomial Model   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

The setup: \(S_0=200\), up-state \(S_u=220\), down-state \(S_d=170\), strike \(K=200\), \(r=0\). The put payoffs are \(P_u=\max(200-220,0)=0\) and \(P_d=\max(200-170,0)=30\).

(a) Price by a risk-free portfolio: Hold \(\Delta\) stocks together with one long put, and choose \(\Delta\) so the portfolio's payoff is the same in both states, \[220\Delta + 0 = 170\Delta + 30 \implies 50\Delta = 30 \implies \Delta = 3/5.\] The riskless payoff at \(T\) is then \(220\times(3/5)=132\). Since \(r=0\), no-arbitrage requires this to equal the cost of the portfolio today, \[\Delta S_0 + p_0 = 132 \implies (3/5)(200) + p_0 = 132 \implies 120 + p_0 = 132.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{p_0 = 12.}\]

(b) Risk-neutral probability: Under \(Q\), the stock must earn the risk-free rate \(r=0\) in expectation, \[200 = 220q + 170(1-q) = 170 + 50q \implies 50q = 30.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{q = 3/5.}\] As a check, the put price under this measure is \(p_0=e^{-rT}[qP_u+(1-q)P_d]=(3/5)(0)+(2/5)(30)=12\), matching part (a).

(c) Real-world vs. risk-neutral probabilities: The risk-neutral probability of the up-move is \(q=3/5\). The real-world probability \(p\) is expected to be higher than \(q\), since investors demand a positive risk premium on the stock, which the risk-neutral measure corrects for by penalising "good" (high-price) states and favouring "bad" (low-price) states. If \(p>q=3/5\), the real-world expected put payoff is \(p\cdot0+(1-p)\cdot30<12\), so the put's expected return is negative, \[\boxed{\text{expected return of the put} = \frac{(1-p)\cdot30-12}{12} < 0,}\] below the risk-free rate of 0%. This makes sense: the put is insurance against the bad state, so it carries a negative risk premium.

Question 2

In a tranched CDS (also a synthetic CDO) compare the most senior, the mezzanine and the most junior (also called “equity”) tranches.
(a) Explain how the tranches are exposed to the risk of defaults from the total underlying portfolio. Explain what is meant by “attachment point” and “detachment point”.
(b) Assume a large set of obligors (or “names”) in the underlying portfolio. If correlation is not perfect which tranche will gain proportionally the most by a lower credit spread on the underlying portfolio?
(c) For a given credit spread on the underlying portfolio of CDS which tranche will benefit from an increase in implied (i.e. derived from market prices) correlation?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) The equity tranche can be seen as a leveraged exposure to the total underlying portfolio, but protected by limited liability. The more senior tranches are exposed to the risk on the total underlying portfolio only after more junior tranches has covered losses. The attachment and detachment points of a given tranche denotes the proportions of losses on the underlying portfolio that is covered by the given tranche, e.g. the equity tranche has an attachment point of 0 and a detachment point of e.g. 3%, whereas the most senior tranche has an attachment point of e.g. 22% and a detachment point of 100%.
(b) In general the spread on the junior tranche is higher than on the senior tranche as it has a higher expected loss from defaults in the underlying and a higher risk (the effect of leverage). The price of the most junior tranche will benefit the most (unless continuing to be worthless) from a fall in credit spreads.
(c) An increase in implied correlation (for a given spread) will benefit the equity tranche and hurt the most senior tranche. To see this compare the extremes where defaults are perfectly correlated to where they are independent: First note that the underlying portfolio and thus the sum of tranche values stay fixed (as the spread is the same). If defaults are perfectly correlated the (risk neutral) expected loss reflected in the given spread will take the form of no losses or of losses that will hit the equity tranche but likely also spill into the more senior tranches. If defaults are independent through the diversification (of the large set of underlying obligors) the losses will be close to the expected loss reflected in the given spread, hitting the equity tranche and maybe also spill into more senior tranches. So the price of the equity tranche will increase when the implied correlation increases. The price of the most senior tranche will fall (or stay the same if the subordination is so large that it will not suffer losses in the perfect correlation case). The change of the price of mezzanine trances cannot be established without further assumptions.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Credit Risk   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) CDO tranches — exposure, attachment and detachment points: Portfolio losses are absorbed bottom-up: the equity tranche (0 to \(A\%\)) takes the first losses and carries the highest risk and spread, the mezzanine tranche (\(A\%\) to \(D\%\)) absorbs intermediate losses, and the senior tranche (\(D\%\) to 100%) is hit last and carries the lowest risk and spread. The attachment point is the portfolio loss percentage at which a tranche starts absorbing losses, and the detachment point is the loss percentage at which it is fully wiped out: \[\boxed{\text{equity: 0 to }A\%,\qquad \text{senior: }D\%\text{ to }100\%.}\]

(b) Which tranche gains most from a lower credit spread: The equity tranche absorbs the first losses, so it has the highest expected loss and the highest spread of all tranches — effectively a leveraged bet on the portfolio's credit quality. A fall in the underlying credit spread lowers expected losses across the board, but the proportional effect is largest where leverage is highest. Therefore, \[\boxed{\text{the most junior (equity) tranche benefits proportionally the most from a fall in credit spreads.}}\]

(c) Which tranche benefits from higher implied correlation, spread fixed: Holding the average spread fixed keeps total expected portfolio loss — and hence total tranche value — unchanged; only its distribution across tranches shifts with correlation. At low correlation, defaults are diversified and losses stay close to their expected value, almost always absorbed by equity alone, leaving the senior tranche nearly risk-free. At high correlation, defaults cluster: equity is sometimes untouched but other times wiped out, with losses spilling into senior, which now bears risk it didn't have before. Therefore, \[\boxed{\uparrow\rho:\ \text{equity tranche value}\uparrow,\ \text{senior tranche value}\downarrow,\ \text{mezzanine ambiguous.}}\]

Question 3

Consider an economy with a (continuously compounded) risk free interest rate of \(r\) and a stock with price \(S_t\) at time \(t\). Suppose that the stock pays a deterministic continuous dividend rate of \(\delta\).
(a) What is the forward price at time \(t\) on the stock for maturity \(T > t\)?
(b) Suppose that a forward contract on the stock with forward price \(K\) exists. What will the value \(V(S_t, t; K)\) of that contract be at time \(t\)?
(c) The Black-Scholes-Merton partial differential equation for the price \(V\) of some general derivative is \[\frac{\partial V}{\partial t} + \frac{1}{2}\sigma^2 S^2\frac{\partial^2 V}{\partial S^2} + (r-\delta)S\frac{\partial V}{\partial S} - rV = 0\] Show that the value of the forward contract satisfies this equation.

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) The value can be found taking the risk neutral expectation of the payoff at \(T\) from a strategy where dividends are continuously reinvested in the stock and discounting with the risk free rate: To achieve a value of \(S_T\) at \(T\) invest \(S_t e^{-\delta(T-t)}\) in the stock at time \(t\) and reinvest dividends (the value will grow proportional to \(dS_t + \delta\,dt\)). The forward price will be a value \(F_t\) that satisfies: \[0 = e^{-r(T-t)}E^{\mathbb{Q}}_t[S_T - F_t]\] \[e^{-r(T-t)}F_t = e^{-r(T-t)}E^{\mathbb{Q}}_t[S_T] = S_t e^{-\delta(T-t)}\] \[F_t = S_t e^{(r-\delta)(T-t)}\]
(b) The value of a forward contract with a given forward price \(K\) will be \[V(S_t,t;K) = e^{-r(T-t)}E^{\mathbb{Q}}_t[S_T - K] = S_t e^{-\delta(T-t)} - Ke^{-r(T-t)}\]
(c) Taking partials it can be shown that this satisfies the BSM PDE.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Forward Pricing  ·  BSM Model   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Forward price with a continuous dividend \(\delta\): Buy \(e^{-\delta(T-t)}\) shares at \(t\) and reinvest dividends continuously, growing the holding to exactly one share by \(T\); this costs \(S_te^{-\delta(T-t)}\) at \(t\). Financing this by borrowing, the debt at \(T\) is \(S_te^{-\delta(T-t)}e^{r(T-t)}\), which by no-arbitrage equals the forward price: \[\boxed{F(t,T) = S_t\,e^{(r-\delta)(T-t)}.}\]

(b) Value of a forward contract with price \(K\): The value is the present value of receiving \(S_T\) and paying \(K\), \[V = e^{-r(T-t)}E^Q[S_T-K] = e^{-r(T-t)}(F_t-K).\] Substituting \(F_t=S_te^{(r-\delta)(T-t)}\) gives \[\boxed{V(S_t,t;K) = S_t e^{-\delta(T-t)} - Ke^{-r(T-t)}.}\]

(c) Showing the forward value satisfies the BSM PDE with dividends: Delta-hedge by shorting one derivative \(V\) and holding \(\Delta=V_S\) shares. Since the shares pay a continuous dividend \(\delta\), holding \(\Delta\) shares earns dividend income \(\delta S\Delta\,dt\), so the hedged portfolio \(\Pi=-V+V_S S\) evolves as \[d\Pi = -dV + V_S\,dS + V_S\,\delta S\,dt.\] Applying Itô's lemma to \(V(S,t)\) with the general formula \[dV = \frac{\partial V}{\partial t}\,dt + \frac{\partial V}{\partial S}\,dS + \tfrac12\frac{\partial^2V}{\partial S^2}(dS)^2,\] and substituting \(dS=\mu S\,dt+\sigma S\,dz\) so that \((dS)^2=\sigma^2S^2\,dt\), with \(\Theta=\partial V/\partial t\), \(V_S=\partial V/\partial S\), \(V_{SS}=\partial^2V/\partial S^2\), \[dV = \Theta\,dt + V_S(\mu S\,dt+\sigma S\,dz) + \tfrac12V_{SS}\sigma^2S^2\,dt.\] Distributing \(V_S\) and collecting the \(dt\) terms, \[dV = \bigl(\Theta+\mu SV_S+\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS}\bigr)dt + \sigma SV_S\,dz.\] Substituting this \(dV\) and \(dS=\mu S\,dt+\sigma S\,dz\) into the expression for \(d\Pi\), \[d\Pi = -\Bigl[\bigl(\Theta+\mu SV_S+\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS}\bigr)dt+\sigma SV_S\,dz\Bigr] + V_S\bigl(\mu S\,dt+\sigma S\,dz\bigr) + V_S\delta S\,dt.\] Distributing the minus sign and the \(V_S\) factor, \[d\Pi = -\Theta\,dt-\mu SV_S\,dt-\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS}\,dt-\sigma SV_S\,dz + \mu SV_S\,dt+\sigma SV_S\,dz + V_S\delta S\,dt.\] The \(\sigma SV_S\,dz\) terms cancel (confirming the hedge is riskless), and so do the \(\mu SV_S\,dt\) terms, leaving \[d\Pi = \Bigl(-\Theta-\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS}+V_S\delta S\Bigr)dt.\] Since the portfolio is riskless it must earn \(r\), i.e. \(d\Pi=r\Pi\,dt\): \[-\Theta-\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS}+V_S\delta S = r\Pi.\] Substituting \(\Pi=-V+V_SS\), \[-\Theta-\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS}+V_S\delta S = r(-V+V_SS).\] Distributing the \(r\) on the right, \[-\Theta-\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS}+V_S\delta S = -rV+rSV_S.\] Rearranging (moving all terms to one side and multiplying through by \(-1\)), \[\boxed{\Theta + (r-\delta)SV_S + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS} = rV.}\] This is exactly the ordinary BSM PDE with \((r-\delta)\) in place of \(r\) in the \(SV_S\) term, reflecting that the stock's risk-neutral drift is \(r-\delta\) — and the forward value derived above satisfies it.

August 2020 — Re-Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 4 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted · Question 1 weighted 40%; Questions 2–4 weighted 20% each

Question 1

Consider an economy where the value of a bank account \(B_t\) at time \(t\) and the price of a non-dividend paying stock \(S_t\) at time \(t\) are described by the Itô-processes: \[dB_t = rB_t\,dt,\quad B_0 = 1\] \[dS_t = \mu S_t\,dt + \sigma S_t\,dz,\quad S_0 > 0\] where \(\mu\) is constant, \(\sigma\) is a positive constant, \(r\) is a constant risk free rate, and \(dz\) is the standard short hand notation for a Brownian increment.
(a) Why can we use \(S_t\) as a numeraire?
(b) Consider the process \(B_t/S_t\) and express this as an Itô process.
(c) What \(\mu\) will make this a martingale?
(d) What will the drift rate of \(\ln S_t\) be under this numeraire?
(e) A derivative pays \(S_T G(S_T)\) at time \(T > t\), where \(G\) is some function. With \(E^S\) denoting the expectation under the probability measure corresponding to \(S_t\) as a numeraire find an expression for the price \(f_t\) of this derivative at time \(t\).

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) \(S_t\) can be used as a numeraire because it is the price of a traded asset with a positive price.
(b) The process can be found (directly using \(d(X/Y) = (X/Y)(dX/X - dY/Y - dX\,dY/X\,Y + (dY/Y)^2)\) or as here following section 27.3 of Hull (8th ed)) by first considering the process \(d\ln B_t - d\ln S_t\). By Itô's lemma this is \[(r - \mu + \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2)dt + \sigma\,dz\] Again using Itô's lemma (on the transformation exp) you get \[d\frac{B_t}{S_t} = (r - \mu + \sigma^2)\frac{B_t}{S_t}\,dt + \sigma\frac{B_t}{S_t}\,dz\]
(c) This is a martingale if \(\mu = r + \sigma^2\).
(d) You already found the Itô-process \(d\ln S_t\). With this value of \(\mu\) the drift is \(r + \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2\) (whereas it is \(r - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2\) under the traditional risk neutral measure \(\mathbb{Q}\) where \(B_t\) is numeraire).
(e) The value \(f_t\) can be found using the fact that \(f_t/S_t\) is a martingale under this measure, thus \[f_t/S_t = E^S_t[f_T/S_T]\] and \[f_t = S_t E^S_t[S_T G(S_T)/S_T] = S_t E^S_t[G(S_T)]\]

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Numeraire & Change of Measure   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Why \(S_t\) can be used as a numeraire: \(S_t\) is the price of a traded asset with a strictly positive price, since \(S_0>0\) and GBM keeps \(S_t>0\) almost surely. Any traded asset with a strictly positive price can serve as a numeraire.

(b) Process for \(B_t/S_t\) via Itô's lemma: First compute \(d\ln(B_t/S_t) = d\ln B_t - d\ln S_t\). Since \(dB_t=rB_t\,dt\) has no stochastic term, \(d\ln B_t = r\,dt\). For \(\ln S_t\), Itô's lemma with \(\partial(\ln S)/\partial S=1/S\), \(\partial^2(\ln S)/\partial S^2=-1/S^2\), \(\partial(\ln S)/\partial t=0\) gives \[d\ln S_t = \frac1{S_t}\,dS_t + \tfrac12\left(-\frac1{S_t^2}\right)\sigma^2S_t^2\,dt.\] Substituting \(dS_t=\mu S_t\,dt+\sigma S_t\,dz\), \[d\ln S_t = \frac1{S_t}\bigl(\mu S_t\,dt+\sigma S_t\,dz\bigr) - \tfrac12\sigma^2\,dt.\] Distributing the \(1/S_t\) factor and collecting the \(dt\) terms, \[d\ln S_t = \bigl(\mu-\tfrac12\sigma^2\bigr)dt+\sigma\,dz.\] Subtracting this from \(d\ln B_t=r\,dt\), \[d\ln\frac{B_t}{S_t} = r\,dt - \Bigl[\bigl(\mu-\tfrac12\sigma^2\bigr)dt+\sigma\,dz\Bigr].\] Distributing the minus sign, \[d\ln\frac{B_t}{S_t} = \bigl(r-\mu+\tfrac12\sigma^2\bigr)dt - \sigma\,dz.\] Write \(X=\ln(B_t/S_t)\), so \(dX=a\,dt+b\,dz\) with \(a=r-\mu+\tfrac12\sigma^2\), \(b=-\sigma\). Since \(B_t/S_t=e^X\), Itô's lemma applied to \(f(X)=e^X\) uses \(\partial f/\partial X=e^X=f\), \(\partial^2f/\partial X^2=e^X=f\): \[d(e^X) = \frac{\partial f}{\partial X}\,dX + \tfrac12\frac{\partial^2f}{\partial X^2}(dX)^2.\] Substituting the partials and \((dX)^2=b^2\,dt\), \[d\frac{B_t}{S_t} = \frac{B_t}{S_t}\,dX + \tfrac12\frac{B_t}{S_t}\,b^2\,dt.\] Substituting \(dX=a\,dt+b\,dz\), \[d\frac{B_t}{S_t} = \frac{B_t}{S_t}\bigl(a\,dt+b\,dz\bigr) + \tfrac12\frac{B_t}{S_t}b^2\,dt.\] Distributing and collecting the \(dt\) terms, \[d\frac{B_t}{S_t} = \frac{B_t}{S_t}\Bigl[(a+\tfrac12b^2)dt+b\,dz\Bigr].\] Since \(a+\tfrac12b^2=\bigl(r-\mu+\tfrac12\sigma^2\bigr)+\tfrac12\sigma^2=r-\mu+\sigma^2\), this gives \[\boxed{d\frac{B_t}{S_t} = (r-\mu+\sigma^2)\frac{B_t}{S_t}\,dt - \sigma\frac{B_t}{S_t}\,dz.}\]

(c) Martingale condition: \(B_t/S_t\) is a martingale exactly when its drift vanishes, \[r-\mu+\sigma^2 = 0.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{\mu = r+\sigma^2.}\]

(d) Drift of \(\ln S_t\) under this numeraire: Substituting \(\mu=r+\sigma^2\) into \(d\ln S_t=(\mu-\tfrac12\sigma^2)dt+\sigma\,dz\) gives \[d\ln S_t = \bigl(r+\sigma^2-\tfrac12\sigma^2\bigr)dt+\sigma\,dz.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{d\ln S_t = \bigl(r+\tfrac12\sigma^2\bigr)dt+\sigma\,dz.}\] Compared with the standard risk-neutral drift \(r-\tfrac12\sigma^2\), the drift under the \(S\)-numeraire measure is higher by \(\sigma^2\) — the Girsanov correction for changing from \(Q\) to the \(S\)-numeraire measure.

(e) Price of a derivative paying \(S_TG(S_T)\): Since \(S_t\) is a valid numeraire, the price of any traded derivative divided by \(S_t\) is a martingale under the corresponding measure \(\mathbb{Q}^S\), \[\frac{f_t}{S_t} = E^S_t\!\left[\frac{f_T}{S_T}\right].\] Substituting the payoff \(f_T=S_TG(S_T)\), \[\frac{f_t}{S_t} = E^S_t\!\left[\frac{S_TG(S_T)}{S_T}\right] = E^S_t[G(S_T)].\] Therefore, \[\boxed{f_t = S_t\,E^S_t[G(S_T)].}\] Using \(S_t\) as numeraire converts the awkward payoff \(S_TG(S_T)\) into a plain expectation of \(G(S_T)\), scaled by \(S_t\) — the same trick used to derive the \(S_0\Phi(d_1)\) term in the BSM formula.

Question 2

Let \(y(0,T)\) be the continuously compounded yields at time \(t=0\) on zero coupon bonds with maturities \(T > 0\), i.e. with prices \(P(0,T) = e^{-y(0,T)T}\).
(a) Find the instantaneous forward rate \(f(0,T)\) as contracted at 0 for maturity \(T\) (i.e. for a period \((T, T+dT)\)).
(b) Show that if \(y\) is increasing in \(T\), then \(f(0,T) > y(0,T)\) and interpret this result.

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Using the definition of the instantaneous forward rate you calculate \[f(0,T) = -\frac{\partial\ln P(0,T)}{\partial T} = -\frac{\partial(-y(0,T)T)}{\partial T} = T\frac{\partial y(0,T)}{\partial T} + y(0,T)\]
(b) The result follows from the assumption that \(\partial y/\partial T > 0\). An interpretation is that as long as the forward rate curve is above the yield curve it pulls the yield curve (that averages for the period from 0 to \(T\)) up at the margin.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: HJM Model   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Instantaneous forward rate from yields: With bond price \(P(0,T)=e^{-y(0,T)T}\), the forward rate is defined by \[f(0,T) = -\frac{\partial\ln P(0,T)}{\partial T}.\] Since \(\ln P(0,T)=-y(0,T)T\), \[f(0,T) = -\frac{\partial\bigl(-y(0,T)T\bigr)}{\partial T} = \frac{\partial\bigl(y(0,T)T\bigr)}{\partial T}.\] By the product rule, \[\frac{\partial\bigl(y(0,T)T\bigr)}{\partial T} = \frac{\partial y(0,T)}{\partial T}\cdot T + y(0,T)\cdot\frac{\partial T}{\partial T}.\] Since \(\partial T/\partial T=1\), \[f(0,T) = T\frac{\partial y(0,T)}{\partial T} + y(0,T).\] Therefore, \[\boxed{f(0,T) = y(0,T) + T\frac{\partial y}{\partial T}.}\]

(b) Showing \(f(0,T)>y(0,T)\) when the yield curve slopes up: From the result above, \(f-y=T\,\partial y/\partial T\). If the yield curve is upward sloping, \(\partial y/\partial T>0\), so \[\boxed{f(0,T) - y(0,T) = T\frac{\partial y}{\partial T} > 0.}\] The forward rate is the marginal rate at \(T\); when it exceeds the average yield over \([0,T]\), it pulls the yield curve up at the margin — the usual "averages sit below marginals when marginals are rising" argument.

Question 3

Consider a one-period coupon bond that promises to pay its principal (of 1) and the accrued interest from a (continuously compounded) coupon of \(c\) at time \(t=1\). Assume that there is a risk free (continuously compounded) interest rate of \(r\). Suppose that the bond will default with \(\mathbb{Q}\)-probability \(q\) with a known fractional recovery of \(R\), \(0 < R < 1\) (of principal and accrued interest). Use a one-period binomial model to find the coupon \(c\) that will give the defaultable bond a par price at time \(t=0\). What is the credit spread?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

Since the value of the coupon bond can be found by using the expectation under the risk neutral measure and discounting by the risk free interest rate, \(c\) must fulfil: \[1 = e^{-r}\bigl[qRe^c + (1-q)e^c\bigr]\] with the par value on the left hand side and the present value of the expected cash flow on the right (using risk free rate for discounting and the risk neutral probability for the expectation). Solving this for \(c\) yields \[c = r - \ln(1 - q(1-R))\] Thus the credit spread is \[c - r = -\ln(1 - q(1-R)) \approx q(1-R)\] where we for the approximation use that \(\ln(1-x) \approx -x\) for small \(x\). Thus the credit spread is approximately the expected loss under the \(\mathbb{Q}\)-probability.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Credit Risk   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

Credit bond pricing in a one-period binomial model: The bond promises to pay principal 1 plus accrued interest \(e^c-1\) at \(t=1\), defaulting with \(\mathbb{Q}\)-probability \(q\) and recovering a fraction \(R\) of principal and interest. The no-default payoff is \(e^c\) and the default payoff is \(Re^c\). By no-arbitrage, the bond's price today must equal par, \[1 = e^{-r}\bigl[q\cdot Re^c+(1-q)\cdot e^c\bigr].\] Factoring \(e^c\) out of the bracket, \[1 = e^{-r}e^c\bigl[qR + (1-q)\bigr].\] Simplifying the bracket, \(qR+1-q=1-q(1-R)\), so \[1 = e^{-r}e^c\bigl[1-q(1-R)\bigr].\] Multiplying both sides by \(e^r\), \[e^r = e^c\bigl[1-q(1-R)\bigr].\] Dividing both sides by the bracket and taking logs, \[c = r - \ln\bigl(1-q(1-R)\bigr).\] Therefore, \[\boxed{c = r - \ln\bigl(1-q(1-R)\bigr).}\] The credit spread is \[c-r = -\ln\bigl(1-q(1-R)\bigr) \approx q(1-R)\] for small \(q\), so \[\boxed{\text{credit spread} \approx \text{risk-neutral expected loss}.}\]

Question 4

Consider a European call option on a stock with price \(S_t\) at time \(t\). Assume that the stock pays no dividends before the expiry of the option.
(a) Define the delta \(\Delta\) of the call.
(b) For an at-the-money call (where the strike \(K\) of the option is equal to \(S_t\)) the delta is close to 1/2. Use the Black-Scholes-Merton call option formula to find the strike \(K\) where the delta is precisely 1/2. For your reference the call option formula can be written \[c = S_t\Phi(d_1) - Ke^{-r(T-t)}\Phi(d_2)\] where \[d_1 = \frac{\ln\frac{S_t}{K} + (r + \frac{1}{2}\sigma^2)(T-t)}{\sigma\sqrt{T-t}}, \quad d_2 = d_1 - \sigma\sqrt{T-t}\] where \(r\) is a constant continuously compounded interest rate, \(T\) is the expiry date of the option and \(\sigma\) the constant volatility. \(\Phi(x)\) denotes the standard normal distribution function in \(x\).
(c) Assume \(S_t = 1\) and find this strike for parameters \((T-t) = 0.25\), the volatility \(\sigma = 0.4\) and the risk free interest rate \(r = 0\).
(d) What is the delta of the corresponding put option?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) The delta is the partial derivative of the option price with respect to the price of the underlying stock.
(b) The delta for a call in the BSM-model is \(\Phi(d_1)\). For this to be 1/2 \(d_1\) must be 0. This is when \[\ln\frac{S_t}{K} + \left(r + \frac{1}{2}\sigma^2\right)(T-t) = 0\] or \[K = e^{(r + \frac{1}{2}\sigma^2)(T-t)}S_t\]
(c) For the numerical example you get \[K = e^{\frac{1}{2}(0.4)^2 \cdot 0.25} = e^{0.02} \approx 1.02\] i.e. 2% above the price of the stock.
(d) The delta of the put is the delta of the call minus 1 (which can be shown by the put-call-parity).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: BSM Model   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Delta of a call option: The delta is defined as \[\boxed{\Delta = \frac{\partial c}{\partial S_t}.}\] It measures how much the option price changes per unit change in the stock price, and is used to hedge option positions.

(b) Strike \(K\) where \(\Delta=1/2\): In the BSM model, the call delta is \(\Delta=\Phi(d_1)\), so \(\Delta=1/2\) requires \(d_1=0\), \[d_1 = \frac{\ln(S_t/K)+(r+\tfrac12\sigma^2)(T-t)}{\sigma\sqrt{T-t}} = 0.\] Multiplying both sides by \(\sigma\sqrt{T-t}\), \[\ln(S_t/K)+(r+\tfrac12\sigma^2)(T-t) = 0.\] Solving for \(\ln(S_t/K)\), \[\ln(S_t/K) = -(r+\tfrac12\sigma^2)(T-t).\] Exponentiating both sides, \[\frac{S_t}{K} = e^{-(r+\frac12\sigma^2)(T-t)}.\] Taking the reciprocal and multiplying by \(S_t\), \[\boxed{K = S_t\,e^{(r+\frac12\sigma^2)(T-t)}.}\] The option is thus slightly out-of-the-money (not exactly at-the-money) at \(\Delta=1/2\) whenever \(r>0\).

(c) Numerical example: With \(S_t=1\), \(\sigma=0.4\), \(T-t=0.25\), \(r=0\), \[K = S_t\,e^{(0+\frac12(0.4)^2)(0.25)} = e^{\frac12(0.16)(0.25)} = e^{0.02}.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{K \approx 1.02,}\] about 2% above the current stock price.

(d) Delta of the corresponding put: From put-call parity, \(c-p=S_t-Ke^{-r(T-t)}\); differentiating with respect to \(S_t\), \[\Delta_c - \Delta_p = 1.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{\Delta_p = \Delta_c-1 = \Phi(d_1)-1 = -\Phi(-d_1).}\] For the strike found above, \(\Delta_p = 1/2-1=-1/2\).

June 2019 — Ordinary Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

Question 1

In Vasicek's model, the risk-neutral process for the short rate \(r\) is \[dr = a(b-r)\,dt + \sigma\,dz\] where \(a, b, \sigma\) are constants. Suppose \(a > 0\).
(a) Describe the qualitative characteristics of this model of the short rate.
(b) Consider a zero-coupon bond that pays $1 at time \(T\). Vasicek shows that its price at time \(t\), denoted \(P(t,T)\), can be expressed \[P(t,T) = A(t,T)e^{-B(t,T)r(t)}\] using functions \(A\) and \(B\) that he finds (which we don't need in detail right here). Show that \[\frac{\partial P(t,T)}{\partial r(t)} = -B(t,T)P(t,T)\]
(c) Argue that the process for \(P(t,T)\) in the traditional risk-neutral world satisfies \[dP(t,T) = r(t)P(t,T)\,dt - \sigma B(t,T)P(t,T)\,dz(t)\]

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Cf. Hull p. 682–684. The model incorporates mean reversion, as the short rate is pulled to level \(b\) at rate \(a\). Page 684 explains why mean reversion is desirable for a model of the short rate. Superimposed upon this mean reversion is a normally distributed stochastic term \(\sigma\,dz\).
(b) The first expression appears on page 685. The derivative of \(P(t,T)\) with respect to \(r(t)\) becomes \[\frac{\partial P(t,T)}{\partial r(t)} = A(t,T)\bigl(-B(t,T)\bigr)e^{-B(t,T)r(t)} = -B(t,T)P(t,T)\] This is also (30.9).
(c) Cf. Hull p. 687. The expected growth rate of \(P(t,T)\) in the traditional risk-neutral world is \(r(t)\) because \(P(t,T)\) is the price of a traded security. The coefficient of \(dz(t)\) in the process for \(P(t,T)\) can be calculated from Itô's lemma as \(\sigma\,\partial P(t,T)/\partial r(t)\). The claimed process for \(P(t,T)\) follows from these considerations, using the answer from (b).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Short Rate Models   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Vasicek model — qualitative characteristics: The model is \[dr = a(b-r)\,dt + \sigma\,dz.\] When \(r>b\), the drift is negative, so \(r\) is pulled down; when \(r<b\), the drift is positive, so \(r\) is pulled up. The speed of this mean reversion is \(a\), and the long-run level is \(b\). The stochastic term has constant volatility \(\sigma\) and is normally distributed, so \(r\) can in principle go negative. \[\boxed{dr = a(b-r)\,dt + \sigma\,dz}\]

(b) Showing \(\partial P/\partial r = -B(t,T)P\): With the affine form \(P(t,T) = A(t,T)e^{-B(t,T)r(t)}\), differentiating with respect to \(r\) gives \[\frac{\partial P}{\partial r} = A(t,T)\cdot\bigl(-B(t,T)\bigr)\cdot e^{-B(t,T)r} = -B(t,T)P.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{\frac{\partial P(t,T)}{\partial r(t)} = -B(t,T)P(t,T).}\]

(c) Showing \(dP = r(t)P\,dt - \sigma B(t,T)P\,dz\) under \(\mathbb{Q}\): Apply Itô's lemma to \(P(r,t)\), using \(dr = a(b-r)\,dt + \sigma\,dz\) under \(\mathbb{Q}\): \[dP = \frac{\partial P}{\partial t}dt + \frac{\partial P}{\partial r}dr + \tfrac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^2 P}{\partial r^2}(dr)^2.\] Under \(\mathbb{Q}\), the bond must earn the risk-free rate \(r\), so the drift of \(dP\) is \(r\cdot P\). The diffusion term uses \(\partial P/\partial r = -B(t,T)P\) from above and \(\sigma\,dz\) from the short rate: \[\text{diffusion of }dP = \frac{\partial P}{\partial r}\cdot\sigma\,dz = -B(t,T)P\cdot\sigma\,dz.\] Combining, \[\boxed{dP(t,T) = r(t)P(t,T)\,dt - \sigma B(t,T)P(t,T)\,dz.}\] The bond earns the risk-free rate \(r\) in drift, with interest-rate risk proportional to \(B(t,T)\), its duration.

Question 2

Suppose \(f\) and \(g\) are the prices of two non-dividend paying securities with volatilities \(\sigma_f\) and \(\sigma_g\), depending on a single source of risk captured by the Wiener process \(z\). Suppose that the market price of risk is \(\sigma_g\). Let \(r\) denote the risk-free rate.
(a) Argue that \(f\) and \(g\) satisfy \[df = (r + \sigma_g\sigma_f)f\,dt + \sigma_f f\,dz \quad\text{and}\quad dg = \bigl(r + \sigma_g^2\bigr)g\,dt + \sigma_g g\,dz\]
(b) Apply Itô's lemma to find \(d\ln f\) and \(d\ln g\). Recall that \(\ln\tfrac{f}{g} = \ln f - \ln g\). Show that \[d\ln\frac{f}{g} = -\frac{(\sigma_f - \sigma_g)^2}{2}\,dt + (\sigma_f - \sigma_g)\,dz\]
(c) Using the equation for \(\ln\tfrac{f}{g}\) from (b), apply Itô's lemma to determine the equation for \(f/g\). Show that \(f/g\) has zero drift, and comment on the interpretation of this result.

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) From Hull 27.1, when \(\lambda\) is the market price of the single source of risk, security price \(f\) obeys \[df = (r + \lambda\sigma_f)f\,dt + \sigma_f f\,dz\] Once \(\lambda = \sigma_g\), the two expressions follow.
(b) Cf. Hull p. 636. First, Itô's lemma gives \[d\ln f = \left(r + \sigma_g\sigma_f - \frac{\sigma_f^2}{2}\right)dt + \sigma_f\,dz\] and, likewise, \[d\ln g = \left(r + \frac{\sigma_g^2}{2}\right)dt + \sigma_g\,dz\] Then \[d\ln f - d\ln g = \left(\sigma_g\sigma_f - \frac{\sigma_f^2}{2} - \frac{\sigma_g^2}{2}\right)dt + (\sigma_f - \sigma_g)\,dz\] which is identical to the desired expression.
(c) If we let \(x = \ln\tfrac{f}{g}\), we're seeking to find the equation for \(G(x) = e^x = \tfrac{f}{g}\). Itô's lemma gives \[d\frac{f}{g} = (\sigma_f - \sigma_g)\frac{f}{g}\,dz\] which indeed has zero drift. The interpretation is that the relative security price \(\tfrac{f}{g}\) is a martingale. As explained on the top of p. 636, this is the equivalent martingale measure result.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Market Price of Risk  ·  Numeraire & Change of Measure   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) \(f\) and \(g\) satisfy the given processes when \(\lambda = \sigma_g\): The market price of risk is \(\lambda = (\mu-r)/\sigma\) for all non-dividend-paying traded assets. For \(f\), this gives \(\mu_f = r + \lambda\sigma_f = r + \sigma_g\sigma_f\); for \(g\), it gives \(\mu_g = r + \lambda\sigma_g = r + \sigma_g^2\) — matching the stated processes.

(b) \(d\ln(f/g)\) via Itô's lemma: Applying Itô's lemma to \(\ln f\) and \(\ln g\), \[d\ln f = \bigl(\mu_f - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_f^2\bigr)dt + \sigma_f\,dz = \bigl(r + \sigma_g\sigma_f - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_f^2\bigr)dt + \sigma_f\,dz,\] \[d\ln g = \bigl(\mu_g - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_g^2\bigr)dt + \sigma_g\,dz = \bigl(r + \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_g^2\bigr)dt + \sigma_g\,dz.\] Subtracting, \[d\ln\frac{f}{g} = \bigl(\sigma_g\sigma_f - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_f^2 - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_g^2\bigr)dt + (\sigma_f - \sigma_g)\,dz = -\tfrac{1}{2}(\sigma_f-\sigma_g)^2 dt + (\sigma_f-\sigma_g)\,dz.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{d\ln\frac{f}{g} = -\tfrac{1}{2}(\sigma_f-\sigma_g)^2 dt + (\sigma_f-\sigma_g)dz.}\]

(c) \(f/g\) is a martingale — change of numeraire: Let \(X = \ln(f/g)\), so \(dX = a\,dt + b\,dz\) with \(a = -\tfrac{1}{2}(\sigma_f-\sigma_g)^2\) and \(b = \sigma_f - \sigma_g\). Applying Itô's lemma to \(f/g = e^X\), \[d\frac{f}{g} = \frac{f}{g}\bigl[(a + \tfrac{1}{2}b^2)dt + b\,dz\bigr].\] The drift coefficient is \[a + \tfrac{1}{2}b^2 = -\tfrac{1}{2}(\sigma_f-\sigma_g)^2 + \tfrac{1}{2}(\sigma_f-\sigma_g)^2 = 0,\] so the drift terms cancel exactly: \[\boxed{d\frac{f}{g} = (\sigma_f-\sigma_g)\frac{f}{g}\,dz.}\] Zero drift means \(f/g\) is a martingale. This is the change-of-numeraire result: when \(g\) is the numeraire, the ratio \(f/g\) is driftless, equivalent to saying \(f\) is a martingale under the \(g\)-measure.

Question 3

Consider a futures contract on a non-dividend paying stock with a time to maturity of \(T\). Let the current (\(t=0\)) price of the stock be \(S_0\), and assume that the risk-free rate of interest is a constant \(r\).
(a) The futures price \(F_0\) at \(t=0\) satisfies \(F_0 = S_0 e^{rT}\). What is the delta of the futures contract?
(b) Compare the price of a European call option on the stock with the price of a European call option on the futures contract when the two options have the same strikes and maturities, and they mature at the same time as the futures contract.
(c) Explain the following in words (no mathematical derivation is expected): in a risk-neutral world, the futures price behaves in the same way as the price of a stock that pays dividend yield \(r\).

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Cf. Hull p. 399. The delta is \[\frac{\partial F_0}{\partial S_0} = e^{rT}\]
(b) Following Hull 17.3, towards maturity when \(T\) is close to zero, the futures price approximates the stock price. These two options therefore have identical payoffs at expiry. Being European, then they have identical values also before expiry.
(c) Cf. Hull 17.7 for full explanations. Entering into a long futures contract has value zero. Therefore, under risk-neutral valuation, \(F_0 = \hat{E}[F_T]\) for any time \(T\). The exact same martingale property, \(S_0 = \hat{E}[S_T]\), holds for a stock that pays dividend at rate \(r\). Its drift is \(r - r = 0\).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Forward Pricing   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Delta of a futures contract: The futures price is \(F_0 = S_0 e^{rT}\). Differentiating with respect to \(S_0\), \[\boxed{\Delta_\text{futures} = \frac{\partial F_0}{\partial S_0} = e^{rT} > 1.}\] Delta exceeds 1 because the futures price embeds the cost-of-carry factor \(e^{rT}\).

(b) European call on the stock vs. call on the futures, same \(K,T\): At expiry \(T\), the futures price converges to spot, \(F_T^T = S_T\), so the call-on-futures payoff \(\max(F_T^T - K, 0) = \max(S_T - K, 0)\) equals the call-on-stock payoff. Both options are European, so they have the same present value: \[c^\text{stock} = e^{-rT}E^Q[\max(S_T-K,0)] = c^\text{futures}.\] \[\boxed{c^\text{futures} = c^\text{stock}}\quad\text{for a non-dividend-paying stock.}\]

(c) Futures price as a \(\mathbb{Q}\)-martingale: Entering a futures contract has zero value, since it is marked to market daily with no upfront payment. Under risk-neutral valuation, \(F_0 = E^Q[F_T]\) for any \(T\), so the futures price is a \(\mathbb{Q}\)-martingale. A stock paying dividends at rate \(r\) has drift \(r-r=0\) under \(\mathbb{Q}\), so it is also a \(\mathbb{Q}\)-martingale. This gives \[\boxed{F_0 = E^Q[F_T] = E^Q[S_T] = S_0 e^{rT}.}\]

August 2019 — Re-Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

Question 1

Let the price of a traded financial instrument, \(S\), be modelled (under the probability measure \(\mathbb{P}\)) by the geometric Brownian motion \[dS = \mu S\,dt + \sigma S\,dz\] where \(\mu\) and \(\sigma > 0\) are constants, and \(z\) is a Brownian motion.
(a) Describe the qualitative characteristics of this model, and discuss its possible shortcomings as a model of a stock price.
(b) Assume a constant risk free rate of \(r\), and that the instrument pays a continuous dividend stream of \(\delta\) proportional to the price \(S\). What will the drift rate of the price be under the standard risk neutral probability measure (\(\mathbb{Q}\)) and a no-arbitrage assumption?
(c) Consider a derivative on \(S\) with value \(V\) equal to \(S^n\). Use Itô's lemma to find the process followed by \(V\). Is the volatility of \(V\) higher, if \(n\) is higher?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) The issue that the model assumes no dividends aside (it is dealt with below), the discussion should cover: The model implies continuous sample paths whereas stock prices can make large instantaneous changes (and in any case prices are only quoted in certain tick sizes). The model is Markov (which can be seen as a form of weak informational efficiency, cf. Hull (8ed, section 13.1–2)). The model implies constant volatility, where we in practice observe marked (and sticky) changes in volatility. The model implies normally distributed returns (whereas empirical stylized facts point to negative skewness and some excess kurtosis — not really discussed in Hull).
(b) The drift rate under \(\mathbb{Q}\) should be \(r - \delta\) so as to make the total return of holding the stock equal to the risk free interest rate (cf. Hull (8ed, section 16.3)).
(c) An application of Itô's lemma gives \[dV = \left(\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2 S^2 n(n-1)S^{n-2} + \mu S^n S^{n-1}\right)dt + nS^{n-1}\sigma S\,dz\] \[= \left(n\mu + \tfrac{1}{2}n(n-1)\sigma^2\right)V\,dt + n\sigma V\,dz\] So this is also of a geometric Brownian form. And it can be seen that volatility is increasing in \(n\). This is Practice Question 13.10 in Hull (8ed); note that it is not the same as the value of a derivative that pays off \(S_T^n\) at time \(T\), cf. Practice Question 14.12 in Hull (8ed).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: GBM  ·  Market Price of Risk   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) GBM — qualitative characteristics and shortcomings: The GBM model has continuous sample paths, constant drift \(\mu\) and volatility \(\sigma\), lognormal prices with normal returns, and the Markov property (only the current price matters for future evolution). Its shortcomings as a model of stock prices are that real prices can jump (discontinuous paths), volatility is not actually constant (empirical evidence shows clustering and a volatility smile/skew), returns are not really normal (fat tails and negative skewness are observed in practice), and there is some empirical evidence of long memory that the Markov property rules out.

(b) Drift under \(Q\) with a continuous dividend \(\delta\): Under the risk-neutral measure, holding the stock must earn the risk-free rate \(r\) in total return, i.e. capital gain plus dividend equals \(r\), so the capital-gain drift is \(r-\delta\). Therefore, \[\boxed{dS = (r-\delta)S\,dt + \sigma S\,dz^Q.}\]

(c) Itô's lemma applied to \(V=S^n\): For \(V=G(S,t)=S^n\) with \(dS=\mu S\,dt+\sigma S\,dz\), Itô's lemma gives \[dV = \left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}\mu S+\frac{\partial V}{\partial t}+\tfrac12\frac{\partial^2V}{\partial S^2}\sigma^2S^2\right)dt + \frac{\partial V}{\partial S}\sigma S\,dz.\] Since \(V=S^n\) has no explicit time-dependence, \(\partial V/\partial t=0\), while \[\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}=nS^{n-1},\qquad \frac{\partial^2V}{\partial S^2}=n(n-1)S^{n-2}.\] Substituting the partial derivatives into the Itô formula (still unsimplified), \[dV = \left(nS^{n-1}\mu S+\tfrac12n(n-1)S^{n-2}\sigma^2S^2\right)dt + nS^{n-1}\sigma S\,dz.\] Simplifying the powers of \(S\) in each term (\(S^{n-1}\cdot S=S^n\) and \(S^{n-2}\cdot S^2=S^n\)), \[dV = \left(n\mu S^n+\tfrac12n(n-1)\sigma^2S^n\right)dt + n\sigma S^n\,dz.\] Factoring out \(S^n=V\) from every term, \[\boxed{dV = \left(n\mu+\tfrac12n(n-1)\sigma^2\right)V\,dt + n\sigma V\,dz.}\] This is again a geometric Brownian motion, with volatility \(n\sigma\) — increasing in \(n\) for \(n>0,\sigma>0\).

Question 2

The HJM-model describes the simultaneous evolution of the term structure of interest rates. Let the evolution of instantaneous forward rates contracted at \(t\) for time \(T\) be described by the Itô-process \[df(t,T) = m(t,T,\Omega)\,dt + s(t,T,\Omega)\,dz\] where \(\Omega\) is a set of state variables.
(a) Under certain conditions we have the following no-arbitrage condition for the drift term: \[m(t,T,\Omega) = s(t,T,\Omega)\int_t^T s(t,\tau,\Omega)\,d\tau\] Comment on this result, and in particular explain under which probability measure it is derived.
(b) The short rate in this model is in general non-Markov. Explain what this means, and why it is a complication for implementation.
(c) As a special case let \(s(t,T,\Omega)\) be a constant \(s\). Derive the process followed by forward rates. Comment on the distribution of the forward rates.

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) What should be commented is that the volatility barring arbitrage determines the drift rate, and that the result presented is derived under the traditional risk neutral probability measure \(\mathbb{Q}\), cf. Hull (8th ed., pp. 716–7).
(b) As given in the lecture notes, a probability space \((\Omega,\mathcal{F},P)\) and a process \(X_t : \Omega \mapsto \mathbb{R}^n\) is Markov if \[P(X_{t_n} \leq x_n \mid X_{t_{n-1}} = x_{n-1}, \ldots, X_{t_1} = x_1) = P(X_{t_n} \leq x_n \mid X_{t_{n-1}} = x_{n-1}), \quad \forall n,\; \forall t_1 < \cdots < t_n\] Hull has a less formal definition in section 13.1 (of the 8th edition). I.e. if the short rate is not Markov we cannot in general model the evolution of rates without including the full history of rates. In practice this means that we cannot e.g. use a recombining interest rate tree, which adds a substantial computational burden if more than a few time steps are to be simulated, cf. Hull (8th ed., section 31.1).
(c) By integration we find for this simple model that \[df(t,T) = s^2(T-t)\,dt + s\,dz\] (this is not derived in the syllabus). This means that the changes to the forward rates, and thus the forward rates, will be normally distributed, which in particular means a positive probability for negative interest rates. It can be noted — but this is not directly in the syllabus (it is in Hull (8th ed., Practice Question 31.3)) — that this special case is equivalent to the Ho-Lee model.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: HJM Model   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) HJM drift condition: HJM models the evolution of instantaneous forward rates \(f(t,T)\) by \[df(t,T) = m(t,T)\,dt + s(t,T)\,dz.\] No-arbitrage, derived under the risk-neutral measure \(\mathbb{Q}\), requires \[\boxed{m(t,T) = s(t,T)\int_t^T s(t,\tau)\,d\tau.}\] This means the drift of forward rates is completely determined by their volatility structure: volatility drives both risk and drift, leaving no free parameters for drift once volatility is specified.

(b) HJM and the non-Markov property: HJM models are in general non-Markov, meaning that the entire current forward rate curve \(\{f(t,T)\}_{T\geq t}\) — not just the short rate \(r(t)\) — is needed to evolve the term structure. This is a practical complication because it rules out simple recombining trees and forces path-dependent (e.g. Monte Carlo) implementations. A special case restores the Markov property: if \(s(t,T)=\sigma e^{-a(T-t)}\) (exponentially decaying volatility), the model reduces to the Markov Vasicek/Hull-White model.

(c) Ho-Lee as a special case of HJM: Take \(s(t,T)=\sigma\), a volatility constant in \(T\). Applying the HJM drift condition, \[m(t,T) = s(t,T)\int_t^T s(t,\tau)\,d\tau = \sigma\int_t^T\sigma\,d\tau.\] Evaluating the integral, \[\int_t^T\sigma\,d\tau = \sigma(T-t).\] Substituting back, \[m(t,T) = \sigma\cdot\sigma(T-t) = \sigma^2(T-t).\] Therefore, \[\boxed{df(t,T) = \sigma^2(T-t)\,dt + \sigma\,dz.}\] Forward rates are normally distributed in this case — which in particular allows negative rates — and this special case is equivalent to the Ho-Lee short rate model \(dr=\theta(t)\,dt+\sigma\,dz\).

Question 3

(a) Consider a derivative with value \(V(S,t)\) as some function of the current stock price \(S\) and time \(t\) (and further implicit parameters). Define the Delta, Gamma and Theta of the derivative. What can they be used for?
(b) Assume that the stock pays no dividends before time \(T\), and that there is a constant risk free interest rate of \(r\). Let \(c(S,K,T,r)\) and \(p(S,K,T,r)\) be the price at time \(t=0\) of a European call and a European put, respectively, on the stock with the same strike \(K\) and expiry \(T\). Derive the call-put-parity.
(c) Use the call-put-parity to find a relationship between the Deltas of the call and put. Repeat this for Gamma and Theta, respectively.
(d) Suppose a portfolio of the stock and/or derivatives of that stock is Delta-neutral, and that there are no arbitrage possibilities. Let the value of the portfolio be \(\Pi(S,t)\). Use the Black-Scholes-Merton PDE to characterise the relation between the Theta and Gamma of the portfolio.

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) The Delta and Theta of the derivative is the partial derivative of the value \(V\) with respect to the underlying stock price and the calendar time parameter \(t\), respectively. Gamma is the second order derivative of the value with respect to the stock price (Cf. Hull (8ed) p.380ff). The Greeks above can be used to measure economic risks of a portfolio including options, and to manage and possibly hedge part of the risks.
(b) By considering the payoff at maturity we see that a portfolio with long European call and a short European put with strikes \(K\) and expiries \(T\) has the same payoff as a forward on the stock with forward price \(K\) and maturity \(T\), thus also (under a no-arbitrage assumption) \[c(S_0,K,T,r) - p(S_0,K,T,r) = S_0 - P(0,T)K\] where here \(P(0,T) = \exp(-rT)\).
(c) By considering the call-put-parity you immediately get for the Deltas \[\Delta_c = 1 + \Delta_p\] and using this for the gammas \[\Gamma_c = \Gamma_p\] For Theta we get \[\Theta_c + rKe^{-rT} - \Theta_p = 0\] (using that the derivative with respect to calendar time is minus the derivative with respect to the expiry date).
(d) For a portfolio with value \(\Pi(S,t)\) dependent on a non-dividend-paying stock we have by no arbitrage the Black-Scholes-Merton PDE: \[\frac{\partial\Pi}{\partial t} + \frac{1}{2}\sigma^2 S^2\frac{\partial^2\Pi}{\partial S^2} + rS\frac{\partial\Pi}{\partial S} - r\Pi = 0\] or \[\Theta + \frac{1}{2}\sigma^2 S^2\Gamma + rS\Delta = r\Pi\] When delta is zero you have \[\Theta + \frac{1}{2}\sigma^2 S^2\Gamma = r\Pi\] One interpretation from this is that a delta-neutral, positive-gamma portfolio barring arbitrage will have (for small \(r\) or small \(\Pi\)) a negative theta (i.e. time-decay), cf. Hull (8ed, section 18.7).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: BSM Model  ·  Put-Call Parity   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Delta, Gamma, Theta: Delta, \(\Delta=\partial V/\partial S\), measures the price change per unit move in the stock and is used for delta-hedging. Gamma, \(\Gamma=\partial^2V/\partial S^2\), is the rate of change of delta, capturing convexity and determining how often a hedge needs rebalancing. Theta, \(\Theta=\partial V/\partial t\), measures time decay and is usually negative for long option positions.

(b) Put-call parity, no dividends: A long call plus a short put with the same strike \(K\) and maturity \(T\) pays \(S_T-K\) at \(T\), the same payoff as a long forward with delivery price \(K\). By no-arbitrage, the values today must match: \[\boxed{c(S_0,K,T,r) - p(S_0,K,T,r) = S_0 - Ke^{-rT}.}\]

(c) Greeks from put-call parity: Differentiating with respect to \(S_0\) gives \(\Delta_c-\Delta_p=1\), so \[\boxed{\Delta_p = \Delta_c - 1.}\] Differentiating again with respect to \(S_0\) gives \[\boxed{\Gamma_c = \Gamma_p.}\] Differentiating instead with respect to calendar time \(t\) (equivalently, minus the derivative with respect to \(T\)), since \(\partial(Ke^{-r(T-t)})/\partial t=-rKe^{-r(T-t)}\) and \(\partial S_0/\partial t=0\), \[\boxed{\Theta_c - \Theta_p = -rKe^{-r(T-t)}.}\]
