(d) BSM PDE and the delta-neutral Theta–Gamma relationship: For any derivative or portfolio \(\Pi(S,t)\) on a non-dividend-paying stock, the BSM PDE reads \[\Theta + rS\Delta + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\Gamma = r\Pi.\] Imposing delta-neutrality (\(\Delta=0\)) removes the middle term: \[\boxed{\Theta + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\Gamma = r\Pi.}\] So for a delta-neutral portfolio with small \(r\Pi\), positive gamma must be offset by negative theta: a long-gamma position pays for its convexity through time decay, while a short-gamma position earns theta but loses on large moves.

June 2018 — Ordinary Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

Question 1

Consider a commodity with current price \(S_0\). Assume that it can be stored, that there are commodities at storage (so that it may be sold), and that the net storage costs of the commodity can be described as a constant proportional continuously paid cost of \(\delta\).
(a) Describe the workings of a forward contract on the commodity.
(b) Assume a constant continuously compounded risk-free interest rate of \(r\). Using an arbitrage argument find the forward price \(F_0\) at time 0 on the forward contract that matures at time \(T\). (Hint: You may think at an analogy with a stock paying a continuously paid dividend rate.)
(c) Consider the value of a forward contract with forward price \(K\) at time \(t < T\) and assume that the commodity price follows the geometric Brownian motion \[dS_t = \mu S_t\,dt + \sigma S_t\,dz\] Using Itô's lemma find the process followed by the value of the forward contract. What will the drift be under the risk neutral measure?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Definition (Forward Contract). A Forward Contract is an agreement to buy or sell a quantity of an asset of a specified quality and type (called the underlying asset) delivered at a specified future time \(T\) (the expiration date) and at a specified place for an agreed delivery price \(K\) as measured in some defined numeraire/currency. The price is initially (at \(t\)) set at a level \(K = F(t,T)\), such that the value \(V_K(t,T)\) of the contract is 0, i.e. by definition \(V_{F(t,T)}(t,T) = 0\). The price \(F(t,T)\) is called the forward price at \(t\) for delivery at \(T\).
(b) Cf. e.g. Hull (8ed) p. 312–3 and p.292. Since the commodity can be stored there is an arbitrage between spot and forward purchase, and the argument is analogous to the case of a stock with a continuously paid dividend rate: Buy the commodity spot financed by a loan at an interest of \(r\) and accumulate interest and storage costs. The accumulated liability at \(T\) will be \(S_0 e^{(r+\delta)T}\), and you have the commodity. An alternative is to enter into a forward contract with delivery at \(T\). To eliminate arbitrage it must be that the forward price at 0 for delivery at \(T\) is \(F_0 = S_0 e^{(r+\delta)T}\).
(c) For a forward contract with forward price \(K\) the value is \(V_K = S_t e^{\delta(T-t)} - Ke^{-r(T-t)}\). Using Itô's lemma you get that the differential form for the value process for the forward: \[dV_K = \bigl[(\mu-\delta)e^{\delta(T-t)}S_t - rKe^{-r(T-t)}\bigr]dt + \sigma S_t e^{\delta(T-t)}\,dz\] For a currently priced forward (i.e. where \(K\) gives the forward contract a zero net present value \(K = F_t = e^{(r+\delta)(T-t)}S_t\)) we see that the drift is 0 under the risk neutral measure (by setting \((\mu-\delta) = r\)), while a non-zero market value will grow at a rate \(r\). It will be considered ok just to derive this for the currently priced forward.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Forward Pricing   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Forward contract on a commodity: A forward contract is an agreement to buy or sell a quantity of a commodity at a specified future date \(T\) for a delivery price \(K\) agreed at inception. The forward price \(F(0,T)\) is set so the contract has zero value at initiation.

(b) Forward price — commodity with storage cost \(\delta\): Compare two strategies to own 1 unit at \(T\). A) Enter a forward at \(F_0\), pay at \(T\). B) Buy 1 unit of the commodity now at \(S_0\) and pay storage cost \(\delta\) (proportional, continuous); the total cost at \(T\) is \(S_0e^{(r+\delta)T}\). By no-arbitrage, \[\boxed{F_0 = S_0\,e^{(r+\delta)T}.}\] Storage costs increase the forward price, since they behave like a negative dividend yield.

(c) Value of the commodity forward at \(t\): The same no-arbitrage argument as above, replicating the payoff \(S_T-K\) by holding the commodity (with storage costs accruing) against a discounted cash position, gives \[V_K(t) = S_t e^{\delta(T-t)} - Ke^{-r(T-t)}.\] Apply Itô's lemma to \(V_K(S_t,t)\), with \(dS_t = \mu S_t\,dt + \sigma S_t\,dz\): \[dV_K = \frac{\partial V_K}{\partial t}dt + \frac{\partial V_K}{\partial S_t}dS_t + \tfrac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^2 V_K}{\partial S_t^2}(dS_t)^2.\] Since \(V_K\) is linear in \(S_t\), the second-order term drops out. The partial derivatives are \[\frac{\partial V_K}{\partial t} = -\delta S_t e^{\delta(T-t)} - rKe^{-r(T-t)},\qquad \frac{\partial V_K}{\partial S_t} = e^{\delta(T-t)}.\] Since the second-order term vanishes, Itô's lemma reduces to \[dV_K = \frac{\partial V_K}{\partial t}\,dt + \frac{\partial V_K}{\partial S_t}\,dS_t.\] Substituting the two partial derivatives found above: \[dV_K = \bigl[-\delta S_t e^{\delta(T-t)} - rKe^{-r(T-t)}\bigr]dt + e^{\delta(T-t)}\,dS_t.\] Substituting \(dS_t = \mu S_t\,dt+\sigma S_t\,dz\): \[dV_K = \bigl[-\delta S_t e^{\delta(T-t)} - rKe^{-r(T-t)}\bigr]dt + e^{\delta(T-t)}\bigl(\mu S_t\,dt+\sigma S_t\,dz\bigr).\] Distributing \(e^{\delta(T-t)}\) into the bracket: \[dV_K = \bigl[-\delta S_t e^{\delta(T-t)} - rKe^{-r(T-t)}\bigr]dt + \mu S_t e^{\delta(T-t)}\,dt + \sigma S_t e^{\delta(T-t)}\,dz.\] Collecting the \(dt\)-terms, \[\boxed{dV_K = \bigl[(\mu-\delta)e^{\delta(T-t)}S_t - rKe^{-r(T-t)}\bigr]dt + \sigma S_t e^{\delta(T-t)}\,dz.}\] Under \(\mathbb{Q}\), the commodity's total risk-neutral return — capital gain \(\mu\) net of the storage cost \(\delta\) — equals \(r\), i.e. \((\mu-\delta)=r\). Substituting into the drift above, \[(\mu-\delta)e^{\delta(T-t)}S_t - rKe^{-r(T-t)} = re^{\delta(T-t)}S_t - rKe^{-r(T-t)} = r\bigl(e^{\delta(T-t)}S_t - Ke^{-r(T-t)}\bigr) = rV_K.\] For the currently priced forward \(K=F_t\), \(V_K=0\), so the drift is 0. A forward with non-zero market value instead grows at rate \(r\): \(dV_K = rV_K\,dt + \sigma S_t e^{\delta(T-t)}\,dz\) under \(\mathbb{Q}\).

Question 2

Suppose certain derivatives have values that depend on a single state variable given by the process \[\frac{d\theta}{\theta} = m\,dt + s\,dz\] where \(dz\) is a Wiener process.
(a) Consider two such derivatives, assume a continuously compounded risk-free interest rate of \(r\), and use an arbitrage argument to derive and define the market price of (\(\theta\)-)risk, \(\lambda\) (You may assume that the prices of the derivatives follow geometric Brownian motions).
(b) If \(\theta\) is itself a traded asset, what can we say about the relation between \(m\), \(s\) and the market price of risk?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Consider two derivatives with prices following two different GBMs with the same underlying Wiener process \(dz\). Use these to form a locally risk free portfolio, and using that this, barring arbitrage, must return the risk free rate you can derive the market price per unit of risk (\(\lambda\)) as the drift of the derivative price in excess of the risk free rate divided by the volatility, i.e. of the form \[\frac{\mu - r}{\sigma} = \lambda\] This is the same for both, hence all such, derivatives (Hull, 8ed, section 27.1).
(b) If \(\theta\) is itself a traded asset, then (in arbitrage equilibrium) \[\frac{m - r}{s} = \lambda\] too.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Market Price of Risk   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Market price of risk — full arbitrage argument: Consider two derivatives on the same \(\theta\), \[df_i = \mu_i f_i\,dt + \sigma_i f_i\,dz,\quad i=1,2.\] Form a riskless portfolio by holding \(+\sigma_2 f_2\) of \(f_1\) and \(-\sigma_1 f_1\) of \(f_2\): \[d\Pi = \sigma_2 f_2\,df_1 - \sigma_1 f_1\,df_2 = \sigma_2 f_2(\mu_1 f_1\,dt + \sigma_1 f_1\,dz) - \sigma_1 f_1(\mu_2 f_2\,dt + \sigma_2 f_2\,dz)\] \[= f_1 f_2(\mu_1\sigma_2 - \mu_2\sigma_1)\,dt + f_1 f_2 \underbrace{(\sigma_1\sigma_2 - \sigma_1\sigma_2)}_{=0}\,dz.\] The risk cancels, so \(d\Pi\) is deterministic. By no-arbitrage, this riskless portfolio, with value \(\Pi = (\sigma_2-\sigma_1)f_1f_2\), must earn \(r\): \[f_1f_2(\mu_1\sigma_2 - \mu_2\sigma_1)\,dt = r(\sigma_2-\sigma_1)f_1f_2\,dt.\] Dividing by \(f_1f_2\) and rearranging gives \((\mu_1-r)\sigma_2 = (\mu_2-r)\sigma_1\), so \[\boxed{\lambda := \frac{\mu_1-r}{\sigma_1} = \frac{\mu_2-r}{\sigma_2}.}\]

(b) If \(\theta\) is itself a traded asset: Then \(\theta\) satisfies no-arbitrage too, so \(\lambda = (m-r)/s\). The market price of \(\theta\)-risk therefore equals \(\theta\)'s own excess return per unit of its volatility.

Question 3

Suppose a company \(i\) is in default before or at time \(t\) with probability \(Q_i(t)\).
(a) We will often assume that \(Q_i(t)\) is weakly increasing in \(t\). Why?
(b) In a model of defaults a random variable \(x_i\) is used to indicate a default before or at time \(T\). It is assumed that \(x_i\) has a standard normal distribution, and that a default occurs at or before time \(T\) if \[\Phi(x_i) \leq Q_i(T)\] where \(\Phi\) denotes the standard normal cumulative distribution function. Interpret this. Why is such a model of computational value?
(c) In the Vasicek one-factor model of credit risk the default indicators \(x_i\) for different companies \(i = 1,\ldots,n\) are given by \[x_i = a_i F + \sqrt{1-a_i^2}\,Z_i\] where the \(a_i\)s are constants, and \(F\), \((Z_i)_{i=1,\ldots,n}\) are independent standard normal random variables. Describe and interpret this model. When can it be a reasonable description of default?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) This follows directly from the definition. The assumption in the model is that if a company defaults it stays in default.
(b) It is easier to run simulations using the random variable that is normally distributed, and especially easier to simulate correlated defaults when the marginal distributions are standard normal, rather that complicated empirical distributions.
(c) Cf. Hull p.538–40. Note that \(F\) is a common factor determining the covariance structure, whereas \(Z_i\) are idiosyncratic factors, and \(a_i\) are sensitivities to the common factor. Note that \(\text{Var}[x_i] = a_i^2 + (1-a_i^2) = 1\) and \(\text{Cov}[x_i,x_j] = a_i a_j\) using that \(F\) and \(Z_i\) are standard normal and independent. In implementation the \(a_i\) are often approximated as the correlation between \(i\)'s equity return and some broad market index (i.e. P-type correlations). They could also be inferred from e.g. prices of tranched CDOs (i.e. Q-type correlations)).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Credit Risk   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Why \(Q_i(t)\) is weakly increasing in \(t\): Default is an absorbing state — once a firm defaults, it stays in default. The probability of having defaulted by \(t\) can therefore only grow: \[\boxed{t_1 < t_2 \implies Q_i(t_1) \leq Q_i(t_2).}\]

(b) Gaussian threshold model — interpretation: Let \(x_i\sim N(0,1)\) be a latent "credit-quality" variable, with no factor structure assumed yet. Default occurs at or before \(T\) iff \(\Phi(x_i)\leq Q_i(T)\), i.e. \(x_i\leq\Phi^{-1}(Q_i(T))=:C_i(T)\). Since \(x_i\) is standard normal, \(P(x_i\leq C_i(T))=\Phi(C_i(T))=Q_i(T)\) exactly, so the threshold \(C_i(T)\) is defined precisely so the model reproduces the target marginal default probability. For example, \(Q_i(T)=0.05 \implies C_i(T)=\Phi^{-1}(0.05)\approx -1.645\). This is computationally useful: it reduces default simulation to drawing standard normals and comparing to a threshold, and, crucially, makes it easy to impose realistic default correlations via a multivariate normal (Gaussian copula), rather than working directly with each firm's own possibly complicated empirical default-time distribution.

(c) Vasicek one-factor model — description and interpretation: The model sets \(x_i = a_iF + \sqrt{1-a_i^2}Z_i\), with \(F\) a common (systematic) factor shared by all firms, \(Z_i\) an idiosyncratic (firm-specific) factor, and \(a_i\) the firm's loading on the common factor; \(F,Z_i\sim N(0,1)\) are independent. Then \(\text{Var}(x_i)=a_i^2+(1-a_i^2)=1\), so \(x_i\) is still standard normal, consistent with the threshold model above; and \(\text{Cov}(x_i,x_j)=a_ia_j\), so \[\boxed{\rho_{ij}=a_ia_j.}\] Conditioning on \(F\), \(x_i\leq C_i(T) \iff Z_i\leq\dfrac{C_i(T)-a_iF}{\sqrt{1-a_i^2}}\), so \[\boxed{P(\text{default}_i\mid F)=\Phi\!\left(\frac{C_i(T)-a_iF}{\sqrt{1-a_i^2}}\right).}\] Conditional on \(F\), defaults are independent across firms — this is what makes the model tractable, e.g. for large-pool approximations. A single common factor is a strong simplification: it is reasonable when default correlation is driven mainly by one dominant macro/market factor, such as a broadly diversified portfolio within one economy, but less reasonable when several distinct sources of correlated risk exist, such as sector-specific shocks, which would call for a multi-factor extension. In practice, \(a_i\) is often proxied by the correlation between firm \(i\)'s equity return and a broad market index (a "P-type" correlation), or inferred from CDO tranche prices (a "Q-type" correlation).

August 2018 — Re-Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

Question 1

Let the time \(t\) spot price on a commodity be \(S_t\).
(a) Two models for the commodity price are given: \[d\ln S = \bigl(\theta(t) - a\ln S\bigr)dt + \sigma\,dz\] \[dS = \bigl(\theta^*(t) - a\ln S\bigr)S\,dt + \sigma S\,dz\] Use Itô's lemma to show that they are equivalent when \(\theta(t) = \theta^*(t) - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2\).
(b) Interpret the model.
(c) What is required for the above model of the commodity price to be a model of the price under a risk neutral equivalent martingale measure \(\mathbb{Q}\)?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Use Itô's lemma on the transformation \(\ln\) of the second equation.
(b) One may take outset in the first formulation. The model of log prices has a drift rate with a time varying mean reversion. The drift rate of the log price is 0 when \(S = \exp(\theta^*(t) - a)\). The model of the log price has a constant volatility. No further interpretation is necessary, but you can use the flexibility of the \(\theta\)-term to capture productivity gains in the production of the commodity, inflation (if nominal prices are considered) or seasonal variations.
(c) Under a risk neutral equivalent martingale measure the expected return of holding the commodity must be the risk free rate. Here we assume that storage is possible. This constitutes a satisfactory answer (as commodities are not explicitly dealt with in the syllabus). But the analysis may be carried further. Assume the risk free rate to be \(r(t)\), that storage is feasible, and that net storage costs (costs less convenience yield) can be described as a continuous proportional payment stream of \(\delta(S,t)\), then we must have \(\theta^*(t) - a\ln S = r(t) + \delta(S,t)\). Thus the modelled drift rate may be interpreted as a model of the net storage costs.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: GBM   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Showing the two models are equivalent via Itô's lemma: Model 2 is \(dS = (\theta^*(t) - a\ln S)S\,dt + \sigma S\,dz\). Applying Itô's lemma to \(G(S)=\ln S\), with \(G_S=1/S\), \(G_{SS}=-1/S^2\), \(G_t=0\): \[d\ln S = \frac{1}{S}dS - \tfrac{1}{2}\frac{1}{S^2}(dS)^2.\] Substituting \(dS/S = (\theta^*(t)-a\ln S)\,dt+\sigma\,dz\): \[d\ln S = \bigl[(\theta^*(t)-a\ln S)\,dt+\sigma\,dz\bigr] - \tfrac{1}{2}\frac{(dS)^2}{S^2}.\] Substituting \((dS)^2/S^2=\sigma^2\,dt\) (from the quadratic variation of Model 2): \[d\ln S = \bigl[(\theta^*(t)-a\ln S)\,dt+\sigma\,dz\bigr] - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2\,dt.\] Collecting the \(dt\)-terms, \[d\ln S = (\theta^*(t) - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2 - a\ln S)dt + \sigma\,dz.\] Compare this to Model 1, \(d\ln S = (\theta(t) - a\ln S)dt + \sigma\,dz\). Matching drifts gives \(\theta(t) = \theta^*(t) - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2\). Therefore, \[\boxed{\text{both models are equivalent when }\theta(t) = \theta^*(t) - \tfrac{1}{2}\sigma^2.}\]

(b) Interpretation: The log price \(\ln S\) mean-reverts to \(\theta(t)/a\) at speed \(a\). The level \(\theta(t)\) can be time-varying, capturing productivity trends, inflation, or seasonality. Volatility \(\sigma\) is constant in log terms.

(c) Condition for a risk-neutral equivalent martingale measure: Under a risk-neutral measure \(Q\), the expected return on holding the commodity — the whole drift coefficient \(\theta^*(t)-a\ln S\), not just part of it — must equal the risk-free rate \(r(t)\). Absent any storage cost or convenience yield, no-arbitrage requires \(\theta^*(t)-a\ln S=r(t)\), i.e. \[\boxed{dS = r(t)S\,dt+\sigma S\,dz\quad\text{under }Q.}\] The mean-reversion term must cancel out entirely once we are on the risk-neutral pricing measure for a directly tradeable, costlessly storable asset. If instead a continuous net storage cost (storage cost less convenience yield) \(\delta(S,t)\) applies, no-arbitrage requires \(\theta^*(t)-a\ln S=r(t)+\delta(S,t)\); the mean-reverting drift then plays the role of a model for the net cost of carry.

Question 2

Consider a one factor interest rate model with the following process for the instantaneous short rate \(r\): \[dr = m(r,t)\,dt + s(r,t)\,dz\]
(a) View the price of a zero-coupon bond with expiry at date \(T\) as a function \(P_T(r,t)\). Use Itô's lemma to find an expression for the process that \(dP_T(r,t)\) follows.
(b) Assume that this is modelled under a risk-neutral probability measure \(\mathbb{Q}\). What will be the drift rate of \(P_T\)?
(c) In some models of this form the price of the zero coupon bond can be expressed as \[P_T(t,r) = A(t,T)e^{-B(t,T)r}\] Show that \(B(t,T)\) can be interpreted as a measure of the interest rate risk of the bond.

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Using Itô's lemma we get (omitting the arguments): \[dP_T = \left(\frac{\partial P_T}{\partial r}m + \frac{\partial P_T}{\partial t} + \frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^2 P_T}{\partial r^2}s^2\right)dt + \frac{\partial P_T}{\partial r}s\,dz\]
(b) The expected return must be the risk free rate, so the drift rate of the zero coupon price must be \(rP_T\).
(c) Note that \[\frac{\partial P_T}{\partial r} = -B(t,T)P_T\] Thus \(B\) can be seen to measure the relative capital loss on the zero coupon bond from an increase in the short rate. \(B\) is the duration of the bond (Hull 8ed p.687).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Short Rate Models   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Itô's lemma on the bond price \(P_T(r,t)\): With \(dr = m\,dt + s\,dz\), Itô's lemma gives \[dP_T = \frac{\partial P_T}{\partial t}dt + \frac{\partial P_T}{\partial r}dr + \tfrac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^2 P_T}{\partial r^2}(dr)^2.\] Substituting \((dr)^2 = s^2\,dt\), \[\boxed{dP_T = \underbrace{\left(P_T^{(t)} + mP_T^{(r)} + \tfrac{1}{2}s^2P_T^{(rr)}\right)}_{\text{drift}}\,dt + \underbrace{sP_T^{(r)}}_{\text{diffusion}}\,dz.}\]

(b) Drift under the risk-neutral measure \(\mathbb{Q}\): Under \(\mathbb{Q}\), the bond earns the short rate \(r\), so the drift must equal \(r\cdot P_T\): \[\boxed{P_T^{(t)} + m^Q P_T^{(r)} + \tfrac{1}{2}s^2 P_T^{(rr)} = r P_T.}\] This is the fundamental bond-pricing PDE under \(\mathbb{Q}\).

(c) Duration from the affine bond form: With \(P_T = A(t,T)e^{-B(t,T)r}\), differentiating with respect to \(r\) gives \[P_T^{(r)} = A(t,T)\cdot(-B(t,T))\cdot e^{-B(t,T)r} = -B(t,T)P_T.\] So the duration is \[\boxed{D = -\frac{1}{P_T}\frac{\partial P_T}{\partial r} = B(t,T).}\] \(B(t,T)\) measures the interest-rate sensitivity (duration) of the bond with respect to the short rate \(r\).

Question 3

Consider a Credit Default Swap (CDS).
(a) Describe the instrument and its payment structure.
(b) Consider a tranched CDS. Explain the payment structure and define the terms attachment point and detachment point.
(c) Consider a tranched CDS on a large portfolio on underlying bonds (names). For a given average level of credit risk, e.g. expressed by the average credit spread on the underlying portfolio, explain how different levels of the assessed correlation of defaults of the issuers will influence the relative pricing of the tranches.

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Definition (Credit Default Swap). A Credit Default Swap (CDS) is a contract between a protection seller and a protection buyer based on a specified Nominal Principal. The protection buyer pays a running premium (the CDS spread) until maturity of the contract or a Credit Event on the Reference Entity of the CDS. At a Credit Event the protection seller pays a Specified Amount (Fixed or (1-Recovery) times the Nominal Principal) to the protection buyer. See Hull p. 549.
(b) The structure and terms are defined in Hull p. 560ff.
(c) This discussion is covered in Hull section 24.9 p. 561. A low level of correlation will make it likely that the losses can be absorbed by the tranches low in the capital structure in most states of the world whereas a high correlation will make it more likely that even high tranches occasionally will be hit. Thus for a given average level of credit risk the equity tranche will benefit from an increase in the perceived correlation and the highest (super senior) tranche will suffer. The effect on mezzanine tranches can be ambiguous.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Credit Risk   |   Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) CDS — description and payment structure: A CDS is a bilateral contract between a protection buyer and a protection seller on a reference entity, with nominal \(N\). Before a credit event, the buyer pays the CDS spread \(s\times N\) periodically; at a credit event, the seller pays \((1-R)\times N\), the loss given default, to the buyer. Over a short interval \(dt\), the premium leg pays \(sN\,dt\), while the probability of a credit event is \(\lambda\,dt\) (the hazard rate), triggering an expected protection payment of \(\lambda\,dt\,(1-R)N\). No-arbitrage requires the two legs to have equal expected value: \[sN\,dt = \lambda\,dt\,(1-R)N.\] Dividing both sides by \(N\,dt\): \[\boxed{\text{fair spread: } s = \lambda(1-R),}\] where \(\lambda\) is the risk-neutral hazard rate and \(R\) is the recovery rate.

(b) Tranched CDS — structure: Portfolio losses flow through a waterfall: equity, then mezzanine, then senior. The attachment point is the portfolio loss percentage at which a tranche starts losing value; the detachment point is the loss percentage at which it is fully wiped out: \[\boxed{\text{equity}:[0,A\%],\quad\text{mezzanine}:[A\%,D\%],\quad\text{senior}:[D\%,100\%].}\]

(c) Correlation effect on tranche spreads: With low correlation, defaults are diversified, so losses are predictable and stay within the junior tranches — the senior spread is low and the equity spread is high. With high correlation, defaults cluster, so large losses can hit the senior tranche — the senior spread rises and the equity spread falls. Intuitively, for a given average credit spread, high \(\rho\) makes the loss distribution bimodal (all-or-nothing), creating tail risk for the senior tranche: \[\boxed{\uparrow\rho:\ \text{senior spread}\uparrow,\ \text{equity spread}\downarrow.}\]

August 2017 — Re-Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

Question 1

Let the time-\(t\) spot exchange rate for a foreign currency be \(S_t\), denoting the value of one unit of the foreign currency as measured in the domestic currency. Assume that the exchange rate can be modelled (under the original probability measure \(\mathbb{P}\)) by the geometric Brownian motion \(dS = \mu_S S\,dt + \sigma_S S\,dz\), where \(\mu_S\) and \(\sigma_S>0\) are constants. Assume that the (continuously compounded) domestic and foreign risk-free interest rates are constants \(r\) and \(r_f\), respectively.
(a) What will the drift rate of the exchange rate be under the domestic risk-neutral probability measure (\(\mathbb{Q}\)) and a no-arbitrage assumption? Comment on the influence of the difference of domestic and foreign interest rates on the result.
(b) Consider at time \(t<T\) a forward contract on the foreign currency for payment and delivery at time \(T\). What is the arbitrage-free forward price \(F_t^T\)?
(c) Use Itô's lemma to show that the volatility of \(F_t^T\) goes to zero as time \(t\) approaches maturity \(T\).

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) The drift rate will be \((r-r_f)S\), i.e. determined by the domestic risk-free rate less the foreign rate (analogous to a stock paying a continuous dividend rate). Under \(\mathbb{Q}\), the currency with the higher interest rate is expected to depreciate at a rate corresponding to the interest-rate differential (uncovered interest rate parity).
(b) Compare two strategies to end up with the foreign currency at \(T\): (A) enter a forward contract at \(t\), paying \(F_t^T\) at \(T\); (B) borrow \(S_te^{-r_f(T-t)}\) domestically and buy the currency spot, depositing it at the foreign rate — at \(T\) you hold exactly 1 unit of foreign currency, and repay the domestic loan, now grown to \(S_te^{(r-r_f)(T-t)}\). No-arbitrage ⇒ \(F_t^T = S_te^{(r-r_f)(T-t)}\) (covered interest rate parity).
(c) The statement cannot be shown as stated — it is false. By Itô's lemma, the volatility of \(F_t^T\) is \(\frac{\partial F_t^T}{\partial S_t}\sigma_S S_t = e^{(r-r_f)(T-t)}S_t\sigma_S\), which tends to the currency's own volatility \(S_t\sigma_S\) as the maturity of the forward goes to zero — not to zero. For a forward contract already agreed at forward price \(K=F_t^T\), the value develops as \(V(S,t;K)=Se^{-r_f(T-t)}-Ke^{-r(T-t)}\), with volatility arising only from the first term; this too tends to the currency's own volatility as the maturity goes to zero, not to zero itself.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Forward Pricing | Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Risk-neutral drift of the exchange rate: Treat \(r_f\) like a continuous dividend yield on the "asset" \(S_t\), since holding foreign currency earns \(r_f\). Denoting the risk-neutral drift by \(\mu_S^{\mathbb{Q}}\), the total risk-neutral return (capital gain plus the foreign yield) must equal the domestic rate \(r\): \[\mu_S^{\mathbb{Q}} + r_f = r.\] Solving for \(\mu_S^{\mathbb{Q}}\): \[\mu_S^{\mathbb{Q}} = r-r_f.\] Substituting this drift into the GBM dynamics, no-arbitrage gives \[\boxed{dS_t = (r-r_f)S_t\,dt + \sigma_S S_t\,dz^{\mathbb{Q}}.}\] If \(r>r_f\), the risk-neutral drift is positive: the foreign currency must be expected to appreciate to offset the extra interest earned abroad, or a carry-trade arbitrage would exist.

(b) Forward price — covered interest rate parity: Compare two strategies to hold 1 unit of foreign currency at \(T\). Strategy A: enter a forward, paying \(F_t^T\) at \(T\). Strategy B: buy \(e^{-r_f(T-t)}\) units of foreign currency today, at cost \(S_te^{-r_f(T-t)}\), and invest at \(r_f\) so it grows to exactly 1 unit by \(T\); finance this by borrowing domestically at \(r\), so you owe \(S_te^{-r_f(T-t)}e^{r(T-t)}\) at \(T\). Both strategies deliver exactly 1 unit of foreign currency at \(T\), so by no-arbitrage their domestic-currency costs must match: \[\boxed{F_t^T = S_t\,e^{(r-r_f)(T-t)}.}\]

(c) Testing the claim — it is false: By Itô's lemma on \(F(S_t,t)=S_te^{(r-r_f)(T-t)}\), the diffusion coefficient is \[\frac{\partial F}{\partial S_t}\sigma_S S_t = e^{(r-r_f)(T-t)}S_t\sigma_S.\] As \(t\to T\), \(e^{(r-r_f)(T-t)}\to1\), so the volatility tends to \(S_t\sigma_S\) — the currency's own volatility, not zero: \[\boxed{\text{as }t\to T,\ \text{volatility}\to S_t\sigma_S \ne 0 \text{ — the claim in the question is false.}}\] The same conclusion holds for an existing contract struck at \(K\): \(V(S,t;K)=Se^{-r_f(T-t)}-Ke^{-r(T-t)}\), whose volatility comes only from the first term and likewise tends to \(S_t\sigma_S\), not zero, as \(t\to T\).

Question 2

Let \(S_t\) be the price in the domestic currency of one unit of a foreign currency. Assume that there are constant risk-free, continuously compounded interest rates of \(r\) and \(r_f\) in the domestic and foreign currencies, respectively. Consider a derivative with price \(V(S,t)\) as some function of the current exchange rate \(S_t\) and time \(t\) (and further implicit parameters).
(a) Define and interpret the Delta, Gamma and Theta of the derivative.
(b) Let \(c(S,K,T,r,r_f)\) and \(p(S,K,T,r,r_f)\) be the price at time \(t=0\) of a European call and a European put, respectively, on the currency with the same strike \(K\) and expiry \(T\). Derive the call-put parity.
(c) Use the call-put parity to find a relationship between the Deltas of the call and put. Repeat this for Gamma.
(d) Suppose a portfolio of the foreign currency and/or derivatives on the currency is Delta-neutral, and that there are no arbitrage possibilities. Let the value of the portfolio be \(\Pi(S,t)\). What can we say about the relation between the Theta and Gamma of the portfolio?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Delta, Gamma and Theta are defined as for derivatives on stocks: \(\Delta=\partial V/\partial S\), \(\Gamma=\partial^2V/\partial S^2\), \(\Theta=\partial V/\partial t\).
(b) A portfolio of a long call and a short put can be seen as a forward contract on the currency with \(K\) as the forward price; this contract has current value \(S_0e^{-r_fT}-Ke^{-rT}\) (equivalently, by an arbitrage argument: the same payoff as the forward can be achieved by holding \(S_0e^{-r_fT}\) of foreign currency at time 0, accumulating at the foreign rate until \(T\), and borrowing \(Ke^{-rT}\) domestically, repaid at \(T\)). Thus, barring arbitrage, \(c+Ke^{-rT}=p+S_0e^{-r_fT}\).
(c) This follows directly: for Delta, \(\Delta_c=\Delta_p+e^{-r_fT}\); then the Gammas are also identical, \(\Gamma_c=\Gamma_p\).
(d) From the fundamental Black-Scholes-Merton PDE, \(\dfrac{\partial V}{\partial t}+\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\dfrac{\partial^2V}{\partial S^2}+(r-r_f)S\dfrac{\partial V}{\partial S}-rV=0\). Substituting the definitions and using \(\Delta=0\): \(\Theta+\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\Gamma=rV\). Thus, beyond the risk-free rate, Theta and Gamma work in opposite directions: a portfolio that is long Gamma (e.g. a long option) is in this sense short Theta — you earn from Gamma when there is price movement, but the Theta loss dominates when there is not.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Put-Call Parity & Greeks | Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Definitions of Delta, Gamma, Theta: As for stock options, \[\Delta=\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}\quad\text{(spot sensitivity)},\qquad \Gamma=\frac{\partial^2 V}{\partial S^2}\quad\text{(convexity)},\qquad \Theta=\frac{\partial V}{\partial t}\quad\text{(time decay).}\]

(b) Call-put parity for FX options: A long call and a short put replicate a forward: payoff \(S_T-K\) at \(T\), currently worth \(S_0e^{-r_fT}-Ke^{-rT}\) — buy \(e^{-r_fT}\) units of foreign currency today, earning \(r_f\) until it equals 1 unit at \(T\), and borrow \(Ke^{-rT}\) domestically. By no-arbitrage, \[\boxed{c+Ke^{-rT} = p+S_0e^{-r_fT}.}\]

(c) Greeks from parity: Differentiating with respect to \(S_0\), \[\boxed{\Delta_c = \Delta_p+e^{-r_fT}.}\] Differentiating again, \[\boxed{\Gamma_c=\Gamma_p.}\]

(d) Delta-neutral portfolio: Treating \(r_f\) as a dividend yield, the FX-BSM PDE is \[\frac{\partial V}{\partial t}+\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\frac{\partial^2V}{\partial S^2}+(r-r_f)S\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}=rV.\] Applying this to \(\Pi\) and setting \(\Delta_\Pi=0\), \[\boxed{\Theta_\Pi + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\Gamma_\Pi = r\Pi.}\] Beyond the risk-free carry, Theta and Gamma trade off: a long-Gamma (e.g. long-option) delta-neutral position is short Theta — it earns from price movement but bleeds value when the market is quiet.

Question 3

The LMM model describes the evolution of the term structure of simple interest rates. Let \(F_k(t)\) be the (simple) forward rate between times \(t_k\) and \(t_{k+1}\) that can be contracted for at time \(t\le t_k<t_{k+1}\), and let \(\tau_k\) be the compounding period between \(t_k\) and \(t_{k+1}\). Let \(P(t,t_k)\) denote the price at \(t\) of a zero-coupon bond that matures at time \(t_k\).
(a) What is the relationship between a particular forward rate \(F_k\) and prices of zero-coupon bonds?
(b) Assume that the forward rates are driven by one factor. What choice of numeraire will lead to the forward rate \(F_k(t)\) being a martingale, e.g. of the form \(dF_k(t) = \zeta_k(t)F_k(t)\,dz\), where \(dz\) is the standard shorthand for the change of a Brownian motion?
(c) In the development of the LMM model, a rolling numeraire is used. Explain in general terms how this is constructed. (Explicit formulas are not required, but you should discuss the change of numeraire.)

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) We define, or see as an arbitrage relationship, \(1+\tau_kF_k = \dfrac{P(t,t_k)}{P(t,t_{k+1})}\).
(b) The numeraire must be the zero-coupon bond used for discounting when there is a payment on the forward-rate contract, i.e. the numeraire \(P(t,t_{k+1})\). This is the choice said to be forward risk-neutral with respect to this numeraire.
(c) In the LMM model there is a rolling change of numeraire, using the next reset date of discretely-compounded (LIBOR) rates. If the current date is \(t\), we use \(m(t)\) for the next reset date. Using \(P(t,t_{m(t)})\) as numeraire, the forward rate will not be a martingale if \(m(t)<k\). The change in drift is determined by the difference in volatilities of the two numeraires. One may also note that, assuming zero-coupon bond prices are driven by geometric Brownian motions, and letting \(v_t\) denote the volatility of the zero-coupon bond maturing at \(t\): \(dF_k(t) = \zeta_k(t)\bigl[v_{m(t)}(t) - v_{k+1}(t)\bigr]F_k(t)\,dt + \zeta_k(t)F_k(t)\,dz(t)\).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: LMM | Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Forward rate / bond price relation: No-arbitrage requires \[\boxed{1+\tau_kF_k(t) = \frac{P(t,t_k)}{P(t,t_{k+1})}.}\]

(b) Which numeraire makes \(F_k\) a martingale: By the numeraire-change theorem, under the measure associated with numeraire \(N_t\), the ratio (traded asset price)/\(N_t\) is a martingale. The ratio \(P(t,t_k)/P(t,t_{k+1})\) is exactly (traded asset \(P(\cdot,t_k)\)) divided by (numeraire \(P(\cdot,t_{k+1})\)), so it is a martingale under \(\mathbb{Q}^{k+1}\). Since \(F_k(t)=\tfrac1{\tau_k}\bigl[P(t,t_k)/P(t,t_{k+1})-1\bigr]\) is an affine transform of it, \(F_k\) is a martingale too: \[\boxed{\text{numeraire }P(\cdot,t_{k+1})\ \Rightarrow\ F_k(t)\text{ driftless under }\mathbb{Q}^{k+1}.}\]

(c) The rolling numeraire: Each \(F_k\) is driftless only under its own \(t_{k+1}\)-forward measure, but simulating the whole curve needs one common measure. The LMM instead uses \(P(t,t_{m(t)})\), where \(m(t)\) is the next reset date after \(t\) — this "rolls" the discretely-compounded LIBOR numeraire forward through time. Under this common numeraire, \(F_k\) is no longer driftless once \(m(t)<k\); the drift correction is set by the difference in volatilities of the two numeraire bonds, via a Girsanov-type change of measure: \[\boxed{dF_k(t) = \zeta_k(t)\bigl[v_{m(t)}(t)-v_{k+1}(t)\bigr]F_k(t)\,dt + \zeta_k(t)F_k(t)\,dz(t).}\]

June 2015 — Ordinary Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

Question 1

Let the price of a traded financial instrument \(S\) be modelled (under the probability measure \(\mathbb{P}\)) by the geometric Brownian motion \(dS = \mu S\,dt + \sigma S\,dz\), where \(\mu\) and \(\sigma>0\) are constants.
(a) Describe the qualitative characteristics of this model, and discuss its possible shortcomings as a model of a stock price.
(b) Assume that the instrument pays a continuous dividend stream of \(q\) proportional to the price \(S\). What will the drift rate of the price be under the standard risk-neutral probability measure (\(\mathbb{Q}\)) and a no-arbitrage assumption?
(c) Consider the transformation \(G\) of \(S\) given by the natural logarithm (\(\ln\)), i.e. \(G(x)=\ln(x)\). Use Itô's lemma to find the process followed by \(G(S)\).
(d) Suppose that for \(t=0\) the price of the instrument is \(S_0\). What is the expectation (under \(\mathbb{P}\)) of the natural log of the price at \(t=T\ge0\)?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) The answer should discuss: the drift rate and the volatility; the continuous sample paths taken by prices; the distribution of prices and returns; and that these characteristics are often at odds with empirical findings for stock prices, where volatility is not constant, prices may jump, and return distributions exhibit fatter tails.
(b) Under the risk-neutral probability measure the drift should be \(r-q\), where \(r\) is the (constant) short-term risk-free rate.
(c) By Itô's lemma, \(d\ln S = (\mu-\tfrac12\sigma^2)dt+\sigma\,dz\).
(d) \(\mathbb{E}[\ln S_T] = \ln S_0+(\mu-0.5\sigma^2)T\), following directly from (c).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: GBM | Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Qualitative characteristics of GBM: GBM implies lognormal prices, normally distributed and i.i.d. log-returns, and constant volatility \(\sigma\). Real markets instead show fat tails, volatility clustering, and occasional jumps, so GBM is a tractable benchmark rather than a fully realistic model.

(b) Risk-neutral drift with a dividend \(q\): Under \(\mathbb{Q}\), the total risk-neutral return — capital gain plus dividend yield — must equal \(r\). Denoting the risk-neutral drift rate of \(S\) by \(\mu^{\mathbb{Q}}\), \[\mu^{\mathbb{Q}} + q = r.\] Solving for \(\mu^{\mathbb{Q}}\): \[\mu^{\mathbb{Q}} = r-q.\] Substituting this drift into the GBM dynamics: \[\boxed{dS = (r-q)S\,dt + \sigma S\,dz^{\mathbb{Q}}.}\]

(c) Itô's lemma on \(G=\ln S\): Let \(G(S)=\ln S\). Its derivatives are \[G_S=\frac1S,\qquad G_{SS}=-\frac1{S^2},\qquad G_t=0.\] Itô's lemma states \[dG = \Bigl(G_t+\mu S G_S+\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2G_{SS}\Bigr)dt+\sigma S G_S\,dz.\] Substituting the derivatives above: \[dG = \Bigl(0+\mu S\cdot\frac1S+\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\cdot\Bigl(-\frac1{S^2}\Bigr)\Bigr)dt+\sigma S\cdot\frac1S\,dz,\] \[dG = \Bigl(\mu-\tfrac12\sigma^2\Bigr)dt+\sigma\,dz.\] Hence \[\boxed{d\ln S = \Bigl(\mu-\tfrac12\sigma^2\Bigr)dt + \sigma\,dz.}\]

(d) Expectation of \(\ln S_T\): Integrating from \(0\) to \(T\) with constant coefficients, \[\ln S_T = \ln S_0 + \Bigl(\mu-\tfrac12\sigma^2\Bigr)T + \sigma z_T,\qquad z_T\sim N(0,T).\] Since \(\mathbb{E}[z_T]=0\), \[\boxed{\mathbb{E}[\ln S_T] = \ln S_0 + \Bigl(\mu-\tfrac12\sigma^2\Bigr)T.}\]

Question 2

(a) Consider a derivative with price \(V(S,t)\) as some function of the current stock price \(S\) and time \(t\) (and further implicit parameters). Define and interpret the Delta, Gamma and Theta of the derivative.
(b) Assume that the stock pays no dividends before time \(T\), and that there is a constant risk-free interest rate of \(r\). Let \(c(S,K,T,r)\) and \(p(S,K,T,r)\) be the price at time \(t=0\) of a European call and a European put, respectively, on the stock with the same strike \(K\) and expiry \(T\). Derive the call-put parity.
(c) Use the call-put parity to find a relationship between the Deltas of the call and put. Repeat this for Gamma and Theta, respectively.
(d) Suppose a portfolio of the stock and/or derivatives of that stock is Delta-neutral, and that there are no arbitrage possibilities. Let the value of the portfolio be \(\Pi(S,t)\). What can we say about the relation between the Theta and Gamma of the portfolio?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) \(\Delta=\partial V/\partial S\) (sensitivity to the underlying), \(\Gamma=\partial^2V/\partial S^2\) (convexity / rate of change of Delta), \(\Theta=\partial V/\partial t\) (time decay).
(b) By considering the payoff at maturity, a portfolio with a long call and a short put has the same payoff as a forward on the stock with forward price \(K\); thus, under no-arbitrage, \(c_t(S_t,K,T)-p_t(S_t,K,T) = S_t - P(t,T)K\).
(c) By the call-put parity, \(\Delta_c = 1+\Delta_p\); for Gamma, \(\Gamma_c=\Gamma_p\); for Theta, \(\Theta_c + rKe^{-rT} - \Theta_p = 0\).
(d) For a portfolio with value \(\Pi(S,t)\) on a non-dividend-paying stock, no-arbitrage gives the Black-Scholes-Merton PDE \(\Theta+\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\Gamma+rS\Delta=r\Pi\). When Delta is zero, \(\Theta+\tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\Gamma=r\Pi\). One interpretation: barring arbitrage, a delta-neutral, positive-gamma portfolio will (for small \(r\)) have negative theta (time decay).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Put-Call Parity & Greeks | Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Definitions of Delta, Gamma, Theta: For a derivative price \(V(S,t)\), \[\Delta=\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}\quad\text{(sensitivity to the stock)},\qquad \Gamma=\frac{\partial^2 V}{\partial S^2}\quad\text{(convexity)},\qquad \Theta=\frac{\partial V}{\partial t}\quad\text{(time decay).}\]

(b) Call-put parity via replication: A long call and a short put have payoff at \(T\) \[\max(S_T-K,0)-\max(K-S_T,0)=S_T-K,\] the same as a forward with delivery price \(K\). Compare this to portfolio B: one share held long, financed partly by a short position of \(K\) zero-coupon bonds maturing at \(T\). Its payoff at \(T\) is \[S_T - K,\] identical to the payoff of the long call/short put portfolio. Since the two portfolios have the same payoff at \(T\) in every state of the world, no-arbitrage requires them to have the same value at every earlier time \(t\); the value of portfolio B at \(t\) is \(S_t - P(t,T)K\), so \[\boxed{c_t(S_t,K,T)-p_t(S_t,K,T) = S_t-P(t,T)K.}\]

(c) Greeks from parity: Differentiating the parity relation with respect to \(S_t\) gives \[\boxed{\Delta_c = 1+\Delta_p.}\] Differentiating again, \[\boxed{\Gamma_c = \Gamma_p.}\] Differentiating instead with respect to \(t\), \[\boxed{\Theta_c + rKe^{-rT} - \Theta_p = 0.}\]

(d) Delta-neutral portfolio and the BSM PDE: Any derivative \(V(S,t)\) on a non-dividend stock satisfies the BSM PDE \[\Theta + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\Gamma + rS\Delta = r\Pi.\] Setting \(\Delta_\Pi=0\) for a delta-neutral portfolio gives \[\boxed{\Theta_\Pi + \tfrac12\sigma^2S^2\Gamma_\Pi = r\Pi.}\] For small \(r\), a delta-neutral, positive-Gamma portfolio therefore has negative Theta.

Question 3

Consider an interest rate floor with a life of \(T\), a principal of \(N\), and a floor rate of \(L_K\). Consider reset dates \(0=t_0<t_1<t_2<\ldots<t_n\), and let \(L_k\) be the LIBOR rate for the period from \(t_k\) to \(t_{k+1}\), known at \(t_k\).
(a) Describe the payments of the floor, and define a floorlet.
(b) Show that the floor can be considered as a portfolio of European call options on zero-coupon bonds (for notational ease you may assume \(N=1\)). (Hint: start by analysing a single floorlet.)
(c) A standard market practice is to price a floorlet (at \(t=0\)) with the Black formula: \[\text{Floorlet}_{\text{Black}}(0,k,N,L_K,\sigma_k) = NP(0,t_{k+1})\tau_k\bigl(L_K\Phi(-d_2)-F_k\Phi(-d_1)\bigr)\] with \(d_1=\dfrac{\ln(F_k/L_K)+0.5\sigma_k^2t_k}{\sigma_k\sqrt{t_k}}\), \(d_2=d_1-\sigma_k\sqrt{t_k}\), where \(P(t,T)\) is the price at \(t\) of a zero-coupon bond maturing at \(T\), \(F_k\) is the forward rate at 0 for the interval \((t_k,t_{k+1})\) of length \(\tau_k\), and \(\sigma_k\) is the volatility of this rate. \(\Phi\) is the standard normal CDF. What assumptions can justify this formula?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) A floor gives its holder the payments \(N\tau_k\max(L_K-L_k,0)\) at time \(t_{k+1}\) (in arrears), where \(\tau_k\) is the time length between \(t_k\) and \(t_{k+1}\). Each such payment defines a single floorlet, starting on the previous reset date.
(b) A floor can equivalently be seen as a portfolio of calls on properly defined zero-coupon bonds (ZCBs): \(\dfrac{N\tau_k}{1+L_k\tau_k}\max(L_K-L_k,0) = \dfrac{N\tau_k}{1+L_k\tau_k}\max\bigl((1/\tau_k+L_K)-(1/\tau_k+L_k),0\bigr) = \max\Bigl(N\dfrac{1+L_K\tau_k}{1+L_k\tau_k}-N,0\Bigr)\). The first term in the max is the value at \(t_k\) of a ZCB with payoff \(N(1+L_K\tau_k)\) at \(t_{k+1}\); the payoff is thus a call on this ZCB, struck at \(N\).
(c) The application of the Black model is consistent with a world that is forward risk-neutral with respect to a ZCB maturing at \(t_{k+1}\). Under this probability measure, assume \(L_k\) has a lognormal distribution with a standard deviation of \(\ln(L_k)\) equal to \(\sigma_k\sqrt{t_k}\). We also assume that, under this measure, the expectation of \(L_k\) is the forward rate \(F_k\).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Interest Rate Derivatives | Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Floor and floorlet — definition: Floorlet \(k\) pays, at \(t_{k+1}\), \[\boxed{N\tau_k\max(L_K-L_k,0).}\] A floor is a strip of floorlets across all reset dates.

(b) Floorlet as a call on a zero-coupon bond: Set \(N=1\) and discount the floorlet payoff back one period to \(t_k\) by dividing by \(1+L_k\tau_k\): \[\frac{\tau_k}{1+L_k\tau_k}\max(L_K-L_k,0).\] Adding and subtracting \(1/\tau_k\) inside the max turns this into a bond-price comparison: \[\frac{\tau_k}{1+L_k\tau_k}\max\Bigl(\bigl(\tfrac1{\tau_k}+L_K\bigr)-\bigl(\tfrac1{\tau_k}+L_k\bigr),0\Bigr) = \max\Bigl(\frac{1+L_K\tau_k}{1+L_k\tau_k}-1,0\Bigr).\] Since \(1/(1+L_k\tau_k)=P(t_k,t_{k+1})\), the term \((1+L_K\tau_k)P(t_k,t_{k+1})\) is the \(t_k\)-value of a zero-coupon bond paying \(1+L_K\tau_k\) at \(t_{k+1}\). Therefore, \[\boxed{\text{Floorlet}_k\ (\text{at }t_k) = \max\bigl((1+L_K\tau_k)P(t_k,t_{k+1})-1,0\bigr)}\] which is a call on that bond, struck at 1.

(c) Why Black's formula applies: Work under the \(t_{k+1}\)-forward measure, with numeraire \(P(\cdot,t_{k+1})\) — the world that is forward risk-neutral for this maturity. Black's formula then applies under two assumptions: \[\boxed{L_k\text{ is lognormal under this measure, with sd}(\ln L_k)=\sigma_k\sqrt{t_k},\text{ and }\mathbb{E}[L_k]=F_k\text{ under this measure.}}\] These are exactly the conditions under which Black's formula for an option on a lognormal forward applies.

June 2016 — Ordinary Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

⚠️ Note: the source PDF for this exam contains only the question paper — no official marking guide was provided. The solution below is a worked solution constructed for this study guide (in the same style as the official guides for other years), not a verbatim official answer.

Question 1

Let the spot exchange rate for a foreign currency be \(S\), denoting the value of one unit of the foreign currency as measured in the domestic currency. Assume that the exchange rate can be modelled (under the original probability measure \(\mathbb{P}\)) by the geometric Brownian motion \(dS = \mu_S S\,dt + \sigma_S S\,dz\), where \(\mu_S\) and \(\sigma_S>0\) are constants.
(a) Describe the qualitative characteristics of this model, and discuss its possible shortcomings as a model of an exchange rate.
(b) Assume that the domestic and foreign risk-free interest rates are constants \(r\) and \(r_f\), respectively. What will the drift rate of the exchange rate be under the domestic risk-neutral probability measure (\(\mathbb{Q}\)) and a no-arbitrage assumption? Comment on the influence of the difference of domestic and foreign interest rates on the result.
(c) Let \(Z = 1/S\) be the exchange rate as measured in the foreign currency. Use Itô's lemma to find the process followed by \(Z\), where you eventually substitute \(S\) by \(1/Z\).

► Show worked solution
WORKED SOLUTION (not official)

(a) GBM implies \(S_t\) is lognormally distributed, with i.i.d. normally-distributed log-returns over non-overlapping intervals and constant volatility \(\sigma\); the rate can never become negative. As with equities, this is a tractable benchmark but real FX rates show fatter-tailed returns and time-varying (stochastic) volatility.
(b) A no-arbitrage argument (analogous to a stock paying continuous dividend yield \(r_f\)) gives risk-neutral drift \((r-r_f)\): \(dS_t=(r-r_f)S_t\,dt+\sigma S_t\,dz^{\mathbb{Q}}\).
(c) By Itô's lemma on \(Z_t=1/S_t\): \(dZ_t = (\sigma^2-\mu)Z_t\,dt - \sigma Z_t\,dz\). From the foreign investor's perspective, \(Z_t\) is the exchange rate (foreign currency per unit domestic), and under the foreign risk-neutral measure its drift is \((r_f-r)\) — exactly the domestic/foreign roles reversed, consistent with symmetry of the FX-forward relation.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: GBM | Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Qualitative properties of GBM for FX: GBM implies lognormal levels, normal log-returns, constant volatility \(\sigma\), and a rate that stays always positive. Real FX data instead show volatility clustering, fat tails, and occasional jumps (e.g. from central bank interventions), so GBM is a useful benchmark rather than a fully realistic model.

(b) Risk-neutral drift: Treat \(r_f\) as a continuous dividend yield on the "asset" \(S_t\), since holding foreign currency earns \(r_f\). Denoting the risk-neutral drift by \(\mu_S^{\mathbb{Q}}\), the total risk-neutral return (capital gain plus the foreign yield) must equal the domestic rate \(r\): \[\mu_S^{\mathbb{Q}} + r_f = r.\] Solving for \(\mu_S^{\mathbb{Q}}\): \[\mu_S^{\mathbb{Q}} = r-r_f.\] Substituting into the GBM dynamics, no-arbitrage gives \[\boxed{dS_t = (r-r_f)S_t\,dt + \sigma S_t\,dz^{\mathbb{Q}}.}\]

(c) Process for \(Z_t=1/S_t\): Apply Itô's lemma to \(G(S_t)=1/S_t\), with \(G_S=-1/S_t^2\) and \(G_{SS}=2/S_t^3\): \[dZ_t = -\frac{1}{S_t^2}dS_t + \tfrac12\cdot\frac{2}{S_t^3}(dS_t)^2.\] Substituting \(dS_t=\mu S_t\,dt+\sigma S_t\,dz\) and \((dS_t)^2=\sigma^2S_t^2\,dt\): \[dZ_t = -\frac{1}{S_t^2}(\mu S_t\,dt+\sigma S_t\,dz) + \frac{1}{S_t^3}\sigma^2S_t^2\,dt.\] Distributing each term: \[dZ_t = -\frac{\mu}{S_t}\,dt - \frac{\sigma}{S_t}\,dz + \frac{\sigma^2}{S_t}\,dt.\] Since \(1/S_t=Z_t\), each term is proportional to \(Z_t\): \[dZ_t = -\mu Z_t\,dt - \sigma Z_t\,dz + \sigma^2 Z_t\,dt.\] Collecting the \(dt\)-terms, \[\boxed{dZ_t = (\sigma^2-\mu)Z_t\,dt - \sigma Z_t\,dz.}\] For the foreign investor, \(Z_t\) is "their" exchange rate, and the domestic currency pays \(r\) like a dividend. The same argument as before, with roles reversed, gives \(Z_t\)'s risk-neutral drift as \((r_f-r)\) — \(r\) and \(r_f\) simply swap.

Question 2

In the Black-Scholes-Merton formula for options on a stock paying no dividends before expiry, \(c=S_0\Phi(d_1)-Ke^{-rT}\Phi(d_2)\), where \(S_0\) is the current stock price, \(K\) the exercise price, \(\Phi\) the cumulative standard normal, \(r\) the constant continuously compounded risk-free rate, \(d_1=\dfrac{\ln(S_0/K)+(r+0.5\sigma^2)T}{\sigma\sqrt T}\), \(d_2=d_1-\sigma\sqrt T\), and the stock follows \(dS=\mu S\,dt+\sigma S\,dz\).
(a) In a model formulated by Merton, the value of equity in a limited liability company with zero-coupon debt of notional \(N\) maturing at \(T\) is considered as a call option on the value \(V\) of company assets. Assuming the value of these assets can be described by a geometric Brownian motion, derive a formula for pricing the zero-coupon debt using the above model.
(b) Comment on the model and its potential limitations.
(c) Let \(L=Ne^{-rT}/V_0\) and show that the credit spread on the zero-coupon bond is \[-\frac{1}{T}\ln\!\left[\Phi(d_2)+\frac{\Phi(-d_1)}{L}\right]\]

► Show worked solution
WORKED SOLUTION (not official)

(a) \(B_0=V_0-E_0=V_0-\bigl[V_0\Phi(d_1)-Ne^{-rT}\Phi(d_2)\bigr]=V_0\Phi(-d_1)+Ne^{-rT}\Phi(d_2)\), i.e. the risky bond value equals (the present value of the face value, weighted by survival probability) plus (the firm's assets, weighted by default probability) — equivalently \(B_0=Ne^{-rT}-p(V_0,N,r,\sigma_V,T)\) (risk-free bond minus a put on the firm's assets, by put-call parity).
(b) Limitations: (i) default is assumed to occur only at \(T\) (no early/intermediate default, e.g. from a covenant breach or running out of cash); (ii) capital structure is assumed to be a single zero-coupon bond, unrealistic for firms with multiple, coupon-paying, differently-seniored debt; (iii) \(V_0\) and \(\sigma_V\) are not directly observable/tradeable and must be inferred (typically from equity value and its volatility); (iv) GBM for \(V_t\) implies continuous paths, so credit spreads on very short-maturity debt go to zero, counter to empirical evidence of positive short-term spreads.
(c) The credit spread satisfies \(B_0=Ne^{-(r+s)T}\). Substituting the result from (a) and dividing by \(N\): \(e^{-(r+s)T}=\frac{V_0}{N}\Phi(-d_1)+e^{-rT}\Phi(d_2)\). Multiply by \(e^{rT}\) and use \(L=Ne^{-rT}/V_0 \Rightarrow V_0/N = 1/(Le^{rT})\): \(e^{-sT}=\Phi(d_2)+\frac{\Phi(-d_1)}{L}\), giving \(s=-\frac1T\ln\bigl[\Phi(d_2)+\Phi(-d_1)/L\bigr]\).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Credit Risk & Merton Model | Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Risky bond value from equity-as-call: Equity is a BSM call on the firm's assets: \[E_0 = V_0\Phi(d_1)-Ne^{-rT}\Phi(d_2),\qquad d_1=\frac{\ln(V_0/N)+(r+\tfrac12\sigma_V^2)T}{\sigma_V\sqrt T},\quad d_2=d_1-\sigma_V\sqrt T.\] The balance-sheet identity \(B_0=V_0-E_0\) then gives \[\boxed{B_0 = V_0\bigl(1-\Phi(d_1)\bigr)+Ne^{-rT}\Phi(d_2) = V_0\Phi(-d_1)+Ne^{-rT}\Phi(d_2).}\] Equivalently, since \(p_0=Ne^{-rT}\Phi(-d_2)-V_0\Phi(-d_1)\), also \(B_0=Ne^{-rT}-p_0\): a risky bond is a risk-free bond minus a put on the firm's assets, since limited liability is a put the shareholders own.

(b) Limitations of the model: The capital structure is a single zero-coupon debt; default is possible only at \(T\); \(V_0\) and \(\sigma_V\) are unobservable; and the model implies unrealistically low short-term credit spreads.

(c) Credit spread formula: Define the spread \(s\) via \(B_0=Ne^{-(r+s)T}\), so \[e^{-(r+s)T}=\frac{B_0}{N}=\frac{V_0}{N}\Phi(-d_1)+e^{-rT}\Phi(d_2).\] Substituting the leverage \(L=Ne^{-rT}/V_0\), i.e. \(V_0/N = e^{-rT}/L\), gives \[e^{-(r+s)T}=e^{-rT}\Bigl[\frac{\Phi(-d_1)}{L}+\Phi(d_2)\Bigr].\] Cancelling \(e^{-rT}\) and taking logs, \[e^{-sT}=\Phi(d_2)+\frac{\Phi(-d_1)}{L},\] \[\boxed{s=-\frac1T\ln\!\left[\Phi(d_2)+\frac{\Phi(-d_1)}{L}\right].}\]

Question 3

Assume that the stochastic instantaneous risk-free rate is modelled by the one-factor process \(dr=m(r,t)\,dt+s(r,t)\,dz\), where \(s(r,t)>0\). Consider two zero-coupon bonds with different maturities \(T_i\) and values \(V_i(r,t,T_i)\), \(i\in\{1,2\}\), at \(t<\min[T_1,T_2]\).
(a) Construct a portfolio of a long position in 1 of the first bond and a short of \(\Delta\) of the second. Denote the value of this portfolio \(\Pi\), and use Itô's lemma to find the \(\Delta\) that makes \(\Pi\) locally risk-free.
(b) Find the drift rate of \(\Pi\) with this \(\Delta\), and use an arbitrage argument to characterise it.

► Show worked solution
WORKED SOLUTION (not official)

(a) Using Itô's lemma, \(dV_i = (V_i^{(t)}+mV_i^{(r)}+\tfrac12s^2V_i^{(rr)})dt+sV_i^{(r)}dz\), \(i=1,2\). The portfolio \(\Pi=V_1-\Delta V_2\) has diffusion \(sV_1^{(r)}-\Delta sV_2^{(r)}\), which is zero iff \(\Delta = V_1^{(r)}/V_2^{(r)}\).
(b) Risk-free, the portfolio must earn \(r\): \(d\Pi = r\Pi\,dt\). Expanding and substituting \(\Delta\) from (a), the \(T_1\)- and \(T_2\)-specific terms separate into an equality of the same expression evaluated at \(T_1\) and at \(T_2\), so this expression (the market price of risk, up to sign) cannot depend on \(T\); by no-arbitrage it is a single function \(\lambda(r,t)\) common to all zero-coupon bonds (and indeed to all interest-rate derivatives) in the model.

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Short Rate Models | Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Risk-free hedge ratio \(\Delta\): Itô's lemma on each bond price \(V_i(r,t,T_i)\), \(i=1,2\), gives \[dV_i = \Bigl(V_i^{(t)}+mV_i^{(r)}+\tfrac12s^2V_i^{(rr)}\Bigr)dt + sV_i^{(r)}\,dz.\] The portfolio \(\Pi = V_1-\Delta V_2\) has diffusion coefficient \[sV_1^{(r)} - \Delta sV_2^{(r)}.\] Setting this to zero for a locally risk-free portfolio, \[\boxed{\Delta = \frac{V_1^{(r)}}{V_2^{(r)}}.}\]

(b) No-arbitrage implies the market price of risk is maturity-independent: The drift of \(\Pi\) is \[d\Pi = \Bigl[\Bigl(V_1^{(t)}+mV_1^{(r)}+\tfrac12s^2V_1^{(rr)}\Bigr) - \Delta\Bigl(V_2^{(t)}+mV_2^{(r)}+\tfrac12s^2V_2^{(rr)}\Bigr)\Bigr]dt.\] Since the portfolio is risk-free, it must earn \(r\): \[\Bigl(V_1^{(t)}+mV_1^{(r)}+\tfrac12s^2V_1^{(rr)}\Bigr) - \Delta\Bigl(V_2^{(t)}+mV_2^{(r)}+\tfrac12s^2V_2^{(rr)}\Bigr) = r\bigl(V_1-\Delta V_2\bigr).\] Moving the \(V_1\)-terms to the left and the \(\Delta V_2\)-terms to the right: \[\Bigl(V_1^{(t)}+mV_1^{(r)}+\tfrac12s^2V_1^{(rr)}-rV_1\Bigr) = \Delta\Bigl(V_2^{(t)}+mV_2^{(r)}+\tfrac12s^2V_2^{(rr)}-rV_2\Bigr).\] Substituting \(\Delta=V_1^{(r)}/V_2^{(r)}\): \[V_1^{(t)}+mV_1^{(r)}+\tfrac12s^2V_1^{(rr)}-rV_1 = \frac{V_1^{(r)}}{V_2^{(r)}}\Bigl(V_2^{(t)}+mV_2^{(r)}+\tfrac12s^2V_2^{(rr)}-rV_2\Bigr).\] Dividing both sides by \(V_1^{(r)}\): \[\frac{V_1^{(t)}+mV_1^{(r)}+\tfrac12s^2V_1^{(rr)}-rV_1}{V_1^{(r)}} = \frac{V_2^{(t)}+mV_2^{(r)}+\tfrac12s^2V_2^{(rr)}-rV_2}{V_2^{(r)}}.\] Dividing both sides by \(s\) (to express the denominators as the actual diffusion coefficients \(sV_i^{(r)}\)): \[\frac{V_1^{(t)}+mV_1^{(r)}+\tfrac12s^2V_1^{(rr)}-rV_1}{sV_1^{(r)}} = \frac{V_2^{(t)}+mV_2^{(r)}+\tfrac12s^2V_2^{(rr)}-rV_2}{sV_2^{(r)}}.\] Since \(T_1,T_2\) were arbitrary, this ratio must be the same for every maturity: \[\boxed{\frac{mV^{(r)}+\tfrac12s^2V^{(rr)}+V^{(t)}-rV}{sV^{(r)}} = \lambda(r,t)\quad\text{(independent of }T).}\] \(\lambda(r,t)\) is (minus) the market price of interest-rate risk — the same for every bond and interest-rate derivative in a one-factor model.

June 2014 — Ordinary Exam

Pricing of Financial Assets · University of Copenhagen · 3 questions · 3 hours · No aids permitted

Question 1

Consider a stock with current price \(S_0\) that does not pay dividends. Assume a constant continuously compounded interest rate of \(r\).
(a) Define a forward contract on a stock.
(b) Using an arbitrage argument, find the forward price \(F_0\) at time 0 on a forward contract on the stock that matures at time \(T\).
(c) Consider the value of a forward contract with forward price \(K\) at time \(t<T\), and assume that the stock price follows the geometric Brownian motion \(dS_t=\mu S_t\,dt+\sigma S_t\,dz\). Using Itô's lemma, find the process followed by the value of the forward contract. What will the drift be under the risk-neutral measure?
(d) Assume now that the stock pays a constant continuous dividend rate of \(q\). What will the forward price be?

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Definition (Forward Contract). A forward contract is an agreement to buy or sell a quantity of an asset of a specified quality and type (the underlying asset), delivered at a specified future time \(T\) (the expiration date) and place, for an agreed delivery price \(K\). The price is set at \(t\) at a level \(K=F(t,T)\) such that the value of the contract is zero, i.e. \(V_{F(t,T)}(t,T)=0\); \(F(t,T)\) is called the forward price at \(t\) for delivery at \(T\).
(b) Buy the stock spot financed by a loan repaid at \(T\); the only cash flow is the repayment \(S_0e^{rT}\), leaving you with the stock. Compare with entering a forward contract with delivery at \(T\). To eliminate arbitrage, \(F_0=S_0e^{rT}\).
(c) The value of a forward contract with forward price \(K\) is \(V_t=S_t-Ke^{-r(T-t)}\). Using Itô's lemma, \(dV_t=\bigl[\mu S_t-rKe^{-r(T-t)}\bigr]dt+\sigma S_t\,dz\). For a currently-priced forward (\(K=F_t=e^{r(T-t)}S_t\)), the drift is 0 under the risk-neutral measure (setting \(\mu=r\)), while a non-zero market value grows at rate \(r\).
(d) In the arbitrage argument above you receive too much if you buy the dividend-paying stock outright, but you can repay and reduce the interest payments continuously, leaving only \(S_0e^{(r-q)T}\) to repay at \(T\). So \(F_0=S_0e^{(r-q)T}\).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Forward Pricing | Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Forward contract — definition: A forward contract is an agreement to buy or sell an asset at a future time \(T\) for a delivery price \(K\) fixed today. The price is set at inception so the contract has zero value: \[K = F(t,T), \qquad V_{F(t,T)}(t,T) = 0.\]

(b) Forward price with no dividends: Borrow \(S_0\) at the risk-free rate \(r\) and use the proceeds to buy one share. At time \(T\), the position is worth \[S_T - S_0e^{rT}.\] A long forward has payoff \[S_T - F_0.\] By no-arbitrage, the payoffs must be equal: \[S_T - S_0e^{rT} = S_T - F_0.\] Therefore, \[\boxed{F_0 = S_0e^{rT}}.\]

(c) Process for the forward value under Itô: The value of a forward with delivery price \(K\) is \[V_t = S_t - Ke^{-r(T-t)}.\] Its partial derivatives are \[\frac{\partial V_t}{\partial S_t}=1,\qquad \frac{\partial V_t}{\partial t}=-rKe^{-r(T-t)},\qquad \frac{\partial^2 V_t}{\partial S_t^2}=0.\] By Itô's lemma, \[dV_t = \left[\frac{\partial V_t}{\partial t}+\mu S_t\frac{\partial V_t}{\partial S_t}+\tfrac12\sigma^2S_t^2\frac{\partial^2 V_t}{\partial S_t^2}\right]dt+\sigma S_t\frac{\partial V_t}{\partial S_t}\,dz.\] Substituting the partial derivatives found above: \[dV_t = \bigl[-rKe^{-r(T-t)}+\mu S_t\cdot 1+\tfrac12\sigma^2S_t^2\cdot 0\bigr]dt+\sigma S_t\cdot 1\,dz,\] \[\boxed{dV_t = \bigl[\mu S_t-rKe^{-r(T-t)}\bigr]dt + \sigma S_t\,dz.}\] For a freshly-priced forward, \(K=F_t=e^{r(T-t)}S_t\), so \[Ke^{-r(T-t)} = e^{r(T-t)}S_t\cdot e^{-r(T-t)} = S_t.\] Substituting this and setting \(\mu=r\) under the risk-neutral measure: \[dV_t = \bigl[rS_t-rS_t\bigr]dt+\sigma S_t\,dz,\] \[\boxed{dV_t = \sigma S_t\,dz\quad\text{under }\mathbb{Q},\text{ when }K=F_t.}\] A contract with nonzero current value instead grows at rate \(r\).

(d) Forward price with a continuous dividend \(q\): Buying \(e^{-qT}\) shares today and reinvesting the dividends grows the holding to exactly one share by \(T\). This costs \(S_0e^{-qT}\) today, financed by borrowing at \(r\), so the debt at \(T\) is \[S_0e^{-qT}e^{rT} = S_0e^{(r-q)T}.\] By no-arbitrage this must equal the forward price: \[\boxed{F_0 = S_0e^{(r-q)T}.}\]

Question 2

(a) Let the probability of a borrower not defaulting at or before time \(t\) be given by \(V(t)\). What is the probability that the borrower will default between time \(t\) and \(t+\Delta t\), conditional on not being in default at time \(t\)? Use this to define the continuously compounded default hazard rate.
(b) How, and under which assumptions, may we estimate the hazard rate from the interest rate spread on bonds issued by the borrower? Under what probability measure would we say this estimate is derived? Compare this to a hazard rate that is derived from default frequencies and recovery ratios published by a rating agency.
(c) In one model by Merton, the value of a claim on a company with limited liability is modelled using a variation of the Black-Scholes-Merton option model. What is the option feature embedded in such a claim? What parameters that are not directly observable must be determined to price the claim in this model? Comment on the model.

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) The conditional default probability from \(t\) to \(t+\Delta t\) is \(\dfrac{V(t)-V(t+\Delta t)}{V(t)}\). Given \(V(t)\), the hazard rate is the rate of decay of survivors; assuming differentiability, \(\lambda(t) = -\dfrac{\partial V(t)/\partial t}{V(t)}\). This is motivated by considering the discrete time step and letting it approach zero.
(b) Under a risk-neutral probability measure, the hazard rate may be derived from prices of traded assets, e.g. bonds from the company. Assuming the recovery rate \(R\) is known, estimate the average hazard rate as the one that makes the discounted expected payoff (accounting for defaults) equal the price of a risk-free bond; if the risky bond pays a spread \(s\) over the risk-free bond, \(\bar\lambda = s/(1-R)\). You may similarly bootstrap a hazard rate structure from a term structure of credit spreads. This analysis is conducted under a risk-neutral (\(\mathbb{Q}\)) measure, so \((\lambda,R)\) will not equal real-world (\(\mathbb{P}\)) frequencies and averages published by rating agencies.
(c) In the Merton model, risky debt is modelled as the value of the entity's assets less the value of equity; due to limited liability, the owners effectively hold a call option on the assets. Assuming a single zero-coupon debt of face value \(D\) maturing at \(T\), the BSM model applies, with the (typically unknown) asset value and asset volatility as underlying/volatility. From Itô's lemma you can derive a relationship between equity volatility and asset volatility; with equity value and equity volatility known from markets, you have two equations in two unknowns, solved numerically for the value of the debt. A problem with the model: values are assumed to follow Itô processes with continuous sample paths, making the likelihood of default over a short step very small (no "jump to default") and credit spreads go to zero as maturity goes to zero — not in line with empirical evidence (possibly due to asset values that jump at default, or asymmetric information between owners and creditors, revealed at default).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Credit Risk & Hazard Rates | Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Hazard rate — definition: The probability of default between \(t\) and \(t+\Delta t\), given survival to \(t\), is \[\frac{V(t)-V(t+\Delta t)}{V(t)}.\] Taking \(\Delta t\to0\), the hazard rate is the instantaneous rate of decay of survivors: \[\boxed{\lambda(t) = -\frac{\partial V(t)/\partial t}{V(t)}.}\]

(b) Hazard rate from the credit spread: If the risky bond pays a spread \(s\) over the risk-free rate and the recovery rate (fraction of exposure recovered on default) is \(R\), the expected loss rate per unit time is the hazard rate times the fractional loss given default: \[\text{expected loss rate} = \bar\lambda(1-R).\] No-arbitrage requires this expected loss rate to be exactly compensated by the extra continuously-compounded yield \(s\) demanded on the risky bond: \[s = \bar\lambda(1-R).\] Solving for \(\bar\lambda\): \[\boxed{\bar\lambda = \frac{s}{1-R}.}\] A full term structure of spreads can similarly be bootstrapped into a term structure of hazard rates. This is a risk-neutral (\(\mathbb{Q}\)) estimate, so \((\lambda,R)\) generally differ from the real-world (\(\mathbb{P}\)) default frequencies and recoveries published by rating agencies.

(c) Merton model — embedded option and limitations: Equity is modelled as a call option on the firm's assets \(V_0\), struck at the debt's face value \(D\), maturing at \(T\): \[E_0 = V_0\Phi(d_1)-De^{-rT}\Phi(d_2).\] The asset value \(V_0\) and asset volatility \(\sigma_V\) are not directly observable. They are solved for numerically from two equations — the BSM equity formula above and the Itô-derived link between equity volatility and asset volatility — given the observed equity value and equity volatility. The model's main limitation is that \[\boxed{\text{continuous (Itô) asset paths}\ \Rightarrow\ \text{near-zero short-term default risk}\ \Rightarrow\ \text{unrealistically low short-term spreads.}}\]

Question 3

(a) Give a definition of an interest rate swaption (also called a swap option).
(b) Give an argument that a receiver swaption can be seen as a call option on a properly defined fixed-rate bond. What can you say about the coupon and principal of such a bond?
(c) Let \(PS\) denote the value of a swaption to pay a fixed rate \(s_K\) and receive LIBOR between times \(T_1\) and \(T_2\), let \(RS\) denote the value of a swaption to receive a fixed rate \(s_K\) and pay LIBOR between times \(T_1\) and \(T_2\), and let \(RFS\) denote the value of a forward-starting swap that receives a fixed rate \(s_K\) and pays LIBOR between times \(T_1\) and \(T_2\). Assume no arbitrage opportunities and (i) show that \(PS+RFS=RS\), and (ii) deduce that \(PS=RS\) when \(s_K\) equals the current forward swap rate.

► Show official solution
OFFICIAL MARKING GUIDE

(a) Definition (Swaption). A payer (receiver) swaption or swap option gives the holder the right to enter into a payer (receiver) interest rate swap in the future.
(b) If the fixed-rate coupon on the bond equals the swap rate, the principal equals the par value of the bond (barring credit risk, liquidity considerations, etc.). The receiver swaption can be seen as an option to receive this bond by delivery of the principal (idealised to equal the continuing value of the floating leg). Both the swaption and the bond option increase in value as longer rates fall.
(c) Consider the payments from \(T_1\) to \(T_2\) depending on the realised swap rate \(s_{T_1}\) at \(T_1\): if \(s_K<s_{T_1}\), \(PS\) pays \(s_K\) and receives LIBOR, \(RFS\) receives \(s_K\) and pays LIBOR, \(RS=0\); if \(s_K\ge s_{T_1}\), \(PS=0\), \(RFS\) receives \(s_K\) and pays LIBOR, \(RS\) receives \(s_K\) and pays LIBOR. In both cases, the combined payments of \(PS\) and \(RFS\) equal the payments of \(RS\). So, barring arbitrage, \(PS+RFS=RS\). If \(s_K\) is the current forward swap rate, \(RFS\) is worth zero by definition, so \(PS=RS\).

► Show step-by-step solution
STEP-BY-STEP
📖 Study Guide: Interest Rate Derivatives — Swaptions | Flashcards: review these topics in the Flashcards tab

(a) Swaption — definition: A payer (receiver) swaption gives the holder the right, but not the obligation, to enter a payer (receiver) interest rate swap at a future date.

(b) Receiver swaption as a bond call: If the bond's fixed coupon equals the swap rate, its principal equals its par value. A receiver swaption can then be seen as \[\boxed{\text{an option to receive this bond in exchange for its principal (par).}}\] Both the swaption and the corresponding bond option increase in value as longer-term rates fall.

(c) Swaption parity — \(PS+RFS=RS\): Compare the payments from \(T_1\) to \(T_2\) under the realised swap rate \(s_{T_1}\). If \(s_K<s_{T_1}\), \(PS\) pays \(s_K\) and receives LIBOR, \(RFS\) receives \(s_K\) and pays LIBOR, and \(RS=0\). If \(s_K\ge s_{T_1}\), \(PS=0\), while \(RFS\) and \(RS\) both receive \(s_K\) and pay LIBOR. In both cases \(PS\)'s payments plus \(RFS\)'s payments equal \(RS\)'s payments exactly. By no-arbitrage, \[\boxed{PS+RFS=RS.}\] If \(s_K\) equals the current forward swap rate, then \(RFS=0\) by definition, so \[\boxed{PS=RS.}\]

Flashcard Trainer
